Nonstationary Markov Partitions and Multidimensional Continued Fraction Algorithms
Pierre Arnoux, Valérie Berthé, Milton Minervino, Wolfgang Steiner, Jörg M. Thuswaldner

Abstract
. It is well known from results of Sinaĭ and Bowen that a hyperbolic toral automorphism admits a Markov partition. Our aim is to generalize this concept to the nonstationary case, i.e., we associate Markov partitions to nonstationary sequences of toral automorphisms. Special emphasis is placed on sequences of toral automorphisms produced by strongly convergent multidimensional continued fraction algorithms. The convergence of the algorithms is expressed in terms of a Pisot type condition which yields hyperbolicity for the nonstationary dynamics. For a multidimensional continued fraction map, we first consider its natural extension, whose orbits are given by bi-infinite sequences of matrices with determinant ± 1 \pm 1 . The hyperbolicity property allows us to interpret almost every orbit of this natural extension as an Anosov mapping family, i.e., as a bi-infinite sequence of toral automorphisms with well-defined stable and unstable manifolds. We prove that this Anosov mapping family admits a bi-infinite sequence of explicit nonstationary Markov partitions. To obtain the atoms of the Markov partitions, a combinatorial structure, expressed in terms of substitutions and 𝒮 \mathcal{S} -adic dynamical systems, has to be superimposed on the Anosov mapping family. In particular, the atoms of the Markov partitions are geometric realizations of 𝒮 \mathcal{S} -adic dynamical systems, defined by suspensions of 𝒮 \mathcal{S} -adic Rauzy fractals. These Markov partitions then provide a symbolic model as a nonstationary edge shift for the Anosov mapping family. As a guiding example, allowing explicit realization results, we use Anosov mapping families on 2- and 3-dimensional tori associated to various versions of the Brun continued fraction algorithm.

[name=notation, title=Notation, columns=2]

The authors are indebted to Niels Langeveld, Maria Clara Werneck, and Reem Yassawi for their careful reading of the manuscript.

Chapter I
Introduction

The classical theory of
dynamical systems
deals with a space
X
together with a single transformation
f:X\to X
acting on that space. The iterates of this transformation
f
on a given point give rise to
orbits
, whose dynamical properties can be studied. It is also meaningful to study a “nonstationary” analog of this situation. Namely, in this monograph we consider a sequence of spaces
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
equipped with a sequence of transformations
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
that map along this sequence of spaces according to the diagram

(
*
)

\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{-2}}X_{-1}\xrightarrow{f_{-1}}X_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{0}}X_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}\cdots.

At first glance, because of the lack of recurrence, the dynamics of such a
mapping family
is trivial. Nevertheless, for the case of so-called
Anosov mapping families
one can find the whole richness of a classical recurrent dynamical system defined by a single map
f\colon X\to X
. An Anosov mapping family is given by a diagram of the form (

∗

‣
I
), for which the transformations
f_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, are diffeomorphisms along a sequence
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
of compact Riemannian manifolds equipped with an invariant sequence of splittings of the tangent bundle into expanding and contracting subspaces. Anosov mapping families have been introduced in
[
AF05
]
as a nonstationary generalization of Anosov diffeomorphisms and have been further studied for instance in
[
Fis09
,
Ste11
,
KL16
,
MA19
,
CRV19
]
.
We focus here on the case of
linear Anosov mapping families
, i.e., on Anosov mapping families whose transformations
f_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, are linear mappings. For a given linear Anosov mapping family we will construct a nonstationary version of a Markov partition, which provides a symbolic model of this mapping family in the form of a nonstationary symbolic shift.

In nonstationary dynamics the different maps
f_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, are often chosen according to some probability process, or by a dynamical system equipped with a certain skew product construction, such as developed for instance in the setting of random matrices or of differentiable dynamical systems; see e.g.
[
Kif86
,
Arn98
,
KL06
,
Via14
]
.

Our motivation as well as our guiding examples come from the theory of
unimodular multidimensional continued fraction algorithms
. For a given vector of real numbers, a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm provides increasingly good rational Diophantine approximations in form of vectors whose rational entries all have the same denominator. Classical continued fraction algorithms provide extremely good (and even the best) rational Diophantine approximations; see
[
Cas72
,
Lag93
,
Sch00
,
BDK+24
]
and Chapter
III
for details.

To be more precise, let
d\geq 2
be fixed. A multidimensional continued fraction algorithm dynamically generates an infinite sequence
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
of
d{\times}d
matrices with integer entries and determinant
\pm 1
in a way that each column of the product
M_{0}\cdots M_{n-1}
contains the numerators and the denominator of an
n
-th rational Diophantine approximation (
n\in\mathbb{N}
).
1
1

1

As we will see later, the negative part of the sequence
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is related to the natural extension of the algorithm.
The matrices
M_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, can be regarded as linear automorphisms that map between elements of a sequence
(\mathbb{T}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
of
d
-dimensional
tori, according to the diagram
2
2

2

It will become apparent later why we take the inverse of the matrices here.

(
**
)

\cdots\xrightarrow{M_{-2}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}_{-1}\xrightarrow{M_{-1}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}_{0}\xrightarrow{M_{0}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}_{1}\xrightarrow{M_{1}^{-1}}\cdots.

Therefore, a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm defines mapping families. The Anosov property of these mapping families is related to convergence properties of the algorithm, which also
play an important role in the theory of Diophantine approximation. Well known notions of convergence depend on whether the angles between the (column) vectors of the matrices
M_{0}\cdots M_{n-1}
tend to
0
(
weak convergence
), or whether the distances between the (column) vectors tend to
0
(
strong convergence
); see Definition
7.7
. A gauge for the “strongness” of convergence of a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm is provided by its (first and second) Lyapunov exponents; see
[
Lag93
]
. In fact, Lyapunov exponents describe the asymptotic behavior of the singular values of large products of matrices. If the second Lyapunov exponent of an algorithm is (generically) negative, we say that it satisfies the
Pisot condition
.
This condition, which plays a crucial role in our investigations, yields hyperbolicity for the nonstationary dynamics of the algorithm, and, more precisely, guarantees that the mapping families in (

∗
⁣
∗

‣
I
) are (generically) “codimension one” linear Anosov mapping families in the sense that in the associated splittings of tangent spaces, the contracting spaces have dimension
1
.

As above, let
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a sequence of
d{\times}d
-matrices of determinant
\pm 1
generated by the natural extension of a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm with a strong convergence property. We call such a sequence an
orbit
of the algorithm.
If we assume that
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is periodic with period
p
, we can block this sequence by forming products of blocks of consecutive matrices of length
p
. By this blocking,
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
becomes the constant sequence
(M)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, where
M=M_{0}\cdots M_{p-1}
is a hyperbolic integer matrix of determinant
\pm 1
. In this case, the nonstationary diagram (

∗
⁣
∗

‣
I
) defined by
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
becomes stationary, i.e., it degenerates to the hyperbolic toral automorphism
M^{-1}:\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{T}^{d}
on the
d
-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
. Since it is well known that each hyperbolic toral automorphism admits a
Markov partition

[
Sin68a
,
Sin68b
,
Bow70
,
Bed86
]
, one can associate a Markov partition to a periodic orbit of a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm. As an illustration, we refer to
[
IO93
]
, where this was done for periodic orbits of the Brun continued fraction algorithm.

In the present work, we generalize this and associate nonstationary Markov partitions with arbitrary orbits of multidimensional continued fraction algorithms with a strong convergence property (namely, the Pisot condition). As mentioned before, by strong convergence, a (generic) orbit of the algorithm
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
gives rise to an Anosov mapping family (

∗
⁣
∗

‣
I
). In particular, it implies the existence of a well-defined invariant sequence of splittings of the tangent bundles of the tori in stable and unstable manifolds with a uniform upper bound for the contraction and a uniform lower bound for the expansion. As in the stationary case, nonstationary Markov partitions then allow us to build symbolic models for linear Anosov mapping families and, hence, for orbits of multidimensional continued fraction algorithms, as nonstationary edge shifts. Nonstationary edge shifts can be considered as nonstationary subshifts of finite type; see e.g.
[
AF05
,
Fis09
]
. They are also related to the (two-sided) constructions of Markov compacta and Bratteli diagrams introduced in
[
Buf13
,
Buf14
]
.

For the case
d=2
, this has been done already in
[
AF05
]
; see also
[
AF01
]
. In these papers a linear Anosov mapping family on
2
-dimensional tori is associated to each orbit of the classical continued fraction algorithm, and nonstationary Markov partitions with polygonal atoms are constructed for the associated sequences of
2{\times}2
matrices. The situation becomes much more delicate for
d\geq 3
. Indeed, as in the case of a single toral hyperbolic automorphism, the atoms of nonstationary Markov partitions are no longer polygonal in dimension
d\geq 3
. Our aim is to prove that these atoms are provided by so-called
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy fractals
that were first defined in
[
BST19
]
; see also
[
BST23
]
.

The sections of this introduction are organized as follows. Section
1
briefly introduces Markov partitions for hyperbolic toral automorphisms. In Section
2
, we review the rich dynamical interpretations of the classical continued fraction algorithm, Section
3
then discusses how to generalize this setting to higher dimensions.
Section
4
introduces the main results of this monograph, and Section
5
lays out its overall organization.

1.
Markov partitions for hyperbolic toral automorphisms

One of our main aims is to associate a nonstationary Markov partition to a sequence of toral automorphisms. This generalizes the concept of a (stationary) Markov partition for a single toral automorphism, which we discuss briefly in the present section. A
toral automorphism

f
is an invertible linear map on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}=\mathbb{R}^{d}/\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, i.e.,
f:\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{T}^{d}
,
\mathbf{x}\mapsto M\mathbf{x}\pmod{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
, for some integer matrix
M
with determinant
\pm 1
. It is called
hyperbolic
if none of the eigenvalues of the matrix
M
is on the unit circle.
As already mentioned before, it is well known since the work of Sinaĭ and Bowen
[
Sin68a
,
Sin68b
,
Bow70
]
that each hyperbolic toral automorphism
f
admits a
Markov partition
. If the Markov partition is fine enough, then it provides a symbolic representation of
f
in the sense that it allows to conjugate the dynamical system
(\mathbb{T}^{d},f)
to a symbolic dynamical system; cf. Section
6.3
. To illustrate this, we discuss Markov partitions of (the inverse of)
3
3

3

We consider
f(\mathbf{x})=M^{-1}\mathbf{x}
instead of
f(\mathbf{x})=M\mathbf{x}
, with
M=\big(\begin{smallmatrix}2&1\\
1&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)
, for consistency with the rest of the paper.
Arnold’s famous
cat map

(1.1)

f:\,\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{T}^{2},\quad\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\end{pmatrix}\pmod{\mathbb{Z}^{2}},

a hyperbolic toral automorphism of
\mathbb{T}^{2}
. For an extensive survey of the theory of Markov partitions for toral automorphisms, we refer to
[
Adl98
]
.

R_{1}
R_{2}
\xrightarrow[\displaystyle f^{-1}]{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}2&1\\
1&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}

\{R_{1},R_{2}\}
for the cat map and its image.

Consider the collection
\{R_{1},R_{2}\}
of the two disjoint open squares depicted in the left side of Figure
1
. The construction of the squares
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
is surveyed for instance in
[
KH95
, Chapter 2, Section 5d]
. The collection
\{R_{1},R_{2}\}
forms a
topological partition
of
\mathbb{T}^{2}
in the sense that
L=\overline{R_{1}}\cup\overline{R_{2}}
forms a fundamental domain of
\mathbb{R}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
R_{1}\cap R_{2}=\emptyset
; see Figure
1
.
To illustrate what happens when we apply the cat map
f^{-1}
to
R_{i}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
, we do this in two steps.
We first consider
R_{i}
as a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and apply the matrix
M
to this set. Because
M
is hyperbolic and
R_{i}
is chosen in a way that its edges are parallel to eigenvectors of
M
, the image
MR_{i}
is a thinner and longer version of
R_{i}
whose edges are parallel to the edges of
R_{i}
; see the middle of Figure
1
. To get the representative of the image
f^{-1}(R_{i})
of
R_{i}
in the fundamental region
L
, we have to “restack” pieces of
MR_{i}
back to
L
. This leads to the right side of Figure
1
, which shows an important feature of the collection
\{R_{1},R_{2}\}
: The collection
\{f^{-1}(R_{1}),f^{-1}(R_{2})\}
of its images tessellates
L
in a way that
f^{-1}(R_{i})
consists of finitely many rectangles each of which is a subset of some set
R_{j}
and has “full height”. A similar “full width” property holds if we apply
f
to the partition
\{R_{1},R_{2}\}
, see Figure
2
.

The Markov partition
\{R_{1},R_{2}\}
and its image under

.

The “full height” and “full width” property have an important implication: If we choose
i_{-1},i_{0},i_{1}\in\{1,2\}
, then

f(R_{i_{-1}})\cap R_{i_{0}}\neq\emptyset,\,R_{i_{0}}\cap f^{-1}(R_{i_{1}})\neq\emptyset\ \mbox{implies}\ f(R_{i_{-1}})\cap(R_{i_{0}})\cap f^{-1}(R_{i_{1}})\neq\emptyset.

This implication is illustrated in Figure
3
.

Illustration of the intersection properties of the Markov partition
\{R_{1},R_{2}\}
.

This tessellation property is even maintained if we consider iterates
f^{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
: The collection
\{f^{n}(R_{1}),f^{n}(R_{2})\}
tessellates
L
in a way that the images
f^{n}(R_{i})
consist of finitely many “full height” (if
n<0
) or “full width” (if
n>0
) rectangles, each of which is a subset of some
R_{j}
.
This fact can be made exact in the following way. For each
n\geq 1
and each sequence of elements
i_{0},\ldots,i_{n}\in\{1,2\}
, the property

(1.2)

R_{i_{k}}\cap f^{-1}(R_{i_{k+1}})\neq\emptyset\quad\mbox{for all}\ k\in\{0,\ldots,n{-}1\}

implies that

(1.3)

R_{i_{0}}\cap f^{-1}(R_{i_{1}})\cap\cdots\cap f^{-n}(R_{i_{n}})\neq\emptyset.

This implication makes, by definition,
\{R_{1},R_{2}\}
a
Markov partition
for the hyperbolic toral automorphism
f
.

The partition
\{R_{1},R_{2}\}
has a drawback. As can be seen in the first panel of Figure
3
, the image
f^{-1}(R_{1})
intersects
R_{1}
in the two open rectangles
R^{\prime}_{1}
and
R^{\prime}_{2}
. (This is due to the
(1,1)
entry of
M
being
2
.)
This leads to ambiguities when one tries to use this partition to construct a symbolic representation of
f
.
More precisely, if we consider a sequence
(i_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\{1,2\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
f^{n}(x)\in R_{i_{n}}
for some
x\in R^{\prime}_{1}
, then we also find a point
x\in R^{\prime}_{2}
with the same property.
To avoid such ambiguities, we need to refine the partition
\{R_{1},R_{2}\}
.
It turns out that the partition
\{R^{\prime}_{1},\dots,R^{\prime}_{5}\}
consisting of the five rectangles in the first panel of Figure
3
is an appropriate refinement. Indeed,
\{R^{\prime}_{1},\dots,R^{\prime}_{5}\}
forms a Markov partition again, and this time
f(R^{\prime}_{j})
intersects
R^{\prime}_{i}
in at most one rectangle. This entails that the Markov partition
\{R^{\prime}_{1},\dots,R^{\prime}_{5}\}
is
generating
in the sense that

\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\bigcap_{|k|\leq n}f^{-k}(S_{i_{k}})}

contains at most one point for each sequence
(i_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\{1,\dots,5\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. Thus almost every element of
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
can be “coded” by a sequence
(i_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\{1,\dots,5\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. It turns out that the closure of the set of sequences in
\{1,\dots,5\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
that occur as codings of orbits is a
shift of finite type
, i.e., it is a subset
X_{M}\subset\{1,\dots,5\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
whose elements are characterized by the fact that they do not contain certain substrings (of length
2
). In other words, the shift space
(X_{M},\Sigma)
with the shift map
\Sigma:X_{M}\to X_{M}
,
(i_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mapsto(i_{n+1})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, is a symbolic representation of
(\mathbb{T}^{2},f)
in the sense that it allows to conjugate
f
to a shift of finite type. The set
X_{M}
is explicitly described in
[
KH95
, Chapter 2, Section 5d]
.

We develop the theory of nonstationary Markov partitions in Section
6.2
, consider symbolic representations in Section
6.3
and apply it to sequences of toral automorphisms in Sections
15
and
16
. Its relation with multidimensional continued fraction algorithms will be described in the subsequent section of this introduction.

2.
Dynamical interpretations of the classical continued fraction algorithm

One motivation for the present work is to give conditions on multidimensional continued fraction algorithms that allow an extension of the dynamical interpretations of the classical continued fraction algorithm, such as described for instance in
[
AN93
,
Arn94
,
AF01
]
, to these algorithms. In the classical case, these dynamical interpretations rely on two ingredients. Firstly, the classical continued fraction algorithm can be interpreted in terms of a cascade of
inductions
(i.e.,
first return maps
) of circle rotations (cf. Definition
8.7
), an insight that goes back to Rauzy, see e.g.
[
AR91
]
. Secondly, it can be viewed as a first return map to a section of the
geodesic flow
on the unit tangent bundle of the modular surface. Geometrically, this return map can be viewed as a restacking process that is interlinked with a nonstationary Markov partition. This idea dates back to
[
Art24
]
and has been pursued e.g. in
[
AF82
,
AF84
,
Ser85
,
BKS91
,
AF01
,
AF05
]
. We will now give a short account of these interpretations.

The classical continued fraction algorithm can be viewed as a map acting on the parameter space
[0,1)
of a family of circle rotations, in particular, of the rotations
\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha}:\,x\mapsto x{+}\alpha\mod 1
on the one-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
; here,
\alpha\in[0,1)
is the parameter. The continued fraction expansion of a real number
\alpha
is linked with the recurrence properties of the rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha}
. In particular, the iterates
n
for which
\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha}^{n}(0)
is closer to
0
than each of the previous iterates
\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha}^{m}(0)
,
m\in\{1,\dots,n{-}1\}
, are just the denominators of the convergents in the continued fraction expansion of
\alpha
.

When viewing the rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha}
as an exchange of two intervals on
[0,1)
, the
additive
or
Farey
continued fraction algorithm (see e.g.
[
Sch00
]
and Section
9.1
) can be recovered by considering the first return map of
\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha}
on the larger one of the intervals
[0,1{-}\alpha)
and
[1{-}\alpha,1)
(cf. Definition
8.7
), renormalized in a way that the length of this interval becomes
1
. Since this return map is again an exchange of two intervals, it can again be viewed as a rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
for some other parameter
\alpha^{\prime}\in[0,1)
. The ensuing map
\alpha\mapsto\alpha^{\prime}
is the
Farey map

(2.1)

T_{\mathrm{F}}:\,[0,1)\to[0,1],\quad x\mapsto\begin{cases}\frac{x}{1-x},&x<\frac{1}{2},\\
\frac{1-x}{x},&x\geq\frac{1}{2}\\
\end{cases}

and, iterating this induction process yields a cascade of rotations that models the iterates of the Farey map; see e.g.
[
AFH99
,
Arn01
]
.

One can give a very explicit symbolic model of this process: The rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha}
, viewed as an exchange of two intervals, can be coded by these two intervals on a two-letter alphabet. This coding gives the classical
Sturmian sequences
; see Definition
11.2
.
Sturmian sequences can also be defined by attaching substitutions to the
2{\times}2
integer matrices generated by the Farey continued fraction algorithm. This is done by using the two
Sturmian substitutions

(2.2)

\sigma_{\mathrm{F},1}:\begin{cases}1\mapsto 1,\\
2\mapsto 21,\end{cases}\qquad\sigma_{\mathrm{F},2}:\begin{cases}1\mapsto 12,\\
2\mapsto 2,\end{cases}

that are related to the two alternatives of the Farey map, that is, to the two matrices

(2.3)

M_{\mathrm{F},1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\quad\mbox{and}\quad M_{\mathrm{F},2}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}.

corresponding to the homographies in (
2.1
).
With help of the substitutions in (
2.2
), we can define an induction process that forms a symbolic version of the Farey map; see
[
AF01
,
Fog02
]
and Example
11.3
.
This is a special instance of Rauzy induction, as performed more generally in the setting of interval exchange transformations
[
Yoc06
]
. Roughly speaking, a sequence which is obtained as a coding of a map can be recovered by applying a substitution to the sequence coding the induced map; each step in the induction process corresponds to a “desubstitution” of the original sequence.

By accelerating the Farey map, we obtain the
Gauss map

(2.4)

T_{\mathrm{G}}:\,(0,1)\to[0,1),\quad\alpha\mapsto\frac{1}{\alpha}-\Big\lfloor\frac{1}{\alpha}\Big\rfloor

of the classical continued fraction algorithm (cf. Example
9.3
) that is related to an analogous induction process leading again to a symbolic interpretation in terms of Sturmian sequences. In this case, one “desubstitutes” by certain compositions of the substitutions in (
2.2
) that correspond to the partial quotients of the continued fraction expansion; see Example
11.3
for more details on Sturmian sequences. Summing up, by “superimposing” the combinatorics of Sturmian sequences, we can interpret the classical continued fraction algorithm as acting on a sequence of induced rotations.

To illustrate the link between the classical continued fraction algorithm and nonstationary Markov partitions via first return maps of the geodesic flow on the unit tangent bundle of the modular surface, we start with
[
AF05
]
. In this paper, another type of symbolic coding associated to continued fraction algorithms is considered. The authors start from a
2
-dimensional hyperbolic toral automorphism. Clearly, the slopes of the stable and unstable foliations of such an automorphism are always quadratic irrationals. As we saw in Section
1
, the associated Markov partition is made of two boxes, with axes parallel to these slopes, and whose union forms an
L
-shaped region. In
[
AF05
]
, the authors ask if this picture can be generalized to arbitrary slopes. This leads them to replace a single toral automorphism by a sequence of toral automorphisms of the form (

∗
⁣
∗

‣
I
). Starting from two given arbitrary slopes
G^{s}
and
G^{u}
for the stable and unstable foliations, respectively, they use the classical continued fraction algorithm in order to set up a sequence of
2{\times}2
-matrices
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
having determinant
\pm 1
whose associated linear Anosov mapping family (

∗
⁣
∗

‣
I
) has
G^{s}
as stable and
G^{u}
as unstable foliation. Besides that, they are able to extend classical Markov partitions and associate a sequence of (nonstationary) Markov partitions to these Anosov mapping families. As in the stationary case illustrated in Section
1
, these nonstationary Markov partitions are
L
-shaped regions whose boundary pieces are parallel to
G^{s}
and
G^{u}
.

\xrightarrow{\displaystyle f_{0}}
\xrightarrow{\mbox{restacking}}
\xrightarrow{\mbox{recoloring}}
\xrightarrow{\displaystyle f_{1}}

Figure 4.
Illustration of the restacking and renormalization process on the atoms of the nonstationary Markov partition furnished by the classical continued fraction algorithm. The “recoloring” is just done in order to visualize the Markov partition of the restacked
L
-shaped region.

The
(n{+}1)
-st Markov partition of the sequence emerges from the
n
-th one by a
restacking and renormalization process
(see e.g.
[
AF05
,
Thu20
]
), which is illustrated in Figure
4
. However, contrary to the stationary Markov partition (like the one in Figure
1
), the first
L
-shaped region in Figure
4
is not the same as the fourth
L
-shaped region. This reflects the “nonstationarity” of this sequence of Markov partitions.

On this restacking and renormalization process visualized in Figure
4
we can see the Gauss map on the axis parallel to
G_{s}
. Moreover, it can be reinterpreted as a section of a geodesic flow on the unit tangent bundle of the modular surface. To make this precise, it is convenient to transform the
L
-shaped regions affinely (and volume preserving) in a way that the boundary pieces become axis parallel; see Figure
5
.

1
\vphantom{1}\alpha
\xrightarrow{\mbox{restacking}}
\vphantom{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\alpha
1{-}\lfloor\frac{1}{\alpha}\rfloor\alpha
\xrightarrow[\mbox{and recoloring}]{\mbox{renormalizing}}
\vphantom{\frac{1}{\alpha}}1
\frac{1}{\alpha}{-}\lfloor\frac{1}{\alpha}\rfloor

Figure 5.
The axis-parallel version of the restacking process of Figure
4
.

On the
x
-axis, this transformed restacking process performs one step of the underlying classical continued fraction algorithm, i.e., the Gauss map
g
. By restacking, the interval on
x
-axis becomes smaller. It is this smaller interval to which we induced the rotation earlier, so this restacking process is intimately related to the induction process described above. The whole restacking process can be seen as a
natural extension of the Gauss map
, that is, an invertible dynamical system that admits the original noninvertible (Gauss) dynamics as a factor; see Section
9.2
for precise definitions.

Because each of these
L
-shaped regions are fundamental domains of a lattice, it induces a tiling of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
of covolume
1
; see Figure
6
. Using this lattice viewpoint, the above restacking and renormalization process can now be reinterpreted as a section of a geodesic flow. Indeed, it is well known that the unit tangent bundle of the modular surface can be modeled by the space
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})\backslash\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
that we just encountered.

Figure 6.
A tiling with covolume
1
induced by an
L
-shaped Markov partition. The restacked partition, which is illustrated in bright colors, represents the same lattice.

In this model, the geodesic flow is given by the right action of the diagonal group
g_{t}=\mathrm{diag}(e^{t/2},e^{-t/2})
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
. The action of
g_{t}
consists in expanding the horizontal coordinates and contracting the vertical ones. Each point in
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})\backslash\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
corresponds to a flat torus represented by an appropriate
L
-shaped region. One can now find a transverse section to the geodesic flow on the modular surface such that the first return map of the flow can be viewed geometrically by restacking and renormalizing the
L
-shaped nonstationary Markov partitions as illustrated in Figure
5
. This return map provides a geometric model of the natural extension of the Gauss map by coding geodesics as bi-infinite sequences of symbols in terms of the continued fraction expansions.

On top of this, in each
L
-shaped region, i.e., in each point in
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})\backslash\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, there is a vertical flow, whose first return map to a well chosen transverse section (which is given by the bottom line of the
L
-shaped region) is the rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha}
we considered in the first place. The particular case of periodic orbits has been studied in detail, and according to the classical theorem of Galois, they correspond to special quadratic units. For periodic orbits, the corresponding rotations are self-induced, i.e., they are conjugate to their first return map on some subspace. They also correspond to periodic geodesics on the modular surface.

Note also that the modular surface can be interpreted as the moduli space of the torus (as a quotient of the Teichmüller space by the modular group), and an element of its tangent space corresponds to a torus equipped with two transverse measured foliations, a
vertical
and a
horizontal
foliation; the first return map of the flow along the vertical foliation on a suitable horizontal interval is again the rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha}
considered above. In this moduli space of the torus, a starting point on a closed geodesic corresponds to a pair of transverse measured foliations on the torus, and the action of the geodesic flow along the geodesic corresponds to a hyperbolic automorphism of the torus, which admits these two foliations as stable and unstable foliation.
One can even consider a fiber bundle, with torus fiber, over the unit tangent bundle of the modular surface. This fiber bundle also has an algebraic model, which is given by the affine quotient
\mathrm{SA}(2,\mathbb{Z})\backslash\mathrm{SA}(2,\mathbb{R})
. The right action of
\mathrm{SA}(2,\mathbb{R})
on this quotient contains all the dynamics we have seen above. This action is the
scenery flow
that has been studied in detail in
[
AF01
]
.

3.
Generalization to multidimensional continued fraction algorithms

In the present work, we provide an extension of the theory described in Section
2
to multidimensional continued fraction algorithms. The most classical examples of such algorithms are the Jacobi–Perron
[
Ber71
,
HJ68
,
Per07
,
Sch73
]
, the Brun
[
Bru19
,
Bru20
,
Bru58
]
, and the Selmer algorithms
[
Sel61
,
Sch00
]
.
Our goal is to view a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm in terms of inductions of toral rotations and associate nonstationary Markov partitions to it. Again, it turns out that this induction process and the Markov partitions are intimately related.

More precisely, let
F
be a multidimensional continued fraction map acting on a subset
X
of the projective space
\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
. (Since the matrices aim at providing rational approximations, it is natural to work in the projective space.)
To a point
\mathbf{x}\in X
, we associate a rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{\mathbf{x}}
acting on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
.
Using the sequence of matrices
(M_{n})_{n}
corresponding to the
F
-orbit of
\mathbf{x}
, we will find an explicit subset
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{x}}
of the torus such that, up to some renormalization process, the induced map of
\mathfrak{r}_{\mathbf{x}}
on
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{x}}
is
\mathfrak{r}_{F(\mathbf{x})}
. It turns out that the set can also be used to define suitable atoms for nonstationary Markov partitions for the underlying linear Anosov mapping families (

∗
⁣
∗

‣
I
); see Section
14
. Indeed, it occurs in a restacking process of the atoms of these Markov partitions as it did in the classical case.

Besides the classical case treated in the previous section, also the case of periodic orbits is a strong source of inspiration. It is indeed known how to set up the induction process mentioned above for periodic orbits of the continued fraction map
F
. In fact, periodic orbits correspond to self-induced dynamical systems, i.e., systems that are topologically conjugate to their first return map on some subspace. For self-induced systems, the induction process is studied for instance in
[
KV98
,
AI01
,
BS05
,
IR06
,
ST09
]
, and it gives rise to symbolic codings of a particular class of rotations on
\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
. Moreover, in the periodic case, the linear Anosov mapping family (

∗
⁣
∗

‣
I
) degenerates to a hyperbolic toral automorphism
M^{-1}\colon\mathbb{T}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
, where
M
is a nonnegative integer matrix with determinant
\pm 1
. It is known that, from the subset
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{x}}
of
\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
on which one induces, one can build a stationary Markov partition for this hyperbolic toral automorphism by a suspension. Important geometric objects that play a decisive role in these results are the
Rauzy fractals
together with their suspensions, the
Rauzy boxes
. Indeed, Rauzy fractals can be used to define appropriate sets for the induction, while atoms for the Markov partitions can be defined using Rauzy boxes. They play the role of the intervals and of the
L
-shaped region in the classical case, respectively. Rauzy fractals were first introduced in
[
Rau82
]
for the so-called Tribonacci substitution; see also
[
Thu89
]
. See Figure
7
for an illustration and Section
12
for precise definitions.

Figure 7.
The Rauzy fractal and the Rauzy box for the Tribonacci substitution
\sigma:1\mapsto 12,2\mapsto 13,3\mapsto 1
.

They can more generally be associated to Pisot substitutions (see
[
AI01
]
and the surveys
[
Fog02
,
BS05
,
ST09
,
BST10
]
), as well as to Pisot beta-transformations and beta-shifts under the name of central tiles
[
Aki02
,
BSS+11
]
. The idea is as follows. To a matrix
M
defining a hyperbolic
Pisot
toral automorphism (i.e., the characteristic polynomial of
M
is the minimal polynomial of a Pisot number), one attaches a
substitution

\sigma
, i.e., a combinatorial rule that replaces letters by words, in a way that the matrix
M
counts the number of occurrences of letters in the images of each letter under the substitution
\sigma
; see Section
7
and the survey
[
ABB+15
]
for more on the Pisot assumption. We can view
\sigma
as a combinatorial version of
M
and we can even attach a
broken line
to
\sigma
. This broken line connects two successive points in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and stays within bounded distance of the line in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
defined by the Perron–Frobenius eigenvector of
M
under the Pisot property of the matrix
M
; see Figure
8
.

Figure 8.
An illustration of a broken line defining a Rauzy fractal.

By taking the closure of the projection of the vertices of this broken line on a transverse direction, one obtains the Rauzy fractal of
\sigma
, which codes the desired rotation on
\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
; see again
[
Fog02
,
BS05
,
ST09
,
BST10
]
and also Section
12
. Note that from the periodic substitutive case, one only obtains a very particular set of algebraic rotations. Note also that substitutive dynamical systems defined in terms of Pisot substitutions are conjectured to have pure discrete spectrum, that is, to be measurably conjugate to a translation on a compact abelian group
[
ABB+15
]
.
So, as in the classical case, substitutions are used and by superimposing a combinatorial structure on our periodic orbits again leads to the desired induction process as well as to atoms for the Markov process.

Two strongly interlinked problems arise in the attempt to generalize this to the nonperiodic case.

Problem I
.

For a rotation on a torus
\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
of dimension
d\geq 3
, find a subset of
\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
on which the induced map is measurably conjugate to a rotation.

Problem II
.

Find atoms of a nonstationary Markov partition associated to a nonperiodic orbit of a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm.

Problem
Problem I
.
is difficult because one usually gets an exchange of pieces with some discontinuities when inducing on a “simple” subset of
\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
. Moreover, we know from the periodic case that the construction of the subsets on which the induced rotation lives is intimately related to the atoms of the Markov partition for a hyperbolic toral automorphism.
Hence, we expect that Problem
Problem I
.
is strongly related to Problem
Problem II
.
. Thus we are left with finding atoms of a nonstationary Markov partition associated to a nonperiodic orbit, which is our main goal. However, a well-known result of
[
Bow78
]
states that, already in the periodic case (except in trivial cases), such a partition cannot be smooth and has “fractal” boundary.
In particular, the boundaries of the atoms of a Markov partition for an algebraically irreducible linear Anosov diffeomorphisms of the
3
-torus cannot be smooth. This indicates that, in the nonperiodic higher-dimensional case, it is hopeless to try simple sets, as for instance polytopes, in order to tackle the above problems. We are thus lead to look more closely at the Rauzy fractals discussed before.

Indeed, the following strategy, which was developed in
[
BST19
,
BST23
]
, combines features from the classical Sturmian and the higher dimensional periodic situation and will thereby lead to a solution to Problems
Problem I
.
and
Problem II
.
. Inspired by the known situations described above, one introduces symbolic dynamical systems defined in terms of substitutions, by superimposing a combinatorial structure upon the multidimensional continued fraction map
F
. This is done by a so-called
substitutive realization
of the algorithm
F
: One assigns to each square matrix
M
that can be produced by the algorithm
F
a substitution
\sigma
. Using this assignment, to each sequence
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n}
of matrices produced by the map
F
, we can associate a sequence of substitutions
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n}
; see Definition
11.17
below. This
\mathcal{S}
-adic
framework (see also
[
BD14
]
) then gives rise to a family of symbolic dynamical systems
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma)
(where
\Sigma
stands for the shift map) consisting of infinite sequences of substitutions generated by the multidimensional continued fraction algorithm
F
.

Let us emphasize how this symbolic
\mathcal{S}
-adic superstructure can be used to solve our problems. Continued fraction algorithms provide better and better rational approximation vectors of a given vector
(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Geometrically, this amounts to exhibiting integer points in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
that lie close to the line
L
defined by
(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{d})
. These points are column vectors of products
M_{1}\cdots M_{n}
of matrices
M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}
produced by the continued fraction algorithm
F
, with increasing length
n
. We recall that the algorithm is
strongly convergent
if these column vectors converge in distance (and not only in angle) to the line
L
; see Definition
7.7
. As in the periodic case, the substitutions of the substitutive realization provide a broken line connecting points in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. By strong convergence, this broken line stays (usually) within bounded distance of
L
; see again Figure
8
. In particular, in this general case, the infinite broken line is given by an element of the symbolic dynamical system
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma)
. By taking the closure of the projection of the vertices of this broken line on a transverse direction, one obtains a bounded
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy fractal. These fractals were first defined in
[
BST19
,
Thu20
,
BST23
]
, where they were used to relate orbits of multidimensional continued fraction algorithms to rotations on a torus
\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
. Contrary to the periodic case discussed above,
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy fractals do not admit a self-affine structure, which makes them harder to deal with. Nevertheless, they can be used to solve Problems
Problem I
.
and
Problem II
.
stated above.

As a first application,
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy fractals lead to a solution to a problem that has been open for a long time (see
[
AR91
]
): One wants to find good symbolic codings for rotations
\mathfrak{r}_{\mathbf{x}}
on the torus that enjoy the beautiful properties of Sturmian sequences like low factor complexity and good symbolic discrepancy properties; see Example
11.7
. It was shown in
[
BST19
,
BST23
]
that
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy fractals are the right objects to obtain such codings. This allows us to associate a rotation on
\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
to an orbit of multidimensional continued fraction algorithms, and it is this rotation on which we start our induction process. In particular, we revisit
[
BST19
,
BST23
]
to get the induced rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{F(\mathbf{x})}
from
\mathfrak{r}_{\mathbf{x}}
by taking first return maps on natural subtiles of
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy fractals for strongly convergent multidimensional continued fraction maps
F
; see Section
14
. Thus, the continued fraction map
F
renormalizes rotations of the torus by a hyperbolic system. This situation generalizes the way the Gauss map renormalizes rotations of the circle and solves Problem
Problem I
.
. As explained in
[
For06
, Introduction]
, this renormalization allows one to understand the dynamics of a generic system in a given family.

We want to relate sequences of matrices produced by multidimensional continued fraction algorithms to mapping families of the form (

∗
⁣
∗

‣
I
). Since mapping families are defined in terms of bi-infinite sequences of matrices, we need to equip the orbit
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with a “past”. This can be done by considering its
natural extension
, which is discussed in Section
9.2
. Indeed, the natural extension of a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm generates orbits of the form
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
. We can now use these orbits to define linear Anosov mapping families of the form (

∗
⁣
∗

‣
I
). We then use
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy fractals and their suspensions to construct the desired nonstationary Markov partitions and thereby solve Problem
Problem II
.
; see Chapter
V
.
Using these nonstationary Markov partitions, we are able to associate symbolic models as nonstationary edge and vertex shifts (see Definition
6.11
) to such sequences, extending the well-known construction for a single toral automorphism; see e.g.
[
Adl98
]
.

More generally, our approach via symbolic dynamics is motivated by the search for symbolic models for automorphisms of compact abelian groups, which elicited a wide literature. Algebraic constructions were initiated by Vershik for hyperbolic toral automorphisms with the use of homoclinic points; see
[
Ver92
,
KV98
,
SV98
]
. Among these symbolic models, the one associated with Markov partitions are particularly interesting. Note also that the present approach is reminiscent of the generating method of fractal curves due to Dekking
[
Dek82
]
, used in
[
Bed86
,
IO93
]
to produce Markov partitions with fractal boundaries for a class of hyperbolic toral automorphism on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
.

Besides that, with our theory we want to set the stage for viewing multidimensional continued fraction algorithms as Poincaré sections of flows in the same spirit as this was done in
[
AF01
]
for the classical continued fraction algorithm. This is evoked in Chapter
VI
.

4.
Discussion of the main results

Most of our main results need preparations and definitions. For this reason, their exact statements will be given later. However, for the convenience of the reader, we provide informal versions of these results already here.

We use the notation

\mathcal{M}_{d}=\{M\in\mathbb{N}^{d\times d}\,:\,|\!\det M|=1\}

for the set of square matrices of determinant
\pm 1
with nonnegative integer entries.
We consider a multidimensional continued fraction map
F
acting on a subset of the projective space
\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
that produces sequences
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{N}}
of matrices.
When considering (a geometric realization of) the natural extension
\widehat{F}
of the continued fraction map
F
, even
bi-infinite
sequences
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
of matrices are produced. These sequences are often
primitive
in the sense that products
M_{k}\cdots M_{\ell-1}
of large blocks of matrices are positive; see Definition
7.1
for details.
In the theory of multidimensional continued fraction algorithms, it is desirable that the produced sequences of matrices
\mathbf{M}
have good convergence properties. These convergence properties are efficiently described in terms of negativity of the second Lyapunov exponent; see e.g.
[
Lag93
,
Sch00
]
. We thoroughly study such convergence properties even if the Lyapunov exponents of a single sequence
\mathbf{M}
do not exist by providing a weaker condition, stating that the second largest singular value
\delta_{2}(M_{0}\cdots M_{n-1})
of
M_{0}\cdots M_{n-1}
tends to zero exponentially fast; see Definition
7.9
. This condition turns out to be much easier to verify. We then superimpose a substitutive realization of a sequence
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
which gives a sequence of substitutions
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
; see Section
11.6
for details. Sequences of substitutions are of interest for us as they are needed to define
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy fractals, the central objects required to obtain induced rotations as well as nonstationary Markov partitions. Good convergence properties entail that these
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy fractals are bounded, which will be of great importance in our theory.

4.1.
Convergence results

We provide results for single sequences of matrices and single sequences of substitutions, as well as metric results that hold in the setting of measure-theoretic dynamical systems of the form
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
, where
\Sigma
stands for the shift and
D
is a shift invariant subset of the set of sequences of unimodular
d{\times}d
matrices
\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. The following theorem concerning single sequences of matrices is stated as part of Theorem
7.21
; note that in this theorem only the future of the bi-binfinite sequence
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is relevant. This theorem is a first step toward the obtention of a hyperbolic splitting; see Section
4.3
.

Theorem A
.

Let
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a primitive sequence of matrices in
\mathcal{M}_{d}
for which the second largest singular value
\delta_{2}(M_{0}\cdots M_{n-1})
of
M_{0}\cdots M_{n-1}
tends to zero exponentially fast in the sense that
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\delta_{2}(M_{0}\cdots M_{n-1})<0
.
Then, under the mild growth condition
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert M_{n}\rVert=0
, the sequence
\mathbf{M}
converges exponentially fast to a vector with rationally independent entries.

The fact that only the
\limsup
is required to be less than zero (i.e., the limit and, hence, the second Lyapunov exponent, need not exist) makes this condition checkable and we are able to use this theorem to construct concrete sequences of matrices that admit exponentially fast convergence; see e.g. Example
7.22
or Propositions
10.4
and
10.8
.

Because the
\limsup
condition ensures that the sequence of matrices
\mathbf{M}
grows only in one direction, it is reminiscent of the growth behavior of powers of a matrix having a characteristic polynomial that is the minimal polynomial of a Pisot number. For this reason, the
\limsup
condition will be called
local Pisot condition
(for a single sequence of matrices); see Definition
7.9
. In terms of symbolic dynamics, when imposing a symbolic structure upon sequences of matrices with substitutions, the analog of Theorem
Theorem A
.
is stated as Theorem
11.8
. In this case, the local Pisot condition implies on top of the exponentially fast convergence also the combinatorial property of
balance
(see Definition
11.4
), which can also be phrased as
bounded symbolic discrepancy
(see Section
11.2
).

Besides that, Lyapunov exponents are studied from a metric and ergodic viewpoint for measure-theoretic dynamical systems of the form
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
, where
\Sigma
stands for the shift and
D
is a shift invariant subset of the set of sequences of unimodular
d{\times}d
matrices
\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. We say that such a dynamical system satisfies the
metric version of the Pisot condition
(see Definition
8.12
) if its second Lyapunov exponent is less than zero. Similarly as Theorem
Theorem A
.
does for single sequences of matrices, this condition implies exponentially fast convergence for a generic sequence
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in D
, including strong convergence and a certain irreducibility property; see Theorem
8.15
. The metric theory for sequences of substitutions is covered in Section
13.2
, see in particular, Theorem
13.6
.

In Theorem
Theorem B
.
below, we use the local Pisot condition to get a linear Anosov mapping family that can be regarded as a nonstationary version of a hyperbolic toral automorphism.
In particular, the underlying hyperbolic splitting is “Pisot” in the sense that the stable space has dimension
1
, and the unstable space co-dimension
1
.
Theorem
Theorem B
.
will be made precise in Theorem
7.26
.

Theorem B
.

Let
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a primitive sequence of matrices satisfying the local Pisot condition. Then the sequence of linear toral automorphisms

\cdots\xrightarrow{M_{-2}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}_{-1}\xrightarrow{M_{-1}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}_{0}\xrightarrow{M_{0}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}_{1}\xrightarrow{M_{1}^{-1}}\cdots

associated to
\mathbf{M}
is a linear Anosov mapping family. In particular, for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, in the tangent bundle of each
d
-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}_{n}
, there are directions
\mathbf{u}_{n}
and
\mathbf{v}_{n}
satisfying

\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\mathbf{u}_{n}=\bigcap_{k\geq n}M_{n}\cdots M_{k-1}\,\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\mathbf{v}_{n}=\bigcap_{k<n}\vphantom{(M_{k}\cdots M_{n-1})}^{t}(M_{k}\cdots M_{n-1})\,\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d},

such that the mappings
M_{n}^{-1}\cdots M_{m}^{-1}

contract

\mathbf{u}_{n}
and
expand

\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}
for large
m{-}n
.

Also this theorem has a metric version, see again Theorem
8.15
, in which the generic hyperbolic splitting is related to the Oseledets splitting induced by a certain cocycle which is provided by Oseledets’ Multiplicatice Ergodic Theorem. Both versions of Theorem
Theorem B
.
, the one for single sequences of matrices and the metric one, can also be rephrased for sequences of substitutions, see Theorem
11.16
and, once again, Theorem
13.6
.

4.2.
Pisot conjecture, pure discrete spectrum, and induced rotations

In Section
12
, we review the theory of
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy fractals and Rauzy boxes attached to a sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
of substitutions over
d
letters. Under mild assumptions,
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy fractals are fundamental domains for the action of rotations on
\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
, which can be also stated as a tiling property:
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy fractals periodically tile the
(d{-}1)
-dimensional plane; see Definition
12.6
for a precise statement in the present context. This
tiling condition
has been studied in
[
BST19
,
BST23
]
in the
\mathcal{S}
-adic setting, and its validity is conjectured in an even wider scope. The according conjecture is strongly related to an
\mathcal{S}
-adic generalization of the well-known
Pisot conjecture
; see e.g.
[
ABB+15
]
. It says that each
\mathcal{S}
-adic dynamical system satisfying a Pisot property has pure discrete spectrum, in other words, it is measurably conjugate to a rotation on a compact abelian group.
This important conjecture is formulated below as Conjecture
18.1
. Many partial results exist for the substitutive case (see e.g.
[
HS03
,
ABB+15
,
Bar16
,
Bar18
]
). Results on the
\mathcal{S}
-adic case are contained in
[
BST19
,
BST23
]
, and we provide slight variants of these results in Theorems
12.9
,
13.11
, and
13.12
.

If we assume that a given sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
of substitutions gives rise to a dynamical system
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma)
that has pure discrete spectrum, the associated rotation can be “seen” on the
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy fractal
\mathcal{R}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
. It turns out that if we induce this rotation on a shrunk version of this
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy fractal, we obtain again a rotation. Iterating this induction process leads to a cascade of rotations that allows the interpretation of a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm as an action on a family of torus rotations; see Section
14
. This is analogous to the situation of the classical continued fraction algorithm discussed in Section
2
. The following is an informal statement of Theorem
14.1
. The tiling condition below refers to Definition
12.6
.

Theorem C
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a primitive sequence of substitutions. If the
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy fractal of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
satisfies a certain tiling condition, then we can associate a sequence of rotations
(\mathfrak{r}_{\Sigma^{n}\boldsymbol{\sigma}})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
such that each
\mathfrak{r}_{\Sigma^{n}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, is the rotation obtained by inducing the rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
on the subset
M_{\sigma_{0}}\cdots M_{\sigma_{n-1}}\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma^{n}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
of
\mathcal{R}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
.

In Section
14
we also state a metric version of this result, as well as versions for multidimensional continued fraction algorithms.

4.3.
Results on nonstationary Markov partitions

In Sections
15
and
16
, we provide the core results motivating the present work by exhibiting “nonstationary Markov partitions” and symbolic models as “nonstationary edge shifts” for the hyperbolic linear Anosov mapping families (

∗
⁣
∗

‣
I
) of a sequence
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. The pieces of the corresponding generating nonstationary Markov partitions are obtained by associating a sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
of substitutions to
\mathbf{M}
. More precisely, these pieces are the Rauzy boxes, defined as suspensions of the
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy fractals of the sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
.
In particular, the Rauzy fractals are given by the “future”
(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\dots)
and the heights of the suspensions are given by the “past”
(\sigma_{-1},\sigma_{-2},\dots)
.
For an exact statement of the next theorem, we refer to Theorem
15.4
, which is a refinement in terms of generating partitions of Theorem
15.1
.
Again, the tiling condition below refers to Definition
12.6
.

Theorem D
.

Let
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a primitive sequence of matrices with a superimposed substitutive structure
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
. If the
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy fractals of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
satisfy a certain tiling condition, then the associated linear Anosov mapping family

\cdots\xrightarrow{M_{-2}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}_{-1}\xrightarrow{M_{-1}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}_{0}\xrightarrow{M_{0}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}_{1}\xrightarrow{M_{1}^{-1}}\cdots

admits a generating nonstationary Markov partition, whose atoms are explicitly given by Rauzy boxes associated to the substitutive structure
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
. This Markov partition also provides a symbolic model for this Anosov mapping family as a nonstationary edge shift.

Theorem
Theorem E
.
below provides a metric version of Theorem
Theorem D
.
in which the crucial condition is again the generic Pisot condition. Indeed, we will show that the generic Pisot condition implies the tiling condition almost everywhere under mild additional requirements. The exact statement of Theorem
Theorem E
.
is contained in Theorem
16.2
.

Theorem E
.

Let
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
, with
D\subset\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, be an ergodic dynamical system.
Suppose that
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
satisfies the Pisot condition, and that there exists a single substitutive dynamical system that has pure discrete spectrum that corresponds to a periodic sequence in
D
. Then, for
\nu
-almost every
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in D
, the associated sequence

\cdots\xrightarrow{M_{-2}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}_{-1}\xrightarrow{M_{-1}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}_{0}\xrightarrow{M_{0}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}_{1}\xrightarrow{M_{1}^{-1}}\cdots

is a linear Anosov mapping family that admits a generating nonstationary Markov partition, whose atoms are explicitly given by Rauzy boxes. This Markov partition also provides a symbolic model for this Anosov mapping family as a nonstationary edge shift.

Theorems
Theorem D
.
and
Theorem E
.
can be formulated also for sequences of substitutions, see for instance Theorem
16.1
. A version of Theorem
Theorem E
.
for multidimensional continued fraction algorithms is stated as Theorem
16.4
.

The following Theorem
Theorem F
.
provides an “accelerated” version of Theorem
Theorem E
.
. We state this theorem in the framework of multidimensional continued fraction algorithms. In particular, if
(X,F,\nu)
is a positive
(d{-}1)
-dimensional continued fraction algorithm satisfying the Pisot condition, then it can be “accelerated” in such a way that the generated Markov partition exists almost everywhere without further conditions. By an acceleration we mean that, instead of applying
F
to an element of
X
, we apply
F^{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
that has to be chosen appropriately. This is a classical approach; see for instance
[
Yoc05
]
for accelerations of Rauzy induction in the setting of interval exchange transformations. The reason is that such an acceleration gives us matrices with larger entries and, hence, more freedom in choosing substitutions for the construction of the required Markov partition. Summing up, Theorem
Theorem F
.
provides Markov partitions under the Pisot condition without further combinatorial conditions. The exact statement for Theorem
Theorem F
.
is given in Theorem
16.5
, a version for sequences of matrices is stated as Theorem
16.3
.

Theorem F
.

Let
(X,F,\nu)
be a positive
(d{-}1)
-dimensional continued fraction algorithm satisfying the Pisot condition, and let
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{\nu})
be a natural extension of
(X,F,\nu)
.
Then there exists an acceleration
F^{k}
of
F
such that the cocycle of the acceleration
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F}^{k},\widehat{\nu})
generates sequences of matrices
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
that
\widehat{\nu}
-almost always give rise to an Anosov mapping family

\cdots\xrightarrow{M_{-2}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}_{-1}\xrightarrow{M_{-1}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}_{0}\xrightarrow{M_{0}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}_{1}\xrightarrow{M_{1}^{-1}}\cdots

admitting a generating nonstationary Markov partition, with explicitly given Rauzy boxes as atoms. This Markov partition also provides a symbolic model for this Anosov mapping family as a nonstationary edge shift.

In the previous theorems, we needed two conditions. Firstly, the generic Pisot condition, which is formulated in terms of negativity of the second Lyapunov exponent. Secondly, only in Theorem
Theorem E
.
, we require that our set of
\mathcal{S}
-adic sequences contains a substitutive dynamical system that has pure discrete spectrum. These conditions suffice to show that the tiling condition holds true almost everywhere and, hence, enable us to derive Theorems
Theorem E
.
and
Theorem F
.
from Theorem
Theorem D
.
. Since there exist many algorithms for checking pure discrete spectrum for a single unimodular Pisot substitution (see e.g.
[
Liv87
,
Liv92
,
SS02
,
BST10
,
AL11
]
), the only crucial condition is the Pisot condition.

4.4.
The Brun algorithm, our running example

The Brun algorithm, which will be discussed in detail in Section
10
, serves as our running example.
It is a generalization of the Farey continued fraction algorithm to higher dimensions.
For a given vector in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
, it produces a new vector by subtracting the second largest coordinate from the largest one.
We will consider versions of the Brun algorithm where the coordinates of the vector are ordered or unordered.
We also study the multiplicative acceleration known as modified Jacobi–Perron algorithm, which generalizes the classical continued fraction algorithm.
For all these versions of the Brun algorithm, the Pisot condition holds in dimensions
d{-}1=2
and
d{-}1=3
; see Proposition
10.13
.

We mainly focus on the Anosov mapping family on the
3
-dimensional tori
\mathbb{T}^{3}
associated to the
2
-dimensional Brun continued fraction algorithm, but also discuss the
4
-dimensional case (related to the
3
-dimensional Brun algorithm). With help of the Brun algorithm we are able to construct Anosov mapping families with generating Markov partitions for hyperbolic foliations in dimension
3
and
4
as detailed in the following realization result; see Corollary
17.3
for the exact statement.
The restriction on the dimension comes from the fact that these results require, for the Pisot condition to hold, the negativity of the second Lyapunov exponent, which is only established for small dimensions.
Observe that numerical results from
[
BST21
]
indicate that classical multidimensional continued fraction algorithms cease to be strongly convergent for high dimensions; cf. Conjecture
18.4
.

Corollary G
.

For almost every pair of subspaces
(G^{s},G^{u})
with
G^{s}\oplus G^{u}=\mathbb{R}^{3}
, there is a linear Anosov mapping family (

∗
⁣
∗

‣
I
) with stable foliation
G^{s}
and unstable foliation
G^{u}
admitting a generating Markov partition and, hence, a symbolic model as a nonstationary edge shift.

For almost every pair of subspaces
(G^{s},G^{u})
with
G^{s}\oplus G^{u}=\mathbb{R}^{4}
and
\dim(G^{s})\,{\neq}\,2
, there is a linear Anosov mapping family (

∗
⁣
∗

‣
I
) with stable foliation
G^{s}
and unstable foliation
G^{u}
admitting a generating Markov partition and, hence, a symbolic model as a nonstationary edge shift.

The sequences
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathbb{Z}}
of matrices defining the claimed Anosov mapping families in this corollary correspond to expansions of pairs of vectors in the natural extension of the Brun continued fraction algorithm.
Therefore, Corollary
Corollary G
.
is formulated only for splittings where one of the foliations has dimension
1
.

In the cases covered by Corollary
Corollary G
.
, it makes sense to speak of a Markov partition of a hyperbolic foliation
(G^{s},G^{u})
because such a foliation gives rise to a sequence of matrices to which we can associate a Markov partition. This is in the spirit of
[
AF05
, Introduction]
. If one looks at hyperbolic toral automorphisms in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\mathbb{R}^{4}
, the slopes of the stable and unstable manifolds can only be cubic and quartic algebraic integers, respectively. What we do here completes the picture in the sense that (almost all) arbitrary slopes are covered when we turn to Anosov mapping families.
Moreover, in this context, for our realization result from Corollary
Corollary G
.
, it is important that there exists a set of positive Lebesgue measure on which the natural extension of the underlying continued fraction algorithm is defined; see Remark
9.6
. This is crucial for being able to cover almost all slopes.
For the Brun continued fraction algorithm, positivity of the measure is proved in
[
AN93
]
, and this motivates choosing this algorithm as the main example for our realization results.

5.
Outline

Let us briefly sketch the contents of this monograph. Chapter
II
is organized as follows. Section
6
recalls general results on Anosov mapping families and on nonstationary Markov partitions. In Section
7
, convergence properties for bi-infinite sequences of matrices under the Pisot condition are investigated. These results are revisited in Section
8
from a metric viewpoint by considering shifts of sequences of matrices endowed with an invariant measure.

Chapter
III
provides definitions and results concerning (unimodular) multidimensional continued fraction algorithms.
General results concerning their natural extension, their dual versions, their invariant measures, and their associated linear Anosov mapping families are given in Section
9
.
Section
10
aims at applying this formalism and the previous results to the Brun algorithm, which will be used as a running example in the monograph, in its additive (ordered and unordered) and multiplicative form.

Chapter
IV
deals with sequences of substitutions. Section
11
develops symbolic dynamics for a single bi-infinite sequence of substitutions. Here, properties of the incidence matrices of the substitutions play a key role. Rauzy fractals and Rauzy boxes are defined in Section
12
, where also their tiling properties and related pure discrete spectrum results are thoroughly discussed. Section
13
covers the metric theory of bi-infinite sequences of substitutions and gives metric conditions on tiling properties of Rauzy fractals and Rauzy boxes as well as metric results on pure discrete spectrum.

Chapter
V
is devoted to the construction of induced rotations an nonstationary Markov partitions for sequences of matrices, sequences of substitutions, and multidimensional continued fraction algorithms. The theory of induced rotations is covered in Section
14
.
Section
15
shows how to use Rauzy boxes in order to define nonstationary Markov partitions for
\mathcal{S}
-adic linear Anosov mapping families. In Section
16
, a metric theory of nonstationary Markov partitions for Anosov mapping families and multidimensional continued fraction algorithms is laid out. This section ends with a thorough discussion of our standard example, the Brun algorithm, in Section
17
. We apply our theory to this algorithm and give the exact statement and proof of Corollary
Corollary G
.
.

We conclude this monograph with Chapter
VI
, which contains a discussion of perspectives and directions on further research. This includes a list of open problems and research questions, viewing the natural extension of a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm as a Poincaré section of a flow, and the discussion of the interplay between different dynamical systems that played a major role in the present work.

Chapter II
Sequences of integer matrices and mapping families

In this chapter, we deal with properties of bi-infinite sequences of (nonnegative) matrices that are relevant for our theory of nonstationary Markov partitions. We organize these sequences into linear mapping families. If certain convergence properties hold, these linear mapping families turn out to be Anosov. We start in Section
6
with a discussion of mapping families with special emphasis on Anosov mapping families, and introduce the concept of nonstationary Markov partition for these objects. Section
7
is devoted to bi-infinite sequences of matrices. Our main aim is to introduce notions of convergence of such sequences and to give criteria to check convergence. In this context, the so-called Pisot condition (see Definition
7.9
) plays a major role. This Pisot condition can be viewed as a “codimension one” Anosov property of a mapping family associated to a bi-infinite sequence of matrices in the sense that the splitting of the tangent space gives contracting spaces with dimension
1
, and thus expanding space with codimension
1
. Section
8
finally gives metric results for bi-infinite sequences of matrices and the associated mapping families. Our main tool here will be Oseledets’ Multiplicative Ergodic Theorem.

6.
Mapping families

In this section, we define basic objects that will play an important role throughout the manuscript. Section
6.1
is devoted to the concept of a
mapping family
which forms the framework for our nonstationary dynamics. It first appears in
[
AF05
]
. In Section
6.2
we introduce the notion of a nonstationary Markov partition of a mapping family and define the so-called Property M, a checkable criterion for a sequence of partitions to form a nonstationary Markov partition. In Section
6.3
, using Bratteli diagrams, we define nonstationary edge and vertex shifts. By using nonstationary Markov partitions, these shifts will be shown later to serve as a symbolic representations of a mapping family.

6.1.
Eventually Anosov mapping families

Consider a bi-infinite sequence of
d
-dimensional Riemannian manifolds
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
with uniformly bounded diameters.
Let
X=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}X_{n}
be the disjoint union of these spaces equipped with a metric whose restriction to
X_{n}
equals the metric on
X_{n}
.
For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
f_{n}:X_{n}\to X_{n+1}
be a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism and define
f:X\to X
by
f(x)=f_{n}(x)
for
x\in X_{n}
.
We call the pair
(X,f)
a
mapping family
.
We will sometimes write out
(X,f)
in the form

(6.1)

\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{-2}}X_{-1}\xrightarrow{f_{-1}}X_{0}\xrightarrow{\;f_{0}\;}X_{1}\xrightarrow{\;f_{1}\;}\cdots.

Let
TX
be the tangent space of
X
,
T_{p}X\cong\mathbb{R}^{d}
its fiber over
p\in X
.
The derivative of
f
(defined on
TX
) will be denoted by
D(f)
.

In the sequel, for an unspecified norm, we will use the notation
\lVert\cdot\rVert
. The following definition of an
eventually Anosov mapping family
is inspired by
[
AF05
, Definition 2.10]
.

Let
(X,f)
be a mapping family. Assume that there exists an
f
-invariant splitting
G^{s}\oplus G^{u}
of the tangent space
TX
of
X
, and set
G^{s}_{p}=G^{s}\cap T_{p}X
,
G^{u}_{p}=G^{u}\cap T_{p}X
for
p\in X
.
If, for all
p\in X
,

(i)

\lim\limits_{n\to+\infty}\sup\{\lVert D(f^{n})\,\mathbf{x}\rVert/\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert\,:\,\mathbf{x}\in G^{s}_{p}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\}=0
,

(ii)

\lim\limits_{n\to+\infty}\inf\{\lVert D(f^{n})\,\mathbf{x}\rVert/\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert\,:\,\mathbf{x}\in G^{u}_{p}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\}=+\infty
,

(iii)

\lim\limits_{n\to-\infty}\inf\{\lVert D(f^{n})\,\mathbf{x}\rVert/\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert\,:\,\mathbf{x}\in G^{s}_{p}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\}=+\infty
,

(iv)

\lim\limits_{n\to-\infty}\sup\{\lVert D(f^{n})\,\mathbf{x}\rVert/\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert\,:\,\mathbf{x}\in G^{u}_{p}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\}=0
,

we say that
(X,f)
is
(two-sided) eventually Anosov
.

Remark 6.1
.

For an eventually Anosov mapping family, none of the conditions (
i
)–(
iv
) follows from the others; see for instance
[
AF05
, Remark 2.11 and Example 9]
. If just (
i
) and (
ii
) hold, then
(X,f)
is “eventually Anosov in the future”, and if only (
iii
) and (
iv
) are in force, then it is “eventually Anosov in the past”.
However, we do not need these one-sided versions in the present monograph.

For the more specialized concept of
strictly Anosov mapping family
defined in
[
AF05
, Definition 2.7]
, where the contraction and expansion ratios are uniformly bounded away from
1
, the
f
-invariance implies that contraction for
n\to{+}\infty
is equivalent to expansion for
n\to{-}\infty
and vice versa.
Here, we use eventually Anosov rather than strictly Anosov because the latter property is not maintained by most sequences generated by multidimensional continued fraction algorithms.

Remark 6.2
.

In an eventually Anosov mapping familiy
(X,f)
, a single function
f_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, may well be nonhyperbolic. This is illustrated for instance by the generators of
2{\times}2
integer matrix with determinant
1
related to the additive continued fraction algorithm, see
[
AF05
, Example 6]
and Example
6.3
below.
Also, even if
f_{n}
is hyperbolic, the associated stationary splitting of the tangent space
TX_{n}
may be different from the (local) splitting of
TX_{n}
induced by the (global) splitting
G^{s}\oplus G^{u}
of
TX
.

Example 6.3
.

Let
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a bi-infinite sequence of positive integers. To this sequence, we can attach the sequence of matrices
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
with

M_{n}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&a_{n}\end{pmatrix}\qquad(n\in\mathbb{Z}).

Since the matrices
M_{n}
have determinant
-1
, they can be regarded as automorphisms on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}
. Therefore they give rise to the mapping family

(6.2)

\cdots\xrightarrow{M_{-2}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}^{2}\xrightarrow{M_{-1}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}^{2}\xrightarrow{M_{0}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}^{2}\xrightarrow{M_{1}^{-1}}\cdots,

where
X_{n}=\mathbb{T}^{2}
and
f_{n}=M_{n}^{-1}
for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. This mapping family is related to the classical continued fraction algorithm. Indeed, write

[a_{0};a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\ldots]=a_{0}+\cfrac{1}{a_{1}+\cfrac{1}{a_{2}+\cfrac{1}{a_{3}+\ddots}}}.\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt

Then, because the classical continued fraction algorithm has good convergence properties, the mapping family (
6.2
) is Anosov with the hyperbolic splitting

G^{s}\oplus G^{u}=\bigg(\mathbb{R}\binom{1}{[a_{n};a_{n+1},a_{n+2},\ldots]}\oplus\mathbb{R}\binom{[a_{n-1};a_{n-2},a_{n-3},\ldots]}{-1}\bigg)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}.

In view of generalizations to higher dimensions, note that
\mathbb{R}([a_{n-1};a_{n-2},\ldots],-1)
is the subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
orthogonal to the vector
(1,[a_{n-1};a_{n-2},\ldots])
.

6.2.
Nonstationary Markov partitions

We shall now define Markov partitions for mapping families. To this matter, we will generalize the classical stationary Markov partitions discussed in Section
1
; see also
[
Adl98
, Definition 6.1]
. We first recall that a
topological partition
of a compact topological space
R
is a finite collection
\{R_{1},\dots,R_{q}\}
of pairwise disjoint open sets satisfying
\overline{R_{1}}\cup\cdots\cup\overline{R_{q}}=X
. Using this terminology, we are able to state the following definition (compare the analogy to the stationary case in Section
1
), which roughly speaking states that for any finite pseudo-orbit, there exists a finite orbit with the same symbolic dynamics.
All definitions of this section will be illustrated in Example
6.9
.

Definition 6.4

(Nonstationary Markov partition)
.

Let
(X,f)
be the mapping family

\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{-2}}X_{-1}\xrightarrow{f_{-1}}X_{0}\xrightarrow{\;f_{0}\;}X_{1}\xrightarrow{\;f_{1}\;}\cdots.

For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\mathcal{P}_{n}=\{R_{n,1},\dots,R_{n,q_{n}}\}
be a topological partition of
X_{n}
. The sequence
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is called a
(nonstationary) Markov partition
for
(X,f)
if, for any sequence
(i_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\{1,\dots,q_{n}\}
, for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and each
m\geq 2
, we have that

R_{k,i_{k}}\cap f^{-1}(R_{k+1,i_{k+1}})\neq\emptyset\quad\mbox{for all}\ k\in\{n,\dots,n{+}m{-}1\}

implies that

R_{n,i_{n}}\cap f^{-1}(R_{n+1,i_{n+1}})\cap\cdots\cap f^{-m}(R_{n+m,i_{n+m}})\neq\emptyset.

The nonstationary Markov partition
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is called
generating
if, for any sequence
(i_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\{1,\dots,q_{n}\}
,

\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\bigcap_{|k|<n}f^{-k}(R_{k,i_{k}})}

contains at most one point.

Remark 6.5
.

Note that, by the definition of the mapping family
(X,f)
, the mapping
f:X\to X
equals
f_{n}
on
X_{n}
. Thus Definition
6.4
can be written out in the following way: A sequence
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, with
\mathcal{P}_{n}=\{R_{n,1},\dots,R_{n,q_{n}}\}
, is called a
(nonstationary) Markov partition
for
(X,f)
if, for any sequence
(i_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\{1,\dots,q_{n}\}
, for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and each
m\geq 2
, we have that

R_{k,i_{k}}\cap f_{k}^{-1}(R_{k+1,i_{k+1}})\neq\emptyset\quad\mbox{for all}\ k\in\{n,\dots,n{+}m{-}1\}

implies that

R_{n,i_{n}}\cap f_{n}^{-1}(R_{n+1,i_{n+1}})\cap\cdots\cap(f_{n}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{n+m-1}^{-1})(R_{n+m,i_{n+m}})\neq\emptyset.

The nonstationary Markov partition
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is called
generating
if, for any sequence
(i_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\{1,\dots,q_{n}\}
,

\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\bigcap_{0\leq k<n}(f_{-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{-k})(R_{-k,i_{-k}})\cap\cdots\cap R_{0,i_{0}}\cap\cdots\cap(f_{0}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{k-1}^{-1})(R_{k,i_{k}})}

contains at most one point.

Nonstationary Markov partitions can also be interpreted in the following way:
For any sequence
(i_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, if we have
(x_{k})_{n\leq k<n+m}
such that
x_{k}\in R_{k,i_{k}}
and
f_{k}(x_{k})\in R_{k+1,i_{k+1}}
for all
k\in\{n,\dots,n{+}m{-}1\}
, then there exists a point
y\in R_{n,i_{n}}
such that
f_{k}\circ\cdots\circ f_{n}(y)\in R_{k,i_{k}}
for all
k\in\{n,\dots,n{+}m\}
. In other words, as mentioned above, for any finite pseudo-orbit, there exists a finite orbit with the same symbolic dynamics. If the partition is generating, then the symbolic dynamics uniquely defines the point.

We now introduce a nonstationary version of
Property M
from Adler
[
Adl98
, Section 7]
. This property is easy to check, and we will see below that it implies the nonstationary Markov property of Definition
6.4
. We need some terminology first.

Definition 6.6

(Transverse partitions; cf.
[
Adl98
, Definitions 7.1 and 7.2]
)
.

To each point
p
in a set
R
, we associate two subsets
h(p),v(p)\subset R
that both contain
p
in a way that each of the collections

H=\{h(p):p\in R\}\quad\text{and}\quad V=\{v(p):p\in R\}

forms a partition of
R
. We call
H
and
V

transverse partitions
of
R
if
h(p)\cap v(q)\neq\emptyset
for all
p,q\in R
.

Using transverse partitions, we can now define a nonstationary version of Property M given in
[
Adl98
, Definition 7.4]
.

Definition 6.7

(Nonstationary Property M)
.

Let
(X,f)
be a mapping family.
For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\mathcal{P}_{n}=\{R_{n,1},\dots,R_{n,q_{n}}\}
be a topological partition of
X_{n}
.
The sequence
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is said to have
(nonstationary) Property M
for
(X,f)
if, for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
i\in\{1,\dots,q_{n}\}
, the atom
R_{n,i}
has a pair of transverse partitions

H_{n,i}=\{h_{n}(p)\,:\,p\in R_{n,i}\},\quad V_{n,i}=\{v_{n}(p)\,:\,p\in R_{n,i}\},

such that

(6.3)

h_{n+1}(p)\subset f_{n}\big(h_{n}(f_{n}^{-1}p)\big)\quad\mbox{and}\quad f_{n}\big(v_{n}(f_{n}^{-1}p)\big)\subset v_{n+1}(p)

for all
p\in f_{n}(R_{n,i})\cap R_{n+1,j}
,
j\in\{1,\dots,q_{n+1}\}
.

Property M implies the nonstationary Markov property of Definition
6.4
. Indeed, the following proposition can be derived in exactly the same way as its stationary analog in
[
Adl98
, Theorem 7.9]
.
4
4

4

Contrarily to
[
Adl98
]
, we do not put the index
i
in
h_{n}(p)
and
v_{n}(p)
because
i
is given by the location of
p
within the topological partition
\mathcal{P}_{n}=\{R_{n,1},\dots,R_{n,q_{n}}\}
.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
(X,f)
be a mapping family and assume that the sequence of partitions
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
has the nonstationary Property M for
(X,f)
.
Then
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a nonstationary Markov partition for
(X,f)
.

We illustrate the concepts of this section for a special instance of Example
6.3
.

Example 6.9
.

Two well-known continued fraction expansions are given by

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle=[1;1,1,1,1,\ldots]\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle=[2;1,2,1,1,4,1,1,6,1,1,8,\ldots],

where
\phi=\frac{1+\sqrt{5}}{2}
is the golden ratio.
We combine these expansions to a bi-infinite sequence

(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}=(\ldots,1,1,1,1;2,1,2,1,1,4,1,1,6,1,1,8,\ldots),

where the semicolon is between
a_{-1}
and
a_{0}
.
As in Example
6.3
, this sequence gives rise to a sequence
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
of matrices that defines the mapping family

\cdots\xrightarrow{M_{-2}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}^{2}\xrightarrow{M_{-1}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}^{2}\xrightarrow{M_{0}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}^{2}\xrightarrow{M_{1}^{-1}}\cdots,

where
X_{n}=\mathbb{T}^{2}
and
f_{n}=M_{n}^{-1}
for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

), with
p
replaced by

, on level

In Figure

, the

. If we “restack” these linear images (at levels

) back to the according fundamental domain of the torus, we obtain Figure

.

Suppose that we got a Markov partition
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
of a mapping family
(X,f)
