. The following result, which will be needed in Section
15
, shows how to construct a refined Markov partition
(\mathcal{Q}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
from
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
having the property that
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is generating if and only if
(\mathcal{Q}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is.

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
(X,f)
be a mapping family that is given as in (
6.1
) and let
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
with
\mathcal{P}_{n}=\{R_{n,1},\dots,R_{n,q_{n}}\}
be a nonstationary Markov partition for
(X,f)
. Define
(\mathcal{Q}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
by

\mathcal{Q}_{n}=\{S_{n,i,j}=R_{n,i}\cap f^{-1}(R_{n+1,j})\colon S_{n,i,j}\not=\emptyset\}
. Then the following assertions are true.

(i)

(\mathcal{Q}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a nonstationary Markov partition for
(X,f)
.

(ii)

(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is generating if and only if
(\mathcal{Q}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is generating.

Proof.

To prove (i) fix a sequence sequence
(i_{n},j_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\{1,\dots,q_{n}\}^{2}
satisfying

S_{k,i_{k},j_{k}}\cap f^{-1}(S_{k+1,i_{k+1}j_{k+1}})\neq\emptyset\quad\mbox{for all}\ k\in\{n,\ldots,n+m-1\}.

By the definition of
S_{n,i,j}
this can be written as

R_{k,i_{k}}\cap f^{-1}(R_{k+1,j_{k}})\cap f^{-1}(R_{k+1,i_{k+1}})\cap f^{-2}(R_{k+2,j_{k+1}})\neq\emptyset

for all
k\in\{n,\ldots,n+m-1\}
. Because
f^{-1}(R_{n,i})
,
i\in\{1,\ldots,q_{n}\}
, are pairwise disjoint this implies that
j_{k}=i_{k+1}
for
k\in\{n,\ldots,n+m-1\}
. Hence, redefining
i_{n+m+1}=j_{n+m}
for convenience, we gain

R_{k,i_{k}}\cap f^{-1}(R_{k+1,i_{k+1}})\cap f^{-2}(R_{k+2,i_{k+2}})\neq\emptyset\quad\mbox{for all}\ k\in\{n,\ldots,n+m-1\}.

Because
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a nonstationary Markov partition for
(X,f)
, using the definition of
S_{i,j,n}
we finally get

\displaystyle S_{n,i_{n},i_{n+1}}\cap f^{-1}(S_{n+1,i_{n+1},i_{n+2}})\cap\cdots\cap f^{-m}(S_{n+m,i_{n+m},i_{n+m+1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R_{n,i_{n}}\cap f^{-1}(R_{n+1,i_{n+1}})\cap\cdots\cap f^{-m-1}(R_{n+m,i_{n+m+1}})

\displaystyle\neq\emptyset.

This proves that
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a nonstationary Markov partition for
(X,f)
.

To prove (ii) assume first that
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is generating. Because
\mathcal{Q}_{n}
is a refinement of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
this immediately implies that
(\mathcal{Q}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is generating as well. To see the other direction, assume that
(\mathcal{Q}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is generating. Then by the definition of
(\mathcal{Q}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
in terms of
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
we have

\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\bigcap_{|k|<n}f^{-k}(R_{k,i_{k}})}=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\bigcap_{|k|<n}f^{-k}(S_{k,i_{k},i_{k+1}})},

hence, also
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is generating.
∎

In
[
AF05
]
, nonstationary Markov partitions are treated in a slightly different way building on Bowen’s definition of a stationary Markov partition
[
Bow75
]
. Property M is reminiscent of the definition of Bowen proposed in
[
Bow75
, Section 3C]
. Note that the partitions
H
(for “horizontal”) and
V
(for “vertical”) are abstract models for the unstable and stable foliations used by Bowen
[
Bow75
]
.

6.3.
Symbolic representation of a mapping family

We now discuss how to associate appropriate symbolic representations to eventually Anosov mapping families; these symbolic representations are defined as nonstationary versions of (1-step) finite type shifts, and more precisely as nonstationary versions of the well-known vertex and edge shifts; see e.g.
[
LM95
, Definitions 2.2.5 and 2.3.7]
.

We now define these objects.
Let
\mathcal{D}
be some set. We recall that the shift operator
\Sigma
maps a sequence
(\omega_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{Z}}
to
(\omega_{n+1})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
.
5
5

5

The fact that shift operators on different spaces are always denoted by
\Sigma
should cause no confusion.
A dynamical system
(X,\Sigma)
with
X\subset\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{Z}}
is a
shift
if
X
is closed w.r.t. the product topology of the discrete topology on
X\subset\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.
A
bi-infinite Bratteli diagram
is an infinite directed graph
\mathscr{B}=(V,E)
, where
V=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{n}
,
E=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}E_{n}
, with source and range maps
s,r:E\to V
satisfying
s(E_{n})=V_{n}
and
r(E_{n})=V_{n+1}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. We assume that
|V_{n}|
and
|E_{n}|
are finite for all
n
.
With a Bratteli diagram
\mathscr{B}
, we associate an infinite sequence of transition matrices
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
.
For each “level”
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the matrix
A_{n}=(a_{n,w,v})
has dimension
|V_{n+1}|{\times}|V_{n}|
and entries
a_{n,w,v}=|\{e\in E_{n}:s(e)=v,\,r(e)=w\}|
, with
v\in V_{n}
,
w\in V_{n+1}
.

Definition 6.11

(Nonstationary edge and vertex shift)
.

Define

\begin{gathered}\begin{aligned} X_{\mathscr{B}}^{(0)}&=\bigg\{(e_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}E_{n}\,:\,r(e_{n})=s(e_{n+1})\ \text{for all}\ n\in\mathbb{Z}\bigg\},&X_{\mathscr{B}}^{(n)}&=\Sigma^{n}\big(X_{\mathscr{B}}^{(0)}\big),\\
\hat{X}_{\mathscr{B}}^{(0)}&=\bigg\{(v_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{n}\,:\,a_{n,v_{n+1},v_{n}}>0\ \text{for all}\ n\in\mathbb{Z}\bigg\},&\hat{X}_{\mathscr{B}}^{(n)}&=\Sigma^{n}\big(\hat{X}_{\mathscr{B}}^{(0)}\big),\end{aligned}\\
X_{\mathscr{B}}=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}X_{\mathscr{B}}^{(n)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\hat{X}_{\mathscr{B}}=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\hat{X}_{\mathscr{B}}^{(n)}.\end{gathered}

We call
(X_{\mathscr{B}},\Sigma)
and
(\hat{X}_{\mathscr{B}},\Sigma)
the
nonstationary edge shift
and the
nonstationary vertex shift
associated to
\mathscr{B}
, respectively.

In
[
AF05
,
Fis09
]
, the nonstationary edge shifts we defined here are called
nonstationary subshifts of finite type
.
6
6

6

We do not adopt this terminology because already in the stationary case edge shifts are only special cases of 1-step shifts of finite type.
Nonstationary edge shifts are also related to the two-sided constructions of Markov compacta (and Bratteli diagrams) introduced in
[
Buf13
,
Buf14
]
.

One can naturally associate a Bratelli diagram to a given sequence of nonnegative
d{\times}d
integer matrices
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
. Indeed, it follows from the definition that a Bratelli diagram is completely defined by providing its sequence
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
of transition matrices. Thus, setting
A_{n}=\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we get the
Bratteli diagram
\mathscr{B}
associated to the sequence of matrices
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
.
7
7

7

It will become clear in Section
15.3
why we use the transposed matrices here.
The Bratteli diagram associated to the sequence of matrices
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
from Example
6.9
is depicted in Figure
12
.

1
2
1
2
1
2
V_{-1}
V_{0}
V_{1}
\cdots
\cdots

Figure 12.
The Bratteli diagram from Example
6.9
(levels
-1,0,1
).

We now want to use nonstationary vertex shifts to provide a symbolic representation of a mapping family. Consider a mapping family
(X,f)
defined as in (
6.1
) and assume that
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
with
\mathcal{P}_{n}=\{R_{n,1},\dots,R_{n,q_{n}}\}
is a nonstationary Markov partition for
(X,f)
. With this Markov partition we associate a bi-infinite Bratteli diagram
\mathscr{B}=(V,E)
in the following way. The sets of vertices are given by
V_{n}=\{1,\dots,q_{n}\}
and the sets of edges are

E_{n}=\big\{(i,j)\;:\;i\in V_{n},j\in V_{n+1}\text{ with }R_{n,i}\cap f^{-1}(R_{n+1,j})\not=\emptyset\big\}.

Here
(i,j)
denotes an edge with
s(i,j)=i
and
r(i,j)=j
.

We provide the following result from
[
AF05
, Proposition 3.6]
, which forms a nonstationary version of
[
Adl98
, Theorem 6.5]
.

Proposition 6.12

(
[
AF05
, Proposition 3.6]
)
.

Let
(X,f)
be a mapping family with a generating nonstationary Markov partition
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
. Let
(\hat{X}_{\mathscr{B}},\Sigma)
be the vertex shift associated to this Markov partition. Then the maps

\psi_{n}:\,X_{\mathscr{B}}^{(n)}\to X_{n};\quad(i_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mapsto\mathbf{x},\;\text{ where}\;\bigcap_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\bigcap_{|k|<\ell}f^{n-k}(R_{k,i_{k}})}=\{\mathbf{x}\}.

are continuous and one-to-one except on the set
\psi_{n}^{-1}\big(\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\bigcup_{i\in\{1,\ldots,q_{k}\}}f^{n-k}(\partial R_{k,i})\big)
of pullbacks of the boundaries of the atoms
R_{k,i}
, and the diagram

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma}
\textstyle{X_{\mathscr{B}}^{(-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma}
\textstyle{X_{\mathscr{B}}^{(0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma}
\textstyle{X_{\mathscr{B}}^{(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma}
\textstyle{X_{\mathscr{B}}^{(2)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\cdots}

commutes. In this case
(\hat{X}_{\mathscr{B}},\Sigma)
is called a
topological symbolic representation
of
(X,f)
.

Consider a mapping family
(X,f)
defined as in (
6.1
) and assume that
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
with
\mathcal{P}_{n}=\{R_{n,1},\dots,R_{n,q_{n}}\}
is a nonstationary Markov partition for
(X,f)
. Sometimes one wants to associate a nonstationary edge shift with a Markov partition of
(X,f)
. In this case, the edges of the Bratteli diagram are given by
E_{n}=\{i,\dots,q_{n}\}
. If the vertices are suitably defined, one gets a result that is analogous to Proposition
6.12
; see Theorem
15.7
.

7.
Bi-infinite sequences of matrices

In this section, we develop the basic theory of bi-infinite sequences of matrices with special emphasis on convergence properties of such sequences. In particular, our main aim is to give natural criteria for strong convergence of a bi-infinite sequence of matrices. These criteria are of independent interest, as strong convergence is of importance for instance in the context of Diophantine approximation properties of multidimensional continued fractions. The results of this section lay the foundation for our main results in Chapter
V
.

Section
7.1
contains the definition of primitivity and recurrence, two properties of a sequence of matrices that will be basic for all that follows. In Section
7.2
we attach generalized eigenvectors to a sequence of matrices. Moreover, we show that these eigenvalues always exist for primitive and recurrent sequences of matrices. Using the generalized right eigenvector, we then define three notions of convergence: weak, strong, and exponential convergence. Section
7.3
defines Lyapunov exponents for a sequence of matrices and introduces the so-called (local) Pisot condition. This condition assures that a sequence of matrices has one expanding direction and contracts in all the other directions, a behavior that is reminiscent of a Pisot number. As we see in Section
7.4
, the Pisot condition is crucial when deriving convergence properties of a sequence of matrices. The main part of this subsection is occupied by the proof of Lemma
7.14
, a local version of the famous Oseledets Multiplicative Ergodic Theorem. This lemma is the key ingredient of the proof of the fact that the Pisot condition is equivalent to exponential convergence under quite mild conditions. Another result shows the effect of the Pisot condition on the behaviour of the eigenvalues of products of consecutive matrices of the underlying sequence of matrices. Section
7.5
defines the concept of algebraic irreducibility. Algebraic irreducibility entails that the generalized right eigenvector has rationally independent coordinated, a property that will be crucial later, when we need that certain Rauzy fractals have non-empty interior. This subsection also contains our main convergence result, formulated as Theorem
7.21
, which was already stated as Theorem
Theorem A
.
in the introduction.
We need strong convergence in Section
7.6
to get an Anosov splitting associated to a sequence of matrices. This will be crucial later when we define a general class of Rauzy fractals that is used in Chapter
V
to define induced rotations and atoms of nonstationary Markov partitions for multidimensional continued fraction algorithms.

We start with some basic terminology. We say that a real number
x
is
nonnegative
if
x\geq 0
, and
positive
if
x>0
; we say that a vector or a matrix is
nonnegative
if all its coefficients are nonnegative, and
positive
if all its coefficients are positive.
Let
d\geq 1
. For a semiring
R
, we denote the set of
d{\times}d
matrices with entries taken from
R
by
R^{d\times d}
. The
general linear group
of degree
d
over
\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathbb{R}
will be denoted by

\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})=\{M\in\mathbb{Z}^{d\times d}:\det M=\pm 1\}\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ \operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{R})=\{M\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}:\det M\neq 0\},

respectively. A matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with
\det M=\pm 1
is called
unimodular
.

To avoid trivialities, in the sequel we will always assume that
d\geq 2
.

7.1.
Primitivity and recurrence

For
d\geq 2
, let
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a bi-infinite sequence of nonnegative matrices in
\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{R})
. Because we are interested in the product of consecutive matrices, it is convenient to use the notation

M_{[m,n)}=M_{m}M_{m+1}\cdots M_{n-1}\quad\text{for}\ m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\ \text{with}\ m\leq n,

where
M_{[m,m)}
denotes the
d{\times}d
identity matrix.
The first important property of a bi-infinite sequence
\mathbf{M}
of nonnegative matrices in
\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{R})
,
d\geq 2
, is the following notion of primitivity.

Definition 7.1

(Primitivity)
.

We say that a sequence
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
of nonnegative matrices in
\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{R})
is
primitive (in the future)
if, for each
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is
n>m
such that the matrix
M_{[m,n)}
is positive.
We say that it is
primitive in the past
if, for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is
m<n
such that the matrix
M_{[m,n)}
is positive.
If
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is primitive in the future and in the past, we say that it is
two-sided primitive
.

Remark 7.2
.

In the course of this monograph we will define several notions that have two directions, one
in the future
and one
in the past
. Besides that, two-sided versions will occur. Since the future will be of greater importance for us, the “default version” for each of these properties is the future.
Thus the suffix “in the future” will not be written out.
So if we say that a sequence
\mathbf{M}
is
primitive
, we mean that it is
primitive in the future
.

Let
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a primitive sequence of nonnegative matrices with values in
\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{R})
. Then there is
n_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for each
n\geq n_{1}
,
M_{[0,n)}
is positive and, hence, by the Perron–Frobenius theorem,
M_{[0,n)}
has a dominant positive eigenvalue
\mbox{l}{}_{1}(n)
, which is simple and associated to a positive eigenvector. If the matrices in
\mathbf{M}
are integer matrices, then, since
d\geq 2
, we even have
\mbox{l}{}_{1}(n)>1
because each entry of each positive vector increases strictly when multiplied by the positive integer matrix
M_{[0,n)}
.
Furthermore, for each
k>0
there is
n_{k}>0
such that for each
n\geq n_{k}
the matrix
M_{[0,n)}
can be written as the product of
k
positive integer matrices. Thus each coefficient of
M_{[0,n)}
(for
n\geq n_{k}
) is larger than
d^{k-1}
. This implies that
\mbox{l}{}_{1}(n)
tends to infinity for
n\to\infty
.

The next property we will need is the following notion of recurrence.

Definition 7.3

(Eventual recurrence)
.

We say that a sequence
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
of matrices in
\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{R})
is
eventually recurrent (in the future)
if there is
m\in\mathbb{Z}
such that, for each
\ell\geq 1
, we have
(M_{n},\dots,M_{n+\ell})=(M_{m},\dots,M_{m+\ell})
for some
n>m
.
Analogously, we say that
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is
eventually recurrent in the past
if there is
m\in\mathbb{Z}
such that, for each
\ell\geq 1
, we have
(M_{n-\ell},\dots,M_{n})=(M_{m-\ell},\dots,M_{m})
for some
n<m
.

In this Definition
7.3
we never use the fact that the sequence
\mathbf{M}
is specifically a sequence of matrices. Indeed, eventual recurrence is meaningful for arbitrary bi-infinite sequences
(\omega_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
with values in any alphabet.

7.2.
Generalized eigenvectors and convergence

Primitivity and recurrence entail the following convergence result, which follows from
[
Fur60
, pp. 91–95]
.

Proposition 7.4
.

If a sequence
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
of nonnegative matrices in
\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{R})
is primitive and eventually recurrent, then there exists a positive vector
\mathbf{u}
such that

(7.1)

\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}M_{[0,n)}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\mathbf{u}.

If
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is primitive in the past and eventually recurrent in the past,
then there exists a positive vector
\mathbf{v}
such that

(7.2)

\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\vphantom{\!M}{}^{t}\!M_{[-n,0)}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\mathbf{v}.

Proof.

Because Assertion (
7.1
) concerns only matrices with nonnegative index, its proof is the same as the proof of
[
Thu20
, Proposition 3.5.5]
. Assertion (
7.2
) follows by applying (
7.1
) to the sequence
(\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{-n-1})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
.
∎

In the important case where the primitive sequence
\mathbf{M}=(M)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is constant, that is, if we consider the powers of a primitive matrix
M
in the intersections in (
7.1
) and (
7.2
), the vectors
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{v}
are the right and left Perron–Frobenius eigenvector of
M
, respectively.
This motivates the following definition.

Definition 7.5

(Generalized right and left eigenvector)
.

We say that a sequence
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
of matrices in
\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{R})

admits a generalized eigenvector (in the future)
if there exists a vector
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
such that
\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}M_{[0,n)}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\mathbf{u}
.
The vector
\mathbf{u}
is called a
generalized right eigenvector
of
\mathbf{M}
.

We say that
\mathbf{M}

admits a generalized eigenvector in the past
if there exists a vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
such that
\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[-n,0)}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\mathbf{v}
, and
\mathbf{v}
is called a
generalized left eigenvector
of
\mathbf{M}
.

Transposed matrices are used for generalized eigenvectors in the past in order to get nested cones. The generalized eigenvectors can be seen as a nonstationary analog of the Perron–Frobenius eigenvectors of a primitive matrix.

If a given bi-infinite sequence
\mathbf{M}
of matrices in
\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{R})
admits a generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{u}
and a generalized left eigenvector
\mathbf{v}
, respectively, then we easily see that, for each
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, the shifted sequence
\Sigma^{m}\mathbf{M}=(M_{n+m})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
admits the generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{u}_{m}
and the generalized left eigenvector
\mathbf{v}_{m}
, respectively, with

(7.3)

\mathbf{u}_{m}=\begin{cases}M_{[0,m)}^{-1}\,\mathbf{u}&\text{for }m\geq 0,\\[2.15277pt]
M_{[m,0)}\,\mathbf{u}&\text{for}\ m<0,\end{cases}\qquad\mathbf{v}_{m}=\begin{cases}\vphantom{\!M}{}^{t}\!M_{[0,m)}\,\mathbf{v}&\text{for }m\geq 0,\\[2.15277pt]
\vphantom{\!M}{}^{t}\!M_{[m,0)}^{-1}\,\mathbf{v}&\text{for}\ m<0;\end{cases}

note that
\mathbf{u}_{0}=\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{v}_{0}=\mathbf{v}
.

Example 7.6

(Farey matrices)
.

Consider a two-sided eventually recurrent sequence
\mathbf{M}
that contains both of the
Farey matrices

M_{\mathrm{F},1}
and
M_{\mathrm{F},2}
from (
2.3
).
Since
M_{\mathrm{F},1}M_{\mathrm{F},2}
as well as
M_{\mathrm{F},2}M_{\mathrm{F},1}
is positive,
\mathbf{M}
is two-sided primitive and, hence, satisfies the conditions of Proposition
7.4
in the past and in the future. Thus
\mathbf{M}
admits a generalized left and a generalized right eigenvector. The same is true for all shifts
\Sigma^{m}\mathbf{M}
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, of this sequence. The matrices
M_{\mathrm{F},1}
and
M_{\mathrm{F},2}
are intimately related to the Farey algorithm (
2.1
), see also Section
9.1
.

We now want to define some notions of convergence for sequences of matrices that play a role in the context of multidimensional continued fraction algorithms; cf. e.g.
[
Sch00
, Definitions 10 and 11]
. To state these definitions, we need some notations. The standard basis vectors of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
are denoted by
\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{d}
. We will write
\mathrm{d}(\cdot,\cdot)
for the Euclidean distance on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and if
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then
\mathrm{d}(\mathbf{w},X):=\inf_{\mathbf{x}\in X}\mathrm{d}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{x})
. Moreover, for
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
let

\mathbf{w}^{\perp}=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\,\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w}\rangle=0\}.

For any
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
with
\mathbf{u}\notin\mathbf{v}^{\perp}
, let

(7.4)

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}:

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbf{v}^{\perp},

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbf{x}-\frac{\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{v}\rangle}{\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\rangle}\mathbf{u},\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\tilde{\pi}_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}:

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u},

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{v}\rangle}{\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\rangle}\mathbf{u},

denote the projection along
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}
to
\mathbf{v}^{\perp}
and the projection along
\mathbf{v}^{\perp}
to
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}
, respectively.

Definition 7.7

(Weak, strong, exponential convergence)
.

Let
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a sequence of matrices in
\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{R})
and let
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
.
We say that
\mathbf{M}
is
weakly convergent (in the future)
to
\mathbf{u}
if

(7.5)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\mathrm{d}\bigg(\frac{M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{e}_{i}}{\lVert M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{e}_{i}\rVert},\frac{\mathbf{u}}{\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert}\bigg)=0\quad\hbox{for all}\ i\in\{1,\dots,d\}.

We say that
\mathbf{M}
is
strongly convergent (in the future)
to
\mathbf{u}
if

(7.6)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\mathrm{d}(M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{e}_{i},\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u})=0\quad\hbox{for all}\ i\in\{1,\dots,d\}.

We say that
\mathbf{M}
is
exponentially convergent (in the future)
to
\mathbf{u}
if there exist
C,\alpha>0
such that

(7.7)

\mathrm{d}(M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{e}_{i},\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u})\leq Ce^{-\alpha n}\quad\hbox{for all}\ n\in\mathbb{N},\,i\in\{1,\dots,d\}.

If
\mathbf{M}
is replaced by
(\vphantom{\!}^{t}\!M_{-n-1})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
in these definitions of convergence, we say that
\mathbf{M}
is weakly, strongly, and exponentially convergent in the past, respectively.

These notions of convergence are invariant under shifts in the sense that, for each
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, a sequence
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
converges weakly [strongly, exponentially] if and only if the same is true for the shifted sequence
\Sigma^{m}\mathbf{M}=(M_{n+m})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
.

Weak convergence is convergence in the projective space, while strong convergence is convergence in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
of the sequence of vectors
M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{e}_{i}
to the line
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}
, which is the same as convergence of
\pi_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}}(M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{e}_{i})
to
\mathbf{0}
, for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
. Exponential convergence says that the speed of strong convergence is geometric. Obviously, exponential convergence implies strong convergence, which in turn implies weak convergence. According to Proposition
7.4
, primitivity and eventual recurrence imply weak convergence. It is easy to give examples of nonprimitive not eventually recurrent sequences that are weakly convergent; for example, many sequences of diagonal matrices converge weakly.

Remark 7.8
.

Exponential convergence also implies absolute convergence of any series of the form
\sum_{n\geq 0}\alpha_{n}\lVert\mathrm{d}(M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{e}_{i_{n}},\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u})\rVert
with bounded (or slowly growing) coefficients
\alpha_{n}
; this fact will be of importance later when we give a criterion for balance; see e.g. Theorem
11.8
below.

7.3.
Pisot condition and Lyapunov exponents

Given a sequence
\mathbf{M}
of matrices in
\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})
,
d\geq 2
, our aim is now to give a sufficient condition for strong and even exponential convergence. This condition, which is formulated in Theorem
7.21
(see also the informal version in Theorem
Theorem A
.
in the introduction), is quite general. For that purpose, we first recall basic definitions on convergence and Lyapunov exponents in Section
7.3
, in particular the local Pisot condition, before proving Theorem
7.21
in Sections
7.4
and
7.5
.

Let us first recall some definitions. A
Pisot number
is a real algebraic integer
\mbox{l}{}>1
whose Galois conjugates are all strictly smaller than
1
in modulus. An integer matrix is a
Pisot matrix
if its characteristic polynomial is the minimal polynomial of a Pisot number. This motivates the following definition of a Pisot condition for a sequence of matrices (see also Remark
7.10
). Contrary to the eigenvalues used for a single matrix, in the setting of sequences of matrices, singular values turn out to be more convenient than eigenvalues. Recall that, for a matrix
M\in\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})
,
d\geq 2
, the
singular values

\delta_{1}(M),\ldots,\delta_{d}(M)
are the eigenvalues of the matrix
(\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!MM)^{1/2}
; these values are positive and real, and we always assume that they are ordered in a way that

(7.8)

\delta_{1}(M)\geq\delta_{2}(M)\geq\cdots\geq\delta_{d}(M).

Definition 7.9

(Local Pisot condition)
.

We say that a sequence
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
of nonnegative matrices in
\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})
satisfies the
local Pisot condition (in the future)
8
8

8

The terminology “local” refers to the fact we consider here a single sequence of matrices.
if

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\delta_{2}(M_{[0,n)})<0.

We say that
\mathbf{M}
satisfies the
local Pisot condition in the past
if
(\vphantom{\!}^{t}\!M_{-n-1})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies the local Pisot condition in the future.
If
\mathbf{M}
satisfies the local Pisot condition in the future and in the past, we say that it satisfies the
two-sided local Pisot condition
.

Remark 7.10
.

For a matrix
M\in\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})
, the product of all singular values of
M
equals
\lvert\det(M)\rvert\!=\!1
. Hence,
\delta_{2}(M)<1
implies that
\delta_{1}(M)>1
, because otherwise, in view of (
7.8
), the product of all singular values of
M
would be strictly less than
1
. Therefore, if a sequence
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})
satisfies the local Pisot condition, we gain

\begin{split}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\delta_{1}(M_{[0,n)})&>0,\\
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\delta_{j}(M_{[0,n)})&<0\qquad(j\in\{2,\ldots,d\}).\end{split}

This illustrates the analogy to the definition of a Pisot number.

The (local) Pisot condition is invariant under shifts of sequences. Moreover, in this condition we consider a
\limsup
instead of a limit since the existence of the limit is hard to prove in general, and negativity of the
\limsup
is strong enough for our purposes. However, if limits of this kind exist for a sequence
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
of matrices in
\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})
, one defines the
(local)
i
-th Lyapunov exponent

\vartheta_{i}
as the limit

(7.9)

\vartheta_{i}:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log(\delta_{i}(M_{[0,n)})).

The
(local) Lyapunov spectrum
is the set of all Lyapunov exponents, each one counted with its multiplicity. The Lyapunov exponents can also be defined recursively using exterior powers
\wedge^{k}
(see for example
[
Arn98
, Proposition 3.2.7]
) by

(7.10)

\vartheta_{1}+\cdots+\vartheta_{k}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert\wedge^{k}M_{[0,n)}\rVert\qquad(1\leq k\leq d),

provided that the limits exist. Each of the two equivalent definitions, (
7.9
) and (
7.10
), yield that
\vartheta_{1}\geq\vartheta_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\vartheta_{d}
. For the powers of a single invertible matrix, i.e., when
\mathbf{M}=(M)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a constant sequence, it is well known that the Lyapunov exponents are just the logarithms of the moduli of the eigenvalues of
M
; see e.g.
[
Arn98
, 3.2.3 Example (ii)]
. If
M
has complex eigenvalues, they come in pairs of conjugates. Thus the corresponding Lyapunov exponent of
\mathbf{M}
cannot be simple.

Definition 7.11

(Strong Pisot condition)
.

A sequence
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
of nonnegative matrices in
\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})
satisfies the
strong (local) Pisot condition
if it admits Lyapunov exponents
\vartheta_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\vartheta_{d}
(listed according to their multiplicity) such that
\vartheta_{1}>0>\vartheta_{2}
.
The sequence
\mathbf{M}
satisfies the
strong (local) Pisot condition in the past
if
(\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{-n-1})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies the strong Pisot condition. If
\mathbf{M}
satisfies the strong local Pisot condition in the future and in the past, we say that it satisfies the
strong two-sided local Pisot condition
.

The following lemma is immediate from the definitions.

Lemma 7.12
.

Let
\mathbf{M}
be a sequence of nonnegative matrices in
\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})
. If
\mathbf{M}
satisfies the strong local Pisot condition, then it satisfies the local Pisot condition; the analogous result is true for the past.

Remark 7.13
.

Here we use the terminlolgy
local
Lyapunov exponent and
local
Pisot condition, since these objects are defined for a single sequence of matrices. We will later define
global
versions of these notions that will concern generic elements of families of sequences of matrices that are defined by some condition (see for instance Definitions
8.3
and
8.6
). Nevertheless, if there is no risk of confusion, we omit the prefix “local”.

In the remaining part of this section, we only work with the local Pisot condition, not the strong one. As mentioned before, this is essential because it is fairly easy to come up with concrete sequences
\mathbf{M}
satisfying the local Pisot condition; see for instance Example
7.22
, or Propositions
10.4
and
10.8
. Exhibiting a nontrivial aperiodic sequence
\mathbf{M}
satisfying the strong Pisot condition seems to be hard (of course, examples using matrices that are simultaneously diagonalizable can easily be constructed).

In all that follows,
\lVert\cdot\rVert
denotes an arbitrary norm,
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{p}
the
p
-norm for
p\in[1,\infty)
(which is the Euclidean norm for
p=2
) and
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty}
the maximum norm on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The matrix norms associated to these norms will also be denoted by
\lVert\cdot\rVert\|
,
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{p}
and
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty}
, respectively. The standard scalar product is denoted by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. We will confine ourselves to unimodular integer matrices with nonnegative entries, i.e., to matrices taken from the class

\mathcal{M}_{d}=\mathbb{N}^{d\times d}\cap\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z}).

7.4.
Pisot condition and exponential convergence

We will now prove that for a sequence
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, the Pisot condition together with a mild growth condition on the matrices
M_{n}
implies exponential convergence. This will be of great importance on our way to prove Theorem
7.21
. We state the according result as Proposition
7.15
, where we establish the existence of a contracting plane defined in terms of a generalized right eigenvector with exponential convergence, given a sequence
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
of nonnegative unimodular integer matrices satisfying the local Pisot condition. This involves the
growth condition

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert M_{n}\rVert=0
. Note that this growth condition is reminiscent of the log-integrability assumption (
8.2
) that holds in the generic setting. The following lemma is a first step toward Proposition
7.15
.

Lemma 7.14
.

Let
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a sequence of nonnegative unimodular integer matrices satisfying the local Pisot condition and the growth condition
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert M_{n}\rVert=0
.
Then there exists a vector
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
such that
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}|_{\mathbf{a}^{\perp}}\rVert<0
.

Proof.

The proof is inspired by the proofs of
[
Rue79
, Proposition 1.3]
and
[
Arn98
, Lemma 3.4.6]
.
By equivalence of norms, it suffices to work with the Euclidean norm.
For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\vphantom{\!M}{}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}=B(n)D(n)A(n)
be the singular value decomposition of
\vphantom{\!M}{}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}
, with diagonal matrix
D(n)=\mathrm{diag}(\delta_{1}(n),\dots,\delta_{d}(n))
, where we write
\delta_{i}(n)=\delta_{i}(\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)})
, and orthogonal matrices
A(n),B(n)
.
Recall that
\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{d}\}
stands for the standard basis.
Let
\mathbf{a}(n)=\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A(n)\mathbf{e}_{1}
.
By the Pisot condition and the growth condition
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert M_{n}\rVert_{2}=0
, we may choose
\beta<0
and
C>0
in a way that

(7.11)

\delta_{2}(n)\lVert M_{n}\rVert_{2}\leq Ce^{\beta n}\qquad(n\in\mathbb{N}).

We first show that there exists
K>0
such that for all
n,k\in\mathbb{N}

(7.12)

\max\big\{|\langle\mathbf{a}(n{+}k),\mathbf{y}(n)\rangle|\,:\,\mathbf{y}(n)\in(\mathbf{a}(n))^{\perp},\,\lVert\mathbf{y}(n)\rVert_{2}=1\big\}\leq K\frac{e^{\beta n}}{\delta_{1}(n)}.

Observe that

(7.13)

\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{a}(n)=B(n)D(n)\mathbf{e}_{1}=\delta_{1}(n)B(n)\mathbf{e}_{1}.

Thus

(7.14)

\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{a}(n)\rVert_{2}=\delta_{1}(n).

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbf{y}(n)\in\mathbf{a}(n)^{\perp}
with
\lVert\mathbf{y}(n)\rVert_{2}=1
be arbitrary but fixed. Then, by orthogonality of
A(n)
, there exist
z_{2},\ldots,z_{d}\in\mathbb{R}
with
z_{2}^{2}{+}\cdots{+}z_{d}^{2}=1
satisfying
\mathbf{y}(n)=\sum_{i=2}^{d}z_{i}\,\vphantom{\!}^{t}\!{A}(n)\mathbf{e}_{i}
. Therefore, we gain

(7.15)

\displaystyle\vphantom{\!M}{}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{y}(n)

\displaystyle=\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}\sum_{i=2}^{d}z_{i}\,\vphantom{\!}^{t}\!{A}(n)\mathbf{e}_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=2}^{d}z_{i}B(n)D(n)\mathbf{e}_{i}=\sum_{i=2}^{d}\delta_{i}(n)z_{i}B(n)\mathbf{e}_{i}.

Because
B(n)
is an orthogonal matrix and
z_{2}^{2}{+}\cdots{+}z_{d}^{2}=1
, this yields

(7.16)

\hskip-3.99994pt\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{y}(n)\rVert_{2}=\bigg\lVert\sum_{i=2}^{d}\delta_{i}(n)z_{i}B(n)\mathbf{e}_{i}\bigg\rVert_{2}\hskip-2.5pt=\!\sum_{i=2}^{d}\delta_{i}(n)^{2}z_{i}^{2}\leq\delta_{2}(n)\sum_{i=2}^{d}z_{i}^{2}=\delta_{2}(n).\hskip-7.5pt

This implies that

(7.17)

\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n+1)}\mathbf{y}(n)\rVert_{2}=\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{n}\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{y}(n)\rVert_{2}\leq\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{n}\rVert_{2}\,\delta_{2}(n).

We now represent
\mathbf{y}(n)
as
\mathbf{y}(n)=\alpha(n)\mathbf{a}(n{+}1)+\mathbf{z}(n)
with
\mathbf{z}(n)\in\mathbf{a}(n{+}1)^{\perp}
.
To prove (
7.12
) we first want to make sure that
|\alpha(n)|
is small. Since
\mathbf{z}(n)\in\mathbf{a}(n{+}1)^{\perp}
, we have, using (
7.13
), that

\displaystyle\langle\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n+1)}\mathbf{z}(n),\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n+1)}\mathbf{a}(n{+}1)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{z}(n),M_{[0,n+1)}\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n+1)}\mathbf{a}(n{+}1)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{z}(n),\delta_{1}(n{+}1)M_{[0,n+1)}B(n{+}1)\mathbf{e}_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{z}(n),\delta_{1}(n{+}1)\,\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A(n{+}1)D(n{+}1)\,\vphantom{B}^{t}B(n{+}1)B(n{+}1)\mathbf{e}_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{z}(n),\delta_{1}(n{+}1)^{2}\,\vphantom{\!A}{}^{t}\!A(n{+}1)\mathbf{e}_{1}\rangle=\delta_{1}(n{+}1)^{2}\langle\mathbf{z}(n),\mathbf{a}(n{+}1)\rangle=0.

This orthogonality implies the inequality in

(7.18)

\displaystyle\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n+1)}\mathbf{y}(n)\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert\alpha(n)\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n+1)}\mathbf{a}(n{+}1)+\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n+1)}\mathbf{z}(n)\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\lVert\alpha(n)\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n+1)}\mathbf{a}(n{+}1)\rVert_{2}=|\alpha(n)|\,\delta_{1}(n{+}1).

Note that the last equality is a consequence of (
7.14
).
Combining (
7.17
) and (
7.18
) we obtain

|\alpha(n)|\leq\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{n}\rVert_{2}\,\frac{\delta_{2}(n)}{\delta_{1}(n{+}1)}

and, hence, (
7.11
) yields

(7.19)

\displaystyle|\langle\mathbf{a}(n{+}1),\mathbf{y}(n)\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\langle\mathbf{a}(n{+}1),\alpha(n)\mathbf{a}(n{+}1)+\mathbf{z}(n)\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\alpha(n)|\leq\frac{\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{n}\rVert_{2}\delta_{2}(n)}{\delta_{1}(n{+}1)}\leq\frac{Ce^{\beta n}}{\delta_{1}(n{+}1)}.

For
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
, let

\gamma_{i,j}=\arccos\big(\max\big\{\langle\mathbf{a}(n{+}i),\mathbf{y}(n{+}j)\rangle\,:\,\mathbf{y}(n{+}j)\in(\mathbf{a}(n{+}j))^{\perp},\,\lVert\mathbf{y}(n{+}j)\rVert_{2}=1\big\}\big)

be the smallest angle between
\mathbf{a}(n{+}i)
and
\mathbf{y}(n{+}j)
, with
\mathbf{y}(n{+}j)\in(\mathbf{a}(n{+}j))^{\bot}
(and
\lVert\mathbf{y}(n{+}j)\rVert_{2}=1
). If
\mathbf{a}(n{+}i)=\mathbf{a}(n{+}j)
, then
\gamma_{i,j}=\frac{\pi}{2}
, otherwise
\gamma_{i,j}\in\big[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\big)
is equal to the angle between
\mathbf{a}(n{+}i)
and the orthogonal projection of
\mathbf{a}(n{+}i)
onto
(\mathbf{a}(n{+}j))^{\bot}
. In each of these cases,
\frac{\pi}{2}-\gamma_{i,j}
is the angle between
\mathbf{a}(n{+}i)
and
\mathbf{a}(n{+}j)
. Thus, by the triangle inequality, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
we have

(7.20)

\tfrac{\pi}{2}-\gamma_{k,0}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(\tfrac{\pi}{2}-\gamma_{j+1,j}).

Now, because (
7.19
) holds for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and all
\mathbf{y}(n)\in(\mathbf{a}(n))^{\bot}
with
\lVert\mathbf{y}(n)\rVert_{2}=1
, it implies together with the well-known fact that
x\leq 2\sin x
for
x\in\big[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\big]
that

(7.21)

\tfrac{\pi}{2}-\gamma_{j+1,j}\leq 2\sin(\tfrac{\pi}{2}-\gamma_{j+1,j})=2\cos\gamma_{j+1,j}\leq\frac{2Ce^{\beta(n+j)}}{\delta_{1}(n{+}j{+}1)}\qquad(j\geq 0).

Because
\sin x\leq x
for
x\geq 0
, we gain from the definition of
\gamma_{i,j}
, (
7.20
), and (
7.21
) that there is
K>0
such that for all
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\displaystyle\max\{|\langle\mathbf{a}({n+k}),\mathbf{y}(n)\rangle|\,:\,\mathbf{y}(n)\in(\mathbf{a}(n))^{\perp},\,\lVert\mathbf{y}(n)\rVert_{2}=1\}=\cos\gamma_{k,0}=\sin(\tfrac{\pi}{2}-\gamma_{k,0})

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{\pi}{2}-\gamma_{k,0}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(\tfrac{\pi}{2}-\gamma_{j+1,j})\leq\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\frac{2Ce^{\beta(n+j)}}{\delta_{1}(n{+}j{+}1)}\leq K\frac{e^{\beta n}}{\delta_{1}(n)}

In the last inequality, we have used the monotonicity of
\delta_{1}(n)
, which holds because the matrices
M_{n}
are nonnegative. Therefore, (
7.12
) holds.

Because (
7.12
) immediately implies that
(\mathbf{a}(n))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence (this also follows directly from (
7.21
)) and, hence, converges to a unit vector
\mathbf{a}
, we conclude from (
7.12
) that

(7.22)

\max\{|\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{y}(n)\rangle|\,:\,\mathbf{y}(n)\in\mathbf{a}(n)^{\perp},\,\lVert\mathbf{y}(n)\rVert_{2}=1\}\leq K\,\frac{e^{\beta n}}{\delta_{1}(n)}\qquad(n\in\mathbb{N}).

This is equivalent to

(7.23)

\max\{|\langle\mathbf{a}(n),\mathbf{y}\rangle|\,:\,\mathbf{y}\in\mathbf{a}^{\perp},\,\lVert\mathbf{y}\rVert_{2}=1\}\leq K\,\frac{e^{\beta n}}{\delta_{1}(n)}\qquad(n\in\mathbb{N}).

If
\mathbf{a}(n)=\mathbf{a}
this is obvious. If not, we consider the vectors

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}_{0}(n)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi_{\mathbf{a}(n),\mathbf{a}(n)}(\mathbf{a})}{\lVert\pi_{\mathbf{a}(n),\mathbf{a}(n)}(\mathbf{a})\rVert_{2}}=\frac{\mathbf{a}{-}\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}(n)\rangle\mathbf{a}(n)}{\lVert\mathbf{a}{-}\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}(n)\rangle\mathbf{a}(n)\rVert_{2}}\quad\mbox{in \eqref{e:orthogonalU1} and}

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}}(\mathbf{a}(n))}{\lVert\pi_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}}(\mathbf{a}(n))\rVert_{2}}\hskip 8.53581pt=\frac{\mathbf{a}(n){-}\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}(n)\rangle\mathbf{a}}{\lVert\mathbf{a}(n){-}\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}(n)\rangle\mathbf{a}\rVert_{2}}\quad\mbox{in \eqref{e:orthogonalU}}.

It is easy to see that the maximum in (
7.22
) is attained for the choice
\mathbf{y}(n)=\mathbf{y}_{0}(n)
, and the maximum in (
7.23
) is attained for the choice
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{y}_{0}
. Since we have
\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{y}_{0}(n)\rangle=\langle\mathbf{a}(n),\mathbf{y}_{0}\rangle
, the two maxima in (
7.22
) and (
7.23
) are equal.

Choose
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbf{a}^{\perp}
with
\lVert\mathbf{y}\rVert_{2}=1
arbitrary. Write
\mathbf{y}=\eta(n)\mathbf{a}(n)+\mathbf{z}(n)
with
\mathbf{z}(n)\in\mathbf{a}(n)^{\perp}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. By (
7.23
) we have
|\eta(n)|\leq K\frac{e^{\beta n}}{\delta_{1}(n)}
, and therefore
\lVert\mathbf{z}(n)\rVert_{2}\leq 1{+}|\eta(n)|
. With (
7.14
), (
7.16
), and (
7.11
) we get

\displaystyle\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{y}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\eta(n)\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{a}(n)\rVert_{2}+\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{z}(n)\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq K\frac{e^{\beta n}}{\delta_{1}(n)}\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{a}(n)\rVert_{2}+\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{z}(n)\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ke^{\beta n}+\delta_{2}(n)(1{+}|\eta(n)|)\leq(K{+}2C)e^{\beta n}

for
n
large, because
|\eta(n)|\leq 1
for
n
large. This immediately yields

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\tfrac{1}{n}\log\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{y}\rVert_{2}\leq\beta\qquad\mbox{for all $\mathbf{y}\in\mathbf{a}^{\perp}$ with $\lVert\mathbf{y}\rVert_{2}=1$}.\qed

We can now relate the local Pisot condition with exponential convergence toward a generalized right eigenvector.

Proposition 7.15
.

Let
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a sequence of nonnegative unimodular integer matrices that satisfies the growth condition
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert M_{n}\rVert=0
.
Then the following assertions are equivalent.

(i)

The sequence
\mathbf{M}
satisfies the local Pisot condition.

(ii)

The sequence
\mathbf{M}
admits a generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{u}
and converges exponentially to
\mathbf{u}
.

(iii)

There exists a vector
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
such that
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}|_{\mathbf{a}^{\perp}}\rVert<0
.

If we replace the growth condition by a similar condition holding in the past, namely
\lim_{n\to-\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert M_{n}\rVert=0
, then the analogous equivalences hold for the past.

Proof.

We only prove the equivalences for the future. The according equivalences for the past follow along the same lines.
Assertion (
i
) implies (
iii
) by Lemma
7.14
.
We now prove that (
iii
) implies (
ii
). Let
\mathbf{a}
be as in (
iii
).
Note that for
\mathbf{b}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, if
\mathbf{x}
stands for the orthogonal projection of
\mathbf{b}_{1}
to
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{b}_{2}
, then, by the definition of the scalar product, we get
|\langle\mathbf{b}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{2}\rangle|=\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert\cdot\lVert\mathbf{b}_{2}\rVert
. Using this fact, for
i\in\{1,\dots,d\}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

(7.24)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{e}_{i},\mathbb{R}\mathbf{a})

\displaystyle=\lVert\pi_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}}M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{e}_{i}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\bigg|\bigg\langle M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{e}_{i},\frac{\pi_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}}M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{e}_{i}}{\lVert\pi_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}}M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{e}_{i}\rVert_{2}}\bigg\rangle\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\max\big\{|\langle M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{e}_{i},\mathbf{x}\rangle|\,:\,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{a}^{\perp},\,\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2}=1\big\}

\displaystyle=\max\big\{|\langle\mathbf{e}_{i},\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{x}\rangle|\,:\,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{a}^{\perp},\,\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2}=1\big\}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}|_{\mathbf{a}^{\perp}}\rVert_{2}.

By assumption, we have
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}|_{\mathbf{a}^{\perp}}\rVert<0
. Together with (
7.24
), this implies

\mathrm{d}(M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{e}_{i},\mathbb{R}\mathbf{a})\leq Ce^{\beta n}
for some
C>0
and
\beta<0
. Thus, by Definition
7.7
, the sequence
\mathbf{M}
converges exponentially to
\mathbf{a}
. Since exponential convergence implies weak convergence,
\mathbf{a}
is a generalized right eigenvector of
\mathbf{M}
. Note that
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
because
M_{[0,n)}
is a nonnegative matrix for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. This proves (
ii
).

It remains to prove that (
ii
) implies (
i
). Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
be arbitrary but fixed. We recall that the image of the unit sphere under
M_{[0,n)}
is an ellipsoid
E(n)
with lengths of its semiaxes given by the singular values
\delta_{1}(n)\geq\cdots\geq\delta_{d}(n)
.
Here, we write
\delta_{i}(n)=\delta_{i}(M_{[0,n)})
. By (ii),
\mathrm{d}(M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{e}_{i},\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u})\leq C_{1}e^{\beta n}
for some
C_{1}>0
and
\beta<0
. The ellipsoid
E(n)
is contained in a cylinder around
\mathbf{u}
of radius
C_{1}e^{\beta n}
.
Thus the Euclidean length of
d{-}1
of its semiaxes has to be bounded by
C_{2}e^{\beta n}
for some
C_{2}>0
. In particular,
\delta_{2}(n)\leq C_{2}e^{\beta n}
, which proves (
i
).
∎

The next step toward the proof of Theorem
7.21
is Proposition
7.17
. For a given a sequence
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfying the Pisot condition,
we establish a “Pisot property” for the
growth of eigenvalues of the products
M_{[0,n)}
, namely an exponential dominant behaviour for the largest eigenvalue. We first need the following preparatory result.

Lemma 7.16
.

Assume that the primitive sequence
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
admits a generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{u}
with
\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert=1
. Choose
m
in a way that
M_{[0,m)}
is positive. For
n\geq m
, let
\mathbf{u}_{1}(n)
be the positive dominant eigenvector of
M_{[0,n)}
satisfying
\lVert\mathbf{u}_{1}(n)\rVert=1
. Then
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{u}_{1}(n)=\mathbf{u}
.

Proof.

Because
\mathbf{M}
admits a generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{u}
, the sequence of vectors
\frac{M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{e}_{i}}{\lVert M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{e}_{i}\rVert}
converges to
\mathbf{u}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,d\}
. Thus, by convexity, for each sequence of positive vectors
(\mathbf{x}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, the sequence
\big(\frac{M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{x}_{n}}{\lVert M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{x}_{n}\rVert}\big)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
\mathbf{u}
.
In particular,
\big(\frac{M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{u}_{1}(n)}{\lVert M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{u}_{1}(n)\rVert}\big)_{n\geq m}
converges to
\mathbf{u}
. Since the dominant eigenvector satisfies
\mathbf{u}_{1}(n)=\frac{M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{u}_{1}(n)}{\lVert M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{u}_{1}(n)\rVert}
, the lemma follows.
∎

Proposition 7.17
.

Assume that
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
is primitive and satisfies the local Pisot condition. Choose
m\geq 0
in a way that
M_{[0,m)}
is a positive matrix.
Then there exist
\alpha>0
and
C>0
such that, for
n\geq m
we have

(7.25)

\mbox{l}{}_{1}(n)\geq C\,e^{\alpha n}

for the dominant eigenvalue
\mbox{l}{}_{1}(n)
of
M_{[0,n)}
and

(7.26)

|\mbox{l}{}(n)|\leq C\,e^{-\alpha n}

for all other eigenvalues
\mbox{l}{}(n)
of
M_{[0,n)}
.

Proof.

Throughout this proof, we work with the Euclidean norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{2}
. For
n\geq m
, let
\mathbf{u}_{1}(n)
be the normalized positive dominant eigenvector of the positive matrix
M_{[0,n)}
.

We first prove (
7.25
). Let
n\geq m
. We recall that the image of the unit sphere under
M_{[0,n)}
is an ellipsoid
E(n)
with lengths of its semiaxes given by the singular values
\delta_{1}(n)\geq\cdots\geq\delta_{d}(n)
.
Here, we write
\delta_{i}(n)=\delta_{i}(M_{[0,n)})
.
Let
\mathbf{w}_{1}(n)
be the positive unit vector in the direction of the largest axis of
E(n)
(where positivity is due to the fact that
\mathbf{w}_{1}(n)
is an eigenvector for the dominant eigenvalue
\delta_{1}(n)^{2}
of the positive and, hence, primitive, matrix
\vphantom{\!M}{}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}M_{[0,n)}
).
The singular values
\delta_{2}(n),\dots,\delta_{d}(n)
are the lengths of the semiaxes that are situated on the intersection of the ellipsoid
E(n)
with the hyperplane
H(n)=\mathbf{w}_{1}(n)^{\perp}
.
This hyperplane contains no nonnegative element other than the origin. Since
M_{[0,n)}^{-1}H(n)=(\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{w}_{1}(n))^{\perp}
and
\vphantom{\!M}{}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{w}_{1}(n)
is a positive vector, we gain that
M_{[0,n)}^{-1}H(n)
contains no positive element.
For any
\mathbf{x}\in M_{[0,n)}^{-1}H(n)
, we have
\lVert M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2}\leq\delta_{2}(n)\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2}
.

Let
\mathbf{w}=\delta_{1}(n)M_{[0,n)}^{-1}\mathbf{w}_{1}(n)
.
Since
H(n)
does not intersect the interior of the positive cone, we have
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}_{1}(n)\oplus M_{[0,n)}^{-1}H(n)=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, hence,

(7.27)

\mathbf{w}=x\,\mathbf{u}_{1}(n)+y\,\mathbf{u}_{2}(n),

for some
\mathbf{u}_{2}(n)\in M_{[0,n)}^{-1}H(n)
of unit norm and some
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
.
As
(\mathbf{u}_{1}(n))_{n\geq m}
converges to the (positive) generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{u}
of
\mathbf{M}
by Lemma
7.16
, there is
m_{2}\geq m
and
\gamma>0
such that the angle between
\mathbf{u}_{1}(n)
and the nonpositive vector
\mathbf{u}_{2}(n)
is bounded from below by
\gamma
for
n\geq m_{2}
.
From now, we assume that
n\geq m_{2}
.
Because
\mathbf{u}_{1}(n),\mathbf{u}_{2}(n)
, and
\mathbf{w}
are all unit vectors, this implies that
|x|,|y|\leq\frac{1}{\sin\gamma}
. Multiplying (
7.27
) by
M_{[0,n)}
gives

\delta_{1}(n)\mathbf{w}_{1}(n)=M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{w}=x\mbox{l}{}_{1}(n)\mathbf{u}_{1}(n)+yM_{[0,n)}\mathbf{u}_{2}(n).

Observing that
\lVert M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{u}_{2}(n)\rVert_{2}\leq\delta_{2}(n)
, taking norms we obtain

(7.28)

\delta_{1}(n)=\lVert M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{w}\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{\mbox{l}{}_{1}(n)+\delta_{2}(n)}{\sin\gamma}

for
n\geq m_{2}
.
By the local Pisot condition, we have
\delta_{2}(n)<e^{-\alpha n}
and thus, by unimodularity of
M_{[0,n)}
,
\delta_{1}(n)>e^{\alpha n}
for some
\alpha>0
and large enough
n
.
This implies that
\mbox{l}{}_{1}(n)>(\sin\gamma)e^{\alpha n}-e^{-\alpha n}
, i.e., (
7.25
) holds for some
C>0
.

We now turn to the proof of (
7.26
).
Let
n\geq m
. Suppose that
\mbox{l}{}(n)
is real, and let
\mathbf{u}(n)
be a unit eigenvector associated to the eigenvalue
\mbox{l}{}(n)
. Since
\wedge^{2}M_{[0,n)}\,(\mathbf{u}_{1}(n){\wedge}\mathbf{u}(n))=\mbox{l}{}_{1}(n)\mbox{l}{}(n)\,(\mathbf{u}_{1}(n){\wedge}\mathbf{u}(n))
, one has

(7.29)

\lVert\wedge^{2}M_{[0,n)}\rVert_{2}\geq\mbox{l}{}_{1}(n)|\mbox{l}{}(n)|.

If
\mbox{l}{}(n)
is not real, then there is a conjugate eigenvalue with a conjugate eigenvector; take the intersection of the 2-dimensional complex space generated by these eigenvectors with the real space: this space is invariant under
M_{[0,n)}
, and the restriction of
M_{[0,n)}
to it is a conformal map which changes norm by
|\mbox{l}{}(n)|
which shows that (
7.29
) also holds in this case.

Combining (
7.29
) with (
7.28
) we have

(7.30)

\mbox{l}{}_{1}(n)|\mbox{l}{}(n)|\leq\lVert\wedge^{2}M_{[0,n)}\rVert_{2}=\delta_{1}(n)\delta_{2}(n)\leq\frac{\mbox{l}{}_{1}(n)+\delta_{2}(n)}{\sin\gamma}\delta_{2}(n).

Thus, since
\mbox{l}{}_{1}(n)\geq Ce^{\alpha n}
by (
7.25
) and
\delta_{2}(n)<e^{-\alpha n}
, for
n
large enough, we have
\mbox{l}{}_{1}(n)\geq\delta_{2}(n)
. Therefore, dividing (
7.30
) by
\mbox{l}{}_{1}(n)
we gain

|\mbox{l}{}(n)|<\frac{2}{\sin\gamma}e^{-\alpha n},

which implies that (
7.26
) holds for some constant
C
.
∎

It follows from the previous result that if
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})
satisfies the Pisot condition, all matrices
M_{[0,n)}
are Pisot matrices for
n
large enough (recall that a Pisot matrix is characterized by the behavior of its eigenvalues, namely all its eigenvalues except the dominant eigenvalue are strictly smaller than 1 in modulus). The converse does not hold according to Example
11.11
below.

7.5.
Consequences of the Pisot condition

We now state further consequences of the local Pisot condition, with the following important irreducibility property, derived from Proposition
7.17
. This irreducibility property will be crucial in proving a criterion for rational independence of the entries of the generalized right eigenvector of
\mathbf{M}
; see Corollary
7.20
, and also Theorem
11.8
in the metric framework.
The following definition goes back to
[
BST19
, Section 2.2]
for one-sided sequences.

Definition 7.18

(Algebraic irreducibility)
.

A sequence
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
is called
algebraically irreducible (in the future)
if for each
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that the characteristic polynomial of
M_{[m,n)}
is irreducible for each
n\geq n_{0}
.

We now relate the Pisot condition to algebraic irreducibility.

Corollary 7.19
.

If a primitive sequence
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies the local Pisot condition, then
\mathbf{M}
is algebraically irreducible.

Proof.

By the shift invariance of the Pisot condition, it suffices to show that for all large enough
n
, the characteristic polynomial of
M_{[0,n)}
is irreducible. By Proposition
7.17
, there is
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
so that each eigenvalue of
M_{[0,n)}
which is not dominant is strictly smaller than
1
for each
n\geq n_{0}
. If the characteristic polynomial of
M_{[0,n)}
were reducible, then the factor which does not have the dominant eigenvalue as a root would be an integral polynomial whose roots all have modulus strictly less than
1
, which is impossible. (Note that it is important here that the matrices are invertible, so that
0
cannot occur as an eigenvalue.)
∎

The following result is a variant of
[
BST19
, Lemma 4.2]
.

Corollary 7.20
.

If a primitive sequence
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies the local Pisot condition and the growth condition
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert M_{n}\rVert=0
, then the coordinates of any generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{u}
are rationally independent.

Proof.

Proposition
7.15
implies that
\mathbf{M}
converges strongly to
\mathbf{u}
.
Suppose that
\mathbf{u}
has rationally dependent coordinates. Then
\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{u}\rangle=0
for some fixed
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
. Thus, by strong convergence to
\mathbf{u}
, (
7.6
) implies that there is a constant
C>0
such that

9
9

9

Here we use the same property of the scalar product as in the proof of Proposition
7.15
.

\displaystyle\langle\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}\,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{e}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{x},M_{[0,n)}\,\mathbf{e}_{i}\rangle=\langle\pi_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}}\mathbf{x},M_{[0,n)}\,\mathbf{e}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\pi_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2}\cdot\lVert\pi_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}}M_{[0,n)}\,\mathbf{e}_{i}\rVert_{2}=\lVert\pi_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2}\cdot\mathrm{d}(M_{[0,n)}\,\mathbf{e}_{i},\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u})<C

for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
and all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Therefore
\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}\,\mathbf{x}\rVert_{\infty}<C
, and because
\vphantom{\!M}{}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}\,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, there is
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\vphantom{\!M}{}^{t}\!M_{[0,\ell)}\,\mathbf{x}=\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,k)}\,\mathbf{x}
for infinitely many
\ell>k
. Being a product of regular matrices, the matrix
M_{[0,k)}
is regular.
Thus
\vphantom{\!M}{}^{t}\!M_{[0,k)}\,\mathbf{x}\neq\mathbf{0}
is an eigenvector of
\vphantom{\!M}{}^{t}\!M_{[k,\ell)}
to the eigenvalue
1
, and, hence,
M_{[k,\ell)}
has reducible characteristic polynomial for large
\ell
. Because this contradicts Corollary
7.19
, the result follows.
∎

Versions for the past can be formulated for Proposition
7.17
and for Corollaries
7.19
and
7.20
, with analogous proofs.

We sum up the most important results of the present section and of Section
7.4
in the following theorem, which emphasizes the strength of the Pisot condition; it is formulated as Theorem
Theorem A
.
in the introduction.

Theorem 7.21
.

For
d\geq 2
, let
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a primitive sequence of nonnegative unimodular integer matrices satisfying the local Pisot condition and the growth condition
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert M_{n}\rVert=0
. Then
\mathbf{M}
is algebraically irreducible and admits a generalized right eigenvector with rationally independent coordinates to which it converges exponentially.

An analogous result holds for the past.

We illustrate our theory by an example that comes from the theory of multidimensional continued fraction algorithms.

Example 7.22
.

We consider the set
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{AR}}=\{M_{\mathrm{AR},1},M_{\mathrm{AR},2},M_{\mathrm{AR},3}\}
of
Arnoux–Rauzy matrices
(see
[
AR91
,
AD19
]
), that generalize the case of Sturmian matrices from (
2.3
) (see also Example
7.6
), defined as

M_{\mathrm{AR},1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&1&1\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad M_{\mathrm{AR},2}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
1&1&1\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad M_{\mathrm{AR},3}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
1&1&1\end{pmatrix}.

Choose a sequence
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{AR}}^{\mathbb{Z}}
for which there exists a constant
h>0
such that
\{M_{n},\ldots,M_{n+h-1}\}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{AR}}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Such a sequence is said to have
strong partial quotients
bounded by
h
.
The sequence
\mathbf{M}
is primitive because each block of length at least
h
is positive. Because
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{AR}}
contains only finitely many matrices, the growth condition holds for
\mathbf{M}
.
By the proof of
[
DHS13
, Theorem 1]
, there exists a constant
C>0
, such that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a hyperplane
\mathbf{w}(n)^{\perp}
such that

\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{[0,n)}|_{\mathbf{w}(n)^{\perp}}\rVert\leq C\,\Big(\frac{2^{h}-3}{2^{h}-1}\Big)^{n/h}.

Hence, the hyperplane
\mathbf{w}(n)^{\perp}
is exponentially contracted by
M_{[0,n)}
, i.e.,

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}|_{\mathbf{w}(n)^{\perp}}\rVert<0.

By the definition of singular values this implies that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\delta_{2}(M_{[0,n)})\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}|_{\mathbf{w}(n)^{\perp}}\rVert<0.

Thus the Pisot condition holds for
\mathbf{M}
and we may apply Theorem
7.21
. This theorem shows that the sequence
\mathbf{M}
is algebraically irreducible and admits a generalized right eigenvector with rationally independent coordinates to which it converges exponentially.

Further examples involving Arnoux–Rauzy matrices are given in
[
BCS13
]
. In particular, in this paper generalizations of Arnoux–Rauzy matrices in
\mathcal{M}_{d}
are defined for arbitrary
d\geq 3
. Our theory can be applied to this more general situation as well.

We provide further examples for sequences that satisfy the conditions of Theorem
7.21
in Propositions
10.4
and
10.8
.

7.6.
Eventually Anosov
\mathcal{M}
-adic mapping families

We now relate a linear mapping family to a sequence of matrices; see Section
6.1
for the general notion of a mapping family. Linear mapping families are reminiscent of multidimensional continued fraction algorithms and will be related to these algorithms in Section
9.4
.

Definition 7.23

(
\mathcal{M}
-adic mapping family)
.

Fix
d\geq 2
and
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathcal{M}_{d}
, and consider a sequence
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\mathbb{T}_{n}
be a copy of the
d
-dimensional torus
\mathbb{R}^{d}/\mathbb{Z}^{d}
equipped with the Euclidean metric inherited from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Moreover, regard the inverse of the matrix
M_{n}
as the automorphism
10
10

10

This is possible since
M_{n}
is a unimodular integer matrix.

M_{n}^{-1}:\mathbb{T}_{n}\to\mathbb{T}_{n+1}
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}/\mathbb{Z}^{d}
given by left multiplication by
M_{n}^{-1}
.
Then we set
\mathbb{T}=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{T}_{n}
and define
f_{\mathbf{M}}:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{T}
by
f_{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{x})=M_{n}^{-1}\mathbf{x}
for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
.
We call
(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}})
the
\mathcal{M}
-adic mapping family
associated to
\mathbf{M}
.
It can be written out as

\cdots\xrightarrow{M_{-2}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}_{-1}\xrightarrow{M_{-1}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}_{0}\xrightarrow{M_{0}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}_{1}\xrightarrow{M_{1}^{-1}}\cdots.

Let
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathcal{M}_{d}
and
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be given. Since the mapping
M_{n}^{-1}:\mathbb{T}_{n}\to\mathbb{T}_{n+1}
of the associated
\mathcal{M}
-adic mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}})
is linear for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the derivative of this mapping
M_{n}^{-1}
at a point
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
is the linear mapping
M_{n}^{-1}:\,\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
(note that
T_{\mathbf{p}}\mathbb{T}_{n}\cong\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
T_{f_{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{p})}\mathbb{T}_{n+1}\cong\mathbb{R}^{d}
).

As in Section
6.1
, assume that there exists an
f_{\mathbf{M}}
-invariant splitting
G^{s}\oplus G^{u}
of the tangent space
T\mathbb{T}
of
\mathbb{T}
, and set
G^{s}_{\mathbf{p}}=G^{s}\cap T_{\mathbf{p}}\mathbb{T}
,
G^{u}_{\mathbf{p}}=G^{u}\cap T_{\mathbf{p}}\mathbb{T}
for
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{T}
. Thus the derivative
D(f_{\mathbf{M}})
and, hence, also the spaces
G_{\mathbf{p}}^{s}
and
G_{\mathbf{p}}^{u}
depend only on the integer
n
satisfying
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
, and we may define (by some abuse of notation)
G_{n}^{s}=G_{\mathbf{p}}^{s}
and
G_{n}^{u}=G_{\mathbf{p}}^{u}
for
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. By
f_{\mathbf{M}}
-invariance of the splitting, one has
M_{n}^{-1}G_{n}^{s}=G_{n+1}^{s}
and
M_{n}^{-1}G_{n}^{u}=G_{n+1}^{u}
for all
n
.

Summing up, in this case, the conditions required for
(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}})
to be eventually Anosov can be written as follows.
For some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
(and, hence, for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
), we have

(i)

\lim\limits_{k\to+\infty}\sup\{\lVert M_{[n,k)}^{-1}\,\mathbf{x}\rVert/\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert\,:\,\mathbf{x}\in G_{n}^{s}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\}=0
,

(ii)

\lim\limits_{k\to+\infty}\inf\{\lVert M_{[n,k)}^{-1}\,\mathbf{x}\rVert/\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert\,:\,\mathbf{x}\in G_{n}^{u}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\}=+\infty
,

(iii)

\lim\limits_{k\to-\infty}\inf\{\lVert M_{[k,n)}\,\mathbf{x}\rVert/\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert\,:\,\mathbf{x}\in G_{n}^{s}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\}=+\infty
,

(iv)

\lim\limits_{k\to-\infty}\sup\{\lVert M_{[k,n)}\,\mathbf{x}\rVert/\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert\,:\,\mathbf{x}\in G_{n}^{u}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\}=0
.

We shall now prove that an
\mathcal{M}
-adic mapping familiy is eventually Anosov if the corresponding sequence of matrices
\mathbf{M}
admits strong convergence in the future and in the past (see Definition
7.7
for the definition of strong convergence).
In this case, the families of generalized right and left eigenvectors
\mathbf{u}_{n}
and
\mathbf{v}_{n}
from (
7.3
) are used to construct the splitting of the tangent space for which the
\mathcal{M}
-adic mapping family is eventually Anosov.

Proposition 7.24
.

For
d\geq 2
and
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathcal{M}_{d}
, let
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a bi-infinite sequence of
d{\times}d
unimodular nonnegative integer matrices.
Assume that
\mathbf{M}
is two-sided primitive and admits strong convergence to the generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{u}
in the future and to the generalized left eigenvector
\mathbf{v}
in the past. Then the
\mathcal{M}
-adic mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}})
associated to
\mathbf{M}
is eventually Anosov for the splitting

(7.31)

G^{s}\oplus G^{u}=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}G_{n}^{s}\oplus G_{n}^{u}=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}_{n}\oplus\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp},

where
\mathbf{u}_{n}
and
\mathbf{v}_{n}
are as in (
7.3
) for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the vectors
\mathbf{u}_{n}
and
\mathbf{v}_{n}
are a right and left eigenvector of the two-sided primitive sequence
\Sigma^{n}\mathbf{M}=(M_{k+n})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
, respectively. Therefore, these vectors have only positive entries.
We first prove that

(7.32)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to+\infty}M_{[n,k)}^{-1}\,\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{0}\qquad\mbox{for all}\ \mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}_{n},

(7.33)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to+\infty}\lVert M_{[n,k)}^{-1}\,\mathbf{x}\rVert

\displaystyle=+\infty\quad\mbox{for all}\ \mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}.

These equations imply that
(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}})
is eventually Anosov in the future with the splitting (
7.31
). By (
7.3
) it suffices to prove (
7.32
) and (
7.33
) for
n=0
.

To prove (
7.32
) for
n=0
, assume that there exists
C>0
such that
\lVert\mathbf{u}_{k}\rVert_{\infty}
is larger than
C
for infinitely many positive integers
k
. Primitivity and positivity of the coordinates of
\mathbf{u}_{k}
implies that the coordinates of
M_{[0,k)}\mathbf{u}_{k}
are unbounded in
k
, which cannot be true because
\mathbf{u}=M_{[0,k)}\mathbf{u}_{k}
holds by (
7.3
). Thus
\lim_{k\to+\infty}\lVert M_{[0,k)}^{-1}\mathbf{u}\rVert_{\infty}=\lim_{k\to+\infty}\lVert\mathbf{u}_{k}\rVert_{\infty}=0
, which yields (
7.32
) for
n=0
.

To prove (
7.33
) for
n=0
, let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{v}^{\perp}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
. Assume that there exists
C>0
such that
\lVert M_{[0,k)}^{-1}\mathbf{x}\lVert\leq C
for infinitely many
k\geq 0
.
Because
\mathbf{u}\not\in\mathbf{v}^{\perp}
, the strong convergence of
\mathbf{M}
to
\mathbf{u}
yields
\lim_{k\to\infty}\pi_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}M_{[0,k)}\mathbf{e}_{i}=\mathbf{0}
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
, thus

\lim_{k\to\infty}\max_{\lVert\mathbf{y}\rVert\leq C}\lVert\pi_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}M_{[0,k)}\mathbf{y}\rVert=0.

Since
\lVert M_{[0,k)}^{-1}\mathbf{x}\rVert\leq C
for infinitely many
k
, this implies that

\lVert\pi_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}\mathbf{x}\rVert=\liminf_{k\to\infty}\lVert\pi_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}M_{[0,k)}M_{[0,k)}^{-1}\mathbf{x}\rVert\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\max_{\lVert\mathbf{y}\rVert\leq C}\lVert\pi_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}M_{[0,k)}\mathbf{y}\rVert=0

and, hence,
\pi_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0}
.
This contradicts the fact that
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{v}^{\perp}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
. This contradiction proves (
7.33
) for
n=0
.

Next, we prove that the mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}})
is eventually Anosov in the past with the splitting (
7.31
).
This amounts to showing that

(7.34)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to+\infty}\lVert M_{[-k,n)}\mathbf{x}\rVert

\displaystyle=+\infty\quad\mbox{for all}\ \mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}_{n}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\},

(7.35)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to+\infty}M_{[-k,n)}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{0}\qquad\mbox{for all}\ \mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}.

To prove these equations we may again assume w.l.o.g. that
n=0
. Equation (
7.34
) follows immediately from primitivity in the past and by the fact that
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u}_{0}
has positive coordinates.

To prove (
7.35
), we use that
\mathbf{M}
converges strongly to
\mathbf{v}
in the past, which implies that

(7.36)

\lim_{k\to+\infty}\mathrm{d}(\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[-k,0)}\,\mathbf{e}_{i},\mathbb{R}\mathbf{v})=0\quad\hbox{for all}\ i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}.

Let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{v}^{\perp}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
(the case
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0}
is trivial).
Suppose that
M_{[-k,0)}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\perp}
does not converge to
\mathbf{0}
for
k\to\infty
.
Then there exists
i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
such that the
i
-th coefficient of
M_{[-k,0)}\mathbf{x}
is greater than some constant
C>0
in modulus, for infinitely many
k
.
Combining this with (
7.36
), we may choose
k\in\mathbb{N}
in a way that there is
\gamma_{k}>0
with
\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[-k,0)}\,\mathbf{e}_{i}-\gamma_{k}\mathbf{v}\rVert_{2}<\frac{C}{\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2}}
and
|\langle\mathbf{x},\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[-k,0)}\,\mathbf{e}_{i}\rangle|>C
.
But then, because
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{v}^{\perp}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
, we have

\displaystyle|\langle\mathbf{x},\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[-k,0)}\,\mathbf{e}_{i}\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\langle\mathbf{x},\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[-k,0)}\,\mathbf{e}_{i}-\gamma_{k}\mathbf{v}\rangle+\langle\mathbf{x},\gamma_{k}\mathbf{v}\rangle|=|\langle\mathbf{x},\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[-k,0)}\,\mathbf{e}_{i}-\gamma_{k}\mathbf{v}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2}\cdot\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[-k,0)}\,\mathbf{e}_{i}-\gamma_{k}\mathbf{v}\rVert_{2}<C,

a contradiction. This proves (
7.35
) for
n=0
.
∎

Remark 7.25
.

If
\mathbf{M}
converges strongly to
\mathbf{u}
in the future and only weakly to
\mathbf{v}
in the past, then we can only prove that
(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}})
is eventually Anosov in the future.
The existence of generalized right and left eigenvectors is needed to define the splitting (directly for the generalized right eigenvector, and by duality for the generalized left eigenvector); weak convergence is enough to prove the desired limits
(
7.32
) and (
7.34
) on the lines
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}_{n}
, but strong convergence is needed to obtain the (
7.33
) and (
7.35
)
limits on the complementary hyperplanes
\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}
.

The following theorem, which is stated as Theorem
Theorem B
.
in the introduction, is an immediate consequence of Proposition
7.24
and Theorem
7.21
; see also Proposition
7.15
.

Theorem 7.26
.

Assume that
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
is two-sided primitive and satisfies the two-sided local Pisot condition and the two-sided growth condition
\lim_{n\to\pm\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\rVert M_{n}\lVert=0
.
Then
\mathbf{M}
admits strong convergence to a generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{u}
in the future and to a generalized left eigenvector
\mathbf{v}
in the past. The mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}})
associated to
\mathbf{M}
is eventually Anosov for the splitting

G^{s}\oplus G^{u}=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}G_{n}^{s}\oplus G_{n}^{u}=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}_{n}\oplus\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp},

where
\mathbf{u}_{n}
and
\mathbf{v}_{n}
are as in (
7.3
) for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

We refer to Example
7.22
and to Propositions
10.4
and
10.8
for examples of sequences
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
that satisfy the conditions of this corollary.

8.
Metric theory for bi-infinite sequences of matrices

In Section
7
, we have set up a theory for a single sequence of matrices
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
d\geq 2
. Our aim in this section is to establish a metric theory that is valid for almost all sequences of matrices in a subset
D
of
\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. In Section
8.1
we define Lyapunov exponents for an invertible dynamical system, state the Oseledets Multiplicative Ergodic Theorem, and provide a metric version of the Pisot condition. Section
8.2
states the definition of an induced dynamical system and shows the effect of inducing on Lyapunov exponents. This will be needed later in the context of multidimensional continued fraction algorithms. In Section
8.3
we define
\mathcal{M}
-adic cocycles for shifts of sequences of matrices. We also clarify how the generic Pisot condition on a shift of sequences of matrices is related to the local Pisot condition on each single sequence of matrices contained in this shift. Finally, in Section
8.4
we provide a metric version of the main results of Section
7
for shifts of sequences of matrices. In particular, we show the effect of the Pisot condition on the Oseledets splitting of such a shift.

8.1.
Oseledets splitting and the Pisot condition

In the present section, we study linear cocycles on ergodic dynamical systems. For convenience, we recall the definition of these objects.
First, a dynamical system
(X,F,\nu)
with invariant measure
\nu
is
ergodic
if
F^{-1}(B)=B
implies that
\nu(B)=0
or
\nu(X\setminus B)=0
.
Two dynamical systems
(X,F,\nu)
and
(X^{\prime},F^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
are
measurably conjugate
if there are measurable subsets
X_{1}\subset X
,
X^{\prime}_{1}\subset X^{\prime}
of measure
1
and a bijection
g:X_{1}\to X^{\prime}_{1}
with
g
and
g^{-1}
being measurable, such that
g\circ F(x)=F^{\prime}\circ g(x)
for every
x\in X_{1}
, and
\nu^{\prime}\circ g(U)=\nu(U)
for every measurable set
U\subset X_{1}
.
The conjugacy is said
topological
if
X_{1}=X
,
X^{\prime}_{1}=X^{\prime}
and
g
is a homeomorphism.

Definition 8.1

(Linear cocycle; cf. e.g.
[
Arn98
, Definition 1.1.1 and Section 2.1.2]
)
.

Let
(X,F,\nu)
be an invertible ergodic dynamical system.
Define a mapping
A:X\to\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})
. Then

(8.1)

A^{(n)}(x)=\begin{cases}A(F^{n-1}(x))\cdots A(F(x))A(x)&\text{if}\ n>0,\\
\mathrm{Id}&\text{if}\ n=0,\\
A(F^{n}(x))^{-1}\cdots A(F^{-1}(x))^{-1}&\text{if}\ n<0,\end{cases}

which is a function from
\mathbb{Z}\times X
to
\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})
,
is called a
linear cocycle on
(X,F,\nu)
. To keep things simple, we call
A
a linear cocycle on
(X,F,\nu)
.

A linear cocycle
A
is defined analogously for a noninvertible ergodic dynamical system
(X,F,\nu)
. In this case,
A^{(n)}
makes sense only for
n\geq 0
.

Oseledets’ Multiplicative Ergodic Theorem is a powerful tool in the study of linear cocycles.
Recommended references to this theorem are for instance
[
Arn98
]
and
[
Via14
]
. In the case of invertible dynamical systems (for instance, a two-sided shift is invertible), Oseledets’ Theorem yields a splitting of the space (see
[
Arn98
, Theorem 3.4.11]
) reflecting the growth behavior of the cocycle. For noninvertible dynamical systems like for instance, one-sided shifts, it only provides a filtration; see
[
Arn98
, Theorem 3.4.1]
. In this section, we state results around Oseledets’ Theorem for general invertible ergodic dynamical systems in a way that suits our purposes and that will be needed later in various special cases. The Furstenberg Kesten theorem is stated in the following proposition.

Proposition 8.2

(cf.
[
Arn98
, Theorem 3.3.3 and 3.3.10]
)
.

Let
(X,F,\nu)
be an invertible ergodic dynamical system with linear cocycle
A:X\to\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})
. Assume that
A
is log-integrable, in the sense that
11
11

11

Note that
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty}
is a matrix norm here. Because
A
has determinant
\pm 1
, by
[
BL85
, Remark 3.5]
we do not need to consider
\int_{X}\log\max\{\lVert A(x)\rVert_{\infty},\lVert A(x)^{-1}\rVert_{\infty}\}d\nu(x)<\infty
here.

(8.2)

\int_{X}\log\lVert A(x)\rVert_{\infty}d\nu(x)<\infty.

Then the quantities
\vartheta_{1},\ldots,\vartheta_{d}
, which are recursively defined by

(8.3)

\vartheta_{1}+\cdots+\vartheta_{k}={\lim_{n\to\infty}}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert\wedge^{k}A^{(n)}(x)\rVert\qquad(1\leq k\leq d)

exist and do not depend on
x
for almost all
x\in X
.

Definition 8.3

(Lyapunov exponents and Lyapunov spectrum for generic sequences of matrices)
.

Let
(X,F,\nu)
be an invertible ergodic dynamical system with log-integrable linear cocycle
A:X\to\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})
. The numbers
\vartheta_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\vartheta_{d}
in (
8.3
) are called the
(generic) Lyapunov exponents
of the system
(X,F,\nu)
for the linear cocycle
A
.

Let
\Theta_{1}>\Theta_{2}>\cdots>\Theta_{p}
be the different elements in the sequence
(\vartheta_{i})_{1\leq i\leq d}
of Lyapunov exponents ordered according to
\vartheta_{1}\geq\vartheta_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\vartheta_{d}
and denote by
d_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq p
) the number of occurences of
\Theta_{i}
in the sequence
(\vartheta_{i})_{1\leq i\leq d}
. Then
\{(\Theta_{i},d_{i}):1\leq i\leq p\}
is called the
(generic) Lyapunov spectrum
of
(X,F,\nu)
.

Remark 8.4
.

Note that only positive integers
n
enter the definition of
\vartheta_{1},\ldots,\vartheta_{d}
in (
8.3
). Nevertheless, the Lyapunov exponents
\vartheta_{1},\ldots,\vartheta_{d}
of Definition
8.3
are “two-sided” Lyapunov exponents and
\{(\Theta_{i},d_{i}):1\leq i\leq p\}
is the “two-sided” Lyapunov spectrum because the matrices on the negative side generically have the same behaviour as the ones on the positive side by
F
-invariance of
\nu
. In particular, we see from
[
Arn98
, Theorem 3.3.3 and 3.3.10]
that for almost all
x\in X
we have

\vartheta_{k}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\delta_{k}(A^{(n)}(x))\qquad(1\leq k\leq d),

and if we go to the negative direction we gain

(8.4)

\vartheta_{d+1-k}=\lim_{n\to-\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\delta_{k}(A^{(n)}(x))\qquad(1\leq k\leq d)

almost all
x\in X
.

The following proposition contains a version of Oseledets’ Multiplicative Ergodic Theorem.

Proposition 8.5

(cf.
[
Arn98
, Theorem 3.4.11]
)
.

Let
(X,F,\nu)
be an invertible ergodic dynamical system with Lyapunov spectrum
\{(\Theta_{i},d_{i}):1\leq i\leq p\}
and log-integrable linear cocycle
A:X\to\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})
in the sense of (
8.2
). Then, for
\nu
-a.e.
x\in X
, there is an
Oseledets splitting

(8.5)

\mathbb{R}^{d}=G^{1}(x)\oplus\cdots\oplus G^{p}(x)

that is dynamically characterized by

(8.6)

{\lim_{n\to\pm\infty}}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert A^{(n)}(x)\mathbf{z}\rVert=\Theta_{i}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \mathbf{z}\in G^{i}(x)\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\qquad(1\leq i\leq p).

The Oseledets splitting in (
8.5
) satisfies
\dim(G^{i}(x))=d_{i}
and the invariance property
A^{(n)}(x)G^{i}(x)=G^{i}(F^{n}x)
, for all
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

We need the following property of the Lyapunov exponents of a dynamical system. Note that this property concerns invertible dynamical systems and, hence, is two-sided; cf. Remark
8.4
.

Definition 8.6

(Generic Pisot condition for a cocycle)
.

Let
(X,F,\nu)
be an invertible ergodic dynamical system with log-integrable linear cocycle
A:X\to\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})
. We say that
(X,F,\nu)
satisfies the
(generic) Pisot condition
for
A
if its generic Lyapunov exponents
\vartheta_{1}\geq\vartheta_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\vartheta_{d}
satisfy
\vartheta_{1}>0>\vartheta_{2}
for
A
.

When we say that
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
satisfies the generic Pisot condition for
A
, then the log-integrability of
A
is implied (because otherwise the Lyapunov exponents are not defined).

8.2.
Induced dynamical systems

We will later consider induced rotations. For this reason we recall the following general definition for induced maps (also called
first return maps
).

Definition 8.7
