(Induced dynamical system and induced map; see for instance
[
Haw21
, Definition 8.11]
)
.

Let a measurable dynamical system
(X,F,\nu)
with
\nu(X)<\infty
and a subset
Y\subset X
be given. For each
x\in Y
define the first return time
12
12

12

The return time exists
\nu
-a.e. by the Poincaré recurrence theorem.

by
n(x)=\min\{m\geq 1\,:\,F^{m}(x)\in Y\}
. The map
G:\,Y\to Y
,
x\mapsto F^{n(x)}(x)
, is called the
induced map
of
F
on
Y
. The resulting dynamical system
(Y,G,\nu|_{Y})
is called the
induced dynamical system
of
(X,F,\nu)
on
Y
.

If
A
is a linear coycle of
(X,F,\nu)
then
B:Y\to\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})
,
y\mapsto A^{(n(y))}(y)
is the linear cocycle of
(Y,G,\nu)
associated to
A
.

For ergodic invertible dynamical systems, the Lyapunov exponents behave quite regularly under induction. In particular, we have the following result.

Lemma 8.8

(see
[
Via14
, Proposition 4.18]
)
.

Let
(X,F,\nu)
with
\nu(X)<\infty
be an invertible ergodic dynamical system with linear cocycle
A

and let
Y\subset X
. Let
(Y,G,\nu)
be the induced dynamical system of
(X,F,\nu)
on
Y
and let
B
be the linear cocycle of
(Y,G,\nu)
associated to
A
.

If
\vartheta_{1}>\ldots>\vartheta_{d}
are the Lyapunov exponents of
A
, then there is a constant
c\geq 1
such that
c\vartheta_{1}>\ldots>c\vartheta_{d}
are the Lyapunov exponents of
B
.

Proof.

This immediately follows from
[
Via14
, Proposition 4.18]
. Just note that the function
c(x)
occurring in this result is a constant in our case, because we assumed ergodicity.
∎

8.3.
Definition of
\mathcal{M}
-adic cocycles

Recall that
\mathcal{M}_{d}
,
d\geq 2
, is the set of unimodular nonnegative integer
d{\times}d
matrices. Equip the set
\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
of two-sided infinite sequences of matrices in
\mathcal{M}_{d}
with the product topology of the discrete topology on
\mathcal{M}_{d}
, and let
D\subset\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a shift invariant set equipped with the subspace topology. Then
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
stands for the measure-theoretical dynamical system which is defined by the shift
\Sigma
on
D
endowed with an ergodic
\Sigma
-invariant Borel measure
\nu
. The set
D
is not assumed to be closed. To the ergodic dynamical system
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
, we relate two linear cocycles.

Definition 8.9

(
\mathcal{M}
-adic cocycles)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathcal{M}_{d}
, and let
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
be an ergodic dynamical system with
D\subset\mathcal{M}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. We define the following cocycles.

The linear cocycle
A_{\mathrm{tr}}:D\to\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})
,
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mapsto\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{0}
is called
(
\mathcal{M}
-adic) transpose cocycle
.

The linear cocycle
A_{\mathrm{inv}}:D\to\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})
,
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mapsto M_{0}^{-1}
is called
(
\mathcal{M}
-adic) inverse cocycle
.

Remark 8.10
.

One can now define the vector bundle morphism as the skew product (
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
)

\Phi_{\mathrm{tr}}:\,D\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to D\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\quad(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{x})\mapsto(\Sigma\mathbf{M},\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{0}\mathbf{x}).

This mapping constitutes a
random dynamical system
in the sense of
[
Arn98
, Definition 1.1.1]
with
base transformation

\Sigma
and
generator

A_{\mathrm{tr}}
. It can be defined for
A_{\mathrm{inv}}
analogously.

For the iterates
A_{\mathrm{tr}}^{(n)}
of
A_{\mathrm{tr}}
, we have, for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, that

(8.7)

A_{\mathrm{tr}}^{(n)}(\mathbf{M})=\begin{cases}A_{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{n-1}\mathbf{M})\cdots A_{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma\mathbf{M})A_{\mathrm{tr}}(\mathbf{M})=\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}&\text{if}\ n>0,\\
\mathrm{Id}&\text{if}\ n=0,\\
A_{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{n}\mathbf{M})^{-1}\cdots A_{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{-1}\mathbf{M})^{-1}=\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[n,0)}^{-1}&\text{if}\ n<0.\end{cases}

Iterates of
A_{\mathrm{inv}}
can be viewed in an analogous way by
A_{\mathrm{inv}}^{(n)}(\mathbf{M})=M_{[0,n)}^{-1}
for
n\geq 0
and
A_{\mathrm{inv}}^{(n)}(\mathbf{M})=M_{[n,0)}
for
n<0
.

Remark 8.11
.

The generic Lyapunov exponents of the system
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
for
A_{\mathrm{tr}}
are
\vartheta_{1},\ldots,\vartheta_{d}
if and only if the generic Lyapunov exponents of the system
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
for
A_{\mathrm{inv}}
are
-\vartheta_{d},\ldots,-\vartheta_{1}
. This follows immediately from the definition of these two cocycles.

Definition 8.12

(Generic Pisot condition for sequences of matrices)
.

The ergodic dynamical system
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
satisfies the
(generic) Pisot condition
(without qualifying the cocycle) if it satisfies the generic Pisot condition for the linear cocycle
A_{\mathrm{tr}}
.

The lemma below relates the generic Pisot condition and the local Pisot conditions; see Definitions
7.9
and
7.11
.

Lemma 8.13
.

For
d\geq 2
, let
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
, with
D\subset\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, be an ergodic dynamical system.

(i)

If
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
satisfies the log-integrability condition (
8.2
), then for
\nu
-almost all
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in D
the growth condition
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert M_{n}\rVert=0
holds.

(ii)

If
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
satisfies the generic Pisot condition, i.e.,
\vartheta_{1}>0>\vartheta_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\vartheta_{d}
for the transpose cocycle
A_{\mathrm{tr}}
, then, for
\nu
-almost all
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in D
, the growth condition, the two-sided strong local Pisot condition, and the two-sided local Pisot condition hold.

Proof.

To prove (
i
), we define a
suspension flow

T_{t}
over the shift
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
. To this end, let

\tau:D\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0};\quad(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mapsto\log\lVert M_{0}\rVert=\log\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{0}\rVert

be the
roof function
and

D_{\tau}=\{(\mathbf{M},x)\in D\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\,:\,0\leq x<\tau(\mathbf{M})\}.

Now we define the suspension flow by

T_{t}:D_{\tau}\to D_{\tau};\quad(\mathbf{M},x)\mapsto(\mathbf{M},x+t),

where
(\mathbf{M},x+\tau(\mathbf{M}))
is identified with
(\Sigma\mathbf{M},x)
.
Let
\lambda
be the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Since
\tau\in L^{1}(\nu)
holds by log-integrability, the measure

\bar{\nu}=\frac{(\nu\times\lambda)|_{D_{\tau}}}{\int\tau d\nu}

is well defined, and the flow
(D_{\tau},T_{t},\bar{\nu})
is ergodic; see
[
EW11
, Lemma 9.24 (2)]
.

We now follow
[
DHL14
, Section 5.2]
. For each
\delta>0
small enough, we have
U=D\times[0,\delta]\subset D_{\tau}
, and by Birkhoff’s ergodic theorem we gain

(8.8)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\lambda(\{s\in[0,t]:T_{s}(\mathbf{M},x)\in U\})}{t}=\bar{\nu}(U)

for a.e.
(\mathbf{M},x)\in D_{\tau}
.
Denote the
n
-th return time of
T_{t}
to
D{\times}\{0\}
by
t_{n}=t_{n}(\mathbf{M},x)
. Then, (
8.8
) implies that for a.e.
(\mathbf{M},x)\in D_{\tau}
we have
\lim_{n\to\infty}{t_{n}}/{n}<\infty

and, hence, for each
\varepsilon>0
, there is
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
t_{n+1}-t_{n}<\varepsilon n
for each
n\geq N
. Thus, for a.e.
(\mathbf{M},x)\in D_{\tau}
, we have
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert M_{n}\rVert=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{t_{n+1}-t_{n}}{n}=0
and, hence, the growth condition holds for a.e.
\mathbf{M}\in D
; a different proof of this fact, which is based on the Borel–Cantelli Lemma, is contained in
[
Arn98
, Lemma 3.4.3 (i)]
.

It remains to prove (
ii
). Because the generic Pisot condition contains the log-integrability condition, the growth condition holds for a.e.
\mathbf{M}\in D
by (i). Thus, in view of Lemma
7.12
, we have to show that a.e.
\mathbf{M}\in D
satisfies the two-sided strong local Pisot condition.

As for the future, because
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
satisfies the Pisot condition for the cocycle
A_{\mathrm{tr}}
, by (
8.3
) we have for a.e.
\mathbf{M}\in D
that

\displaystyle\vartheta_{1}+\cdots+\vartheta_{k}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert\wedge^{k}A^{(n)}(\mathbf{M})\rVert=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert\wedge^{k}\,\vphantom{\!M}{}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}\rVert

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert\wedge^{k}M_{[0,n)}\rVert

holds for all
k\in\{1,\dots,d\}
, with
\vartheta_{1}>0>\vartheta_{2}
. Thus a.e.
\mathbf{M}\in D
satisfies the strong local Pisot condition in the future.

For the past, we argue as follows. By Proposition
8.5
, the Pisot condition of
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
for the cocycle
A_{\mathrm{tr}}
implies that for a.e.
\mathbf{M}\in D
there is a hyperplane
H
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\lim_{n\to-\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[n,0)}^{-1}\mathbf{x}\rVert\leq\vartheta_{2}<0

holds for each
\mathbf{x}\in H\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
. Because
n
tends to
-\infty
, this implies that there exists
N\in\mathbb{Z}
such that for each
\mathbf{x}\in H
with
\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert=1
and each
n\leq N
we have
\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[n,0)}^{-1}\mathbf{x}\rVert\geq e^{\vartheta_{2}n/2}
. As each
\mathbf{x}\in H
with
\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert=1
is a linear combination of the standard basis vectors with coefficients bounded by
1
in modulus,
N
is uniform for all
\mathbf{x}\in H
with
\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert=1
. Thus, by the definition of the
(d{-}1)
-st singular value,
\delta_{d-1}(\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[n,0)}^{-1})\geq e^{\vartheta_{2}n/2}
holds for
n\leq N
, and we get

(8.9)

\displaystyle 0>\frac{\vartheta_{2}}{2}

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{n\to-\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\delta_{d-1}(\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[n,0)}^{-1})

\displaystyle=-\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\delta_{d-1}(\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[-n,0)}^{-1})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\delta_{2}(\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[-n,0)});

note that the a.e. existence of the limit in the first line of (
8.9
) follows from (
8.3
).
Thus a.e.
\mathbf{M}\in D
satisfies the strong local Pisot condition in the past as well.
∎

Remark 8.14
.

For the local Pisot condition in Definition
7.9
, we worked with a limit superior instead of a limit. For the generic Pisot condition in Definition
8.12
we use limits and, hence, Lyapunov exponents, because the limits in (
8.3
) exist a.e. in view of Proposition
8.2
(indeed, these limits even exist in the past). This means that the generic Pisot condition of an invertible dynamical system, although defined only in the future, is always two-sided. So, generically, the Pisot condition in the future coincides with the two-sided one.
Thus Definition
8.12
is the appropriate generic version of the two-sided Pisot condition in Definition
7.9
.

Moreover, we did not include the growth condition
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert M_{n}\rVert=0
in the definition of the local Pisot condition because the local Pisot condition can hold even if the growth condition fails. On the contrary, the log-integrability, which is the generic counterpart of the growth condition, is needed for stating the generic Pisot condition.

8.4.
Hyperbolic splitting for sequences of matrices

By Remark
8.11
, the Pisot condition for
A_{\mathrm{tr}}
guarantees the existence of an invariant Oseledets splitting into a stable space of dimension
1
and an unstable space of codimension
1
for the cocycle
A_{\mathrm{inv}}
. This is one of the assertions of the following theorem which summarizes important consequences of the generic Pisot condition. It forms a metric version of Theorem
7.21
and Corollary
7.26
. In its statement, for a dynamical system
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
, with
D\subset\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, we call a set of the form

[Z_{0},\ldots,Z_{\ell-1}]=\{(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in D\,:\,M_{0}=Z_{0},\ldots,M_{\ell}=Z_{3}\}

a
cylinder set
. Here,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
Z_{0},\ldots,Z_{\ell-1}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}
.

Theorem 8.15
.

For
d\geq 2
, let
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
with
D\subset\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be an ergodic dynamical system which contains a cylinder set
[Z_{0},\ldots,Z_{\ell-1}]
of positive measure corresponding to a positive matrix
Z_{[0,\ell)}
.
If
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
satisfies the Pisot condition for the Lyapunov exponents of the cocycle
A_{\mathrm{tr}}
, then, for
\nu
-almost all
\mathbf{M}\in D
, the following assertions hold.

(i)

The sequence
\mathbf{M}
is two-sided primitive and algebraically irreducible (in the future).

(ii)

The sequence
\mathbf{M}
converges exponentially to a generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{u}
in the future and to the generalized left eigenvector
\mathbf{v}
in the past.

(iii)

The mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}})
associated to
\mathbf{M}
is eventually Anosov for the hyperbolic splitting

(8.10)

G^{s}(\mathbf{M})\oplus G^{u}(\mathbf{M})=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}G_{n}^{s}(\mathbf{M})\oplus G_{n}^{u}(\mathbf{M})

with

G^{s}_{n}(\mathbf{M})=\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}_{n}\quad\hbox{and}\quad G^{u}_{n}(\mathbf{M})=\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp},

where
\mathbf{u}_{n}
and
\mathbf{v}_{n}
are the generalized right and left eigenvectors of
\Sigma^{n}\mathbf{M}
defined in (
7.3
).

(iv)

There is a relation between the Anosov splitting of
(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}})
and the Oseledets splittings (
8.5
) of
\mathbf{M}
for the cocycles
A_{\mathrm{inv}}
and
A_{\mathrm{tr}}
. In particular, for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have (setting
G^{i}=G_{\mathrm{inv}}^{p}
for the inverse and
G^{i}=G_{\mathrm{tr}}^{p}
for the transpose splitting)

(8.11)

\displaystyle G^{s}_{n}(\mathbf{M})

\displaystyle=G_{\mathrm{inv}}^{p}(\Sigma^{n}\mathbf{M})=\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}_{n},

\displaystyle G^{u}_{n}(\mathbf{M})

\displaystyle=G_{\mathrm{inv}}^{1}(\Sigma^{n}\mathbf{M})\oplus\cdots\oplus G_{\mathrm{inv}}^{p-1}(\Sigma^{n}\mathbf{M})=\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp},

\displaystyle G^{s}_{n}(\mathbf{M})^{\perp}

\displaystyle=G_{\mathrm{tr}}^{2}(\Sigma^{n}\mathbf{M})\oplus\cdots\oplus G_{\mathrm{tr}}^{p}(\Sigma^{n}\mathbf{M})=\mathbf{u}_{n}^{\perp},

\displaystyle G^{u}_{n}(\mathbf{M})^{\perp}

\displaystyle=G_{\mathrm{tr}}^{1}(\Sigma^{n}\mathbf{M})=\mathbb{R}\mathbf{v}_{n}.

Proof.

From Lemma
8.13
, a.e.
\mathbf{M}\in D
satisfies the two-sided (local) Pisot condition and the growth condition. Thus we may apply our results from Section
7.4
and
7.5
almost everywhere in
D
.

We start with Assertion (i). By assumption, there exists a cylinder set of positive measure in
D
corresponding to a positive matrix. This implies two-sided primitivity for
\nu
-a.e. sequence
\mathbf{M}\in D
by ergodicity of
D
and the Poincaré recurrence theorem. Since a.e. algebraic irreducibility is a consequence of Corollary
7.19
, Assertion (i) follows. Assertion (ii) follows from Proposition
7.15
, and Corollary
7.26
can be applied to get (iii).

To prove (iv), we have to characterize the Oseledets splitting (
8.6
) for the inverse and the transpose cocycle. Let
\{(\Theta_{i},d_{i}):1\leq i\leq p\}
be the Lyapunov spectrum for
A_{\mathrm{tr}}
. By the definition of Lyapunov exponents in (
8.3
),
A_{\mathrm{inv}}
has the Lyapunov exponents
-\vartheta_{d}\geq\dots\geq-\vartheta_{2}>0>-\vartheta_{1}
and, hence, Lyapunov spectrum
\{(-\Theta_{i},d_{i}):1\leq i\leq p\}
.
We have that
\mathbf{x}\in G_{\mathrm{inv}}^{p}(\mathbf{M})
if and only if
\lVert A_{\mathrm{inv}}^{(n)}(\mathbf{M})\mathbf{x}\rVert
grows like
e^{{-\Theta_{1}}n}
, with
\Theta_{1}=\vartheta_{1}>0
. By Corollary
7.26
, see in particular (
7.32
) and (
7.34
), we have

\lim_{n\to+\infty}M_{[0,n)}^{-1}\,\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0},\quad\lim_{n\to-\infty}\lVert M_{[n,0)}\mathbf{x}\lVert=+\infty,\quad\mbox{for all}\ \mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\},

hence,
G_{\mathrm{inv}}^{p}(\mathbf{M})=\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}
.
The same reasoning applies to
\mathbf{v}^{\perp}
. Again by Corollary
7.26
this time we refer also to (
7.33
) and (
7.35
), we gain

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\lVert M_{[0,n)}^{-1}\,\mathbf{x}\lVert=+\infty,\quad\lim_{n\to-\infty}M_{[n,0)}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0},\quad\mbox{for all}\ \mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{v}^{\perp}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\},

therefore
G_{\mathrm{inv}}^{1}(\mathbf{M})\oplus\cdots\oplus G_{\mathrm{inv}}^{p-1}(\mathbf{M})=\mathbf{v}^{\perp}
. Now, the first two lines of (iv) follow from the invariance property of the Oseledets spectrum stated in Propostion
8.5
and the definition of
\mathbf{u}_{n}
and
\mathbf{v}_{n}
in (
7.3
). The other two lines follow because

G_{\mathrm{tr}}^{i}(\Sigma^{n}\mathbf{M})=\bigg(\prod_{j\neq i}G_{\mathrm{inv}}^{p-j+1}(\Sigma^{n}\mathbf{M})\bigg)^{\perp}\qquad(1\leq i\leq p,\,n\in\mathbb{N}).

To see this, pick
\mathbf{x}\in G_{\mathrm{inv}}^{p-i+1}(\mathbf{M})
and
\mathbf{y}\in\big(\prod_{j\neq i}G_{\mathrm{inv}}^{p-j+1}(\mathbf{M})\big)^{\perp}
satisfying
\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle\not=\mathbf{0}
, which is possible because
\prod_{j=1}^{d}G_{\mathrm{inv}}^{j}(\mathbf{M})=\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Since

\lVert M_{[0,n)}^{-1}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2}\cdot\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{y}\rVert_{2}\geq\langle M_{[0,n)}^{-1}\mathbf{x},\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{y}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle\neq 0,

we gain
{\lim_{n\to\pm\infty}}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{y}\rVert\geq\vartheta_{i}
.
Starting with
i=1
, one recursively checks that this inequality actually has to be an equality.
∎

Example 8.16
.

For the set
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{AR}}=\{M_{\mathrm{AR},1},M_{\mathrm{AR},2},M_{\mathrm{AR},3}\}
of Arnoux–Rauzy matrices defined in Example
7.22
, consider the full shift
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{AR}}^{\mathbb{Z}},\Sigma,\nu)
, where
\nu
is an ergodic shift invariant probability measure with full support. Because
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{AR}}
is finite, the log-integrability condition is trivially satisfied. Moreover, by
[
BST23
, Proposition 6.4]
(see also its proof), the shift
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{AR}}^{\mathbb{Z}},\Sigma,\nu)
satisfies the generic Pisot condition. Finally, because
\nu
has full support, the cylinder set

\displaystyle[M_{\mathrm{AR},1},M_{\mathrm{AR},2},M_{\mathrm{AR},3}]

\displaystyle=\{(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{AR}}^{\mathbb{Z}}\,:\,M_{0}=M_{\mathrm{AR},1},M_{1}=M_{\mathrm{AR},2},M_{2}=M_{\mathrm{AR},3}\}

has positive measure. Because
M_{\mathrm{AR},1}M_{\mathrm{AR},2}M_{\mathrm{AR},3}
is a positive matrix,
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{AR}}^{\mathbb{Z}},\Sigma,\nu)
satisfies all conditions of Theorem
8.15
.

Thus
\nu
-a.e. sequence
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{AR}}^{\mathbb{Z}}
is two-sided primitive and algebraically irreducible. It converges exponentially to a left and right eigenvector in the future and in the past, respectively. Moreover, the mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathcal{M}})
associated to it is eventually Anosov w.r.t. the splittings defined in Theorem
8.15
(iii) and (iv).

We will see in Proposition
10.13
that the Brun continued fraction algorithm gives rise to sequences of matrices that satisfy the conditions of Theorem
8.15
for
d\in\{3,4\}
.

Chapter III
Multidimensional continued fraction algorithms

In this chapter we develop the parts of the theory of multidimensional continued fraction algorithms that are relevant for us. In particular, Section
9
contains the definition of a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm, presents the construction of geometric natural extensions for these algorithms, and shows how they are related to mapping families. In Section
10
we provide a detailed treatment of different versions of the Brun continued fraction algorithm, calculate their natural extensions and invariant measures, and show that they satisfy the Pisot condition.

9.
General theory of multidimensional continued fraction algorithms

As mentioned in the introduction, one of the motivations for studying sequences of integer matrices comes from the theory of multidimensional continued fraction algorithms. In the present section, we show how continued fraction algorithms fit in the theory developed for sequences of matrices developed in Chapter
II
. In particular, in Section
9.1
we provide all necessary definitions around multidimensional continued fraction algorithms and illustrate them for the classical continued fraction algorithm and for the Arnoux–Rauzy algorithm. In Section
9.2
we discuss geometric natural extensions of continued fraction algorithms and show, for the example of the classical continued fraction algorithm, how they can be used to calculate an invariant measure for an algorithm that is equivalent to the Lebesgue measure. Section
9.3
gives some definitions in the context of multidimensional continued fraction algorithms that will be needed later. For instance, Lyapunov exponents and a version of the Pisot condition for such an algorithm are provided. In Section
9.4
we show how multidimensional continued fraction algorithms can be related to
\mathcal{M}
-adic mapping families. This enables us to apply the theory of Chapter
II
to multidimensional continued fraction algorithms.

9.1.
Unimodular algorithms

There exists a variety of formalisms for defining multidimensional continued fraction algorithms (see for instance
[
Lag93
,
Sch00
,
KLDM06
,
AL18
,
Fou20
]
). For our purposes, it turns out to be convenient to define a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm on a projective space. In this setting, a continued fraction algorithm “produces” sequences of matrices, which perfectly matches with our studies in Chapter
II
. In the sequel, the
d
-dimensional projective space is denoted as
\mathbb{P}^{d}
, and
\mathbb{P}^{d}_{\geq 0}
is the nonnegative cone of
\mathbb{P}^{d}
.

Definition 9.1

(Continued fraction algorithm)
.

Let
d\geq 2
. Let

A:\,X\to\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})

be a map from a subset
X
of the
(d{-}1)
-dimensional projective space
\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
to the set
\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})
of unimodular integer
d{\times}d
matrices such that
\vphantom{\!A}{}^{t}\!A(\mathbf{x})^{-1}\mathbf{x}\in X
for all
\mathbf{x}\in X
, and let

(9.1)

F:\,X\to X,\quad\mathbf{x}\mapsto\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A(\mathbf{x})^{-1}\mathbf{x}.

Then
(X,F,A)
is called a
(d{-}1)
-dimensional continued fraction algorithm
.

If the range of
A
is a finite set of matrices, then we say that the continued fraction algorithm is
additive
; otherwise, it is called
multiplicative
.
If all
A(\mathbf{x})
,
\mathbf{x}\in X
, have nonnegative entries, then the algorithm is called
positive
.

The use of the transpose inverse
\vphantom{\!A}{}^{t}\!A(\mathbf{x})^{-1}
in the definition of a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm
(X,F,A)
may look cumbersome at first sight. The inversion is used in (
9.1
) because the
partial quotient matrices

\vphantom{\!A}{}^{t}\!A(F^{n}\mathbf{x})
,
n\geq 0
, are more important in our work than their inverses. Indeed, for each
\mathbf{x}\in X
, iterative application of the algorithm
(X,F,A)
generates a sequence of partial quotient matrices
(\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A(F^{n}\mathbf{x}))_{n\geq 0}
. Taking products of the partial quotient matrices leads to the
convergent matrices

\vphantom{\!A}{}^{t}\!A^{(n)}(\mathbf{x})=\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A(\mathbf{x})\,\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A(F\mathbf{x})\cdots\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A(F^{n-1}\mathbf{x})
,
n\geq 0
that are used for simultaneous Diophantine approximation of
\mathbf{x}=[x_{1}:\dots:x_{d}]\in X
.
Indeed, the column vectors of the convergent matrices

\vphantom{\!A}{}^{t}\!A^{(n)}(\mathbf{x})=\begin{pmatrix}p_{1,1}^{(n)}&p_{1,2}^{(n)}&\cdots&p_{d,1}^{(n)}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
p_{d-1,1}^{(n)}&p_{d-1,2}^{(n)}&\cdots&p_{d-1,d}^{(n)}\\
q_{1}^{(n)}&q_{2}^{(n)}&\cdots&q_{d}^{(n)}\end{pmatrix}\qquad(n\geq 0)

allow us to define
d
sequences of
rational convergents

(p^{(n)}_{i,j}/q_{j}^{n)})_{1\leq i<d}
, one for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
, that are supposed to converge in
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
to
(\frac{x_{1}}{x_{d}},\dots,\frac{x_{d-1}}{x_{d}})
(recall that
\mathbf{x}=[x_{1}:\dots:x_{d}]
). Here, we have chosen to use the last line for the denominators of convergents. This is arbitrary but it corresponds to a situation met for classical
algorithms such as the Brun algorithm discussed in Section
10
below, where the integers
q_{i}^{(n)}
in the last line are the largest entries for each column.

The transposition used in (
9.1
) comes from the fact that we are interested in the
linear cocycle
of the algorithm
(X,F,A)
, which is given by
A
. Indeed

A^{(n)}(\mathbf{x})=A(F^{n-1}\mathbf{x})\cdots A(F\mathbf{x})\,A(\mathbf{x}),

satisfies the
cocycle property

A^{(m+n)}(\mathbf{x})=A^{(m)}(F^{n}\mathbf{x})\,A^{(n)}(\mathbf{x})
; see also Section
8.3
and Definition
8.9
. In other words,
A(\mathbf{x})
and
\vphantom{\!A}{}^{t}\!A(\mathbf{x})^{-1}
are covariant, while
{A}(\mathbf{x})^{-1}
and
\vphantom{\!A}{}^{t}\!A(\mathbf{x})
are contravariant.

We will need the following notions of cylinder sets and follower sets.

Definition 9.2

(Cylinder set and follower set for multidimensional continued fraction algorithms)
.

The
cylinder sets
of a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm
(X,F,A)
are given by

X_{[0,n)}(\mathbf{x})=\{\mathbf{y}\in X\,:\,A(\mathbf{y})=A(\mathbf{x}),A(F\mathbf{y})=A(F\mathbf{x}),\dots,A(F^{n-1}\mathbf{y})=A(F^{n-1}\mathbf{x})\},

with
X_{[0,0)}(\mathbf{x})=X
.
Moreover, for
M\in A(X)
we write

X_{M}=\{\mathbf{y}\in X\,:\,A(\mathbf{y})=M\}=A^{-1}(M)

for the cylinder set associated to a given matrix
M
.

A
follower set
is a set of the form
F^{n}X_{[0,n)}(\mathbf{x})
, where
\mathbf{x}\in X
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
X_{[0,n)}(\mathbf{x})
is a cylinder set of
(X,F,A)
.

Instead of working in the homogeneous space
\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
, one can choose a norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(various norms can be convenient for the normalization; see for instance Section
10
) and select for each element of
\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
a representative in

\Delta^{d-1}=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\,\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert=1\}.

In other words, via the corresponding (injective) map
13
13

13

We will mostly stay in the nonnegative cone
\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0}^{d-1}
of
\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
, where the restriction of
\chi:\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0}^{d-1}\rightarrow\chi(\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0}^{d-1})
can be regarded as an affine chart; of course, we need more than one chart to obtain an affine atlas for
\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
.

(9.2)

\chi:\mathbb{P}^{d-1}\to\Delta^{d-1},

which assigns to each
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
a representative with norm
1
, we can equip
X
with affine coordinates. Therefore, setting
\Delta=\chi(X)
, in the coordinates
\chi
the continued fraction map
F
is given by the map
T=\chi\circ F\circ\chi^{-1}|_{\Delta}
, which can be written out as

(9.3)

T:\,\Delta\to\Delta,\quad\mathbf{x}\mapsto\frac{\vphantom{\!A}{}^{t}\!A(\mathbf{x})^{-1}\mathbf{x}}{\lVert\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A(\mathbf{x})^{-1}\mathbf{x}\rVert},

and
(\Delta,T,A)
is the
affine version
of the continued fraction algorithm
(X,F,A)
. In our applications,
X
is always small enough to guarantee that the restriction of
\chi
to
X
is bijective from
X
onto
\Delta=\chi(X)
.

A multidimensional continued fraction algorithm
(X,F,A)
as in Definition
9.1
as well as its affine version (
9.3
) is called
linear simplex-splitting
in
[
Lag93
, Section 2]
. This kind of algorithms contain classical algorithms like the algorithms of Brun
[
Bru19
,
Bru20
,
Bru58
]
, Jacobi–Perron
[
HJ68
,
Per07
,
Ber71
,
Sch73
]
, and Selmer
[
Sel61
]
.
As the following example shows, also the classical continued fraction algorithm fits into the scheme of Definition
9.1
.

Example 9.3

(Classical continued fraction algorithm)
.

To define the
classical continued fraction algorithm
we set
d=2
and
X_{\mathrm{G}}=\{[x:1]\,:\,0<x<1\}\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\geq 0}
, and define the cocycle
A_{\mathrm{G}}
by

(9.4)

A_{\mathrm{G}}:X\to\operatorname{GL}(2,\mathbb{Z});\quad\begin{bmatrix}x\\
1\end{bmatrix}\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&a\end{pmatrix}\quad\hbox{with }a=\left\lfloor\frac{1}{x}\right\rfloor.

The mapping
F_{\mathrm{G}}
is therefore given by

F_{\mathrm{G}}:X_{\mathrm{G}}\to X_{\mathrm{G}};\quad\begin{bmatrix}x\\
1\end{bmatrix}\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}-a&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x\\
1\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}1-ax\\
x\\
\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1-ax}{x}\\
1\end{bmatrix}.

If we choose the maximum norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty}
to define
\Delta^{1}
we get

\Delta^{1}=\chi(X_{\mathrm{G}})=\{(x,1)\,:\,0<x<1\}.

According to (
9.3
), the affine version of the classical continued fraction map is given by

T_{\mathrm{G}}:\Delta^{1}\to\Delta^{1};\quad\begin{pmatrix}x\\
1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{x}-a\\
1\end{pmatrix}.

Because there is no information in the second coordinate in the definition of
T_{\mathrm{G}}
, we may identify
\Delta^{1}
with
(0,1)
. Because
a=\lfloor\frac{1}{x}\rfloor
by the definition of
A_{\mathrm{G}}
we gain

T_{\mathrm{G}}:\,(0,1)\to(0,1),\quad x\mapsto\frac{1}{x}-\left\lfloor\frac{1}{x}\right\rfloor,

which is exactly the well-known Gauss map that we already mentioned in (
2.4
).
14
14

14

Since we are mainly interested in metric results, we didn’t take care of the fact that
0
is a possible image of
T_{\mathrm{G}}
.
Note that, since
A_{\mathrm{G}}
can take infinitely many values, the classical continued fraction algorithm is a multiplicative algorithm.

We note that the Farey map (
2.1
) can be defined in terms of Definition
9.1
as an additive continued fraction algorithm. The range of its cocycle equals the finite set
\{M_{\mathrm{F},1},M_{\mathrm{F},2}\}
, where
M_{\mathrm{F},1}
and
M_{\mathrm{F},2}
are the Farey matrices introduced in Example
7.6
.
The following example contains a well-known generalization of the Farey algorithm to higher dimensions.

Example 9.4

(Arnoux–Rauzy continued fraction algorithm)
.

To define the
Arnoux–Rauzy continued fraction algorithm
for
d=3
, recall the set of Arnoux–Rauzy matrices
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{AR}}=\{M_{\mathrm{AR},1},M_{\mathrm{AR},2},M_{\mathrm{AR},3}\}
from Example
7.22
. The cocyle
A_{\mathrm{AR}}:\mathbb{P}^{2}_{\geq 0}\to\operatorname{GL}(3,\mathbb{Z})
for this algorithm is given by

A_{\mathrm{AR}}([x_{1}:x_{2}:x_{3}])=M_{\mathrm{AR},a}^{-1}\quad\hbox{if }x_{a}=\max\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\};\;a\in\{1,2,3\}.

Using this cocycle, we define a mapping
F:\mathbb{P}^{2}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
by
F(\mathbf{x})=\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A_{\mathrm{AR}}(\mathbf{x})^{-1}\mathbf{x}
, which can be written explicitly as

F:\mathbb{P}^{2}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{P}^{2},\quad[x_{1}:x_{2}:x_{3}]\mapsto\begin{cases}[x_{1}{-}x_{2}{-}x_{3}:x_{2}:x_{3}]&\mbox{ if }x_{1}=\max\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\},\\
[x_{1}:x_{2}{-}x_{1}{-}x_{3}:x_{3}]&\mbox{ if }x_{2}=\max\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\},\\
[x_{1}:x_{2}:x_{3}{-}x_{1}{-}x_{2}]&\mbox{ if }x_{3}=\max\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\}.\end{cases}

In general, the image of
F
is not contained in the positive cone of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
and, hence,
F
cannot always be iterated. We therefore restrict
F
to the set

X_{\mathrm{AR}}=\big\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{\geq 0}\,:\,F^{n}(\mathbf{x})\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{\geq 0}\ \mbox{for all}\ n\in\mathbb{N}\big\},

which is a projective version of the
Rauzy gasket
, see
[
AS13
]
. This set allows us to define the
Arnoux–Rauzy map

(9.5)

F_{\mathrm{AR}}:\,X_{\mathrm{AR}}\to X_{\mathrm{AR}},\quad\mathbf{x}\mapsto F(\mathbf{x}),

and, hence, the
Arnoux–Rauzy continued fraction algorithm

(X_{\mathrm{AR}},F_{\mathrm{AR}},A_{\mathrm{AR}})
.
If we choose the norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{1}
in the definition of the chart
\chi
in (
9.2
) to define
\Delta_{\mathrm{AR}}
we get the Rauzy gasket

\Delta_{\mathrm{AR}}=\chi(X_{\mathrm{AR}})
. According to (
9.5
), the affine version of the Arnoux–Rauzy map
T_{\mathrm{AR}}:\Delta_{\mathrm{AR}}\to\Delta_{\mathrm{AR}}
is given by

T_{\mathrm{AR}}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{x_{1}}(x_{1}{-}x_{2}{-}x_{3},x_{2},x_{3})&\hbox{ if }x_{1}=\max\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\},\\
\frac{1}{x_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2}{-}x_{1}{-}x_{3},x_{3})&\hbox{ if }x_{2}=\max\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\},\\
\frac{1}{x_{3}}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}{-}x_{1}{-}x_{2})&\hbox{ if }x_{3}=\max\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\}.\end{cases}

Since
A_{\mathrm{AR}}
can take only 3 different values, the Arnoux–Rauzy algorithm is an additive continued fraction algorithm. It can be defined in an analogous way for each
d\geq 3
, see for instance
[
BST23
, Section 6.3]
.

9.2.
Natural extension and invariant measure

Our main goal in this section is to construct an invariant measure for a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm
(X,F,A)
. Because invariant measures are easier to find for invertible dynamical system, we first extend
(X,F,A)
to an invertible dynamical system, the so-called natural extension
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A})
of
(X,F,A)
. By constructing an invariant measure
\widehat{\nu}
on some geometric version of
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A})
, we can then obtain the desired invariant measure
\nu
of
(X,F,A)
as a pushed forward measure of
\widehat{\nu}
. For technical reasons we will mainly work in the affine version
(\Delta,T,A)
of
(X,F,A)
. The section ends with a series of important definitions related to multidimensional continued fraction algorithms like the Pisot condition, positive range, and periodic Pisot point.

A multidimensional continued fraction algorithm
(X,F,A)
is usually not one-to-one, hence, it is not invertible. To produce an invertible version of the algorithm, we use the well-known concept of inverse limit. Let
F:X\to X
be a surjective map. Then we can always find a set
\widehat{X}
, an invertible map
\widehat{F}:\widehat{X}\to\widehat{X}
, and a surjective map
\pi:\widehat{X}\to X
such that

(9.6)

\pi\circ\widehat{F}=F\circ\pi.

The system
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F})
is called the
inverse limit
of
(X,F)
. It can be constructed as

(9.7)

\widehat{X}=\big\{(\mathbf{x}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in X^{\mathbb{Z}}\,:\,F(\mathbf{x}_{n})=\mathbf{x}_{n+1}\ \mbox{for all}\ n\in\mathbb{Z}\big\},

with
\widehat{F}((\mathbf{x}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})=(\mathbf{x}_{n+1})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\pi((\mathbf{x}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})=\mathbf{x}_{0}
.
If, furthermore,
\mu
is an invariant measure for the dynamical system
(X,F)
, then one can easily build an invariant measure
\widehat{\mu}
for
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F})
such that
\mu=\pi_{*}\widehat{\mu}
.
15
15

15

For a function
f
and a measure
\mu
, the pushforward measure
f_{*}\mu
is defined by setting
f_{*}\mu(B):=\mu(f^{-1}(B))
for all measurable sets
B
.
The triple
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{\mu})
is a
natural extension
of the measurable dynamical system
(X,F,\mu)
in the sense of Rokhlin, see e.g.
[
VO16
, Section 2.4.2]
.
The cocycle
A
then extends to a cocycle
\widehat{A}:\widehat{X}\to\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})
, with
\widehat{A}=A\circ\pi
, and
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A})
is called a
natural extension
of the multidimensional continued fraction algorithm
(X,F,A)
.

According to Definition
9.1
, a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm
(X,F,A)
is defined on a subset
X
of the projective space
\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
. While there is no canonical Lebesgue measure in a projective space, there is a Lebesgue measure class: the class of measures that are absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure in any local affine coordinate system. The problem of finding an ergodic invariant measure of
(X,F,A)
in the Lebesgue measure class (i.e., a
Gauss measure
) is difficult. As mentioned before, it is convenient to look for an invariant measure
\widehat{\nu}
in a geometric version of the natural extension of
(X,F,A)
and then get the desired invariant measure as a pushforward measure of
\widehat{\nu}
. Moreover, it is more convenient to do these constructions for the affine version of the algorithm
(X,F,A)
.

Let
d\geq 2
, let
\lVert\cdot\rVert
be a norm on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and let
\Delta^{d-1}=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert=1\}
. Equip
(X,F,A)
with affine coordinates using the map
\chi:\mathbb{P}^{d-1}\to\Delta^{d-1}
, introduced above, which assigns to each
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
a representative with norm
1
, and consider the affine version
(\Delta,T,A)
of
(X,F,A)
with
\Delta=\chi(X)\subset\Delta^{d-1}
and
T
as in (
9.3
).

Our starting point for a geometric version of a natural extension of
(\Delta,T,A)
is the map

(9.8)

\widehat{T}:\,\Delta^{d-1}\times\Delta^{d-1}\to\Delta^{d-1}\times\Delta^{d-1},\quad(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\mapsto\left(\frac{\vphantom{\!A}{}^{t}\!A(\mathbf{x})^{-1}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{x}}{\lVert\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A(\mathbf{x})^{-1}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{x}\rVert},\frac{{A}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{y}}{\lVert{A}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{y}\rVert}\right).

Our task is now twofold. We have to find a suitable subset
\widehat{\Delta}
of
\Delta^{d-1}\times\Delta^{d-1}
on which
\widehat{T}
is bijective, and an invariant measure for
\widehat{T}
on
\widehat{\Delta}
.

We first define a measure
\widehat{\mu}
on
\Delta^{d-1}\times\Delta^{d-1}
which is locally preserved by
\widehat{T}
. We start by a map
h:\Delta^{d-1}\to\Delta^{d-1}
that is locally defined by
h(\mathbf{x})=\frac{M\mathbf{x}}{\lVert M\mathbf{x}\rVert}
for some invertible matrix
M
. An easy computation shows that the Jacobian of
h
at
\mathbf{x}
is
\frac{|\det M|}{\lVert M\mathbf{x}\rVert^{d}}
; for more details on this computation, see e.g. Veech
[
Vee78
, Proposition 5.2]
.
16
16

16

Note that Veech uses
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{1}
, however, his proof goes through for any other norm.
This implies that the Jacobian of the map
\widehat{T}
at
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\Delta^{d-1}\times\Delta^{d-1}
equals
{\lVert\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A(\mathbf{x})^{-1}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{x}\rVert}^{-d}{\lVert{A}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{y}\rVert}^{-d}
, which motivates the following definition. Let
\lambda
be the (piecewise)
17
17

17

If the unit ball for the norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert
is for instance a cube or an octahedron (according to the dimension), then we use the Lebesgue measure on each surface; if it is a sphere, then we use the spherical Lebesgue measure.
Lebesgue measure on
\Delta^{d-1}
and define
\widehat{\mu}
on
\Delta^{d-1}\times\Delta^{d-1}
by
\mathrm{d}\widehat{\mu}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda(\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathrm{d}\lambda{(\mathbf{y})}}{\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle^{d}}
. We obviously have
\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle=\langle\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A(\mathbf{x})^{-1}\mathbf{x},A(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{y}\rangle
. Thus the computation of the Jacobian of
\widehat{T}
shows that

(9.9)

\widehat{\mu}(\widehat{T}(Y))=\widehat{\mu}(Y),\quad\text{if }Y\subset\chi(A^{-1}(M))\times\Delta^{d-1}\text{ for some }M\in\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z}),

because
\widehat{T}
is obviously injective on such a set
Y
. Thus
\widehat{T}
preserves the measure
\widehat{\mu}
on subsets of
\Delta^{d-1}\times\Delta^{d-1}
on which
\chi\circ A
is constant. If we allow for general subsets of
\Delta^{d-1}\times\Delta^{d-1}
, (
9.9
) implies that

(9.10)

\widehat{\mu}(\widehat{T}(Y))\leq\widehat{\mu}(Y)\quad\text{if }Y\subset\Delta^{d-1}\times\Delta^{d-1}.

As for the region of bijectivity for
\widehat{T}
, it turns out that, when the map
T
satisfies some mild contractivity conditions and using the Banach fixed-point argument from
[
AS19
, Theorem 1.1]
, it is possible to construct a set
\widehat{\Delta}\subset\Delta^{d-1}\times\Delta^{d-1}
on which
\widehat{T}
is bijective.
If the set
\widehat{\Delta}
exists, then it is unique by
[
AS19
, Theorem 1.2]
under some mild conditions on
T
. Sometimes (see e.g. Section
10
for the Brun continued fraction algorithm) we obtain a candidate for
\widehat{\Delta}
by guessing. In this case one has to prove bijectivity of
\widehat{T}
on
\widehat{\Delta}
directly and the following lemma, which is an immediate consequence of (
9.10
), is useful (see also
[
AS19
, Theorem 4.7]
).

Lemma 9.5
.

If
\widehat{T}
is surjective on
\widehat{\Delta}
then it is bijective on
\widehat{\Delta}
.

From now on, we regard
\widehat{T}
as a function on
\widehat{\Delta}
and
\widehat{\mu}
as a measure on
\widehat{\Delta}
.
Because
\widehat{T}
is bijective on
\widehat{\Delta}
, it follows from (
9.9
) that in (
9.10
) we have equality for each
Y\subset\widehat{\Delta}
. Again by bijectivity of
\widehat{T}
this implies that
\widehat{T}_{*}\widehat{\mu}=\widehat{\mu}
, i.e.,
\widehat{\mu}
is an invariant measure for
\widehat{T}
that is equivalent to the piecewise Lebesgue measure
\lambda\times\lambda
on
\widehat{\Delta}
.
Using the natural projection

\pi:\,\widehat{\Delta}\to\Delta,\quad(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\mapsto\mathbf{x},

we see that our construction implies that
(\widehat{\Delta},\widehat{T},\widehat{A},\widehat{\mu})
, with
\widehat{A}=A\circ\pi
, is an invertible measurable dynamical system which is a geometric version of the natural extension of
(\Delta,T,A,\mu)
with
\mu=\pi_{*}\widehat{\mu}
.

If
\widehat{\mu}(\widehat{\Delta})>0
and if
\widehat{\mu}
(or, equivalently,
\mu
; see
[
CFS82
, Chapter 10, § 4]
) is ergodic, then the measure
\mu
is a Gauss measure of the affine version
(\Delta,T,A,\mu)
of the algorithm. There is no general method to ensure that
\widehat{\mu}(\widehat{\Delta})>0
, and one has to check this directly for each algorithm. The situation is better for ergodicity. Usually, ergodicity of
\widehat{\mu}
is proved by verifying a Rényi type condition, see e.g.
[
Rén57
]
or
[
Sch00
, Chapter 3]
. Indeed, it suffices to show that there exists an ergodic invariant measure which is equivalent to the Lebesgue measure: If we exhibit such a measure, each invariant set has either zero or full Lebesgue measure and, hence, either zero or full measure
\widehat{\mu}
. Thus
\widehat{\mu}
and, hence,
\mu
, is ergodic. For the Brun continued fraction algorithm, a proof of ergodicity is detailed in
[
Sch00
, Chapter 3]
. This also shows that sometimes, before a Rényi type condition can be applied, one has to consider certain accelerations of
T
, so-called
jump transformations
; see
[
Sch75
]
for a theory of these transformations (we will use a jump transformation in Section
10.3
to define the multiplicative version of the Brun continued fraction algorithm).

It remains to obtain the ergodic invariant measure
\nu
in the Lebesgue measure class for the multidimensional continued fraction algorithm
(X,F,A)
. Via the inverse of the bijective chart given by the restriction of
\chi
from
X
onto
\Delta=\chi(X)
, we can transfer this dynamical system back to
\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
and end up with the system
(X,F,A,\nu)
. The measure
\nu
, which is defined by
\chi_{*}\nu=\mu
, is the desired Gauss measure for
(X,F,A,\nu)
. In our examples we will only provide the Gauss measure
\mu
for the projective version of the algorithm, because it is the more natural object and
\nu
can easily be computed from
\nu
.

By transferring
(\widehat{\Delta},\widehat{T},\widehat{A},\widehat{\mu})
back to
\mathbb{P}^{d-1}{\times}\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
via the bijection
18
18

18

Again we assume that
\widehat{\Delta}
is not too large, otherwise we need more than one chart.

\widehat{\chi}=\chi{\times}\chi
, we see that
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
is a natural extension of
(X,F,A,\nu)
with
\widehat{X}=\widehat{\chi}^{-1}(\widehat{\Delta})
and
\widehat{F}=\chi^{-1}\circ\widehat{T}\circ\chi
. Note that
\widehat{F}
can be written explicitly as

(9.11)

\widehat{F}:\,\widehat{X}\to\widehat{X},\quad(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\mapsto(\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A(\mathbf{x})^{-1}\mathbf{x},A(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{y}).

Remark 9.6
.

Of course, this construction is meaningful only if
\widehat{\Delta}
and, hence
\widehat{X}
, can be chosen in a way that
\widehat{\mu}(\widehat{\Delta})>0
, and equivalently
\widehat{\nu}(\widehat{X})>0
, because otherwise
\mu(\Delta)=0
. As mentioned above, no general method is known at the moment to achieve this positivity.

If
\widehat{\nu}(\widehat{X})>0
and
\widehat{\nu}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, we get that
\widehat{X}
has positive Lebesgue measure. Hence, all the statements that hold a.e. hold Lebesgue a.e. in this case. This turns out to be particularly relevant, since the set of parameters for which the mapping families for multidimensional continued fraction algorithms will be defined in Section
9.4
is precisely
\widehat{X}
.

We illustrate our constructions briefly for the classical continued fraction algorithm discussed in Example
9.3
.

Example 9.7

(Gauss measure for the classical continued fraction algorithm)
.

Let
(\Delta^{1},T_{\mathrm{G}},A_{\mathrm{G}})
be the affine version of the classical continued fraction algorithm defined in Example
9.3
and use the notation of that example. By the definition of the cocycle
A_{\mathrm{G}}
provided in (
9.4
), we gain from (
9.8
) (recall that we use the norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty}
for the classical continued fraction algorithm) that the mapping

\widehat{T}_{\mathrm{G}}:\,\Delta^{1}{\times}\Delta^{1}\to\Delta^{1}{\times}\Delta^{1},\quad\bigg(\binom{x}{1},\binom{y}{1}\bigg)\mapsto\bigg(\binom{\frac{1}{x}-a}{1},\binom{\frac{1}{a+y}}{1}\bigg)\quad\mbox{with}\ a=\Big\lfloor\frac{1}{x}\Big\rfloor

is the starting point in our quest for a natural extension of
T_{\mathrm{G}}
. Because the second coordinates of the occurring vectors are irrelevant, we can regard
\widehat{T}_{\mathrm{G}}
as a mapping from
(0,1)^{2}
to itself defined by

(9.12)

\widehat{T}_{\mathrm{G}}:\,(0,1)^{2}\to(0,1)^{2},\quad(x,y)\mapsto\big(\tfrac{1}{x}-a,\tfrac{1}{a+y}\big)\quad\mbox{with}\ a=\big\lfloor\tfrac{1}{x}\big\rfloor.

With the methods developed in
[
AS19
]
(or directly from
[
TI81
]
) one can see that
\widehat{T}_{\mathrm{G}}
is bijective on
(0,1)^{2}
, thus
\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{G}}=(0,1)^{2}
and
((0,1)^{2},\widehat{T}_{\mathrm{G}},\widehat{A}_{\mathrm{G}})
is a geometric natural extension of the classical continued fraction algorithm. According to the above construction, the invariant measure of the mapping
\widehat{T}_{\mathrm{G}}
is given by
\mathrm{d}\widehat{\mu}_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\frac{\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y}{(1+xy)^{2}}
. Positivity of
\widehat{\mu}_{\mathrm{G}}(\widehat{X})
is easy to see in this case. Pushing forward
\widehat{\mu}_{\mathrm{G}}
to
(0,1)
(i.e., integrating away
y
from
0
to
1
) and renormalizing yields the well-known Gauss measure
\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{G}}(x)=\frac{1}{\log 2}\frac{1}{1+x}
on
(0,1)
. Ergodicity of this Gauss measure can be proved by standard methods, see for instance
[
EW11
, Theorem 3.7]
, or by using the strategy described above.

According to (
9.11
), the natural extension of
F_{\mathrm{G}}
is given by (
a=\lfloor 1/x\rfloor
)

\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{G}}:\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{1}{\times}\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{1}{\times}\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{1};\;\left(\begin{bmatrix}x\\
1\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}y\\
1\end{bmatrix}\right)\mapsto\left(\begin{pmatrix}-a&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x\\
1\end{bmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&a\end{pmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}y\\
1\end{bmatrix}\right)

It is easy to carry over the invariant measure to the projective version of the classical continued fraction algorithm with help of the chart
\chi
. We refrain from giving the details here.

The geometric natural extension
\widehat{T}_{\mathrm{G}}
given in (
9.12
) goes back to
[
NIT77
]
.

9.3.
Positive range, Pisot condition, and periodic Pisot point

The following property of a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm will be of importance for us as well.

Definition 9.8

(Positive range)
.

Let
(X,F,A,\nu)
be a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm
An element
\mathbf{x}\in X
is said to have
positive range
in
(X,F,A,\nu)
if

\inf_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\nu(F^{n}X_{[0,n)}(\mathbf{x}))>0.

Note that measurability of the cylinder sets of
(X,F,A,\nu)
holds because
A
is measurable by assumption.

In all the classical algorithms we are aware of, almost every
\mathbf{x}\in X
has positive range, and we even have the stronger (global)
finite range property
(cf.
[
IY87
]
) stating that the set of follower sets

\mathcal{F}=\{F^{n}X_{[0,n)}(\mathbf{x})\,:\,\mathbf{x}\in X,\,n\in\mathbb{N}\}

is finite, where sets differing only on a set of
\nu
-measure zero are identified. Some of them are even
full
in the sense that
\mathcal{F}=\{X\}
(again up to sets of
\nu
-measure zero). Since our theory works in the much weaker setting of positive range, we develop it under this condition, This could turn out to be useful in other situations.

By the
F
-invariance of
\nu
, the finite range property obviously implies positive range for a.e.
\mathbf{x}\in X
if we suppose that all cylinders satisfy
\nu(X_{[0,n)}(\mathbf{x}))>0
; this will be the case for the algorithms considered in this monograph. If
(X,F,A,\nu)
has the finite range property and
\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}X_{[0,n)}(\mathbf{x})=\{\mathbf{x}\}
for almost all
\mathbf{x}\in X
, i.e., the set of cylinders
\{X(\mathbf{x}):\mathbf{x}\in X\}
is a generating partition, then
\{U\cap X(\mathbf{x}):U\in\mathcal{F},\,\mathbf{x}\in X\}
forms a (measurable countable) generating Markov partition of
(X,F)
; see e.g.
[
Yur95
, Theorem 10.1]
. Most of the classical continued fraction algorithms (like Brun, Selmer, and Jacobi–Perron) are designed in a way that this Markov partition property holds. This Markov condition is not to be confused with the Markov partition we are studying here. Indeed, according to Definition
9.1
, a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm generates sequences of matrices. If
D\subset\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{N}}
is the set of all possible sequences of matrices, then this “global” Markov partition states that the set
D
is a sofic shift; see again
[
Yur95
, Theorem 10.1]
. The Markov partitions we are considering in this monograph are “local” in the sense that they concern a single sequence of matrices
\mathbf{M}
taken from the natural extension
\widehat{D}
of
D
. Regarding such a sequence as a sequence of toral automorphisms, our theorems associate nonstationary Markov partitions to almost all sequences
\mathbf{M}\in\widehat{D}
.

In certain cases, a classical construction leads to a dual algorithm of a given multidimensional continued fraction algorithm
(X,F,A)
(see e.g.
[
Sch00
, Definition 27]
): Indeed, if
(X,F,A)
is a full algorithm, the image
\widehat{F}(\widehat{X})
admits a product structure in the sense that, for all
M
contained in the image
A(X)
of the cocycle,

\widehat{F}((X_{M}\times\mathbb{P}^{d-1})\cap\widehat{X})=(X\times X^{*}_{M})

with the cylinder set
X_{M}
from Definition
9.2
and some
X_{M}^{*}\subset\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
. This implies that the algorithm is full and enables us to define the
dual algorithm

(X^{*},F^{*},A^{*})
on
X^{*}=\bigcup_{M\in A(X)}X_{M}^{*}
(up to measure 0) by

\displaystyle A^{*}:

\displaystyle X^{*}\to\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle\mapsto\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M\quad\mbox{if}\ \mathbf{y}\in X^{*}_{M},

\displaystyle F^{*}:

\displaystyle X^{*}\to X^{*},

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle\mapsto\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A^{*}(\mathbf{y})^{-1}\mathbf{y},

Thus
(X^{*},F^{*},A^{*})
is a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm. Moreover, we have, for almost all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\widehat{X}
,

\widehat{F}^{-1}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=(A^{*}(\mathbf{y})\mathbf{x},\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A^{*}(\mathbf{y})^{-1}\mathbf{y}).

In particular,
X\times X_{M}^{*}=\widehat{F}(X_{M}{\times}X^{*})
and, hence,
X_{M}^{*}
is a cylinder set for the dual algorithm.

The following definition concerns one of our main objects of interest.

Definition 9.9

(Pisot condition for multidimensional continued fractions)
.

Let
(X,F,A,\nu)
be an ergodic continued fraction algorithm as defined in Definition
9.1
and assume that the log-integrability condition

(9.13)

\int_{X}\log\lVert A\rVert_{\infty}d{\nu}<\infty

holds. We say that
(X,F,A,\nu)
satisfies the Pisot condition if the (one-sided) Lyapunov exponents
\vartheta_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\vartheta_{d}
of
A
satisfy
\vartheta_{1}>0>\vartheta_{2}
. (Note that for the definition of Lyapunov exponents only the future is relevant but observe Remark
8.4
.)

From Example
8.16
, we see that the Arnoux–Rauzy algorithm
(X_{\mathrm{AR}},F_{\mathrm{AR}},A_{\mathrm{AR}})
defined in Example
9.4
satisfies the Pisot condition.

Remark 9.10

(Lyapunov exponents and Pisot condition of the natural extension)
.

If
(X,F,A,\nu)
satisfies the Pisot condition then its natural extension
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
, where
\nu=\pi_{*}\widehat{\nu}
, satisfies the Pisot condition from Definition
8.6
. We follow
[
Via14
, p. 99]
. For
f\in L_{1}(\nu)
and
\widehat{f}=f\circ\pi_{0}:\widehat{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
one therefore has
\int\widehat{f}d\widehat{\nu}=\int fd{\nu}
. Thus, in this setting, the two-sided log-integrability condition (
8.2
) for
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
reads

\int_{\widehat{X}}\log\lVert\widehat{A}\rVert_{\infty}\mathrm{d}\widehat{\nu}=\int_{X}\log\lVert A\rVert_{\infty}\mathrm{d}\nu<\infty

and, hence, is the same as (
9.13
).
We conclude that the Lyapunov exponents of
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
are equal to those of
(X,F,A,\nu)
.
19
19

19

This is true even in the general setting of Section
8.1
but we only need it for continued fraction algorithms.

We will need in our main results that any set
E\subset X
with
\nu(E)>0
included in the follower set
F^{n}X_{[0,n)}(\mathbf{x})
leads to an intersection
F^{-n}B\cap X_{[0,n)}(\mathbf{x})
with positive measure.
To this end, we will often assume that

(9.14)

\nu(E)=0\quad\mbox{implies that}\quad\nu\circ F(E)=0\quad\mbox{for each measurable set}\ E.

We will use the notation
\nu\circ F\ll\nu
to indicate that (
9.14
) holds.
Although
\nu\circ F
is usually not additive and therefore not a measure, we use this notation because (
9.14
) is reminiscent of absolute continuity.

Finally, the following notion of
periodic Pisot point
will be of importance later.

Definition 9.11

(Periodic Pisot point)
.

For a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm
(X,F,A)
with natural extension
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A})
, we say that
(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})\in\widehat{X}
is a
periodic Pisot point
if there is
k\geq 1
such that
\widehat{F}^{k}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})=(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})
and
A^{(k)}(\mathbf{x}_{0})
is a Pisot matrix.

In other words, a periodic Pisot point is an element of
\widehat{X}
, on which the algorithm produces a periodic orbit
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathbb{Z}}
that converges exponentially.

Example 9.12

(The golden ratio gives rise to a periodic Pisot point)
.

Let
\phi=\frac{1+\sqrt{5}}{2}
be the golden ratio. If we apply the natural extension
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A})
of the classical continued fraction algorithm
(X,F,A)
constructed in Example
9.7
to the element
([\phi^{-1},1],[\phi^{-1},1])\in\widehat{X}
, we get

\widehat{F}\bigg(\begin{bmatrix}\phi^{-1}\\
1\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\phi^{-1}\\
1\end{bmatrix}\bigg)=\bigg(\begin{pmatrix}-1&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\phi^{-1}\\
1\end{bmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\phi^{-1}\\
1\end{bmatrix}\bigg)=\bigg(\begin{bmatrix}\phi^{-2}\\
\phi^{-1}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\phi\end{bmatrix}\bigg)

and because
[1,\phi]=[\phi^{-1},1]=[\phi^{-2},\phi^{-1}]
, the invariance property of Definition
9.11
is satisfied with
k=1
. Since

A\bigg(\begin{bmatrix}\phi^{-1}\\
1\end{bmatrix}\bigg)=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}

is a Pisot matrix (its eigenvalues are
\phi
and
-1/\phi
), the element
([\phi^{-1},1],[\phi^{-1},1])
is a periodic Pisot point.

9.4.
Multidimensional continued fraction algorithms and mapping families

Let
(X,F,A)
be a positive multidimensional continued fraction algorithm with natural extension
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A})
. We now show how this algorithm is related to the concepts defined in Chapter
II
. Each orbit of the natural extension
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A})
produces a bi-infinite sequence of partial quotient matrices via the mapping

(9.15)

\boldsymbol{\psi}:\,\widehat{X}\to\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}},\quad(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\mapsto(\vphantom{\!\widehat{A}}^{t}\!\widehat{A}(\widehat{F}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})))_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}.

This leads to the shift
(\boldsymbol{\psi}(\widehat{X}),\Sigma)
, for which the diagram

(9.16)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\psi}(\widehat{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\psi}(\widehat{X})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}

commutes. Set
D=\boldsymbol{\psi}(\widehat{X})
.

This commutative diagram can be used to carry over the notions of convergence defined for sequences of matrices in Definition
7.7
to the natural extension
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A})
of a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm. More precisely,
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A})
is
weakly
, and
exponentially convergent
(in the past or in the future) at
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
if the same is true for
\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
in the sense of Definition
7.7
. Also, the local Pisot condition (see Definition
7.9
) can be carried over to
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A})
in this way. The local Pisot condition implies exponential convergence under mild conditions; see Proposition
7.15
.

Recall that
\mathbb{P}^{d-1}_{\geq 0}
stands for the
nonnegative cone
of
\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
. It turns out that the domain
\widehat{X}
of the natural extension
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A})
of
(X,F,A)
is often contained in the direct product
\mathbb{P}^{d-1}_{\geq 0}{\times}\mathbb{P}^{d-1}_{\geq 0}
of nonnegative cones. Thus the following relation of
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A})
to the generalized right and left eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is often useful.

Lemma 9.13
.

Assume that
(X,F,A)
is a positive multiplicative continued fraction algorithm whose natural extension
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A})
satisfies
\widehat{X}\subset X{\times}\mathbb{P}^{d-1}_{\geq 0}
and is two-sided weakly convergent at
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
. Then
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0}^{d}{\times}\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0}^{d}
and any representative of
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}{\times}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0^{d}}
is a pair
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
, where
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{v}
are generalized right and left eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
.

Proof.

Setting
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}=(\vphantom{\!\widehat{A}}^{t}\!\widehat{A}(\widehat{F}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})))_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, the diagram (
9.16
) yields together with the definition of
\widehat{F}
in (
9.11
) that

\widehat{F}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\begin{cases}((M_{[0,n)})^{-1}\mathbf{x},\vphantom{(M_{[0,n)})}^{t}(M_{[0,n)})\mathbf{y}),&\mbox{if}\ n\geq 0,\\
(M_{[n,0)}\mathbf{x},\vphantom{(M_{[n,0)})}^{t}(M_{[n,0)})^{-1}\mathbf{y}),&\mbox{if}\ n<0.\end{cases}

Thus, setting
\widehat{F}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=(\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{n})
, we have

(9.17)

\mathbf{x}=\bigcap_{n\geq 0}M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{x}_{n}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{y}=\bigcap_{n<0}\,\vphantom{\!}^{t}\!M_{[n,0)}\mathbf{y}_{n}.

For an element
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{P}^{d-1}_{\geq 0}
let
\tilde{\mathbf{a}}
be some fixed nonnegative representative of
\tilde{\mathbf{a}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, by two-sided weak convergence of
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, (
9.17
) implies that

(9.18)

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{n\geq 0}M_{[0,n)}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{n}=\bigcap_{n\geq 0}M_{[0,n)}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\quad\text{and}\quad

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{n<0}\,\vphantom{\!}^{t}\!M_{[n,0)}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{n}=\bigcap_{n<0}\,\vphantom{\!}^{t}\!M_{[n,0)}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}.

On the other hand, by Definition
7.5
, a generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{u}
and a generalized left eigenvector
\mathbf{v}
of
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
satisfy

(9.19)

\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\mathbf{u}=\bigcap_{n\geq 0}M_{[0,n)}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\mathbf{v}=\bigcap_{n<0}\vphantom{(M_{[n,0)})}^{t}(M_{[n,0)})\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}.

By comparing (
9.18
) with (
9.19
), we see that
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
is a representative of
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}{\times}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
.
∎

A positive multidimensional continued fraction algorithm
(X,F,A)
produces one-sided sequences of nonegative matrices to which, in case of weak convergence, we can associate a generalized right eigenvector. To get a left eigenvector as well, we need to work with the natural extension of the algorithm, as shown by Lemma
9.13
.

We turn to the metric theory. The following result gives a criterion of
\boldsymbol{\psi}
being a measurable conjugacy.

Proposition 9.14
.

Let
(X,F,A,\nu)
be a positive multidimensional continued fraction algorithm. If
its natural extension
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
satisfies
\widehat{X}\subset X{\times}\mathbb{P}^{d-1}_{\geq 0}
and is two-sided weakly convergent a.e., then the mapping
\boldsymbol{\psi}
in (
9.16
) is injective and, hence,
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
is measurably conjugate to the shift
(\boldsymbol{\psi}(\widehat{X}),\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\psi}_{*}\nu)
.

Proof.

The mapping
\boldsymbol{\psi}
is obviously measurable. Assume that it is not a.e. injective.
Then a.e. weak convergence of
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
implies the existence of distinct elements
(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{1}),(\mathbf{x}_{2},\mathbf{y}_{2})\in\widehat{X}
at which
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
is weakly convergent, such that
\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{1})=\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{x}_{2},\mathbf{y}_{2})
. Hence, by Lemma
9.13
, the sequence
\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{1})=\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{x}_{2},\mathbf{y}_{2})
would have two different pairs of right and left eigenvectors

(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
and
(\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime})
, where
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
or
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
are not collinear. This is a contradiction to two sided weak convergence.
∎

Under the conditions of Proposition
9.14
, the map which associates to each sequence
\mathbf{M}\in D=\boldsymbol{\psi}(\widehat{X})
the pair of generalized eigenvectors
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
, is a.e. injective as well (in order to make this map well-defined, we need to normalize
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{v}
in some way). Thus it allows to view the domain
\widehat{X}
as a set of pairs of generalized eigenvectors. Note that the conditions of Proposition
9.14
conditions are realized for most multidimensional continued fraction algorithms, see e.g. for the Brun algorithm described below in Section
10
.

We also get that
(X,F,A,\nu)
satisfies the Pisot condition from Definition
9.9
if
(\boldsymbol{\psi}(\widehat{X}),\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\psi}_{*}\nu)
satisfies the generic Pisot condition for
A_{\mathrm{tr}}
from Definition
8.6
.

Each sequence of matrices
\mathbf{M}\in D
produced by
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A})
can be used to define the
\mathcal{M}
-adic mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}})
which performs the multidimensional continued fraction algorithm
\widehat{F}
for a suitable element of the domain
\widehat{X}
. In the cocycle fiber
\{\mathbf{M}\}{\times}\mathbb{T}^{d}
, the orbits of
\Phi_{\mathrm{tr}}
are the
\mathcal{M}
-adic mapping families of Definition
7.23
. Thus
D
parametrizes the set of mapping families and the shift on
D
performs a renormalization procedure.

For
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in D=\boldsymbol{\psi}(\widehat{X})
, the linear cocycle
A_{\mathrm{tr}}
defined in Section
8.3
acts on generalized eigenvectors by

(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})\mapsto(\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A_{\mathrm{tr}}^{(n)}(\mathbf{M})^{-1}\mathbf{u},A_{\mathrm{tr}}^{(n)}(\mathbf{M})\mathbf{v})=(M_{[0,n)}^{-1}\mathbf{u},\vphantom{(\!M}^{t}(\!M_{[0,n)}\mathbf{v})=(\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{v}_{n})\quad(n>0).

The vectors
\mathbf{u}_{n}
and
\mathbf{v}_{n}
will be used in the sequel as the vectors whose entries are the measures of the bases and heights of the
Rauzy boxes
(see Definition
12.5
), respectively. As we will see in Section
15
, these Rauzy boxes define nonstationary Markov partitions for
\mathcal{M}
-adic mapping families and, hence, for multidimensional continued fraction algorithms.

10.
Brun continued fraction algorithm

In this section, we want to apply the theory we developed in Sections
7
–
9
to a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm that goes back to Viggo Brun. We start with the unordered version
F_{\mathrm{U}}
of the Brun continued fraction algorithm in Section
10.1
. We then consider its ordered version
F_{\mathrm{B}}
in Section
10.2
. Both versions are additive, they hence rely on a finite set of matrices. The accelerated version
F_{\mathrm{M}}
of the ordered Brun algorithm is handled in Section
10.3
; it is a multiplicative algorithm, i.e., it relies
on an infinite set of matrices. Finally, Section
10.4
investigates the relation between Brun’s algorithm and the generic Pisot condition. A general description for the Brun continued fraction algorithm is as follows:
For a given vector it produces a new vector by subtracting its second largest coordinate from its largest one
.

10.1.
Unordered Brun continued fraction algorithm

We first discuss the unordered Brun continued fraction algorithm
(X_{\mathrm{U}},F_{\mathrm{U}},A_{\mathrm{U}})
, which is an additive multidimensional continued fraction algorithm. Since we will use it in later examples in the 2- and 3-dimensional case, we present this algorithm in arbitrary dimensions; for the 2-dimensional case we also refer to
[
AL18
]
, some aspects of the higher-dimensional case are discussed in
[
DHS13
]
. The algorithm
(X_{\mathrm{U}},F_{\mathrm{U}},A_{\mathrm{U}})
consists in subtracting the second largest coordinate of a given vector from the largest one, without reordering the coordinates.

In the sequel we will use the notation
\mathcal{A}=\{1,\ldots,d\}
.
We first define the sets and matrices needed for the definition of these algorithm. For
d\geq 3
, we define the subset

X_{\mathrm{U}}=\{[w_{1}:w_{2}:\cdots:w_{d}]\in\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0}^{d-1}\,:\,w_{i}\neq w_{j}\ \mbox{for}\ i\neq j\}.

If, for all
i,j\in\mathcal{A}
with
i\neq j
, we set

(10.1)

X_{\mathrm{U},ij}=\{[w_{1}:w_{2}:\cdots:w_{d}]\in\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0}^{d-1}:w_{k}\leq w_{j}\leq w_{i}\ \text{for}\ k\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{i,j\}\}.

then the collection
\{X_{\mathrm{U},ij}\colon i,j\in\mathcal{A},\,i\neq j\}
is, apart from overlaps at the boundaries, a partition of
X_{\mathrm{U}}
.

[0:1:0]
[0:0:1]
[1:0:0]
X_{\mathrm{U},32}
X_{\mathrm{U},23}
X_{\mathrm{U},13}
X_{\mathrm{U},12}
X_{\mathrm{U},21}
X_{\mathrm{U},31}

Figure 13.
The topological partition
X_{\mathrm{U}}=\bigcup_{i,j\in\{1,2,3\},\,i\neq j}X_{\mathrm{U},ij}
for the unordered Brun algorithm in two dimensions.

We also define the
(d{-}1)d
different matrices
M_{\mathrm{U},ij}
,
i,j\in\mathcal{A}
,
i\neq j
,

(10.2)

M_{\mathrm{U},ij}=I_{d}+P_{ij}\qquad(i,j\in\mathcal{A},\,i\neq j),

where
I_{d}
is the
d{\times}d
identity matrix and
P_{ij}
is the
d{\times}d
matrix having entries zero except a
1
at position
(i,j)
. We call the matrices
M_{\mathrm{U},ij}
the
unordered Brun matrices
.

Using this data, we can define our algorithm as follows.

Definition 10.1

(Unordered Brun algorithm)
.

For
d\geq 3
let

A_{{\mathrm{U}}}:\,X_{\mathrm{U}}\to\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z});\quad\mathbf{x}\mapsto\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{\mathrm{U},ij}\qquad\text{if}\ \mathbf{x}\in X_{\mathrm{U},ij}\qquad(i,j\in\mathcal{A},\,i\neq j)

with
X_{\mathrm{U},ij}
as in (
10.1
) and
M_{\mathrm{U},ij}
as in (
10.2
).
This defines the
unordered Brun continued fraction map

(10.3)

F_{\mathrm{U}}:\,X_{\mathrm{U}}\to X_{\mathrm{U}};\quad\mathbf{x}\mapsto\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A_{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbf{x})^{-1}\mathbf{x}.

Then the multidimensional continued fraction algorithm
(X_{{\mathrm{U}}},F_{{\mathrm{U}}},A_{{\mathrm{U}}})
is called the
(d{-}1)
-dimensional
unordered Brun continued fraction algorithm
or the
unordered Brun algorithm
, for short.

The map
F_{\mathrm{U}}
lives on
X_{\mathrm{U}}=\bigcup_{i,j\in\mathcal{A},\,i\neq j}X_{\mathrm{U},ij}
; see Figure
13
for the case
d=3
. It is defined as in (
9.1
) by the cocycle
20
20

20

Note that the definition is ambigous in the small overlaps of the sets
X_{\mathrm{U},ij}
. This can easily be avoided by redefining
X_{\mathrm{U},ij}
on their boundaries. Since we are mainly interested in metric properties of the algorithm, we do not care how
A_{\mathrm{U}}
is defined on these overlaps; this also holds for other algorithms.

Note that
X_{\mathrm{U},ij}=X_{{}^{t}\!{M}_{\mathrm{U},{ij}}}
are exactly the cylinder sets of length one of the unordered Brun algorithm; see Definition
9.2
. This algorithm is
symmetric
in the sense that
F_{\mathrm{U}}(\zeta\mathbf{x})=\zeta F_{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbf{x})
, where the permutation
\zeta\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}
acts on
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
naturally by
\zeta(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})=(x_{\zeta(1)},\dots,x_{\zeta(d)})
. Moreover, since
F_{\mathrm{U}}^{n}(X_{[0,n)}(\mathbf{x}))
is a union of sets of the form
X_{\mathrm{U},ij}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and each
\mathbf{x}\in X_{\mathrm{U}}
, the unordered Brun algorithm has
finite range
.

According to (
9.3
), the
affine version
of
(X_{\mathrm{U}},F_{\mathrm{U}},A_{\mathrm{U}})
is given by

(\Delta_{\mathrm{U}},T_{\mathrm{U}},A_{\mathrm{U}})
, where
\Delta_{\mathrm{U}}=\Delta^{d-1}\cap[0,1]^{d}
and

(10.4)

T_{\mathrm{U}}:\,\Delta_{\mathrm{U}}\to\Delta_{\mathrm{U}},\quad\mathbf{x}\mapsto\frac{\vphantom{\!A}{}^{t}\!A(\mathbf{x})^{-1}\mathbf{x}}{\lVert\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A(\mathbf{x})^{-1}\mathbf{x}\rVert};

recall that
\Delta_{\mathrm{U}}=\chi(X_{\mathrm{U}})
, with
\chi
being the chart defined in (
9.2
).

Our first goal is to construct a natural extension and a Gauss measure for the unordered Brun algorithm. To do this, we proceed by the strategy outlined in Section
9.2
.
In the following lemma we set up the natural extension of
(\Delta_{\mathrm{U}},T_{\mathrm{U}},A_{\mathrm{U}})
.

Lemma 10.2
.

For
i,j\in\mathcal{A}
,
i\neq j
, let

\displaystyle\Delta_{ij}

\displaystyle=\{(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{d})\in\Delta_{\mathrm{U}}\,:\,x_{k}\leq x_{j}\leq x_{i}\ \text{for all}\ k\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{i,j\}\},

\displaystyle\Delta_{ij}^{*}

\displaystyle=\{(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{d})\in\Delta_{\mathrm{U}}\,:\,y_{k}\leq y_{i}\ \text{for all}\ k\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{i,j\}\}.

Then for

(10.5)

\widehat{\Delta}_{\mathrm{U}}=\bigcup_{i,j\in\mathcal{A},\,i\neq j}\Delta_{ij}\times\Delta_{ij}^{*},

the mapping

\widehat{T}_{\mathrm{U}}:\,\widehat{\Delta}_{\mathrm{U}}\to\widehat{\Delta}_{\mathrm{U}},\quad(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\mapsto\left(\frac{\vphantom{\!A_{\mathrm{U}}}{}^{t}\!A_{\mathrm{U}}^{-1}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{x}}{\lVert\vphantom{\!A_{\mathrm{U}}}^{t}\!A_{\mathrm{U}}^{-1}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{x}\rVert},\frac{A_{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{y}}{\lVert A_{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{y}\rVert}\right)

is bijective almost everywhere w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure on
\widehat{\Delta}_{\mathrm{U}}
.

Proof.

In view of Lemma
9.5
, it suffices to prove the surjectivity of
\widehat{T}
. If
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in\Delta_{ij}
and
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{d})\in\Delta_{ij}^{*}
holds for some
i,j\in\mathcal{A}
,
i\neq j
, then

\displaystyle\vphantom{\!A_{\mathrm{U}}}{}^{t}\!A_{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbf{x})^{-1}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=M_{\mathrm{U},ij}^{-1}\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},x_{i}-x_{j},x_{i+1},\dots,x_{d})\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle A_{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle=\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{\mathrm{U},ij}\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{j-1},y_{j}+y_{i},y_{j+1},\dots,y_{d}).

This immediately implies that

(10.6)

\vphantom{\!A_{\mathrm{U}}}^{t}\!A_{\mathrm{U}}(\Delta_{ij})^{-1}\Delta_{ij}\subseteq\Delta_{ji}^{*}\quad\text{and}\quad A_{\mathrm{U}}(\Delta_{ij})\Delta_{ij}^{*}\subseteq\Delta_{ji}

(here and in the sequel we write
A_{\mathrm{U}}(\Delta_{ij})
for the matrix
A_{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbf{x})
with
\mathbf{x}\in\Delta_{ij}
; this is a slight abuse of notation).
Hence, since
\vphantom{\!M_{U,ij}}{}^{t}\!M_{U,ij}=M_{U,ji}
, one has

A_{\mathrm{U}}(\Delta_{ij})^{-1}\Delta_{ji}\subseteq\Delta_{ij}^{*}\quad\text{and}\quad\vphantom{\!A_{\mathrm{U}}}^{t}\!A_{\mathrm{U}}(\Delta_{ij})\Delta_{ji}^{*}\subseteq\Delta_{ij}.

However, together with (
10.6
), this yields

\displaystyle\Delta_{ij}

\displaystyle=\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A_{\mathrm{U}}(\Delta_{ij})\,\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A_{\mathrm{U}}(\Delta_{ij})^{-1}\Delta_{ij}\subseteq\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A_{\mathrm{U}}(\Delta_{ij})\,\Delta_{ji}^{*}\subseteq\Delta_{ij}\quad\text{and}\quad

\displaystyle\Delta_{ij}^{*}

\displaystyle=A_{\mathrm{U}}(\Delta_{ij})^{-1}\,A_{\mathrm{U}}(\Delta_{ij})\Delta_{ij}^{*}\subseteq A_{\mathrm{U}}(\Delta_{ij})^{-1}\Delta_{ji}\subseteq\Delta_{ij}^{*}.

Thus in (
10.6
) we can replace “
\subseteq
” by “
=
” to get

\vphantom{\!A}{}^{t}\!A_{\mathrm{U}}(\Delta_{ij})^{-1}\Delta_{ij}=\Delta_{ji}^{*}\quad\mbox{and}\quad A_{\mathrm{U}}(\Delta_{ij})\Delta_{ij}^{*}=\Delta_{ji}.

We therefore gain
\widehat{T}(\widehat{\Delta}_{\mathrm{U}})=\bigcup_{i,j\in\mathcal{A},\,i\neq j}\Delta_{ij}^{*}{\times}\Delta_{ij}
.
It easily follows from the definitions that
\Delta_{ij}^{*}=\Delta_{ji}\cup\bigcup_{k\in\mathcal{A},k\not=i}\Delta_{ik}
(with measure disjoint unions) for all
i,j\in\mathcal{A}
,
i\not=j
. This implies that
\Delta_{k\ell}\subset\Delta_{ij}^{*}
if and only if
\Delta_{ij}\subset\Delta_{k\ell}^{*}

(i,j,k,\ell\in\mathcal{A},\,i\neq j,\,k\neq\ell)
. Using these facts we immediately gain

\bigcup_{i,j\in\mathcal{A},\,i\neq j}\Delta_{ij}\times\Delta_{ij}^{*}=\bigcup_{i,j\in\mathcal{A},\,i\neq j}\Delta_{ij}^{*}\times\Delta_{ij},

and surjectivity of
\widehat{T}
follows.
∎

Recall from Section
9.2
that
\mathrm{d}\widehat{\mu}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda(\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathrm{d}\lambda{(\mathbf{y})}}{\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle^{d}}
is a measure on
\widehat{\Delta}_{\mathrm{U}}
.
By Lemma
10.2
,
(\widehat{\Delta}_{\mathrm{U}},\widehat{T}_{\mathrm{U}},\widehat{A}_{\mathrm{U}},\widehat{\mu})
is a natural extension of
(\Delta_{\mathrm{U}},T_{\mathrm{U}},A_{\mathrm{U}},\mu_{\mathrm{U}})
, where
\mu_{\mathrm{U}}=\pi_{*}\widehat{\mu}
with
\pi:\widehat{\Delta}\to\Delta
,
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\mapsto\mathbf{x}
is the natural projection. This can easily be transferred to
\widehat{X}
via
\chi{\times}\chi
, and we exhibit a natural extension
(\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{U}},\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{U}},\widehat{A}_{\mathrm{U}},\widehat{\nu})
of
(X_{\mathrm{U}},F_{\mathrm{U}},A_{\mathrm{U}},\nu_{\mathrm{U}})
. We have that
\widehat{\Delta}_{\mathrm{U}}
has positive Lebesgue measure by (
10.5
), and, hence,
\widehat{\nu}_{\mathrm{U}}(\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{U}})>0
(see also Remark
9.6
).

Our next aim is to give the invariant measure
\mu
explicitly.

Lemma 10.3
.

If we use the norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty}
in the definition of
\Delta
, then the invariant measure
\mu_{\mathrm{U}}
of the affine version of the unordered Brun algorithm
(T_{\mathrm{U}},\Delta_{\mathrm{U}},A_{\mathrm{U}},\mu_{\mathrm{U}})
is absolutely continuous w.r.t. the (piecewise) Lebesgue measure on
\Delta_{\mathrm{U}}
and has density

\frac{1}{x_{1}\cdots x_{i-1}x_{i+1}\cdots x_{d}}\sum_{S\subset\mathcal{A}\setminus\{i,j\}}\frac{(-1)^{|S|}}{x_{i}+\sum_{k\in S}x_{k}}

for
(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in\Delta_{ij}
. This can be transfered to an invariant measure
\nu_{\mathrm{U}}
of
F_{\mathrm{U}}
via the bijection
\chi:X_{\mathrm{U}}\to\Delta_{\mathrm{U}}
. The measures
\mu_{\mathrm{U}}
and
\nu_{\mathrm{U}}
are ergodic.

Proof.

To derive the required density, we have to integrate the density
\frac{1}{\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle^{d}}
of the invariant measure
\widehat{\mu}
of
\widehat{T}
over
\mathbf{y}
. Thus we have to compute

I=\int_{\Delta_{ij}^{*}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda{(\mathbf{y})}}{\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle^{d}}.

W.l.o.g., we can consider the case
(i,j)=(1,2)
. From the definition of
\Delta_{12}^{*}
we see that a vector
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{d})\in\Delta_{12}^{*}
satisfies
y_{1}=1
or
y_{2}=1
. According to these two alternatives, we can split
I
into a sum and gain

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\int_{y_{2}=0}^{1}\hskip-10.00002pt\cdots\hskip-1.00006pt\int_{y_{d}=0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}y_{2}\cdots\mathrm{d}y_{d}}{(x_{1}+x_{2}y_{2}+\cdots+x_{d}y_{d})^{d}}}_{\displaystyle I^{(1)}_{d}(x_{1})}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\int_{y_{1}=0}^{1}\int_{y_{3}=0}^{y_{1}}\hskip-10.00002pt\cdots\hskip-1.00006pt\int_{y_{d}=0}^{y_{1}}\frac{\mathrm{d}y_{1}\mathrm{d}y_{3}\cdots\mathrm{d}y_{d}}{(x_{1}y_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}y_{3}+\cdots+x_{d}y_{d})^{d}}}_{\displaystyle I^{(2)}_{d}(x_{1})}.

By evaluating the innermost integral of
I^{(\ell)}_{d}(x_{1})
,
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, we gain

I_{d}^{(\ell)}(x_{1})=\frac{1}{(d-1)x_{d}}(I_{d-1}^{(\ell)}(x_{1})-I_{d-1}^{(\ell)}(x_{1}+x_{d}))\qquad(\ell\in\{1,2\}).

Iterating this procedure for
d{-}2
times yields by induction that

(10.7)

I_{d}^{(\ell)}(x_{1})=\frac{1}{(d{-}1)!\,x_{3}\cdots x_{d}}\sum_{S\subset\mathcal{A}\setminus\{1,2\}}(-1)^{|S|}I_{2}^{(\ell)}\bigg(x_{1}+\sum_{k\in S}x_{k}\bigg)\quad(\ell\in\{1,2\}).

Because

\displaystyle I_{2}^{(1)}(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{y_{2}=0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}y_{2}}{(z+x_{2}y_{2})^{2}}=\frac{1}{z(x_{2}+z)},

\displaystyle I_{2}^{(2)}(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{y_{1}=0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}y_{1}}{(zy_{1}+x_{2})^{2}}=\frac{1}{x_{2}(x_{2}+z)},

and
\frac{1}{z(x_{2}+z)}+\frac{1}{x_{2}(x_{2}+z)}=\frac{1}{x_{2}z}
, the result on the density follows by inserting this in (
10.7
). Note that the constant factor
1/(d{-}1)!
is immaterial because the invariant measure is only defined up to a constant.

Ergodicity of
\mu_{\mathrm{U}}
and
\nu_{\mathrm{U}}
is established in
[
AL18
, Proposition 28]
.
∎

Let
\boldsymbol{\psi}
be defined as in (
9.15
).
Because the collection
\{X_{\mathrm{U},ij}\colon i,j\in\mathcal{A},\,i\not=j\}
form a measurable partition of
X_{\mathrm{U}}
, the diagram (
9.16
) in Section
9.4
defines a measurable conjugacy between
(\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{U}},\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{U}},\widehat{A}_{\mathrm{U}},\widehat{\nu})
and the shift
(\boldsymbol{\psi}(\widehat{X}),\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\psi}_{*}\widehat{\nu})
. It is known that the sequences in
\boldsymbol{\psi}(\widehat{X})
are characterized by a Markov condition, more precisely, we have

(10.8)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\psi}(\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{U}})

\displaystyle=\{(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,:\,(M_{n},M_{n+1})=(M_{\mathrm{U},ij},M_{\mathrm{U},ij})\text{ for }i,j\in\mathcal{A},\,i\neq j,

\displaystyle\text{or}\ (M_{n},M_{n+1})=(M_{\mathrm{U},ij},M_{\mathrm{U},jk})\text{ for }i,j,k\in\mathcal{A},\,i\neq j\neq k\};

see
[
DHS13
, Section 3]
. Via this conjugacy, we attach to each
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{U}}
the mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}})
with
\mathbf{M}=\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
.

As the following result shows, the 2-dimensional unordered Brun algorithm furnishes examples for the theory we developed in Chapters
7
and
8
. Recall that
\chi:\mathbb{P}^{d-1}\to\Delta^{d-1}
assigns to each element of
\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
a representative in
\Delta^{d-1}
. Note that the set of parameters in
X_{\mathrm{U}}
described in Proposition
10.4
has zero measure. Indeed, this statement holds for concrete sequences of matrices. For according metric results we refer to Section
10.4
.

Proposition 10.4
.

Let
(\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{U}},\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{U}},\widehat{A}_{\mathrm{U}},\widehat{\nu}_{\mathrm{U}})
be the natural extension of the 2-dimensional ordered Brun algorithm and let
\boldsymbol{\psi}
be as in (
9.15
). Suppose that for
\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
there exists
h\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(10.9)

\{M_{n},\dots,M_{n+h}\}=\{M_{\mathrm{U},ij}\,:\,i,j\in\{1,2,3\},\,i\neq j\}

for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}.
21
21

21

Note that this condition is what we called “bounded strong partial quotients” in Example
7.22
.

Then the following assertions hold.

•
