\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
satisfies the two-sided Pisot condition.

•

\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is algebraically irreducible.

•

\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
converges strongly to the generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{u}=\chi(\mathbf{x})
in the future and to the generalized left eigenvector
\mathbf{v}=\chi(\mathbf{y})
in the past. The vector
\chi(\mathbf{x})
has rationally independent coordinates.

•

The mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})})
associated to
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is eventually Anosov for the splitting

\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}_{n}\oplus\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}.

Proof.

Primitivity in the future and in the past holds because (
10.9
) implies that
M_{[n,n+h)}
is positive for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. It follows from the proof of
[
DHS13
, Theorem 2]
that
\mathbf{M}
satisfies the two-sided Pisot condition. Since there are only finitely many (namely, six) unordered Brun matrices, the growth condition
\lim_{n\to\pm\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert M_{n}\rVert=0
is again trivially satisfied, and Proposition
7.17
, Theorem
7.21
, Corollary
7.26
, and Lemma
9.13
imply the lemma.
∎

10.2.
Ordered Brun continued fraction algorithm

Let us now turn to the ordered version of the Brun continued fraction algorithm. This is the classical version of this algorithm. Indeed, the 2-dimensional case of the ordered Brun algorithm goes back to
[
Bru19
,
Bru20
,
Bru58
]
. Let
d\geq 3
be fixed. We obtain the ordered algorithm from the unordered one by taking the quotient by the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{d}
of
d
elements, so the ordered algorithm is a
d!
-to-
1
factor of the unordered one. This fact will allow us to immediately derive its invariant measure form the one of the unordered version.

For
d\geq 3
, we define

(10.10)

X_{\mathrm{B}}=\{[w_{1}:w_{2}:\cdots:w_{d}]\in\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0}^{d-1}\,:\,w_{1}\leq w_{2}\leq\cdots\leq w_{d}\},

and its affine version will be defined on

\Delta_{\mathrm{B}}:=\{(x_{1},\ldots x_{d-1},1)\in[0,1]^{d}\,:\,x_{1}\leq x_{2}\leq\cdots\leq x_{d-1}\}.

Let
\mathrm{ord}:\mathbb{P}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
be the
coordinate ordering map
that assigns to each
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
the element
\mathrm{ord}(\mathbf{w})
of
\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
that emerges from
\mathbf{w}
by ordering its coordinates ascendingly. It will cause no confusion that we use the same name also for the coordinate ordering map on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Because
\zeta\circ F_{\mathrm{U}}=F_{\mathrm{U}}\circ\zeta
and
\zeta\circ T_{\mathrm{U}}=T_{\mathrm{U}}\circ\zeta
holds for each
\zeta\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}
, the maps
F_{\mathrm{B}}
and
T_{\mathrm{B}}
are well-defined by the following commutative diagrams.

(10.11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathrm{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathrm{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathrm{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathrm{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\mathrm{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ord}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ord}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\mathrm{B}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\mathrm{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\mathrm{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\mathrm{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\mathrm{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\mathrm{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ord}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ord}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\mathrm{B}}}

Note that
F_{\mathrm{B}}
subtracts the second largest coordinate from the largest one and reorders the result, i.e.,
F_{\mathrm{B}}
performs the operation

[w_{1}:\cdots:w_{d}]\mapsto\mathrm{ord}[w_{1}:\cdots:w_{d-1}:w_{d}{-}w_{d-1}].

Since
w_{d}{-}w_{d-1}
is the only element that is out of order after the subtraction, this element has to be inserted in the appropriate position. Because there are
d
available positions, there are
d
different
ordered Brun matrices
that correspond to the definition of the mapping
F_{\mathrm{B}}
. These
d{\times}d
matrices are defined by

(10.12)

\displaystyle M_{\mathrm{B},k}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&&&&&&&\\
&\ddots&&&&&&\\
&&1&&&&&\\
&&&0&1&&&\\
&&&&\ddots&\ddots&&\\
&&&&&0&1&\\
&&&1&&&1&\end{pmatrix}\text{$\leftarrow$ $k$-th row}\qquad(k\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{d\})

\displaystyle\uparrow

\displaystyle\text{$k$-th column}

and

(10.13)

M_{\mathrm{B},d}=\begin{pmatrix}1&&&\\
&\ddots&&\\
&&1&\\
&&1&1\end{pmatrix}.

For
d=3
, one gets for instance that

(10.14)

M_{\mathrm{B},1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&1\\
1&0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad M_{\mathrm{B},2}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&0&1\\
0&1&1\end{pmatrix},\quad M_{\mathrm{B},3}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&1&1\end{pmatrix}.

One checks immediately that the
d
sets

(10.15)

X_{\mathrm{B},k}=M_{\mathrm{B},k}X_{\mathrm{B}}\subset X_{\mathrm{B}}\qquad(k\in\mathcal{A})

form a partition of
X_{\mathrm{B}}
up to a set of measure zero; see Figure
14
for the case
d=3
.

[1:1:1]
[0:0:1]
[0:1:1]
[1:1:2]
[0:1:2]
X_{\mathrm{B},1}
X_{\mathrm{B},3}
X_{\mathrm{B},2}

Figure 14.
The topological partition
X_{\mathrm{B}}=X_{\mathrm{B},1}\cup X_{\mathrm{B},2}\cup X_{\mathrm{B},3}
for the 2-dimensional ordered Brun algorithm.

By these considerations, the mapping
F_{\mathrm{B}}
defined in (
10.11
) fits into the general definition of a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm given in Definition
9.1
. In particular, we may rewrite
F_{\mathrm{B}}
by using the cocycle
A_{\mathrm{B}}
as in the following definition.

Definition 10.5

(Ordered Brun algorithm)
.

For
d\geq 3
let
22
22

22

As before, there is ambiguity in the definition of
A_{\mathrm{B}}
on the overlap of the sets
X_{\mathrm{B},k}
and we can define
A_{\mathrm{B}}
on these overlaps by any of the possible choices.

A_{\mathrm{B}}:\,X_{\mathrm{B}}\to\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z}),\quad\mathbf{x}\mapsto\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{\mathrm{B},k}\qquad\text{if}\ \mathbf{x}\in X_{\mathrm{B},k}\qquad(k\in\mathcal{A}).

with
X_{\mathrm{B},k}
as in (
10.15
) and
M_{\mathrm{B},ij}
as in (
10.12
) and (
10.13
). This defines the
ordered Brun continued fraction map

(10.16)

F_{\mathrm{B}}:\,X_{\mathrm{B}}\to X_{\mathrm{B}};\quad\mathbf{x}\mapsto\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathbf{x})^{-1}\mathbf{x}.

Then the multidimensional continued fraction algorithm
(X_{{\mathrm{B}}},F_{{\mathrm{B}}},A_{{\mathrm{B}}})
is called the
(d{-}1)
-dimensional
ordered Brun continued fraction algorithm
or the
ordered Brun algorithm
, for short.

This algorithm is additive because it is defined by a finite family of matrices. Again,
X_{\mathrm{B},k}=X_{\vphantom{\!M}{}^{t}\!M_{\mathrm{B},k}}
,
k\in\mathcal{A}
, are the cylinder sets of the ordered Brun algorithm; see Definition
9.2
. Moreover, because
F_{\mathrm{B}}(X_{\mathrm{B},k})=X_{\mathrm{B}}
holds for each
k\in\mathcal{A}
, the ordered Brun algorithm is a
full
algorithm.

The
affine version

(\Delta_{\mathrm{B}},T_{\mathrm{B}},A_{\mathrm{B}})
of the ordered Brun algorithm can now be defined according to (
9.3
). In particular, since we used the norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty}
in the definition of
\Delta_{\mathrm{B}}
, the chart
\chi:X_{\mathrm{B}}\to\Delta^{d-1}
yields that

T_{\mathrm{B}}\big(\tfrac{w_{1}}{w_{d}},\dots,\tfrac{w_{d-1}}{w_{d}}\big)=\big(\tfrac{w_{1}^{\prime}}{w_{d}^{\prime}},\dots,\tfrac{w_{d-1}^{\prime}}{w_{d}^{\prime}}\big)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ F_{\mathrm{B}}(w_{1},\dots,w_{d})=(w_{1}^{\prime},\dots,w_{d}^{\prime}).

If we identify
\Delta_{\mathrm{B}}
with
\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in[0,1]^{2}\,:\,x_{1}\leq x_{2}\}
in the 2-dimensional case
d=3
, the mapping
T_{\mathrm{B}}:\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}\to\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}
is defined explicitly by

(10.17)

T_{\mathrm{B}}:\,(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto\begin{cases}\big(\frac{x_{1}}{1-x_{2}},\frac{x_{2}}{1-x_{2}}\big)&\hbox{if}\ x_{2}\leq\frac{1}{2},\\[2.15277pt]
\big(\frac{x_{1}}{x_{2}},\frac{1-x_{2}}{x_{2}}\big)&\hbox{if}\ \frac{1}{2}\leq x_{2}\leq 1{-}x_{1},\\[2.15277pt]
\big(\frac{1-x_{2}}{x_{2}},\frac{x_{1}}{x_{2}}\big)&\hbox{if}\ 1{-}x_{1}\leq x_{2}.\end{cases}

This is the classical continued fraction algorithm of Brun going back to
[
Bru19
,
Bru20
,
Bru58
]
.

Let us define a set
\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{B}}
for the geometric version of the natural extension of the ordered Brun algorithm. Let

(10.18)

X_{\mathrm{B}}^{*}=\{[w_{1}:\cdots:w_{d}]\in\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0}^{d-1}\,:\,w_{1},\dots,w_{d-2}\leq w_{d}\}

and define the subsets

\displaystyle X_{\mathrm{B},k}^{*}

\displaystyle=\{[w_{1}:\cdots:w_{d}]\in\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0}^{d-1}\,:\,w_{1},\dots,w_{d-1}\leq w_{k}\leq w_{d}\}\quad(1\leq k\leq d{-}2),

\displaystyle X_{\mathrm{B},d-1}^{*}

\displaystyle=\{[w_{1}:\cdots:w_{d}]\in\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0}^{d-1}\,:\,w_{1},\dots,w_{d-2}\leq w_{d-1}\leq w_{d}\},

\displaystyle X_{\mathrm{B},d}^{*}

\displaystyle=\{[w_{1}:\cdots:w_{d}]\in\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0}^{d-1}\,:\,w_{1},\dots,w_{d-2}\leq w_{d}\leq w_{d-1}\}.

For each
k\in\mathcal{A}
, one readily checks that
X_{\mathrm{B},k}^{*}=\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{\mathrm{B},k}X_{\mathrm{B}}^{*}
. Moreover, the collection
\{X_{\mathrm{B},k}^{*}:k\in\mathcal{A}\}
forms a topological partition of
X_{\mathrm{B}}^{*}
, and the map
\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{B}}
defined on
X_{\mathrm{B}}{\times}X_{\mathrm{B}}^{*}
by
\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=(\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathbf{x})^{-1}\mathbf{x},A_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{y})
sends
X_{\mathrm{B},k}{\times}X_{\mathrm{B}}^{*}
to
X_{\mathrm{B}}{\times}X_{\mathrm{B},k}^{*}
, so
\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{B}}
is a one-to-one map from
\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{B}}=X_{\mathrm{B}}{\times}X_{\mathrm{B}}^{*}
to itself.
Thus, with the appropriate measure
\widehat{\nu}_{\mathrm{B}}
chosen according to Section
9.2
, we have found a natural extension
(\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{B}},\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{B}},\widehat{A}_{\mathrm{B}},\widehat{\nu}_{\mathrm{B}})
of
(X_{\mathrm{B}},F_{\mathrm{B}},A_{\mathrm{B}},\nu_{\mathrm{B}})
.

However, we do not need this natural extension in order to get the Gauss measure
\nu_{\mathrm{B}}
for the ordered Brun algorithm. Indeed, from Lemma
10.3
, we immediately obtain the density of the invariant measure of the ordered Brun algorithm; compare this with the equivalent formula for the density in
[
AN93
]
.

Lemma 10.6
.

The invariant measure
\mu_{\mathrm{B}}
of the affine version of the ordered Brun algorithm
(T_{\mathrm{B}},\Delta_{\mathrm{B}},A_{\mathrm{B}},\mu_{\mathrm{B}})
is absolutely continuous w.r.t. the (piecewise) Lebesgue measure on
\Delta_{\mathrm{B}}
and has density
23
23

23

We checked for small values of
d
that this formula coincides with the one in
[
AN93
, p. 661]
.

(10.19)

\frac{1}{x_{1}\cdots x_{d-1}}\sum_{S\subset\{1,\dots,d-2\}}\frac{(-1)^{|S|}}{1+\sum_{k\in S}x_{k}}.

Via the bijection
\chi:X_{\mathrm{B}}\to\Delta_{\mathrm{B}}
, this provides an invariant measure
\nu_{\mathrm{B}}
of
F_{\mathrm{B}}
. The measures
\mu_{\mathrm{B}}
and
\nu_{\mathrm{B}}
are ergodic.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of (
10.11
) and Lemma
10.3
. Indeed, we pushforward the invariant measure of the unordered case via
\mu_{\mathrm{B}}=\mathrm{ord}_{*}\mu_{\mathrm{U}}
. The result follows because
\zeta\circ T_{\mathrm{U}}=T_{\mathrm{U}}\circ\zeta
for each
\zeta\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}
and, hence, the measure
\mu_{\mathrm{U}}
is invariant under the action of
\mathfrak{S}_{d}
on
\Delta_{\mathrm{U}}
.

Ergodicity of the measures
\mu_{\mathrm{B}}
and
\nu_{\mathrm{B}}
follows from
[
Sch00
, Theorem 21]
.
∎

We get the invariant measure of the classical case as a corollary.

Corollary 10.7

(cf.
[
AN93
, p. 646]
)
.

The classical 2-dimensional Brun continued fraction algorithm
T_{\mathrm{B}}:\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}\to\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}
defined in (
10.17
) has an invariant probability measure
\mu_{\mathrm{B},2}
that is absolutely continuous w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure and has density

\frac{12}{\pi^{2}x_{2}(1+x_{1})}.

Proof.

To gain the result, put
d=3
in (
10.19
). The renormalization factor
\frac{12}{\pi^{2}}
is a consequence of
\int_{1\leq x_{1}\leq x_{2}\leq 1}\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}(1+x_{1})}=\frac{\pi^{2}}{12}
.
∎

Because the sets
X_{\mathrm{B},k}
form a partition of
X_{\mathrm{B}}
, the diagram in (
9.16
) defines a (measurable) conjugacy between the multidimensional continued fraction algorithm
(\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{B}},\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{B}},\widehat{A}_{\mathrm{B}},\widehat{\nu})
and the shift
(\{M_{\mathrm{B},1},\dots,M_{\mathrm{B},d}\}^{\mathbb{Z}},\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\psi}_{*}\widehat{\nu})
. Indeed, it follows from the definition, that
\boldsymbol{\psi}(\widehat{X})=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\{M_{\mathrm{B},1},\dots,M_{\mathrm{B},d}\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. Thus
\boldsymbol{\psi}(\widehat{X})
is the full shift which is equivalent to the fact that the algorithm is full.

Also this algorithm serves as an example for the theory we developed in Chapters
7
and
8
. Indeed, we gain the following result.

Proposition 10.8
.

Let
(\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{B}},\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{B}},\widehat{A}_{\mathrm{B}},\widehat{\nu}_{\mathrm{B}})
be the natural extension of the 2-dimensional ordered Brun algorithm and let
\boldsymbol{\psi}
be as in (
9.15
). Let
\tilde{M}
be a product of the 2-dimensional Brun ordered matrices from (
10.14
) that contains
M_{\mathrm{B},3}
. Suppose that for
\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
there exists
h\in\mathbb{N}
such that
M_{[n,n+h)}=\tilde{M}^{3}
occurs with bounded gaps in
\mathbf{M}
. Then the following assertions hold.

•

\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
satisfies the two-sided Pisot condition.

•

\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is algebraically irreducible.

•

\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
converges strongly to the generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{u}=\chi(\mathbf{x})
in the future and to the generalized left eigenvector
\mathbf{v}=\chi(\mathbf{y})
in the past. The vector
\chi(\mathbf{x})
has rationally independent coordinates.

•

The mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})})
associated to
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is eventually Anosov for the splitting

\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}_{n}\oplus\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}.

Proof.

It follows from
[
AD19
, Section 5]
that
\mathbf{M}
is two-sided primitive and satisfies the two-sided Pisot condition. Since there are only finitely many (namely, three) ordered Brun matrices, the growth condition
\lim_{n\to\pm\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert M_{n}\rVert=0
is trivially satisfied. Thus, Proposition
7.17
, Theorem
7.21
, Corollary
7.26
, and Lemma
9.13
imply the lemma.
∎

For according metric results we again refer to Section
10.4
.

10.3.
Multiplicative acceleration of the Brun continued fraction algorithm

We now study an accelerated version of the ordered Brun algorithm.
24
24

24

The unordered Brun algorithm can be accelerated in a similar way but we do not give the details of this acceleration.
This algorithm, which is often called modified Jacobi–Perron algorithm,
was first studied in
[
Pod77
]
and further examined for instance in
[
FIKO96
,
Sch98
,
Mee99
,
Sch01
,
Har02
,
HK02
]
. Because it is a multiplicative algorithm, it is also known as multiplicative Brun algorithm.

Assume that
d\geq 3
and let
X_{\mathrm{B}}
and
X_{\mathrm{B},k}
,
k\in\mathcal{A}
, be given as in (
10.10
) and (
10.15
), respectively. Set
X_{\mathrm{M}}=X_{\mathrm{B}}
and
\Delta_{\mathrm{M}}=\Delta_{\mathrm{B}}
.
We now use the
jump transformation
(in the sense of
[
Sch75
]
)

(10.20)

j:X_{\mathrm{M}}\to\mathbb{N};\quad\mathbf{x}\mapsto\min\{m\geq 1\,:\,F_{\mathrm{B}}^{m-1}(\mathbf{x})\not\in X_{\mathrm{B},d}\}

on the ordered Brun algorithm
(X_{\mathrm{B}},F_{\mathrm{B}},A_{\mathrm{B}})
, i.e., we define the mappings (cf. e.g.
[
BST21
]
)

(10.21)

\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{M}}:\,X_{\mathrm{M}}\to X_{\mathrm{M}},\quad\mathbf{x}\mapsto F_{\mathrm{B}}^{j(\mathbf{x})}(\mathbf{x}),

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{M}}:\,\Delta_{\mathrm{M}}\to\Delta_{\mathrm{M}},\quad\mathbf{x}\mapsto T_{\mathrm{B}}^{j(\mathbf{x})}(\mathbf{x}).

The expected value of
j
, which is the expected value of the partial quotients of the multiplicative Brun algorithm considered in
[
BLV18
, Theorem 1 (d)]
, is finite by this theorem. This implies that
j(\mathbf{x})
is a well-defined positive integer for Lebesgue a.e.
\mathbf{x}\in X_{M}
and, hence, the mappings
F_{\mathrm{M}}
and
T_{\mathrm{M}}
are defined Lebesgue a.e. on their respective domains. Observe that the notion of a jump transformation is reminiscent from the notion of induction from Definition
8.7
.

Note that
F_{\mathrm{M}}
is related to the dynamical system let
(X_{\mathrm{B}}\setminus X_{\mathrm{B},d},G,\nu_{\mathrm{B}}|_{X_{\mathrm{B}}\setminus X_{\mathrm{B},d}})
obtained by inducing
(X_{\mathrm{B}},F_{\mathrm{B}},\nu_{\mathrm{B}})
on
X_{\mathrm{B}}\setminus X_{\mathrm{B},d}
in the sense of Definition
8.7
. From the definition of
j
we see that the diagram

(10.22)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathrm{B}}\setminus X_{\mathrm{B},d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathrm{B}}\setminus X_{\mathrm{B},d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathrm{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathrm{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\mathrm{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\mathrm{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\mathrm{M}}}

commutes.

For
1\leq k<d
and
m\geq 1
, define the
multiplicative Brun matrices
(the matrices
M_{\mathrm{B},k}
are defined in (
10.12
) for
k\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{d\}
and in (
10.13
) for
k=d
)

(10.23)

\displaystyle M_{\mathrm{M},k,m}=M_{\mathrm{B},d}^{m-1}M_{\mathrm{B},k}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&&&&&&&\\
&\ddots&&&&&&\\
&&1&&&&&\\
&&&0&1&&&\\
&&&&\ddots&\ddots&&\\
&&&&&0&1&\\
&&&1&&&m&\end{pmatrix}\text{$\leftarrow$ $k$-th row}

\displaystyle\uparrow

\displaystyle\text{$k$-th column}

and the sets

(10.24)

X_{\mathrm{M},k,m}=\{[w_{1}:\cdots:w_{d}]\,:\,[w_{1}:\cdots:w_{d-1}:w_{d}-(m{-}1)w_{d-1}]\in X_{\mathrm{B},k}\}.

These sets form a measurable partition of
X_{\mathrm{M}}
.

By these considerations, the mapping
F_{\mathrm{M}}
defined in (
10.21
) fits into the general definition of a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm given in Definition
9.1
. In particular, we may rewrite
F_{\mathrm{M}}
(Lebesgue a.e.) by using the cocycle
A_{\mathrm{M}}
as follows.

Definition 10.9

(Modified Jacobi–Perron or multiplicative Brun algorithm)
.

For
d\geq 3
let
25
25

25

As before, there is ambiguity in the definition of
A_{\mathrm{B}}
on the overlap of the sets
X_{\mathrm{B},k}
and we can define
A_{\mathrm{B}}
on these overlaps by any of the possible choices.

A_{\mathrm{M}}:\,X_{\mathrm{M}}\to\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z}),\quad\mathbf{x}\mapsto\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{\mathrm{M},k,m}\quad\text{if}\ \mathbf{x}\in X_{\mathrm{M},k,m}\qquad(1\leq k<d,\,m\geq 1).

with
X_{\mathrm{M},k,m}
as in (
10.24
) and
M_{\mathrm{M},k,m}
as in (
10.23
). This defines the
modified Jacobi–Perron map

(10.25)

F_{\mathrm{M}}:\,X_{\mathrm{M}}\to X_{\mathrm{M}};\quad\mathbf{x}\mapsto\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathbf{x})^{-1}\mathbf{x}.

Then the multidimensional continued fraction algorithm
(X_{{\mathrm{B}}},F_{{\mathrm{B}}},A_{{\mathrm{B}}})
is called the
(d{-}1)
-dimensional
modified Jacobi–Perron algorithm
or the
multiplicative Brun algorithm
.

Again,
X_{M,k,m}=X_{\vphantom{\!M}{}^{t}\!M_{\mathrm{M},k,m}}
,
1\leq k<d
,
m\geq 1
, are the cylinder sets of the multiplicative Brun algorithm; see Definition
9.2
. Since they are all mapped to
X_{\mathrm{M}}
by
F_{\mathrm{M}}
, it is a
full
algorithm.

If we define
\Delta_{\mathrm{M}}
using the norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty}
, which we will do in this section, we get the following explicit representation of the affine version of the modified Jacobi–Perron algorithm (see also
[
HK02
]
; we suppress the last coordinate
x_{d}=1
and set
x_{0}=0
):

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{M}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d-1})=

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{x_{1}}{x_{d-1}},\dots,\frac{x_{k-1}}{x_{d-1}},\frac{1}{x_{d-1}}-\Big\lfloor\frac{1}{x_{d-1}}\Big\rfloor,\frac{x_{k}}{x_{d-1}},\dots,\frac{x_{d-2}}{x_{d-1}}\Big)

\displaystyle\hbox{if}\quad\frac{x_{k-1}}{x_{d-1}}\leq\frac{1}{x_{d-1}}-\Big\lfloor\frac{1}{x_{d-1}}\Big\rfloor\leq\frac{x_{k}}{x_{d-1}}\qquad(1\leq k<d).

It is not hard to see that
F_{\mathrm{M}}
is bijective on
\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{M}}=X_{\mathrm{M}}{\times}X_{\mathrm{M}}^{*}
with

(10.26)

X_{\mathrm{M}}^{*}=\{[w_{1}:\cdots:w_{d}]\,:\,w_{1},\dots,w_{d-1}\leq w_{d}\}.

Thus, using the method developed in Section
9.2
, we get a natural extension
(\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{M}},\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{M}},\widehat{A}_{\mathrm{M}},\widehat{\nu}_{\mathrm{M}})
of
(X_{\mathrm{M}},F_{\mathrm{M}},A_{\mathrm{M}},\nu_{\mathrm{M}})
. Again, we are able to come up with an explicit formula for the invariant measure; see
[
AN93
, p. 661]
for a different expression for the density in (
10.27
).

Lemma 10.10
.

The invariant measure
\mu_{\mathrm{M}}
of the affine version of the modified Jacobi–Perron algorithm
(T_{\mathrm{M}},\Delta_{\mathrm{M}},A_{\mathrm{M}},\mu_{\mathrm{M}})
is absolutely continuous w.r.t. the (piecewise) Lebesgue measure on
\Delta_{\mathrm{M}}
and has density

(10.27)

\frac{1}{x_{1}\cdots x_{d-1}}\sum_{S\subset\{1,\dots,d-1\}}\frac{(-1)^{|S|}}{1+\sum_{k\in S}x_{k}}.

Via the bijection
\chi:X_{\mathrm{M}}\to\Delta_{\mathrm{M}}
, this provides an invariant measure
\nu_{\mathrm{M}}
of
F_{\mathrm{M}}
. The measures
\mu_{\mathrm{M}}
and
\nu_{\mathrm{M}}
are ergodic.

Remark 10.11
.

In
[
AN93
]
, the density of the measure
\mu_{\mathrm{M}}
was represented by the formula

(10.28)

\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}}\prod_{i=1}^{d-1}\frac{1}{1+x_{\sigma(1)}+\cdots+x_{\sigma(i)}},

where
\mathfrak{S}_{d}
is the group of permutations on
n
elements. One can show that the representations (
10.27
) and (
10.28
) are equal.
26
26

26

Note that, a priori, the density of
\mu_{\mathrm{M}}
is only determined up to a constant factor.
However, the representation in (
10.27
) is simpler in the sense that the number of subsets of a set with
d{-}1
elements is much smaller than
\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}
for large
d
.

Proof.

By using affine coordinates we see from (
10.26
) that the natural extension of
T_{\mathrm{M}}
is defined on
\Delta_{\mathrm{M}}{\times}[0,1]^{d-1}{\times}\{1\}
. According to Section
9.2
, to derive the required density we have to compute

I_{d}=\int_{0\leq y_{1},\ldots,y_{d-1}\leq 1}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda{(\mathbf{y})}}{\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle^{d}}.

Using the evaluation of
I_{d}^{(1)}(x_{1})
in the proof of Lemma
10.3
and partial fraction decomposition, we gain

\displaystyle I_{d}

\displaystyle=\int_{y_{1}=0}^{1}\cdots\int_{y_{d-1}=0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}y_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}y_{d-1}}{(x_{1}y_{1}+\cdots+x_{d-1}y_{d-1}+1)^{d}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(d{-}1)!\,x_{1}\cdots x_{d-2}}\sum_{S\subset\{1,\dots,d-2\}}\frac{(-1)^{|S|}}{\big(1+x_{d-1}+\sum_{k\in S}x_{k}\big)\big(1+\sum_{k\in S}x_{k}\big)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(d{-}1)!\,x_{1}\cdots x_{d-1}}\sum_{S\subset\{1,\dots,d-1\}}\frac{(-1)^{|S|}}{1+\sum_{k\in S}x_{k}}.

Since the constant factor
(d{-}1)!^{-1}
is immaterial because the invariant measure is only defined up to a constant, the result follows.

Ergodicity of the measures
\mu_{\mathrm{M}}
and
\nu_{\mathrm{M}}
follows from
[
Sch00
, Theorem 21]
.
∎

Let
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{M}}=\{M_{\mathrm{M},k,m}:1\leq k<d,\,m\geq 1\}
.
Again the sets
X_{\mathrm{M},k,m}
,
1\leq k<d
,
m\geq 1
, form a partition of
X_{\mathrm{M}}
. Thus the diagram (
9.16
) in Section
9.4
defines a measurable conjugacy between
(\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{M}},\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{M}},\widehat{A}_{\mathrm{M}},\widehat{\nu})
and
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathbb{Z}},\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\psi}_{*}\widehat{\nu})
. Indeed, as in the additive ordered case, we have
\boldsymbol{\psi}(\widehat{X})=\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.

We did not work out concrete examples of our theory from Chapters
7
and
8
for this multiplicative version of Brun’s algorithm. However, we will see in Section
10.4
that the 2- and 3-dimensional case of all versions of Brun’s algorithm covered so far furnish examples for the metric theory developed in that chapters.

10.4.
Pisot condition for the Brun continued fraction algorithm

In this section, we provide results on the relation between Brun’s algorithm and the generic Pisot condition of Definition
9.9
. First we show that it suffices to establish the generic Pisot condition for one of the three versions of Brun’s continued fraction algorithm.

Proposition 10.12
.

If one of the
(d{-}1)
-dimensional continued fraction algorithms
(X_{\mathrm{U}},F_{\mathrm{U}},A_{\mathrm{U}},\nu_{\mathrm{U}})
,
(X_{\mathrm{B}},F_{\mathrm{B}},A_{\mathrm{B}},\nu_{\mathrm{B}})
, and
(X_{\mathrm{M}},F_{\mathrm{M}},A_{\mathrm{M}},\nu_{\mathrm{M}})
satisfies the Pisot condition for a given
d\geq 3
, then the other two satisfy the Pisot condition for this
d
as well.

Proof.

Let
d\geq 3
be fixed. By (
10.11
), it is immediate that
(X_{\mathrm{U}},F_{\mathrm{U}},A_{\mathrm{U}},\nu_{\mathrm{U}})
satisfies the Pisot condition if and only if
(X_{\mathrm{B}},F_{\mathrm{B}},A_{\mathrm{B}},\nu_{\mathrm{B}})
satisfies it. It remains to deal with
(X_{\mathrm{M}},F_{\mathrm{M}},A_{\mathrm{M}},\nu_{\mathrm{M}})
, which is a multiplicative acceleration of
(X_{\mathrm{B}},F_{\mathrm{B}},A_{\mathrm{B}},\nu_{\mathrm{B}})
that is obtained by the jump transformation
j
defined in (
10.20
).
In view of Remark
9.10
, it suffices to show that
(\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{B}},\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{B}},\widehat{A}_{\mathrm{B}},\widehat{\nu}_{\mathrm{B}})
satisfies the Pisot condition if and only if
(\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{M}},\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{M}},\widehat{A}_{\mathrm{M}},\widehat{\nu}_{\mathrm{M}})
does. Let

\widehat{Y}=\{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{B}}\colon\mathbf{x}\in X_{\mathrm{B}}\setminus X_{\mathrm{B},d}\}

and let
(\widehat{Y},\widehat{G},\widehat{B},\widehat{\nu}_{\mathrm{B}}|_{\widehat{Y}})
be the dynamical system that we obtain by inducing the system
(\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{B}},\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{B}},\widehat{A}_{\mathrm{B}},\widehat{\nu}_{\mathrm{B}})
on
\widehat{Y}
in the sense of Definition
8.7
. From the definition of
j
, we see that (compare (
10.22
))

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{M}}}

defines a measurable conjugacy. Thus we may apply Lemma
8.8
to see that the Lyapunov exponents of
(\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{M}},\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{M}},\widehat{A}_{\mathrm{M}},\widehat{\nu}_{\mathrm{M}})
are obtained from the Lyapunov exponents from
(\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{B}},\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{B}},\widehat{A}_{\mathrm{B}},\widehat{\nu}_{\mathrm{B}})
by multiplication by a constant
c\geq 1
. In fact, this constant
c
is the expected value of
j
which is finite by
[
BLV18
, Theorem 1 (d)]
. Thus
(X_{\mathrm{M}},F_{\mathrm{M}},A_{\mathrm{M}},\nu_{\mathrm{M}})
satisfies the Pisot condition if and only if the same is true for
(X_{\mathrm{B}},F_{\mathrm{B}},A_{\mathrm{B}},\nu_{\mathrm{B}})
.
∎

This result allows us to show that each version of the Brun continued fraction algorithm satisfies the Pisot condition in low dimensions
d
.

Proposition 10.13
.

For
d\in\{3,4\}
, each of the
(d{-}1)
-dimensional continued fraction algorithms
(X_{\mathrm{U}},F_{\mathrm{U}},A_{\mathrm{U}},\nu_{\mathrm{U}})
,
(X_{\mathrm{B}},F_{\mathrm{B}},A_{\mathrm{B}},\nu_{\mathrm{B}})
, and
(X_{\mathrm{M}},F_{\mathrm{M}},A_{\mathrm{M}},\nu_{\mathrm{M}})
satisfies the Pisot condition. In other words, each of these algorithms is a.e. exponentially convergent in the future and in the past. Moreover for each of these algorithms there is a cylinder of positive measure corresponding to a positive matrix.

Proof.

For
d=3
, it is proved in
[
AD19
]
that
(X_{\mathrm{B}},F_{\mathrm{B}},A_{\mathrm{B}},\nu_{\mathrm{B}})
satisfies the Pisot condition; a proof for
(X_{\mathrm{M}},F_{\mathrm{M}},A_{\mathrm{M}},\nu_{\mathrm{M}})
is contained in
[
Sch98
]
. For
d=4
, the Pisot condition for
(X_{\mathrm{M}},F_{\mathrm{M}},A_{\mathrm{M}},\nu_{\mathrm{M}})
is proved in
[
Sch01
]
; see also
[
HK00
,
Har02
]
. Thus the result on the Pisot condition follows from Proposition
10.12
.
It is easy to see that there exist finite blocks that are positive. The corresponding cylinders have positive measure by equivalence of the invariant measure with the Lebesgue measure.
∎

Proposition
10.13
shows that the conditions of Theorem
8.15
are satisfied for the Brun algorithms
(X_{\mathrm{U}},F_{\mathrm{U}},A_{\mathrm{U}},\nu_{\mathrm{U}})
,
(X_{\mathrm{B}},F_{\mathrm{B}},A_{\mathrm{B}},\nu_{\mathrm{B}})
, and
(X_{\mathrm{M}},F_{\mathrm{M}},A_{\mathrm{M}},\nu_{\mathrm{M}})
if
d\in\{3,4\}
. Thus the conclusions of this theorem hols for a.e. sequence of matrices produced by these algorithms.

Chapter IV
Sequences of substitutions and Rauzy fractals

We now consider bi-infinite sequences of substitutions and their associated dynamical systems. These sequences form a refinement of the sequences of nonnegative integer matrices that we studied so far. Indeed, one can obtain a sequence of substitutions by superimposing a combinatorial structure on a sequence of matrices. In Section
11
, we deal with single bi-infinite sequences of substitutions. Section
12
is devoted to Rauzy fractals and to Rauzy boxes, which are defined as suspensions of Rauzy fractals. These objects are used to give a geometric interpretation of a sequence of substitutions. Finally, the metric viewpoint for shift invariant sets of bi-infinite sequences of substitutions will be treated in Section
13
.

11.
Bi-infinite sequences of substitutions

This section is the analog of Section
7
for sequences of substitutions. In particular, we define
\mathcal{S}
-adic shifts
by using sequences of substitutions. After setting up the basic terminology in Section
11.1
, we see in Section
11.2
that most of the properties we defined for sequences of matrices naturally carry over to sequences of substitutions by looking at
incidence matrices
. However, there is a new property that makes sense only in the context of substitutions, namely
balance
. As we will see in Section
11.3
, besides the Pisot condition, also balance can be used to formulate a criterion for strong convergence in the setting of sequences of substitutions. In Section
11.4
we will establish some spectral properties of
\mathcal{S}
-adic shifts, and Section
11.5
is devoted to
\mathcal{S}
-adic mapping families. In Section
11.6
we show how we can associate
\mathcal{S}
-adic shifts to multidimensional continued fraction algorithms. This is of great importance later because the extra structure furnished by sequences of substitutions is needed in order to define the atoms of nonstationary Markov partitions, that we will associate with multidimensional continued fraction algorithms. Finally, in Section
11.7
we relate
\mathcal{S}
-adic shifts to the Brun algorithm.

11.1.
Languages and
\mathcal{S}
-adic shifts

For
d\geq 2
let
\mathcal{A}=\{1,2,\dots,d\}
be a finite alphabet, and let
\mathcal{A}^{*}
be the set of finite words over
\mathcal{A}
, which is a free monoid with respect to concatenation of words. Moreover, we denote by
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}}
the set of two-sided infinite sequences over
\mathcal{A}
, equipped with the product topology of the discrete topology on
\mathcal{A}
.
For a word
w\in\mathcal{A}^{*}
, we denote by
|w|
the number of letters of
w
, i.e., the
length
of
w
, and by
|w|_{a}
,
a\in\mathcal{A}
, the number of occurrences of the letter
a
in
w
. A word
v
is a
factor
of a word
w
if there exist words
p,s
such that
w=pvs
. Moreover, if
p
is the empty word, then
v
is a
prefix
of
w
, which is denoted by
v\preceq w
. We write
v\prec w
when
v\preceq w
and
v\neq w
.

A
substitution

\sigma
on the alphabet
\mathcal{A}
is an endomorphism of the free monoid
\mathcal{A}^{*}
that is
nonerasing
in the sense that
|\sigma(a)|\geq 1
holds for each
a\in\mathcal{A}
.
With
\sigma
, we associate its
incidence matrix

M_{\sigma}=(|\sigma(b)|_{a})_{a,b\in\mathcal{A}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d\times d}
. This matrix is the abelianized version of
\sigma
in the following sense. Let

\boldsymbol{\ell}:\,\mathcal{A}^{*}\to\mathbb{N}^{d},\quad w\mapsto\vphantom{(|w|_{1},\ldots,|w|_{d})}^{t}(|w|_{1},\ldots,|w|_{d})

be the
abelianization map
, then
\boldsymbol{\ell}(\sigma(w))=M_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{\ell}(w)
holds for all
w\in\mathcal{A}^{*}
. A substitution is called
unimodular
if
|{\det M_{\sigma}}|=1
,
primitive
if
M_{\sigma}
is primitive, and it is called
Pisot
if
M_{\sigma}
is a matrix whose characteristic polynomial is the minimal polynomial of a Pisot number.
27
27

27

Some authors call such a substitution
irreducible Pisot
, and define a Pisot substitution as a substitution whose matrix is primitive, and has a Perron eigenvalue (i.e., a unique largest eigenvalue) which is a Pisot number.
Pisot substitutions are extensively studied in the literature; see e.g.
[
Rau82
,
AI01
,
Fog02
,
ABB+15
,
AD19
]
and the references given there.

Set

\mathcal{S}_{d}=\big\{\sigma\,:\,\sigma\ \text{is a unimodular substitution over the alphabet}\ \mathcal{A}=\{1,\dots,d\}\big\}.

and let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a sequence of substitutions over the alphabet
\mathcal{A}
.
In accordance with our convention for matrices, compositions of consecutive substitutions will be written as

\sigma_{[m,n)}=\sigma_{m}\sigma_{m+1}\cdots\sigma_{n-1}\quad\text{for}\ m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\ \text{with}\ m\leq n;

here,
\sigma_{[m,m)}
is the identity substitution, which maps each letter of
\mathcal{A}
to itself.
Note that the incidence matrix of
\sigma_{[m,n)}
satisfies

M_{\sigma_{[m,n)}}=M_{\sigma_{m}}M_{\sigma_{m+1}}\cdots M_{\sigma_{n-1}}.

Many properties of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
depend only on its
sequence of incidence matrices

\mathbf{M}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=(M_{\sigma_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}.

In particular,
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is called
primitive
if
\mathbf{M}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
is primitive (see Definition
11.5
below).

In order to define the
\mathcal{S}
-adic shift associated to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
, we set, for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(11.1)

\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)}=\{w\in\mathcal{A}^{*}\,:\,\hbox{$w$ is a factor of $\sigma_{[n,m)}(a)$ for some $a\in\mathcal{A}$, $m>n$}\}.

We call
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)}
the
language of level
n
associated to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
, and we define
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}:=\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(0)}
.
We recall that
\Sigma:\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}}\to\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
(\omega_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mapsto(\omega_{n+1})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, denotes the
shift operator
.

Definition 11.1

(
\mathcal{S}
-adic shift)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}}
with
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{S}_{d}
,
d\geq 2
.
We define

X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)}=\{\omega\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}}\,:\,\hbox{each factor of $\omega$ is an element of $\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)}$}\}\qquad(n\in\mathbb{Z}).

The symbolic dynamical system
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)},\Sigma)
is called the
(two-sided)
\mathcal{S}
-adic shift of level
n
associated to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
. We let
X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}:=X^{(0)}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
, and call
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma)
the
\mathcal{S}
-adic shift associated to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
.

In Section
7
, we considered sequences of matrices taken from a set
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathcal{M}_{d}
. Choose a set
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{S}_{d}
of substitutions such that
\mathcal{M}=\{M_{\sigma}:\sigma\in\mathcal{S}\}
and
M_{\sigma}\neq M_{\tau}
for distinct
\sigma,\tau\in\mathcal{S}
. This yields a natural injection
\varrho:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{S}_{d}
with
\mathcal{S}=\varrho(\mathcal{M})
. The mapping
\varrho
is called a
substitution assignment
; see Definition
11.17
). Note that, given
\mathbf{M}
, the choice of
\varrho
is in general not unique and there is no “standard” choice for such a set
\mathcal{S}
. Nevertheless, given such a substitution assignment
\varrho
, we can relate with each sequence
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
a unique sequence
(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
. In view of (
9.16
), a substitution selection associates with each orbit of the natural extension of a continued fraction algorithm a sequence of substitutions with values in
\mathcal{S}
and, hence, an
\mathcal{S}
-adic shift; we will make this precise in Section
11.6
. For now we just illustrate this by a famous example.

Definition 11.2

(Sturmian sequences)
.

A bi-infinite sequence
\omega=(\omega_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\{1,2\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
is called a
Sturmian sequence
if there exist
\alpha\in(0,1)\setminus\mathbb{Q}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
such that, for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\omega_{n}=i\quad\mbox{if and only if}\quad\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha}^{n}(x)=n\alpha+x\in I_{i}\ \pmod{\mathbb{Z}},

where

\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha}:\,\mathbb{T}^{1}\to\mathbb{T}^{1},\quad x\mapsto x+\alpha,

with either
I_{1}=[0,1{-}\alpha)
,
I_{2}=[1{-}\alpha,1)
, or
I_{1}=(0,1{-}\alpha]
,
I_{2}=(1{-}\alpha,1]
. In this sense, a Sturmian sequence is a
coding
of the rotation
(\mathbb{T}^{1},\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha})
w.r.t. the two-interval partition
\{I_{1},I_{2}\}
.

An irrational rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha}
leading to a Sturmian sequence is illustrated in Figure
15
.

I_{1}
I_{2}
\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha}^{-1}(z)
z
\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha}(z)
\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha}^{2}(z)
\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha}^{3}(z)

Figure 15.
Sturmian sequences as codings of the rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha}
on
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
(with
\alpha=\frac{1}{1+e}
), represented on the unit circle

Sturmian sequences are well-known and have many interesting properties like low subword complexity; in fact they have exactly
n+1
subwords of length
n
for each
n
; see e.g.
[
Fog02
, Chapter 6]
or
[
BS02
]
.
For details on the correspondence between Sturmian sequences, the classical continued fraction algorithm, and sequences of low subword complexity, we also refer to
[
AR91
]
or
[
Thu20
, Section 3.2]
.

Example 11.3

(Sturmian sequences and
\mathcal{S}
-adic shifts)
.

We continue Examples
6.3
and
6.9
.
For integers
a\geq 1
, define the matrices and substitutions

M_{\mathrm{G},a}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&a\end{pmatrix},\quad\sigma_{\mathrm{G},a}:\begin{cases}1\mapsto 2,\\
2\mapsto 1\underbrace{2\cdots 2}_{a\text{ \scriptsize times}}.\end{cases}

Let
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{G}}=\{M_{\mathrm{G},a}:a\geq 1\}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{G}}=\{\sigma_{\mathrm{G},a}:a\geq 1\}
.
Then
M_{\mathrm{G},a}
is the incidence matrix of
\sigma_{\mathrm{G},a}
, hence the substitution selection

\varrho:\ \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{G}}\to\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{G}},\quad M_{\mathrm{G},a}\mapsto\sigma_{\mathrm{G},a},

is well-defined.
Let
x,y\in[0,1]\setminus\mathbb{Q}
be irrational numbers with continued fraction expansions
x=[0;a_{0},a_{1},\dots]
,
y=[0;a_{-1},a_{-2},\dots]
.
Then the natural extension
\widehat{F}
of the classical continued fraction algorithm associates to
\big(\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{x}{1},\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{y}{1}\big)\in\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{1}{\times}\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{1}
the sequence of matrices
(M^{\prime}_{a_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
;
see Examples
9.3
and
9.7
.
The set
\mathcal{M}
is the range of the cocycle
A
of the classical continued fraction algorithm defined in (
9.4
).
The substitution selection
\varrho
then associates the sequence of substitutions
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{\mathrm{G},a_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
to
\mathbf{M}=(M_{\mathrm{G},a_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and, hence, an
\mathcal{S}
-adic shift
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma)
.
It was observed already in
[
MH40
,
CH73
]
that the elements of
X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
are
Sturmian sequences
, which are codings of the rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha}
on
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
by
\alpha=\frac{x}{1+x}
with respect to two intervals
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
of length
\alpha
and
1{-}\alpha
respectively; see Figure
15
.
This can be rephrased by saying that the shift
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma)
is mesurably conjugate to the irrational rotation
(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z},\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha})
; this conjugacy is stated in a more general context in Theorem
12.9
below.

Note that
M_{\mathrm{G},a}=M_{\mathrm{F},2}^{a-1}M_{\mathrm{F},1}
and
\sigma_{\mathrm{G},a}=\sigma_{\mathrm{F},2}^{a-1}\circ\sigma_{\mathrm{F},1}
with the matrices
M_{\mathrm{F},i}
from (
2.3
) and the substitutions
\sigma_{\mathrm{F},i}
from (
2.2
).

For surveys on results about
\mathcal{S}
-adic shifts we refer to
[
Fer96
,
DL12
,
DLR13
,
BD14
,
Thu20
]
. In
[
AMS14
,
BST19
,
BST23
]
, relations between
\mathcal{S}
-adic shifts to geometry and arithmetic have been investigated, and in
[
BSTY19
,
BPRS24
]
recognizability results of these shifts have been proved in a quite general context. Recall that a shift
(X,\Sigma)
is
minimal
if
(X,\Sigma)
has no nontrivial closed shift invariant subset. In the same way as the one-sided infinite case (see for instance
[
Thu20
, Proposition 3.5.3 (iii)]
), one can show that the
\mathcal{S}
-adic shift associated to a primitive sequence of substitutions is minimal. Primitivity and minimality are in fact equivalent. To be more precise, if the sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
of substitutions is everywhere growing (in the sense of
[
BD14
, Definition 3.1]
), but not primitive, it results from the definition of primitivity that some letter occurs with unbounded gaps in some element of
X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
. This contradicts minimality of
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma)
. Primitivity is a necessary condition for obtaining meaningful symbolic dynamics in our context.

11.2.
Combinatorial properties and generalized eigenvectors

In this section, we revisit the properties stated in Section
7
for sequences of matrices and carry them over to sequences of substitutions. Besides that, we define notions that are specific to sequences of substitutions. These are mainly two-sided versions of properties defined in
[
BST19
,
BST23
]
.

One of the main notion that depends on
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
and not only on
\mathbf{M}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
is the notion of
balance
(for letters), which is a property that we desire for the languages
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)}
associated to a sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
of substitutions.
We stress the fact that this notion is specific to the symbolic setting and its importance is a reason for superimposing sequences of substitutions on sequences of matrices.

Definition 11.4

(Balance)
.

Let
C\in\mathbb{N}
.
A set of words
\mathcal{L}\subset\mathcal{A}^{*}
is called
C
-balanced
if

|v|_{a}-|w|_{a}\leq C\quad\mbox{for all factors $v,w$ of $\mathcal{L}$ with $|v|=|w|$ and for all $a\in\mathcal{A}$}.

An element of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}}
is called
C
-balanced
if the set of its factors is
C
-balanced.
A shift
(X,\Sigma)
is called
C
-balanced
if the set of all factors of its elements is
C
-balanced. If one of these objects is
C
-balanced for some unspecified
C
, then we just say that it is
balanced
.

The language of a Pisot substitution
\sigma
, i.e., the language
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
for the constant sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma)
, is balanced; see e.g.
[
Ada03
,
Ada04
]
.

Definition 11.5

(Properties of sequences of substitutions inherited from their incidence matrices)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
d\geq 2
, be a sequence of substitutions and
\mathbf{M}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=(M_{\sigma_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
the associated sequence of incidence matrices. If a certain property holds for
\mathbf{M}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
then we say that it also holds for
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
. In particular, this applies to primitivity, the existence of generalized eigenvectors, weak, strong, and exponential convergence, the Pisot condition, and to algebraic irreducibility.

The following notion of letter frequency is related to balance.

Definition 11.6

(Letter frequency)
.

For a sequence
(\omega_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, the
frequency
of a letter
a\in\mathcal{A}
is

f_{a}=\lim_{m,n\to\infty}\frac{|\omega_{-m}\cdots\omega_{n}|_{a}}{n+m+1},

if this limit exists. The vector
\mathbf{f}=\vphantom{(f_{1},\dots,f_{d})}^{t}(f_{1},\dots,f_{d})
is then called
letter frequency vector
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be primitive. As in the case of a primitive substitution, if the generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{u}
exists, then it is proportional to the letter frequency vector for any infinite word in
X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
. In fact, the existence of this generalized right eigenvector provides the existence of the frequency vector, and even in most cases unique ergodicity of the symbolic system
X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
; see e.g.
[
Fis09
,
BKMS13
,
BD14
]
. In other words, the existence of well-defined frequencies for the letters is related to weak convergence.

But we also need balance, which has to do with strong convergence.
Indeed, balance (as defined in Definition
11.4
) implies several important properties in terms of letter frequencies.
It implies first that if
(X,\Sigma)
is a minimal balanced shift (defined over the alphabet
\mathcal{A}
), then letters admit frequencies, and moreover, the (letter)
symbolic discrepancy

(11.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Discr}(X,\Sigma)

\displaystyle=\sup\big\{|\omega_{-n}\cdots\omega_{n}|_{a}-(2n{+}1)f_{a}\,:\,(\omega_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\in X,\,n\geq 1,\,a\in\mathcal{A}\big\}

\displaystyle=\sup\big\{\lVert\boldsymbol{\ell}(w)-|w|\,\mathbf{f}\rVert_{\infty}\,:\,\mbox{$w$ is a factor of an element of~$X$}\big\}

is bounded, where
\mathbf{f}
stands for the letter frequency vector.

We can even characterize balance geometrically by using the projection
\pi_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{1}}
(defined in (
7.4
)) as follows, with
\mathbf{1}={}^{t}(1,\ldots,1)
being the “diagonal vector” in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Note that, for
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
with
\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert_{1}=1
, we have

\pi_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{1}}\,\boldsymbol{\ell}(w)=\boldsymbol{\ell}(w)-|w|\,\mathbf{u},

which is related to local discrepancy when
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{f}
. A minimal shift
(X,\Sigma)
such that all sequences have frequency vector
\mathbf{f}

is balanced if and only if
\lVert\pi_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{1}}\,\boldsymbol{\ell}(w)\rVert
is bounded for factors
w
of elements of
X
(see
[
BST23
, Proposition 4.10]
or
[
Ada03
, Proposition 7]
). This geometric vision is crucial for establishing the boundedness of Rauzy fractals; see Lemma
12.2
. It turns out that balance also implies that the symbolic system is a finite extension of a toral rotation; see e.g.
[
BCB19
, Proposition 2.9]
.

Example 11.7

(Sturmian sequences and balance)
.

We continue Example
11.3
. Consider a Sturmian sequence
\omega=(\omega_{n})_{n}
coding the rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha}
, with
\alpha
irrational, according to Definition
11.2
.
By equidistribution of the sequence
(n\alpha)_{n}\bmod 1
, the Sturmian sequence
\omega
has letter frequencies, given by the lengths
\alpha
and
1{-}\alpha
of the coding intervals. One can easily deduce from Definition
11.2
that Sturmian sequences are
1
-balanced.
Moreover, aperiodic
1
-balanced sequences are Sturmian; see
[
Fog02
, Chapter 6]
.

11.3.
Pisot condition and strong convergence

The condition we are after now is strong convergence. We will need strong convergence (in the future) of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
to
\mathbf{u}
in order to have the eventually Anosov property for the mapping family associated to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
; see Section
11.5
below. Strong convergence is related to balance (see Definition
11.4
above), and will allow us to define the
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy fractals (see Section
12
), which will be important to construct explicit Markov partitions for the mapping families. The generalized left eigenvector
\mathbf{v}
plays an important role in the construction of Markov partitions.

In the stationary case
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma)
, the fact that
\sigma
is a Pisot substitution is equivalent to strong convergence; this can be seen by looking at the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of the incidence matrix
M_{\sigma}
. We now consider the general case of an
\mathcal{S}
-adic system satisfying the Pisot condition and we give sufficient conditions for the strong convergence of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
(in the future). This statement is the substitutive form of Theorem
Theorem A
.
stated in the introduction; see also Theorem
7.21
.

Theorem 11.8
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
d\geq 2
, be a primitive sequence of substitutions with sequence of incidence matrices
\mathbf{M}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=(M_{\sigma_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
that satisfies the local Pisot condition and the growth condition
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert M_{\sigma_{n}}\rVert=0
. Then the follwing assertions hold.

(i)

The sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
converges exponentially and, hence, strongly to
\mathbf{u}
.

(ii)

The coordinates of the generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{u}
of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
are rationally independent,

(iii)

The language
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
is a balanced.

Proof of Theorem
11.8
.

Since
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
satisfies the Pisot condition and the growth condition
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert M_{\sigma_{n}}\rVert=0
, assertion (
i
) is an immediate consequence of Proposition
7.15
. Moreover, Corollary
7.19
yields algebraic irreducibility of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
, and we may apply Corollary
7.20
to get assertion(
ii
).

It remains to prove assertion (
iii
). If we show that there is a constant
C>0
such that, for each
w\in\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
, the vector
\boldsymbol{\ell}(w)
is at distance at most
C
from the ray
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}
, balance follows.
Let
\delta_{1}(n)\geq\cdots\geq\delta_{d}(n)
be the singular values of
M_{\sigma_{[0,n)}}
. By the Pisot condition and the growth condition, we may choose
\beta>0
and
C_{1}>0
in a way that

(11.3)

\delta_{2}(n)\leq C_{1}e^{-\beta n}\quad\mbox{for all}\ n\in\mathbb{N}.

By the growth condition there is
C_{2}>0
such that

\lVert M_{\sigma_{n}}\rVert_{\infty}\leq C_{2}\,e^{\frac{\beta}{2}n}\quad\mbox{for all}\ n\in\mathbb{N}.

Let
w\in\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
, i.e.,
w
is a factor of
\sigma_{[0,n)}(a)
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
a\in\mathcal{A}
.
Then we can write
w=p_{0}\sigma_{0}(w_{1})s_{0}
for some word
w_{1}\in\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(1)}
, some factors
p_{0}
of
\sigma_{0}(a_{0})
and
s_{0}
of
\sigma_{0}(b_{0})
,
a_{0},b_{0}\in\mathcal{A}
.
Recursively, we obtain

(11.4)

w=p_{0}\,\sigma_{0}(p_{1}\cdots\sigma_{n-1}(s_{n}p_{n})\cdots s_{1})s_{0}

for some words
p_{j},s_{j}\in\mathcal{A}^{*}
,
0\leq j\leq n
, that are factors of words
\sigma_{j}(a)
,
a\in\mathcal{A}
.
By the growth condition, we have

(11.5)

\max\{|p_{n}|,|s_{n}|\}\leq\max\{|\sigma_{n}(a)|\,:\,a\in\mathcal{A}\}=\lVert M_{\sigma_{n}}\rVert_{\infty}\leq C_{2}\,e^{\frac{\beta}{2}n}.

Then (
11.4
), (
7.24
), (
11.5
), and (
11.3
) imply that

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(\boldsymbol{\ell}(w),\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=0}^{N}\mathrm{d}\big(M_{\sigma_{[0,n)}}\boldsymbol{\ell}(p_{n}s_{n}),\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}\big)\leq 2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{\sigma_{[0,n)}}|_{\mathbf{u}^{\perp}}\rVert_{2}\cdot\max\{|p_{n}|,|s_{n}|\}

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{\sigma_{[0,n)}}|_{\mathbf{u}^{\perp}}\rVert_{2}\cdot e^{\frac{\beta}{2}n}\leq 2C_{1}C_{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\exp\Big({-}\frac{\beta}{2}n\Big)=\frac{2C_{1}C_{2}}{1+\beta/2}

(with
\mathrm{d}(\boldsymbol{\ell}(w),\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u})
measured by the 2-norm).
This implies that
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
is balanced.
∎

Another condition assuring strong convergence and rational independence of the coordinates of the generalized right eigenvector is contained in the following proposition.

Proposition 11.9
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
d\geq 2
, be a primitive sequence of substitutions that is algebraically irreducible and whose language
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
is balanced. Then the coordinates of its generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{u}
are rationally independent, and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
converges strongly to
\mathbf{u}
.

Proof.

If the language
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
is balanced, then the existence of a frequency vector of
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
and thus a generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{u}
is proved e.g. in
[
PS24
]
. Moreover, balance implies that the distance of
M_{\sigma_{[0,n)}}\mathbf{e}_{a}
from
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}
is bounded for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
(see
[
BST23
, Proposition 4.10]
), thus algebraic irreducibility of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
implies rational independence of
\mathbf{u}
as in the proof of Corollary
7.20
. Now, the strong convergence to
\mathbf{u}
is the content of
[
BST23
, Lemma 5.11]
, the proof of which is quite technical.
∎

Remark 11.10
.

We have seen in Proposition
7.4
that primitivity and eventual recurrence play a fundamental role in order to have the existence of generalized eigenvectors. Theorem
11.8
shows that the Pisot condition (together with the quite weak growth condition, which is even empty for finite
\mathcal{S}
) is much stronger.

Converses of the implications in Theorem
11.8
and Proposition
11.9
are false. Indeed, there exist substitutions with balanced fixed points that do not satisfy the Pisot condition (see
[
BCB19
, Example 2.6]
), and strong convergence does not give in general further information on the speed of convergence, which is exponential in the Pisot case. In general, the proof of the balance property uses the Dumont–Thomas decomposition of a prefix of the fixed point into words of the form
\sigma_{0}\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{n}(a)
(see
[
DT89
]
), and the distance of the corresponding abelianized vector to the generalized eigenline
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}
. We can in this way derive a bound for balance in terms of a series; see the proof of the third assertion of Theorem
11.8
. If the strong convergence is too slow, this series does not converge and the word is not balanced. On the other hand, the Pisot condition implies exponential convergence, but a slower convergence suffices for balance.

As an application of Theorem
11.8
, we provide the following example. It shows that a sequence
(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathbb{Z}}
may not have the local Pisot property even if the substitutions
\sigma_{[0,n)}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, are Pisot substitutions.

Example 11.11
.

Define the set
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{AR}}=\{\sigma_{\mathrm{AR},i}:i\in\mathcal{A}\}
of
Arnoux–Rauzy substitutions
over the alphabet
\mathcal{A}
by

\sigma_{\mathrm{AR},i}:\,i\mapsto i,\ j\mapsto ij\ \mbox{for}\ j\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{i\}\qquad(i\in\mathcal{A}).

An
Arnoux–Rauzy directive sequence
is a sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{AR}}^{\mathbb{Z}}
for which each substitution
\alpha_{i}
occurs infinitely often. These sequences were introduced in
[
AR91
]
.
By
[
AI01
, Example 5]
all finite products of Arnoux–Rauzy substitutions are primitive Pisot substitutions
as soon as they contain each of the three substitutions.
By
[
CFZ00
, Theorem 2.4]
,
28
28

28

The paper
[
CFZ00
]
considers one-sided directive sequences; they can easily be prolongated on the left as two-sided Arnoux–Rauzy directive sequences because the balance property only depends on the future.
there exists a primitive Arnoux–Rauzy directive sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
for which
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma)
is not balanced; it satisfies the growth condition since it is defined on a finite set of substitutions; however it does not satisfy the local Pisot condition by Theorem
11.8
(
iii
), even though all the substitutions

\sigma_{[0,n]}
are Pisot substitutions for
n
large enough.

11.4.
Spectral properties of
\mathcal{S}
-adic shifts

In this section we discuss spectral properties of
\mathcal{S}
-adic shifts that follow as a consequence of the Pisot condition. Before stating the according results, we recall some definitions from ergodic theory.

We say that a directive sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
has
pure discrete spectrum
if the system
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma)
is
uniquely ergodic
(i.e., it has a unique shift invariant probability measure
\mu
), minimal, and has pure discrete measurable spectrum (i.e., the measurable eigenfunctions of the
Koopman operator

U_{T}:L^{2}(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma,\mu)\to L^{2}(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma,\mu)
,
f\mapsto f\circ\Sigma
, span
L^{2}(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma,\mu))
. A complex number
l
is a
continuous eigenvalue
of
(X,T)
if there exists a nonzero continuous function
f:X\to\mathbb{C}
such that
f\circ T=\mbox{l}{}f
.
A
weak topological mixing
shift
X
is one that has no nonconstant continuous (with respect to the topology) eigenfunctions of the shift operator. The Pisot condition implies the existence of a topological factor.

The following lemma shows that our standard assumptions on
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
imply minimality and unique ergodicity of the shift
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma)
.

Lemma 11.12
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
d\geq 2
, be a primitive sequence of unimodular substitutions that admits a generalized right eigenvector.
Then the shift
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma)
is minimal and uniquely ergodic.

Proof.

If
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is primitive, then
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma)
is minimal by
[
BD14
, Theorem 5.2]
and everywhere growing in the sense of
[
BD14
, Definition 3.1]
.
Then the existence of a generalized right eigenvector yields unique ergodicity by
[
BD14
, Theorem 5.7]
.
∎

We also need the following classical consequence of a theorem of Gottschalk and Hedlund
[
GH55
]
which can be found for instance in
[
BCB19
]
.

Lemma 11.13

(see e.g.
[
BCB19
, Proposition 2.8]
)
.

Let
(X,T,\mu)
be a minimal and uniquely ergodic subshift of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. If
X
is balanced on letters, then
\exp(2\pi i\mu([a]))
is a continuous eigenvalue of
(X,T,\mu)
for each
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

Theorem 11.14
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
d\geq 2
, be a primitive sequence of unimodular substitutions with incidence matrices
(M_{\sigma_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
satisfying the Pisot condition and the growth condition
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert M_{\sigma_{n}}\rVert=0
. Then the uniquely ergodic
\mathcal{S}
-adic shift
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma,\mu)
is not weakly mixing. In particular,
\exp(2\pi i\mu([a]))
is a continuous eigenvalue for each letter
a\in\mathcal{A}
. Moreover, the shift admits a minimal rotation on
\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
as a topological factor.

Proof.

It follows from the Pisot condition, which is shift invariant, that
\Sigma^{n}\boldsymbol{\sigma}
converges weakly to
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}_{n}
for some
\mathbf{u}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore, Lemma
11.12
implies that
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma)
satisfies the conditions of Lemma
11.13
, and the assertion on the eigenvalues follows from this lemma. The existence of these eigenvalues implies that
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma)
is not topologically weakly mixing.
Let
\mu
denote its unique invariant measure.
Since, by
[
BD14
, Theorem 5.7]
, the vector
(\mu([a]))_{a\in\mathcal{A}}
is a generalized right eigenvector of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
, Corollary
7.20
implies that the numbers
\mu([a])
,
a\in\mathcal{A}
, are rationally independent and, since
\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mu([a])=1
, the vector
(\mu([a]))_{a\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{d\}}
induces a minimal rotation on
\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
. Then, by a classical result (see e.g.
[
Fog02
, Exercise 1.6.3]
), the shift admits a minimal rotation on
\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
as a topological factor.
∎

11.5.
Eventually Anosov
\mathcal{S}
-adic mapping families

We now revisit the results stated for sequences of matrices in Section
7.6
and we now relate a mapping family to a sequence of substitutions.
We consider again the set
\mathcal{S}_{d}
of substitutions associated to unimodular matrices.

Definition 11.15

(
\mathcal{S}
-adic mapping family)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{S}_{d}
, with
d\geq 2
, be a family of unimodular substitutions and let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\mathbb{T}_{n}
be a copy of the
d
-dimensional torus
\mathbb{R}^{d}/\mathbb{Z}^{d}
equipped with the standard metric inherited from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Moreover, regard the inverse of the incidence matrix of
\sigma_{n}
as the automorphism
29
29

29

This is possible since
M_{\sigma_{n}}
is an integer matrix of determinant
\pm 1
by unimodularity of
\sigma_{n}
.

M_{\sigma_{n}}^{-1}:\mathbb{T}_{n}\to\mathbb{T}_{n+1}
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}/\mathbb{Z}^{d}
given by left multiplication by
M_{\sigma_{n}}^{-1}
. Then we set
\mathbb{T}=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{T}_{n}
and define
f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{T}
by
f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\mathbf{x}=M_{\sigma_{n}}^{-1}\mathbf{x}
for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
.
We call
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
the
\mathcal{S}
-adic mapping family
associated to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
. It can be written out as

\cdots\xrightarrow{M_{\sigma_{-2}}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}_{-1}\xrightarrow{M_{\sigma_{-1}}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}_{0}\xrightarrow{M_{\sigma_{0}}^{-1}}\mathbb{T}_{1}\xrightarrow{M_{\sigma_{1}}^{-1}}\cdots.

We shall now state the
\mathcal{S}
-adic version of Theorem
7.26
, i.e.,
\mathcal{S}
-adic mapping families are eventually Anosov if the corresponding sequences of substitutions
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
admit strong convergence to the generalized eigenvectors (see Definition
11.5
) in the future and in the past. In the following result, also an
\mathcal{S}
-adic version of Proposition
7.24
is included.

Theorem 11.16
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
d\geq 2
, be a two-sided primitive sequence of
unimodular substitution that admits strong convergence to the generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{u}
in the future and to the generalized left eigenvector
\mathbf{v}
in the past. Then the mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
associated to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is eventually Anosov for the splitting

G^{s}\oplus G^{u}=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}G_{n}^{s}\oplus G_{n}^{u}=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}_{n}\oplus\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp},

where
\mathbf{u}_{n}
and
\mathbf{v}_{n}
are defined in (
7.3
).

In particular, this holds when
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is two-sided primitive, satisfies the two-sided Pisot condition and the growth condition
\lim_{n\to\pm\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert M_{\sigma_{n}}\rVert=0
.

11.6.
Substitutive realizations

Our goal is to set up symbolic realizations of multidimensional continued fraction algorithms and sequences of matrices. We follow here
[
BST23
, Section 2.3]
, and we provide a two-sided version of the notion of a substitutive realization. Consider the natural extension
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A})
of a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm
(X,F,A)
; see Chapter
III
for the definition. We associate with each
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\widehat{X}
a bi-infinite sequence of substitutions
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
with generalized right and left eigenvectors
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
.
We regard the partial quotient matrices
\vphantom{\!\widehat{A}}{}^{t}\!\widehat{A}(\widehat{F}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}))
as incidence matrices of substitutions, i.e., for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
we choose
\sigma_{n}
with incidence matrix
M_{\sigma_{n}}=\vphantom{\!\widehat{A}}^{t}\!\widehat{A}(\widehat{F}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}))
.

Definition 11.17

(Substitutive realization)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathcal{M}_{d}
,
d\geq 2
, be a set of matrices. We first call a map
\varrho:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{S}_{d}
a
substitution assignment on
\mathcal{M}
if the incidence matrix of
\varrho(M)
is
M
for each
M\in\mathcal{M}
. Often we will have to apply such a substitution assignment to a sequence of matrices. In this case it is to be understood that
\varrho
is applied component-wise.

We then call a map
\varphi:\widehat{X}\to\mathcal{S}_{d}
a
substitution selection
for a positive
(d{-}1)
-dimensional continued fraction algorithm
(X,F,A)
with natural extension
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A})
if the incidence matrix of
\varphi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is equal to
\vphantom{\!\widehat{A}}{}^{t}\!\widehat{A}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
for all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\widehat{X}
. The corresponding
substitutive realization
of
(X,F,A)
is the map

\boldsymbol{\varphi}:\,\widehat{X}\to\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}},\quad(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\mapsto(\varphi(\widehat{F}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})))_{n\in\mathbb{Z}},

together with the shift
(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X}),\Sigma)
.
For any
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\widehat{X}
, the sequence
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is called an
\mathcal{S}
-adic expansion
of
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
. Because
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, the shift
(X_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})},\Sigma)
is an
\mathcal{S}
-adic dynamical system which we call the
\mathcal{S}
-adic dynamical system of
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
w.r.t.
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\varphi)
.

If
\varphi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\varphi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})
for all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\in\widehat{X}
with
\widehat{A}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\widehat{A}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})
, then
\varphi
is called a
faithful substitution selection
, and
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
is a
faithful substitutive realization
.

If
\varphi
is a faithful substitution selection, then it may be defined by a substitution assignment
\varrho
on
\mathcal{M}=\vphantom{\!\widehat{A}}^{t}\!\widehat{A}(\widehat{X})
, i.e., for a suitable
\varrho
we have
\varphi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\varrho\circ\vphantom{\!\widehat{A}}^{t}\!\widehat{A}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
.

The definition of a substitutive realization implies that the diagram

(11.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}

commutes.
If
(X,F,A)
converges weakly in the future and in the past at
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
for
\nu
-almost all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\widehat{X}
(w.r.t. a measure
\nu
having the properties determined in Section
9
), then the dynamical system
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A})
is measurably conjugate to its substitutive realization, which we write as

(11.7)

(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\nu)\overset{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}{\cong}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X}),\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}\nu).

From Lemma
9.13
, we immediately obtain the following correspondence between
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
and the generalized right and left eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
.

Lemma 11.18
.

Assume that
\widehat{X}\subset\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0}^{d-1}{\times}\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0}^{d-1}
and that
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A})
is weakly convergent. If
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{v}
are generalized right and left eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
, then
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
is a representative of
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}{\times}\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Analogously to the matrix setting in Section
9.4
, each sequence of substitutions
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
, with
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\widehat{X}
, can be used to define the
\mathcal{S}
-adic mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})})
, which performs the multidimensional continued fraction algorithm
\widehat{F}
for a suitable element of the domain
\widehat{X}
. Thus
\widehat{X}
parametrizes the set of mapping families and the shift
(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X}),\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}\nu)
forms a renormalization procedure.

These substitutive realizations will be crucial for the construction of nonstationary Markov partitions for multidimensional continued fraction algorithms; see Chapter
V
.

11.7.
Substitutive realizations of the Brun algorithm

In this section, we illustrate the results of this section by examples related to the version of the Brun continued fraction algorithm studied in Section
10
. We do this in three examples, namely, the ordered, unordered and multiplicative versions of the Brun algorithm.

Example 11.19

(Substitutive realization of the unordered Brun algorithm)
.

For
i,j\in\mathcal{A}
,
i\neq j
, we define the
unordered Brun substitutions
(see also
[
DHS13
]
; we take the mirror images of the images of letters under the substitutions for better comparability to the ordered case) by

(11.8)

\sigma_{\mathrm{U},ij}:\,\begin{cases}j\mapsto ji,\\
k\mapsto k&(k\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{j\}).\end{cases}

These substitutions are chosen in such a way that for all
i,j\in\mathcal{A}
,
i\neq j
, the incidence matrix of
\sigma_{\mathrm{U},ij}
equals the matrix
M_{\mathrm{U},ij}
used in (
10.2
) to define the ordered Brun algorithm. We now use Definition
11.17
to set up a substitutive realization for the ordered Brun algorithm. We start with the definition of a substitution assignment. To this end let
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{U}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{U}}
be the set of unordered Brun matrices and unordered Brun substitutions, respectively, and set

\varrho_{\mathrm{U}}:\,\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{U}}\to\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{U}},\quad M_{\mathrm{U},ij}\mapsto\sigma_{\mathrm{U},ij}\quad(i,j\in\mathcal{A},\,i\neq j).

With
X_{\mathrm{U},ij}
defined as in (
10.1
), we can now define the substitution selection of the unordered Brun algorithm by

\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}:\,\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{U}}\to\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{U}};\quad(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\mapsto\varrho(\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})).

This gives the
substitutive realization of the unordered Brun algorithm
(X_{\mathrm{U}},F_{\mathrm{U}},A)

\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{U}}:\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{U}}\to\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{U}}^{\mathbb{Z}},\quad(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\mapsto(\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(F_{\mathrm{U}}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}))).

From (
10.8
), we see immediately that

(11.9)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X})

\displaystyle=\big\{(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,:\,(\sigma_{n},\sigma_{n+1})=(\sigma_{\mathrm{U},ij},\sigma_{\mathrm{U},ij})\ \text{for}\ i,j\in\mathcal{A},\,i\neq j

\displaystyle\text{or}\ (\sigma_{n},\sigma_{n+1})=(\sigma_{\mathrm{U},ij},\sigma_{\mathrm{U},jk})\ \text{for}\ i,j,k\in\mathcal{A},\,i\neq j\hbox{ and }j\neq k\big\}.

Since the ordered Brun algorithm
(X_{\mathrm{U}},F_{\mathrm{U}},A_{U})
converges weakly almost everywhere (see Section
10
), we gain from (
11.7
) the measurable conjugacy

(\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{U}},\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{U}},\widehat{A}_{\mathrm{U}},\widehat{\nu}_{\mathrm{U}})\overset{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{U}}}{\cong}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{U}}),\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}\widehat{\nu}_{\mathrm{U}}).

We can now prove the following proposition, which is in analogy with Proposition
10.4
apart from the assertion about balance.
Condition (
11.10
) means that the expansion has bounded strong partial quotients in the sense defined in Example
7.22
.

Proposition 11.20
.

Let
(X_{\mathrm{U}},F_{\mathrm{U}},A_{\mathrm{U}},\nu_{\mathrm{U}})
be the natural extension of the 2-dimensional ordered Brun algorithm. Suppose that for
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
there exists
h\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(11.10)

\{\sigma_{n},\ldots,\sigma_{n+h}\}=\{\sigma_{\mathrm{U},ij}\,:\,i,j\in\{1,2,3\},\,i\neq j\}

holds for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then the following assertions hold.

•

\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
satisfies the two-sided Pisot condition.

•

\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is algebraically irreducible.

•

The language
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}
is balanced.

•

\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
converges strongly to the generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{u}=\chi(\mathbf{x})
in the future and to the generalized left eigenvector
\mathbf{v}=\chi(\mathbf{y})
in the past. The vector
\chi(\mathbf{x})
has rationally independent coordinates.

•

The mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})})
associated to
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is eventually Anosov for the splitting

\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}_{n}\oplus\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}.

Proof.

The conditions of Theorems
11.16
and
11.8
are satisfied by reasoning as in the proof of Proposition
10.4
. Thus the result follows from these theorems.
∎

Example 11.21

(Substitutive realization of the ordered Brun algorithm)
.

For
k\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{d\}
we define the
ordered Brun substitutions

(11.11)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mathrm{B},k}:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}i\mapsto i,&(1\leq i<k)\\
k\mapsto d,\\
i\mapsto i{-}1,&(k<i<d)\\
d\mapsto(d{-}1)d,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mathrm{B},d}:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}i\mapsto i,&(i\neq d{-}1)\\
d{-}1\mapsto(d{-}1)d.\end{cases}

For
d=3
, they read

(11.12)

\sigma_{\mathrm{B},1}:\begin{cases}1\mapsto 3,\\
2\mapsto 1,\\
3\mapsto 23,\end{cases}\quad\sigma_{\mathrm{B},2}:\begin{cases}1\mapsto 1,\\
2\mapsto 3,\\
3\mapsto 23,\end{cases}\quad\sigma_{\mathrm{B},3}:\begin{cases}1\mapsto 1,\\
2\mapsto 23,\\
3\mapsto 3.\end{cases}

These substitutions are chosen in a way that that for each
k\in\mathcal{A}
the incidence matrix of
\sigma_{\mathrm{B},k}
equals the matrix
M_{\mathrm{B},k}
used in (
10.2
) to define the ordered Brun algorithm. For this reason, the sequences
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{\sigma_{\mathrm{B},1},\dots,\sigma_{\mathrm{B},d}\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
can be regarded as “combinatorial” analogs of the sequences of matrices generated by the ordered Brun algorithm. In the same way as we did for the unordered Brun algorithm in Example
11.19
, we can now set up a substitutive realization
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{B}}
for
(X_{\mathrm{B}},F_{\mathrm{B}},A_{\mathrm{B}},\nu_{\mathrm{B}})
. This leads to the measurable conjugacy

(\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{B}},\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{B}},\widehat{A}_{\mathrm{B}},\widehat{\nu}_{\mathrm{B}})\overset{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{B}}}{\cong}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{B}}),\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}\widehat{\nu}_{\mathrm{B}}).

We can now prove a substitutive version of Proposition
10.8
leading to the same assertions as Proposition
11.20
, also for the ordered Brun algorithm. Since this is completely analogous to the unordered case covered in Example
11.19
, we omit the details.

Example 11.22

(Substitutive realization of the multiplicative Brun algorithm)
.

For
1\leq k<d
and
m\geq 1
, we define the
multiplicative Brun substitutions

(11.13)

\sigma_{\mathrm{M},k,m}:\,\begin{cases}i\mapsto i,&(1\leq i<k)\\
k\mapsto d,\\
i\mapsto i{-}1,&(k<i<d)\\
d\mapsto(d{-}1)\underbrace{d\cdots d}_{\text{$m$ times}},\end{cases}

and set
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{M}}=\{\sigma_{\mathrm{M},k,m}:1\leq k<d,\,m\geq 1\}
.
In the same way as before, we get a measurable conjugacy to a shift on a substitutive realization
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{M}}
:

(11.14)

(\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{M}},\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{M}},\widehat{A}_{\mathrm{M}},\widehat{\nu}_{\mathrm{M}})\overset{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{M}}}{\cong}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathbb{Z}},\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}\widehat{\nu}_{\mathrm{M}}).

In this case, we established no combinatorial conditions that would lead to an analog of Proposition
11.20
. Besides that, this algorithm is multiplicative and, hence, we need infinitely many substitutions in its substitutive realization. Thus, the balance assertion of Theorem
11.8
is not true. We will see in Section
13
, that in our metric theory we can settle the problem of balance also for infinitely many substitutions.

Note that there are several possible substitutive realizations of the ordered and unordered Brun algorithms.
We have chosen realizations in a way that the two versions of the algorithm provide the same
\mathcal{S}
-adic shifts.
However, since the unordered version of the Brun algorithm is given by a larger set of substitutions than the ordered one, there is also more flexibility in the definition of the substitutive realization, i.e., the unordered case provides a larger class of
\mathcal{S}
-adic shifts.

12.
Rauzy fractals and Rauzy boxes

We now recall the key definition of a Rauzy fractal in the
\mathcal{S}
-adic setting.
These objects were originally defined in
[
BST19
,
BST23
]
. A Rauzy fractal for a sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
of substitutions is obtained as the closure of the projection of abelianizations of certain words taken from the language
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Under suitable hypotheses, a Rauzy fractal allows us to build a geometric model of the symbolic system
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma)
.
The first hypothesis we need is weak convergence of the continued fraction defined in terms of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
. This condition is satisfied by most of the interesting algorithms. The second condition requires that the language of the symbolic system is balanced. This is a much stronger condition, which can be seen as a generalization of the Pisot condition.

We first define Rauzy fractals and their suspensions, Rauzy boxes, in Section
12.1
.
We then prove tiling properties of these sets in Section
12.2
, and in Section
12.3
show that tiling properties imply pure discrete spectrum of the underlying
\mathcal{S}
-adic dynamical systems. Finally, in Section
12.4
, we provide a combinatorial condition that plays an important role in establishing tiling properties of Rauzy fractals. All this will be needed later on when we use them to construct nonstationary Markov partitions for
\mathcal{S}
-adic mapping families.

12.1.
Basic properties of Rauzy fractals and Rauzy boxes

For
d\geq 2
, let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, be a sequence of unimodular substitutions over the alphabet
\mathcal{A}=\{1,\dots,d\}
.
Throughout the section, we assume that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is primitive (in the future), has a generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{u}
and a generalized left eigenvector
\mathbf{v}
with
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\rangle\neq 0
, and we use the abbreviations

(12.1)

\pi_{n}=\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{v}_{n}}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\tilde{\pi}_{n}=\tilde{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{v}_{n}}

for the projections along
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}_{n}
to
\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}
and along
\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}
to
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}_{n}
respectively, with
\mathbf{u}_{n}
and
\mathbf{v}_{n}
as in (
7.3
).
Note that
\pi_{n}
is well defined because
\mathbf{v}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
and
\mathbf{u}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}
by primitivity.

We define
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy fractals as in
[
BST23
, Section 2.4, Definition 2.7]
.
