An alternative definition of a Rauzy fractal is provided in
[
BST19
, Section 2.9]
, which uses prefixes of an an infinite
limit word
of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
(leading to a
broken line
as depicted in Figure
8
) that is defined in terms of
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
. As we do not want to use limit words, we do not pursue this approach.

Here, we mostly consider Rauzy fractals in the hyperplane
\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}
, but we can project them of course to other hyperplanes
\mathbf{w}^{\perp}
, see Section
12.3
below.
Recall that
v\preceq w
if
v
is a prefix of
w
and that
\boldsymbol{\ell}(w)
is the abelianized vector of
w
.

Definition 12.1

(
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy fractal)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
d\geq 2
, be a primitive sequence of unimodular substitutions over the alphabet
\mathcal{A}
that admits generalized right and left eigenvectors.
For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the
Rauzy fractal

\mathcal{R}_{n}
(of level
n
) associated to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is defined as

\mathcal{R}_{n}=\overline{\{\pi_{n}\boldsymbol{\ell}(p)\,:\,p\preceq\sigma_{[n,m)}(b)\ \mbox{for infinitely many}\ m\geq n,\,b\in\mathcal{A}\}},

and, for each
w\in\mathcal{A}^{*}
, a
subtile

\mathcal{R}_{n}(w)
is defined as

(12.2)

\mathcal{R}_{n}(w)=\overline{\{\pi_{n}\boldsymbol{\ell}(p)\,:\,p\,w\preceq\sigma_{[n,m)}(b)\ \mbox{for infinitely many}\ m\geq n,\,b\in\mathcal{A}\}}.

We clearly have
\mathcal{R}_{n}=\bigcup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{R}_{n}(a)
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
The next lemma states that the Rauzy fractals are bounded if and only if the corresponding language is balanced.

Lemma 12.2

(
[
BST19
, Lemmas 4.1 and 5.4]
)
.

Assume that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
is primitive and has generalized right and left eigenvectors.
Then
\mathcal{R}_{n}
is bounded if and only if
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)}
is balanced.
In particular, if
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)}
is
C
-balanced, then
\mathcal{R}_{n}\subset\pi_{n}([-C,C]^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}^{\perp})
.

In the stationary setting, the Rauzy fractals are the attractors of a
graph-directed iterated function system
(GIFS for short) associated in a natural way with the substitution (see
[
SW02
, Equation (4.2)]
). They are thus described by finitely many set equations defined in terms of this GIFS.
In the nonstationary setting, however, we have an infinite sequence of such set equations.

Lemma 12.3

(see
[
BST19
, Proposition 5.6]
,
[
BST23
, Lemma 4.4]
)
.

Assume that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
is primitive and has generalized right and left eigenvectors.
Then

(12.3)

\mathcal{R}_{n}(a)=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\mathcal{A}^{*},\,b\in\mathcal{A}:\\
pa\preceq\sigma_{n}(b)\end{subarray}}\big(\pi_{n}\boldsymbol{\ell}(p)+M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathcal{R}_{n+1}(b)\big)\qquad(n\in\mathbb{Z},\,a\in\mathcal{A}).

Lemma
12.3
is about sets in
\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}
. There is a dual statement for the projection on the line
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}_{n}
, and in that case the sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
yields a set equation for line segments. To state it, we use the following notation for line segments. For a vector
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, the (closed) line segment connecting
\mathbf{x}
and the origin is written as
\llbracket\mathbf{x}\rrbracket
.

Lemma 12.4
.

Assume that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
is primitive and has generalized right and left eigenvectors.
Then

(12.4)

\tilde{\pi}_{n}\llbracket\boldsymbol{\ell}(\sigma_{n}(b))\rrbracket=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\mathcal{A}^{*},\,a\in\mathcal{A}:\\
pa\preceq\sigma_{n}(b)\end{subarray}}\tilde{\pi}_{n}\big(\boldsymbol{\ell}(p)+\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket\big)\qquad(n\in\mathbb{Z},\,b\in\mathcal{A}),

where the union is disjoint up to the endpoints of the line segments.

Proof.

The line segments
\boldsymbol{\ell}(p)+\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket
with
pa\preceq\sigma_{n}(b)
form a broken line from
\mathbf{0}
to
\boldsymbol{\ell}(\sigma_{n}(b))
.
Since
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}
and
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
,
\tilde{\pi}_{n}
projects these line segments to successive line segments of
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}
between
\mathbf{0}
and
\tilde{\pi}_{n}\boldsymbol{\ell}(\sigma_{n}(b))
, and their union is
\llbracket\tilde{\pi}_{n}\boldsymbol{\ell}(\sigma_{n}(b))\rrbracket=\tilde{\pi}_{n}\llbracket\boldsymbol{\ell}(\sigma_{n}(b))\rrbracket
.
∎

Note that, while the union in (
12.4
) is disjoint in measure, some work and additional conditions are needed to show that (
12.3
) is disjoint up to sets of measure zero; in
[
BST19
, Proposition 7.3]
, this is proved under the so-called PRICE property, see Section
12.4
.

Suspending the subtile
\mathcal{R}_{n}(a)
with the line segment
\tilde{\pi}_{n}\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket
, we obtain Rauzy boxes.

Definition 12.5

(Rauzy box)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
d\geq 2
, be a primitive sequence of unimodular substitutions over the alphabet
\mathcal{A}
that admits generalized right and left eigenvectors.
For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the
Rauzy box
(of level
n
) is

\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}=\bigcup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a),

with cylinders
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a)
that are defined as the Minkowski sums

(12.5)

\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a)=\tilde{\pi}_{n}\,\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket-\mathcal{R}_{n}(a)\qquad(n\in\mathbb{Z},\,a\in\mathcal{A}).

Note that the minus sign in the definition of Rauzy boxes is due to the fact that Rauzy fractals will be regarded as duals of the intervals. Rauzy boxes are studied in
[
BST19
]
; they can also be defined for one-sided sequences, by replacing the generalized left eigenvector
\mathbf{v}_{n}
by an arbitary vector
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
.
In the present paper, Rauzy boxes have several applications.
Indeed, each member of the partition by Rauzy boxes has a pair of horizontal and vertical transverses which allows the expression of Property M from Definition
6.7
in an easy way; see the proof of Theorem
15.1
.
Moreover, the tiling property for Rauzy fractals is equivalent to the tiling property for Rauzy boxes; see Proposition
12.8
, working modulo
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
will provide a cut-and-stack process; see Figure
16
and Section
15.1
. Note that Rauzy boxes generalize classical
L
-shaped constructions for fundamental domains for 2-dimensional lattices such as described in
[
Arn94
]
; they are also known as Klotz constructions, see e.g.
[
Kra88
]
.

12.2.
Tilings by Rauzy fractals and Rauzy boxes, and restacking

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
d\geq 2
, be a primitive sequence of unimodular substitutions over the alphabet
\mathcal{A}
with generalized right and left eigenvectors
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{v}
respectively.
In this section, we recall fundamental tiling properties of the
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy boxes and Rauzy fractals.
Our main interest in this paper lies in the collection

(12.6)

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}=\{\mathbf{x}+\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a)\,:\,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,a\in\mathcal{A}\}\qquad(n\in\mathbb{Z})

of Rauzy boxes.
Recall that a
tiling
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a collection of closed sets (so-called “tiles”) that covers
\mathbb{R}^{d}
in a way that the intersection of any two distinct tiles has Lebesgue measure
0
.
If
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
forms a tiling of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
is a fundamental domain of
\mathbb{R}^{d}/\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
The following
tiling condition
will be important for us.

Definition 12.6

(Tiling condition)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
d\geq 2
.
If
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is primitive, admits generalized right and left eigenvectors, and the collection
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
tiles
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, then we say that the
tiling condition
holds for
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
.

We will see in Proposition
12.8
below that the tiling property of
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
is equivalent to a tiling property of the collection of Rauzy fractals

(12.7)

\mathcal{C}_{n}=\{\pi_{n}\mathbf{x}+\mathcal{R}_{n}(a)\,:\,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,a\in\mathcal{A},\,0\leq\langle\mathbf{v}_{n},\mathbf{x}\rangle<\langle\mathbf{v}_{n},\mathbf{e}_{a}\rangle\}.

The elements of
\mathcal{C}_{n}
are subsets of the hyperplane
\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}
, and a tiling of a hyperplane
\mathbf{w}^{\perp}
is defined in the same way as a tiling of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
but with the intersections of tiles having zero
(d{-}1)
-dimensional Lebesgue measure.
The set of pairs
\{(\mathbf{x},a)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}{\times}\mathcal{A}:0\leq\langle\mathbf{v}_{n},\mathbf{x}\rangle<\langle\mathbf{v}_{n},\mathbf{e}_{a}\rangle\}
is called the
discrete hyperplane
approximating the hyperplane
\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}
.
This set is aperiodic whenever the coordinates of
\mathbf{v}_{n}
are rationally independent.
Note that
0\leq\langle\mathbf{v}_{n},\mathbf{x}\rangle\leq\langle\mathbf{v}_{n},\mathbf{e}_{a}\rangle
is equivalent to
\tilde{\pi}_{n}\mathbf{x}\in\tilde{\pi}_{n}\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket
.

In the tiling properties, we do not assume boundedness of the Rauzy fractals. However, if we assume in addition that the language
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
is balanced, Lemma
12.2
yields boundedness, hence compactness of the Rauzy fractals
\mathcal{R}_{n}
and of the Rauzy boxes
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
.
Although later we will require balancedness in some of our results, we did not include it in the definition of the tiling condition because there exist interesting “unbalanced” examples, whose Rauzy fractals deserve to be studied; see for instance
[
CFZ00
,
And21
]
.

The following proposition gives a condition for the tiling property of
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
being independent of
n
.
For the proof, we define subtiles of
\mathcal{R}_{n}(a)
and
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a)
according to (
12.3
).
Let

(12.8)

E_{n}=\big\{(a,p,b)\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}^{*}\times\mathcal{A}\,:\,pa\preceq\sigma_{n}(b)\big\}\qquad(n\in\mathbb{Z})

be the set of prefixes of the substitution
\sigma_{n}
and define, for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
(a,p,b)\in E_{n}
,

(12.9)

\mathcal{R}_{n}(a,p,b)=\pi_{n}\boldsymbol{\ell}(p)+M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathcal{R}_{n+1}(b),\quad\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a,p,b)=\tilde{\pi}_{n}\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket-\mathcal{R}_{n}(a,p,b).

By (
12.3
), we have, for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
a\in\mathcal{A}
,

(12.10)

\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\mathcal{A}^{*},\,b\in\mathcal{A}:\\
(a,p,b)\in E_{n}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{R}_{n}(a,p,b)=\mathcal{R}_{n}(a)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\mathcal{A}^{*},\,b\in\mathcal{A}:\\
(a,p,b)\in E_{n}\end{subarray}}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a,p,b)=\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a).

We set

(12.11)

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\mathrm{gen}}=\big\{\mathbf{x}+\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a,p,b)\,:\,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,(a,p,b)\in E_{n}\big\}

because this collection will define generating Markov partitions in Section
15.2
.

Proposition 12.7
.

Assume that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
is primitive and has generalized right and left eigenvectors. For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the following are equivalent.

(i)

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n+1}
forms a tiling of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(ii)

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\mathrm{gen}}
forms a tiling of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(iii)

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
forms a tiling of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the union in (
12.3
) is disjoint in measure for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
By Lemma
12.4
and since
M_{\sigma_{n}}\tilde{\pi}_{n+1}=\tilde{\pi}_{n}M_{\sigma_{n}}
(cf.
[
BST19
, Lemma 5.2]
), we have, for each
b\in\mathcal{A}
, the topological partitions

(12.12)

\displaystyle M_{\sigma_{n}}\tilde{\pi}_{n+1}\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{b}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\tilde{\pi}_{n}M_{\sigma_{n}}\llbracket M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathbf{e}_{b}\rrbracket=\tilde{\pi}_{n}\llbracket\boldsymbol{\ell}(\sigma_{n}(b))\rrbracket=\hskip-10.00002pt\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\mathcal{A}^{*},\,a\in\mathcal{A}:\\
(a,p,b)\in E_{n}\end{subarray}}\hskip-10.00002pt\tilde{\pi}_{n}\big(\boldsymbol{\ell}(p){+}\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket\big),

\displaystyle M_{\sigma_{n}}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n+1}(b)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\pi}_{n}\llbracket\boldsymbol{\ell}(\sigma_{n}(b))\rrbracket-M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathcal{R}_{n+1}(b)

(12.13)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\mathcal{A}^{*},\,a\in\mathcal{A}:\\
(a,p,b)\in E_{n}\end{subarray}}\big(\boldsymbol{\ell}(p){+}\underbrace{\tilde{\pi}_{n}\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket{-}\pi_{n}\boldsymbol{\ell}(p){-}M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathcal{R}_{n+1}(b)}_{\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a,p,b)}\big).

Since
M_{\sigma_{n}}
is unimodular, the collection
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n+1}
forms a tiling if and only if
\{\mathbf{x}{+}M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathcal{R}_{n+1}(b):\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,b\in\mathcal{A}\}
forms a tiling, and this is equivalent to
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\mathrm{gen}}
being a tiling because the union in (
12.13
) is disjoint in measure for each
b\in\mathcal{A}
.
The collection
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\mathrm{gen}}
forms a tiling if and only if
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
forms a tiling and the union in (
12.10
) is disjoint in measure for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
, i.e., the union in (
12.3
) is disjoint in measure for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
.
∎

The proof of Proposition
12.7
is of particular interest because it uses a restacking procedure that will be of importance for the study of Markov partitions in Chapter
V
.
This is illustrated in Figure
16
for a sequence of Brun substitutions.
More precisely, Figure
16
(i) shows the partition
\{\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n+1}(b):b\in\mathcal{A}\}
of the Rauzy box
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n+1}
.
Multiplying by the incidence matrix
M_{\sigma_{n}}
, we obtain (ii), and subdividing according to (
12.13
) gives (iii). (In our example, only one cylinder is subdivided into two subcylinders.)
Figure
16
(iv) is obtained from (iii) by restacking, i.e., by subtracting the integer vector
\boldsymbol{\ell}(p)
from the subcylinder
\boldsymbol{\ell}(p){+}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a,p,b)
, for each
(a,p,b)\in E_{n}
. (In our example, only one vector
\boldsymbol{\ell}(p)
is nonzero and thus only one subcylinder is restacked.)
Finally, merging the subcylinders
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a,p,b)
with same
a
gives the partition
\{\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a):a\in\mathcal{A}\}
of the Rauzy box
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
, which is depicted in Figure
16
(v).
Note that the generalized right and left eigenvectors
\mathbf{u}_{n}
and
\mathbf{v}_{n}
, which define the hyperplane
\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}
containing
\mathcal{R}_{n}
and the direction
\mathbf{u}_{n}
of the “sides” of the Rauzy box
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
, are different from
\mathbf{u}_{n+1}
and
\mathbf{v}_{n+1}
:
\mathbf{u}_{n}=M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathbf{u}_{n+1}
,
\mathbf{v}_{n}=\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathbf{v}_{n+1}
.
However, both Rauzy boxes
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n+1}
as well as
M_{\sigma_{n}}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n+1}
are fundamental domains of
\mathbb{T}^{d}=\mathbb{R}^{d}/\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

(i)

(ii)

(iii)

(iv)              (v)

Figure 16.
The restacking process described after Proposition
12.7
, illustrated by the unordered Brun substitution
\sigma_{n}=\sigma_{\mathrm{U},12}
, with Rauzy boxes given by the periodic sequence of substitutions
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{\mathrm{U},12},\sigma_{\mathrm{U},23},\sigma_{\mathrm{U},31})^{\mathbb{Z}}
.
We see
\bigcup_{b\in\mathcal{A}}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n+1}(b)
in (i),
\bigcup_{b\in\mathcal{A}}M_{\sigma_{n}}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n+1}(b)
in (ii),
\bigcup_{(a,p,b)\in E_{n}}(\boldsymbol{\ell}(p){+}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a,p,b))
in (iii),
\bigcup_{(a,p,b)\in E_{n}}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a,p,b)
in (iv), and
\bigcup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a)
in (v). Since
\sigma_{n}(1)=1
,
\sigma_{n}(2)=21
,
\sigma_{n}(3)=3
, we have
E_{n}=\{(1,\epsilon,1),(2,\epsilon,2),

(1,2,2),(3,\epsilon,3)\}
, where
\epsilon
denotes the empty word.

We next prove that the tiling properties of
\mathcal{C}_{n}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
are equivalent.
This result is proved in
[
BST19
, Proposition 7.6]
for compact tiles and goes back to
[
IR06
]
in the substitutive setting.

Proposition 12.8
.

Assume that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
is primitive and has generalized right and left eigenvectors. For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the collection
\mathcal{C}_{n}
forms a tiling of
\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}
if and only if
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
forms a tiling of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proof.

For all
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,
a\in\mathcal{A}
, we have

(\mathbf{x}{+}\tilde{\pi}\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket)\cap(\mathbf{z}{+}\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp})=\begin{cases}\{\pi_{n}\mathbf{x}{+}\tilde{\pi}_{n}\mathbf{z}\}&\mbox{if}\ \langle\mathbf{v}_{n},\mathbf{x}\rangle\leq\langle\mathbf{v}_{n},\mathbf{z}\rangle\leq\langle\mathbf{v}_{n},\mathbf{x}{+}\mathbf{e}_{a}\rangle,\\
\emptyset&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Since the second inequality in the defintion of
\mathcal{C}_{n}
in (
12.7
) is strict, we slightly modify the definition of the (collection of) Rauzy boxes by setting

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{n}=\{\mathbf{x}{+}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{n}(a):\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,a\in\mathcal{A}\},\quad\mbox{with}\quad\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{n}(a)=\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a)\setminus(\tilde{\pi}_{n}\mathbf{e}_{a}{-}\mathcal{R}_{n}(a)).

Then we have

(\mathbf{x}{+}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{n}(a))\cap(\mathbf{z}{+}\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp})=\begin{cases}\tilde{\pi}_{n}\mathbf{z}{+}\pi_{n}\mathbf{x}{-}\mathcal{R}_{n}(a)&\mbox{if}\ 0\leq\langle\mathbf{v}_{n},\mathbf{z}{-}\mathbf{x}\rangle<\langle\mathbf{v}_{n},\mathbf{e}_{a}\rangle,\\
\emptyset&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Since
\tilde{\pi}_{n}\mathbf{z}{+}\pi_{n}\mathbf{x}{+}\mathcal{R}_{n}(a)=\mathbf{z}-(\pi_{n}(\mathbf{z}{-}\mathbf{x}){+}\mathcal{R}_{n}(a))
, this means that the intersection of
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{n}
with
\mathbf{z}{+}\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}
is the translation of
-\mathcal{C}_{n}
by
\mathbf{z}
.

Assume first that
\mathcal{C}_{n}
is not a tiling of
\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}
.
If
(\pi_{n}\mathbf{x}{-}\mathcal{R}_{n}(a))\cap(\pi_{n}\mathbf{y}{-}\mathcal{R}_{n}(b))
has positive measure for distinct
(-\mathbf{x},a),(-\mathbf{y},b)
in the discrete hyperplane approximating
\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}
, then we use the fact that the line segment
(\tilde{\pi}_{n}(\mathbf{x}{+}\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket))\cap(\tilde{\pi}_{n}(\mathbf{y}{+}\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{b}\rrbracket))
has positive length (since
\max\{\langle\mathbf{v}_{n},\mathbf{x}\rangle,\langle\mathbf{v}_{n},\mathbf{y}\rangle\}\leq 0<\min\{\langle\mathbf{v}_{n},\mathbf{x}{+}\mathbf{e}_{a}\rangle,\langle\mathbf{v}_{n},\mathbf{y}{+}\mathbf{e}_{b}\rangle\}
) to obtain that
(\mathbf{x}{+}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a))\cap(\mathbf{y}{+}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(b))
has positive measure.
If
\mathcal{C}_{n}
does not cover a point
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}
, then
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{n}
does not cover
\mathbf{z}
and thus there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
does not cover
\mathbf{z}{+}\varepsilon\mathbf{u}_{n}
.
In both cases, the collection
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
is not a tiling of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Assume now that
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
is not a tiling of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Assume that
(\mathbf{x}{+}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{n}(a))\cap(\mathbf{y}{+}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{n}(b))
has positive measure for distinct
(\mathbf{x},a),(\mathbf{y},b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}{\times}\mathcal{A}
.
The set
\{\langle\mathbf{v}_{n},\mathbf{z}\rangle:\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\}
is either dense in
\mathbb{R}
or equal to
c\mathbb{Z}
for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
.
In both cases, we have some
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
such that
\max\{\langle\mathbf{v}_{n},\mathbf{x}\rangle,\langle\mathbf{v}_{n},\mathbf{y}\rangle\}\leq\langle\mathbf{v}_{n},\mathbf{z}\rangle<\min\{\langle\mathbf{v}_{n},\mathbf{x}{+}\mathbf{e}_{a}\rangle,\langle\mathbf{v}_{n},\mathbf{y}{+}\mathbf{e}_{b}\rangle\}
, thus
(\pi_{n}(\mathbf{z}{-}\mathbf{x}){+}\mathcal{R}_{n}(a))\cap(\pi_{n}(\mathbf{z}{-}\mathbf{y}){+}\mathcal{R}_{n}(b))
has positive measure and both
(\mathbf{z}{-}\mathbf{x},a)
,
(\mathbf{z}{-}\mathbf{x},b)
are in the discrete hyperplane approximating
\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}
.
Similarly, if
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
does not cover
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{n}
does not cover
\mathbf{z}{+}\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}
for some
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and thus
\mathcal{C}_{n}
does not cover
\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}
.
In both cases, the collection
\mathcal{C}_{n}
is not a tiling of
\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}
.
∎

12.3.
Tiling implies pure discrete spectrum

Now, we show that the tiling condition implies that
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)},\Sigma)
is measurably conjugate to a rotation on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
and, hence, that it has pure discrete spectrum.

More precisely, we use that the projections of the Rauzy fractals
\mathcal{R}_{n}(a)
to the hyperplane
\mathbf{1}^{\perp}
tile this hyperplane periodically.
More generally, for a sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
admitting a generalized right eigenvector and, for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}
, we consider the collection of tiles

\mathcal{C}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}=\{\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{w}}\mathbf{x}+\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}(a)\,:\,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,a\in\mathcal{A},\,0\leq\langle\mathbf{w},\mathbf{x}\rangle<\langle\mathbf{w},\mathbf{e}_{a}\rangle\}

in
\mathbf{w}^{\perp}
, where

\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}(a)=\overline{\{\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{w}}\boldsymbol{\ell}(p)\,:\,pa\preceq\sigma_{[n,m)}(b)\ \mbox{for infinitely many}\ m\geq n,\,b\in\mathcal{A}\}}.

If
\mathbf{w}
is the generalized left eigenvector
\mathbf{v}_{n}
, then we recover the collection
\mathcal{C}_{n}
of (
12.7
).
Choosing
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{1}
gives a periodic collection of tiles because, for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,
a\in\mathcal{A}
,
0\leq\langle\mathbf{1},\mathbf{x}\rangle<\langle\mathbf{1},\mathbf{e}_{a}\rangle
is equivalent to
\langle\mathbf{1},\mathbf{x}\rangle=0
, and
\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}^{\perp}
is a lattice of
\mathbf{1}^{\perp}
spanned by the vectors
\mathbf{e}_{b}{-}\mathbf{e}_{a}
,
b\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{a\}
.
We also set
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}=\bigcup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}(a)
. Moreover, we define

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}=\{\mathbf{x}+\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}(a)\,:\,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,a\in\mathcal{A}\},\quad\mbox{with}\quad\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}(a)=\tilde{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{w}}\,\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket-\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}(a),

and we set
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}=\bigcup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}(a)
.
We will see in Proposition
12.10
that the tiling property of
\mathcal{C}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}
does not depend on
\mathbf{w}
.
In particular,
\mathcal{C}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}
is a tiling if and only if
\mathcal{C}_{n}
is a tiling (and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
admits a generalized left eigenvector).

For
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
, define the rotation

\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}:\,\mathbb{T}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{T}^{d-1},\ \mathbf{z}\mapsto\mathbf{z}+\boldsymbol{\alpha}.

Theorem 12.9
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
d\geq 2
, be a primitive sequence of unimodular substitutions that admits a generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{u}
, and let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
If
\mathcal{C}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}
forms a tiling of
\mathbf{1}^{\perp}
, then the uniquely ergodic shift
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)},\Sigma)
is measurably conjugate to the minimal rotation
(\mathbb{T}^{d-1},\mathfrak{r}_{(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{d-1})})
, with
(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{d-1},1{-}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\alpha_{i})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\mathbf{u}_{n}
, and
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)},\Sigma)
has pure discrete spectrum.

Proof.

We follow partially
[
BST19
, Section 8]
.
By Lemma
11.12
,
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)},\Sigma)
is minimal and uniquely ergodic.

Next we establish the measurable conjugacy of
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)},\Sigma,\mu)
to the domain exchange
(\mathcal{R}_{n},\mathfrak{h}_{n},\lambda_{\mathbf{1}})
with

(12.14)

\mathfrak{h}_{n}:\ \mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}\to\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}},\quad\mathbf{z}\mapsto\mathbf{z}+\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{1}}\,\mathbf{e}_{a}\quad\mbox{if}\ \mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}(a).

and
\lambda_{\mathbf{1}}
the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbf{1}^{\perp}
, normalized such that the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}^{\perp}
has covolume
1
.
The mapping
\mathfrak{h}_{n}
is well defined a.e. on
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}
because
\mathcal{C}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}
forms a tiling.
By the definition of
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}(a)
, we have that, up to measure zero,

\mathfrak{h}_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}(a))=\overline{\{\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{1}}\boldsymbol{\ell}(pa)\,:\,pa\preceq\sigma_{[n,m)}(b)\ \mbox{for infinitely many}\ m\geq n,\,b\in\mathcal{A}\}}.

Therefore,
\mathfrak{h}_{n}
is a piecewise isometry with
\bigcup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathfrak{h}_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}(a))=\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}
, hence bijective up to measure zero, and its invariant measure is
\lambda_{\mathbf{1}}
.
The representation map

(12.15)

\psi_{n}:\,\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}\to X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)},\ \mathbf{z}\mapsto(\omega_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\ \mbox{such that}\ \mathfrak{h}_{n}^{k}(\mathbf{z})\in\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}(\omega_{k})\ \mbox{for all}\ k\in\mathbb{Z}

is well defined outside the set

D_{n}=\bigcup_{a,b\in\mathcal{A},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\big(\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{1}}\mathbf{y}+(\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}(a)\cap\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}(b))\big),

which has zero Lebesgue measure because
\mathcal{C}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}
is a tiling.
On
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}\setminus D_{n}
, we obviously have
\psi_{n}\circ\mathfrak{h}_{n}=\Sigma\circ\psi_{n}
.
Therefore,
\lambda_{\mathbf{1}}\circ\psi_{n}^{-1}
is an invariant measure of
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)},\Sigma)
and thus equal to the unique invariant measure
\mu
.

Since
\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{1}}\mathbf{e}_{a}\equiv\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{1}}\mathbf{e}_{b}\bmod{\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}^{\perp}}
for all
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
, this implies that the domain exchange
(\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}},\mathfrak{h}_{n},\lambda_{\mathbf{1}})
is measurably conjugate to the rotation by
\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{1}}\mathbf{e}_{d}
on
\mathbf{1}^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}^{\perp})
.
Since
\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{1}}\mathbf{e}_{d}=\mathbf{e}_{d}{-}\mathbf{u}_{n}/\lVert\mathbf{u}_{n}\rVert_{1}=(-\alpha_{1},\dots,-\alpha_{d-1},\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\alpha_{i})
, this rotation is conjugate to the rotation by
(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{d-1})
on
\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
, via the map
(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\mapsto(-x_{1},\dots,-x_{d-1})
.

Moreover,
\mathbf{u}_{n}
has rationally independent coordinates because otherwise the Rauzy fractal
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}
would lie in a countable union of
(d{-}2)
-dimensional subspaces of the
(d{-}1)
-dimensional space
\mathbf{1}^{\perp}
, contradicting the tiling property.
Therefore, the rotation is minimal.
∎

It is conjectured that pure discrete spectrum always holds under the conditions of Proposition
12.13
. This hard and unsolved problem is known as the
\mathcal{S}
-adic Pisot conjecture
; see Conjecture
18.1
.

The tiling property for
\mathcal{C}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}
can be replaced by that for
\mathcal{C}_{n}
due to the following proposition.
Moreover, this proposition shows that the tiling property of
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
depends only on the future
(\sigma_{n},\sigma_{n+1},\dots)
of the sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
.

Proposition 12.10
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
d\geq 2
, be a primitive sequence of unimodular substitutions that admits a generalized right eigenvector, let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}
.
Then the following are equivalent.

(i)

\mathcal{C}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}
forms a tiling of
\mathbf{w}^{\perp}
.

(ii)

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}
forms a tiling of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

(iii)

\mathcal{C}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}
forms a tiling of
\mathbf{1}^{\perp}
.

Proof.

First note that the projections
\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{w}}
are well defined since
\langle\mathbf{w},\mathbf{u}_{n}\rangle>0
.
Then the equivalence between (
i
) and (
ii
) follows directly from Proposition
12.8
and its proof; it suffices to replace
\mathbf{v}_{n}
by
\mathbf{w}
.
Therefore, we only have to show that the tiling property of
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}
does not depend on
\mathbf{w}
(for
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}
).

Let
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}
.
Since
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}=\bigcup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}(\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{1}}\boldsymbol{\ell}(a){+}\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}(a))
(see the proof of Theorem
12.9
), we can find a sequence
(\omega_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}}
such that

\mathbf{z}+\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{1}}\,\mathbf{y}_{k}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}(\omega_{k})\ \mbox{for all}\ k\in\mathbb{Z},\quad\mbox{with}\ \mathbf{y}_{k}=\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{\ell}(\omega_{0}\cdots\omega_{k-1})&\mbox{if}\ k\geq 0,\\
-\boldsymbol{\ell}(\omega_{k}\cdots\omega_{-1})&\mbox{if}\ k<0.\end{cases}

Since
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}=\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{w}}\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}
, we get that
\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{z}{+}\mathbf{y}_{k})\in\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}(\omega_{k})
, thus

\begin{gathered}\tilde{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{y}_{k}{+}\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{\omega_{k}}\rrbracket)-\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{w}}\mathbf{z}\subset\mathbf{y}_{k}+\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}(\omega_{k})\quad\mbox{for all}\ k\in\mathbb{Z},\\
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}_{n}-\mathbf{z}=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\big(\tilde{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{y}_{k}{+}\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{\omega_{k}}\rrbracket)-\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{w}}\mathbf{z}\big)\subset\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\big(\mathbf{y}_{k}+\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}(\omega_{k})\big).\end{gathered}

Note that changing
\mathbf{w}
does not change the decomposition of the line, only the length of the line segments.
We obtain that
\mathbb{Z}^{d}{+}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}=\mathbb{Z}^{d}{+}\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}_{n}{-}\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}
.
Since this set does not depend on
\mathbf{w}
, the covering property of
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}
does not depend on
\mathbf{w}
.

Assume now that
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}
is a covering.
Then
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}
is not a tiling if and only if there is a subset of positive measure of one tile that has an intersection of positive measure with another tile of
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}
.
By
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-invariance, we can assume that this set is contained in
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}_{n}{-}\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}
.
Therefore, we consider w.l.o.g. a subset
\tilde{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x}{+}\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket){-}\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{w}}Z
of
\mathbf{x}{+}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}(a)
with
(\mathbf{x},a)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}{\times}\mathcal{A}
,
Z\subset\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}(a)
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{1}}(Z)>0
.
Then, we can, similarly to the previous paragraph, find a sequence
(\omega_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}}
such that

\mathcal{\lambda}_{\mathbf{1}}\big((\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{1}}\mathbf{y}_{k}{+}Z)\cap\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}(\omega_{k})\big)>0\ \mbox{for all}\ k\in\mathbb{Z},\ \mbox{with}\ \mathbf{y}_{k}=\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{\ell}(\omega_{0}\cdots\omega_{k-1})&\hskip-5.0pt\mbox{if}\ k\geq 0,\\
-\boldsymbol{\ell}(\omega_{k}\cdots\omega_{-1})&\hskip-5.0pt\mbox{if}\ k<0,\end{cases}

and we have, for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\tilde{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{y}_{k}{+}\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{\omega_{k}}\rrbracket)-\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{w}}\big(Z\cap(\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}(\omega_{k}){-}\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{1}}\mathbf{y}_{k})\big)\subset\mathbf{y}_{k}+\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}(\omega_{k}).

Since
\mathbf{y}_{k}{+}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}(\omega_{k})\subset\mathbf{y}_{k}{+}\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{\omega_{k}}\rrbracket{+}\mathbf{w}^{\perp}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, the intersection of two distinct tiles in
\{\mathbf{y}_{k}{+}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}(\omega_{k}):k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
has zero measure.
Therefore, we can assume w.l.o.g. that
(\mathbf{x},a)\notin\{(\mathbf{y}_{k},\omega_{k}):k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.
Now, changing
\mathbf{w}
does not change the property that
\tilde{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x}{+}\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket){-}\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{w}}Z
is a subset of
\mathbf{x}{+}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{a})
and has intersection of positive measure with
\mathbf{y}_{k}{+}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}(\omega_{k})
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
(with
k
depending on
\mathbf{w}
); here, we use that the coordinates of
\mathbf{w}
and thus the lengths of
\tilde{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{w}}\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket
,
a\in\mathcal{A}
, are positive for all
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}
.
This proves that the tiling property of
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}
does not depend on
\mathbf{w}
; in particular, it holds for
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{1}
if and only if it holds for some
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}
.
∎

A similar result to Proposition
12.10
can be found in
[
BST19
, Proposition 7.5]
for
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
, with some additional conditions and a completely different proof.
In our case, it is not difficult to see that the measure of the symmetric difference of
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}
is small when
\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
are close.
This implies that
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\mathbf{w}}
is a tiling even for
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
when
\mathcal{C}_{n}^{\mathbf{1}}
is a tiling, but we do not know if the converse holds.

Theorem
12.9
and Proposition
12.10
give the following corollary.

Corollary 12.11
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
d\geq 2
, be a primitive sequence of unimodular substitutions that admits generalized left and right eigenvectors.
If
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
forms a tiling of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, then the uniquely ergodic shift
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)},\Sigma)
is measurably conjugate to the minimal rotation
(\mathbb{T}^{d-1},\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{d-1})})
, with
(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{d-1},1{-}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\alpha_{i})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\mathbf{u}_{n}
, and
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)},\Sigma)
has pure discrete spectrum.

12.4.
Sufficient conditions for tilings and property PRICE

There are several criteria for tiling that can be expressed in terms of so-called
coincidence conditions
that exist in the usual Pisot framework; see e.g.
[
AL11
,
ABB+15
]
.
In
[
BST19
,
BST23
]
, sufficient conditions for the collections
\mathcal{C}_{n}
to be tilings are given.
These conditions require the so-called property PRICE, for which we introduce a two-sided version that implies the one-sided property PRICE of
[
BST23
, Definition 4.2]
but is simpler to state.
We use the notation

(12.16)

B_{C}^{(n)}=\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}\,:\,\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)}\ \mbox{is $C$-balanced}\}\qquad(n\in\mathbb{Z}).

Note that

(12.17)

\Sigma^{k}B_{C}^{(n)}=B_{C}^{(n-k)}\qquad(k,n\in\mathbb{Z}).

Definition 12.12

(Two-sided property PRICE)
.

Let
d\geq 2
and fix
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. We say that a sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\,{=}\,(\sigma_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\,{\in}\,\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
has the
two-sided property PRICE (at level
n
)
if there are strictly increasing sequences of positive integers
(\ell_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and of integers
(r_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that the following conditions hold.

•

There exists
h\in\mathbb{N}
and a positive matrix
M^{\prime}
such that
M_{\sigma_{[\ell_{k}-h,\ell_{k})}}=M^{\prime}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

We have
(\sigma_{r_{k}-\ell_{k}},\dots,\sigma_{r_{k}+\ell_{k}-1})=(\sigma_{n-\ell_{k}},\dots,\sigma_{n+\ell_{k}-1})
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

The sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is algebraically irreducible.

•

There exists
C>0
such that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in B_{C}^{(r_{k}+\ell_{k})}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

\boldsymbol{\sigma}
admits a generalized left eigenvector
\mathbf{v}
.

We recall some properties implied by PRICE that are proved in
[
BST19
,
BST23
]
for the one-sided property PRICE defined in
[
BST19
, Definition 5.8]
.

Proposition 12.13
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and assume that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies the two-sided property PRICE at level
n
.
Then the following holds.

(i)

The sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
satisfies the two-sided property PRICE at all levels
m\geq n
.

(ii)

The sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
admits a generalized right eigenvector.

(iii)

The sequence
(\sigma_{n},\sigma_{n+1},\dots)
satisfies the one-sided property PRICE.

(iv)

For each
a\in\mathcal{A}
, the subtile
\mathcal{R}_{n}(a)
of the Rauzy fractal is a compact set that is the closure of its interior, its boundary has zero Lebesgue measure, and the union in (
12.3
) is disjoint up to sets of measure zero.

(v)

The collection
\mathcal{C}_{n}
forms a multiple tiling of
\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}
, i.e., there exists
c\geq 1
such that each point of
\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}
is contained in at least
c
elements of
\mathcal{C}_{n}
and a.e. point of
\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}
is contained in exactly
c
elements of
\mathcal{C}_{n}
.

Proof.

Since
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
satisfies the property PRICE at level
n
if and only if
\Sigma^{n}\boldsymbol{\sigma}
satisfies the property PRICE at level
0
, we can assume w.l.o.g. that
n=0
.

Then, for each
m\geq 0
,
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
has the property PRICE at level
m
with sequences
(\ell_{k+m}{-}m)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(r_{k+m}{+}m)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, hence (
i
) holds.
Since
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is primitive and eventually recurrent by (P) and (R), it has a generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{u}
by Proposition
7.4
, i.e., (
ii
) holds.
We have

\mathbf{v}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\vphantom{\!M}{}^{t}\!M_{\sigma_{[-\ell_{k},0)}}\mathbf{v}}{\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{\sigma_{[-\ell_{k},0)}}\mathbf{v}\rVert}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\vphantom{\!M}{}^{t}\!M_{\sigma_{[r_{k}-\ell_{k},r_{k})}}\mathbf{v}}{\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{\sigma_{[r_{k}-\ell_{k},r_{k})}}\mathbf{v}\rVert}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\vphantom{\!M}{}^{t}\!M_{\sigma_{[0,r_{k})}}\mathbf{v}}{\lVert\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{\sigma_{[0,r_{k})}}\mathbf{v}\rVert}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\mathbf{v}_{r_{k}}}{\lVert\mathbf{v}_{r_{k}}\rVert}

when
\mathbf{v}
is the generalized left eigenvector of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
with
\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert=1
.
Therefore, we obtain that
(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\dots)
satisfies the one-sided property PRICE of
[
BST19
, Definition 5.8]
, i.e., (
iii
) holds.
Now, (
iv
) follows from Lemma
12.2
and

[
BST19
, Propositions 6.4, 7.3 and 7.5]
.
Since our collection
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is the collection
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{v}}
from
[
BST19
]
, (
v
) follows from
[
BST19
, Proposition 7.5]
.
∎

Combining Propositions
12.7
,
12.8
and
12.13
, we obtain the following result giving an important sufficient criterion for the tiling condition of Definition
12.6
.

Proposition 12.14
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
d\geq 2
, be a primitive sequence of unimodular substitutions.
If, for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies the two-sided property PRICE at level
n
and
\mathcal{C}_{n}
forms a tiling, then
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
satisfies the tiling condition.

It is a priori not clear how to decide for a given directive sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
if the multiple tiling given by Proposition
12.13
(
v
) is actually a tiling.
In the case where
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is a constant sequence given by the repetition of a single Pisot substitution
\tau
, the tiling property can be checked e.g. with the balanced pair algorithm; see
[
BST23
, Section 6.1]
for examples of application for substitutions associated to continued fractions.
For sequences
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
satisfying the property PRICE, the following (effective version of the)
geometric coincidence condition
defined in
[
BST23
, Definition 4.6]
is equivalent to the tiling property.

Definition 12.15

(Geometric coincidence condition)
.

A sequence of substitutions
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
d\geq 2
, satisfies the
geometric coincidence condition
if it admits a generalized right eigenvector and there are
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbf{1}^{\perp}
,
a\in\mathcal{A}
,
C>0
, with the following property.
We have
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in B_{C}^{(n)}
and, for each
(\mathbf{y},b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}{\times}\mathcal{A}
satisfying
\lVert\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{1}}M_{\sigma_{[0,n)}}^{-1}\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{z}\rVert_{\infty}\leq C
and
0\leq\langle\mathbf{1},\mathbf{y}\rangle<|\sigma_{[0,n)}(b)|
, we have
\mathbf{y}=\boldsymbol{\ell}(p)
for some
p\in\mathcal{A}^{*}
such that
pa\preceq\sigma_{[0,n)}(b)
.

Proposition 12.16
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
d\geq 2
, be a primitive sequence of unimodular substitutions that satisfies the two-sided property PRICE at level
0
.
Then
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
satisfies the tiling condition if and only if it satisfies the geometric coincidence condition.

Proof.

This follows by combining
[
BST23
, Proposition 4.8]
with Propositions
12.13
and
12.14
.
∎

In
[
BST23
]
, we used this result to infer the tiling condition for a sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
containing large power of a substitution
\tau
from the tiling condition for the constant sequence of
\tau
’s.
More precisely, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 12.17
.

Let
\tau
be a unimodular Pisot substitution such that the substitutive dynamical system generated by
\tau
has pure discrete spectrum.
For each
C>0
, there exists
m>0
with the following property:
If
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies the two-sided property PRICE at level
0
and there are
\ell,n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\sigma_{[n,n+\ell)}=\tau^{m}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in B_{C}^{(n+\ell)}
, then
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
satisfies the tiling condition.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of
[
BST23
, Lemma 5.6]
, since “pure discrete spectrum” is equivalent for substitutive systems to the “geometric coincidence condition” by
[
BST23
, Lemma 4.9]
, the one-sided property PRICE follows from the two-sided one by Proposition
12.13
(
iii
), and the geometric coincidence condition (which is equivalent to the effective one by
[
BST23
, Proposition 4.8]
) implies the tiling condition by Proposition
12.14
.
∎

The following result from
[
BST23
]
gives a way to construct substitutions with pure discrete spectrum.

Proposition 12.18

(
[
BST23
, Proposition 5.9]
)
.

Let
M
be a nonnegative unimodular Pisot matrix and
m\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large.
Then there exists a substitution
\sigma
with incidence matrix
M_{\sigma}=M^{m}
that has pure discrete spectrum.
30
30

30

Again in the statement of
[
BST23
, Proposition 5.9]
instead of “pure discrete spectrum” the “geometric coincidence condition” is assumed. However, by
[
BST23
, Lemma 4.9]
these two conditions are equivalent.

13.
Metric theory for bi-infinite sequences of substitutions

In this section, we revisit the metric results of Section
8
by considering sequences of substitutions instead of sequences of matrices, i.e., considering
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
instead of
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. In Section
13.1
we introduce some terminology and relate the positive range property of a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm to an analogous property for subshifts of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. Section
13.2
is devoted to consequences of the Pisot condition and contains Theorem
13.6
, a substitutive version of Theorem
8.15
, providing the consequences of the Pisot condition in the substitutive setting. Apart from an additional result on balance, its conclusions are analogous to the ones of Theorem
8.15
. Theorem
13.6
is applied to the modified Jacobi–Perron algorithm to provide, among other results, strong convergence and an Anosov splitting for this algorithm. In Section
13.3
we give metric conditions for tiling and pure discrete spectrum for subshifts of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. These conditions strongly rely on the generic Pisot condition and, as we will see in Section
13.4
, can be slightly weakened if we accelerate the shift. Finally, in Section
13.5
we provide a metric result on the tiling condition and on pure discrete spectrum of the underlying
\mathcal{S}
-adic dynamical systems in the framework of multidimensional continued fraction algorithms.

13.1.
Some terminology

Let
d\geq 2
.
Let
D
be a
\Sigma
-invariant subset (where
\Sigma
is the shift operator) of the space of bi-infinite sequences of substitutions
\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
and let
\nu
be an ergodic
\Sigma
-invariant measure on
D
. Then
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
defines a measure-theoretic dynamical system (which must not be confused with the
\mathcal{S}
-adic shift
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma,\mu)
defined in Section
11.1
).
Of particular importance for us will be the following cocycles defined by the incidence matrices of the substitutions, to be compared with the cocycles introduced in Section
8.3
for sequences of matrices.

Definition 13.1

(
\mathcal{S}
-adic cocycles)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{S}_{d}
for some
d\geq 2
.
Let
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
be an ergodic measure-theoretic dynamical system with
D\subset\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.

•

The linear cocycle
A_{\mathrm{tr}}:\,D\to\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})
,
(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mapsto\vphantom{\!M}^{t}\!M_{\sigma_{0}}
, is called the
(
\mathcal{S}
-adic) transpose cocycle
.

•

The linear cocycle
A_{\mathrm{inv}}:\,D\to\operatorname{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})
,
(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mapsto M_{\sigma_{0}}^{-1}
, is called the
(
\mathcal{S}
-adic) inverse cocycle
.

The iterates of
A_{\mathrm{tr}}
and
A_{\mathrm{inv}}
are defined analogously to (
8.7
).

To state our main theorems, we need a few more definitions that are in analogy to some definitions provided in Chapters
II
and
III
in the context of sequences of matrices and multidimensional continued fraction algorithms, respectively.

The following
periodic Pisot sequence
corresponds to a Pisot toral automorphism and a Pisot substitution, respectively.

Definition 13.2

(Periodic Pisot sequence)
.

A sequence
(M_{n})\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
[
(\sigma_{n})\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
] is called a
periodic Pisot sequence
if there is
k\geq 1
such that the sequence has period
k
and
M_{[0,k)}
is a Pisot matrix [
\sigma_{[0,k)}
is a Pisot substitution].

Next, we propose an adaptation of the positive range property for one-sided sequences used in
[
BST23
, Section 2.5]
to bi-infinite sequences.

Definition 13.3

(Cylinder set, two-sided positive range)
.

Let
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
be a dynamical system with
D\subset\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}

[D\subset\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}]
and a shift invariant Borel probability measure
\nu
.
Cylinder sets of
D
are defined by
31
31

31

Omitting the dependence on
D
should cause no confusion.

[\omega_{0},\dots,\omega_{n-1}]=\big\{(\upsilon_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\in D\,:\,(\upsilon_{0},\dots,\upsilon_{n-1})=(\omega_{0},\dots,\omega_{n-1})\big\}\qquad(n\in\mathbb{N})

(and images under
\Sigma^{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, of these sets).

A set
E\subset D
is called
left saturated
if
(\omega_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\in E
implies that
(\upsilon_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\in E
for all
(\upsilon_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\in D
such that
(\upsilon_{0},\upsilon_{1},\dots)=(\omega_{0},\omega_{1},\dots)
.

Moreover, we say that
(\omega_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\in D
has
two-sided positive range
in
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
if there is
\varepsilon>0
such that, for each left saturated set
F
with
\nu(E)\geq 1{-}\varepsilon
, we have

\nu(\Sigma^{n}[\omega_{0},\dots,\omega_{n-1}]\cap E)>0\quad\mbox{for all}\ n\in\mathbb{N}.

We recall that
D
is not necessarily closed. However, cylinder sets of
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
are measurable in
D
because they are open sets in the subspace topology on
D
. This is the reason why we assumed
\nu
to be a Borel measure.

In the following lemma, we discuss the relation between the two-sided positive range property for sequences and the (one-sided) positive range property for continued fraction algorithms defined in Definition
9.8
.

Lemma 13.4
.

Let
(X,F,A,\nu)
be a positive multidimensional continued fraction algorithm with
\nu\circ F\ll\nu
, let
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
be a faithful substitutive realization of a natural extension
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
of
(X,F,A,\nu)
, and let
(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})\in\widehat{X}
.
If
\mathbf{x}_{0}
has positive range in
(X,F,A,\nu)
, then
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})
has two-sided positive range in
(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X}),\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}\widehat{\nu})
.

Proof.

Let
0<\varepsilon<\inf_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\nu(F^{n}X_{[0,n)}(\mathbf{x}_{0}))
and consider a left saturated subset
E
of
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X})
with
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}\widehat{\nu}(E)\geq 1{-}\varepsilon
.
Let
E^{\prime}=\pi\circ\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{-1}(E)
; recall that
\pi:\widehat{X}\to X
is defined by
\pi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathbf{x}
.
Then
\nu(E^{\prime})=\widehat{\nu}(\pi^{-1}(E^{\prime}))\geq\widehat{\nu}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{-1}(E))\geq 1{-}\varepsilon
.
For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have thus
\nu(F^{n}X_{[0,n)}(\mathbf{x}_{0})\cap E^{\prime})>0
, hence
\nu(X_{[0,n)}(\mathbf{x}_{0})\cap F^{-n}E^{\prime})>0
by (
9.14
).
We have

\pi^{-1}\big(X_{[0,n)}(\mathbf{x}_{0})\cap F^{-n}E^{\prime}\big)=\pi^{-1}(X_{[0,n)}(\mathbf{x}_{0}))\cap\widehat{F}^{-n}(\pi^{-1}(E^{\prime}))

since
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\widehat{F}^{-n}\widehat{F}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\widehat{F}^{-n}(F^{n}(\mathbf{x}),A^{(n)}(\mathbf{x}_{0})\mathbf{y})
for
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\pi^{-1}(X_{[0,n)}(\mathbf{x}_{0}))
, thus
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\widehat{F}^{-n}(\pi^{-1}(E^{\prime}))
if and only if
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\pi^{-1}(F^{-n}(E^{\prime}))
.
We obtain that

\displaystyle\widehat{\nu}\big(\widehat{F}^{n}(\pi^{-1}(X_{[0,n)}(\mathbf{x}_{0}))\cap\pi^{-1}(E^{\prime})\big)=\widehat{\nu}\big(\pi^{-1}(X_{[0,n)}(\mathbf{x}_{0}))\cap\widehat{F}^{-n}(\pi^{-1}(E^{\prime}))\big)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\nu}\big(\pi^{-1}(X_{[0,n)}(\mathbf{x})\cap F^{-n}E^{\prime})\big)=\nu\big(X_{[0,n)}(\mathbf{x}_{0})\cap F^{-n}E^{\prime}\big)>0.

Writing
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, we have
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{F}^{n}\pi^{-1}(X_{[0,n)}(\mathbf{x}_{0})))=\Sigma^{n}[\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{n-1}]
.
Since
E
is left saturated, we have
E=\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\pi^{-1}(E^{\prime}))
, thus

\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}\widehat{\nu}(\Sigma^{n}[\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{n-1}]\cap E)=\widehat{\nu}\big(\widehat{F}^{n}(\pi^{-1}(X_{[0,n)}(\mathbf{x}_{0}))\cap\pi^{-1}(E^{\prime})\big)>0

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})
has two-sided positive range in
(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X}),\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}\widehat{\nu})
.
∎

13.2.
Generic Pisot condition for bi-infinite sequences of substitutions

According to Definition
8.6
, we consider the following Pisot condition for a dynamical system generating sequences of substitutions.

Definition 13.5

(Generic Pisot condition)
.

Let
d\geq 2
. An ergodic dynamical system
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
with
D\subset\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
is said to satisfy the
(generic) Pisot condition
(without qualifying the cocycle) if it satisfies the (generic) Pisot condition for the linear cocycle
A_{\mathrm{tr}}
.

By Remark
8.11
, the Pisot condition (for
A_{\mathrm{tr}}
) guarantees the existence of an invariant Oseledets splitting into a stable space of dimension
1
and an unstable space of codimension
1
for the cocycle
A_{\mathrm{inv}}
. This is one of the assertions of the following theorem, which summarizes important consequences of the Pisot condition; it is the substitution version of Theorem
8.15
. Note however that the present formulation brings more information with the balance property.

Theorem 13.6
.

Let
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
with
D\subset\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
d\geq 2
, be an ergodic dynamical system that has a cylinder
[\tau_{0},\ldots,\tau_{\ell-1}]
of positive measure corresponding to a substitution
\tau_{[0,\ell)}
with positive incidence matrix.
If
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
satisfies the Pisot condition, then the following assertions hold for
\nu
-almost every
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in D
.

(i)

The sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is two-sided primitive and algebraically irreducible (in the future).

(ii)

The language
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
is balanced.

(iii)

The sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
converges strongly to the generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{u}
in the future and to the generalized left eigenvector
\mathbf{v}
in the past.

(iv)

The mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
associated to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is eventually Anosov for the hyperbolic splitting

(13.1)

G^{s}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})\oplus G^{u}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}G_{n}^{s}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})\oplus G_{n}^{u}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})\ \hbox{with}\ G^{s}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})=\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}_{n}\ \hbox{and}\ G^{u}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})=\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp},

where
\mathbf{u}_{n}
and
\mathbf{v}_{n}
are the generalized right and left eigenvectors defined in (
7.3
).

(v)

There is a relation between the Anosov splitting of
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
and the Oseledets splitting (
8.5
) of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
for the cocycles
A_{\mathrm{inv}}
and
A_{\mathrm{tr}}
. In particular, for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
we have

(13.2)

\displaystyle G^{u}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})

\displaystyle=G_{\mathrm{inv}}^{1}(\Sigma^{n}\boldsymbol{\sigma})\oplus\cdots\oplus G_{\mathrm{inv}}^{p-1}(\Sigma^{n}\boldsymbol{\sigma})=\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp},

\displaystyle G^{s}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})

\displaystyle=G_{\mathrm{inv}}^{p}(\Sigma^{n}\boldsymbol{\sigma})=\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}_{n},

\displaystyle G^{u}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})^{\perp}

\displaystyle=G_{\mathrm{tr}}^{1}(\Sigma^{n}\boldsymbol{\sigma})=\mathbb{R}\mathbf{v}_{n},

\displaystyle G^{s}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})^{\perp}

\displaystyle=(G_{\mathrm{tr}}^{2}(\Sigma^{n}\boldsymbol{\sigma})\oplus\cdots\oplus G_{\mathrm{tr}}^{p}(\Sigma^{n}\boldsymbol{\sigma})=(\mathbf{u}_{n})^{\perp}.

Proof.

All items except (
ii
) depend only on the incidence matrices of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
and, hence, are direct consequences of the according items of Theorem
8.15
. Item (
ii
) follows from Lemma
8.13
(
ii
) and Theorem
11.8
(
iii
).
∎

Oseledets’ theorem only gives an “almost everywhere” result. Even if the Pisot condition holds, like for the Brun algorithm, there are sequences for which the balance condition does not hold (see e.g.
[
CFZ00
,
DHS13
]
). These exceptions constitute a set of measure zero, which is nevertheless dense. In fact, if, for a sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, there is a level
n
at which the Language
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)}
is not balanced, the same holds for all levels
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. This is true because, the incidence matrices
M_{\sigma_{n}}
are invertible, and induce an automorphism of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, hence, the “unbalance” of the language
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)}
propagates forward and backward on all levels.

The metric theory developed in Theorem
13.6
allows us to establish the following result for the modified Jacobi–Perron algorithm.

Lemma 13.7
.

Let
(\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{M}},\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{M}},\widehat{A}_{\mathrm{M}},\widehat{\nu}_{\mathrm{M}})
be the natural extension of the two-dimensional modified Jacobi–Perron algorithm, and let
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{M}}
be a substitutive realization of this algorithm. Then for
\nu_{\mathrm{M}}
-almost every
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{M}}
the following assertions hold.

•

\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
satisfies the two-sided Pisot condition.

•

\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is algebraically irreducible.

•

The language
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}
is balanced.

•

\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
converges strongly to a generalized right eigenvector
\mathbf{x}
in the future and to a generalized left eigenvector
\mathbf{y}
in the past. The vector
\mathbf{x}
has rationally independent coordinates.

•

The mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})})
associated to
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is eventually Anosov for the splitting

\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}\mathbf{u}_{n}\oplus\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}.

Proof.

The first assertion holds because by Proposition
10.13
the modified Jacobi–Perron algorithm satisfies the generic Pisot condition. The remaining assertions are a consequence of Theorem
13.6
.
∎

13.3.
Metric criteria for the tiling condition and pure discrete spectrum

Our goal is to show that the Pisot condition implies property PRICE and, under some weak additional assumption, even the tiling condition from Definition
12.6
as well as pure discrete spectrum almost everywhere. In the proofs, we have to use some results and techniques from
[
BST23
, Section 5]
.

The first lemma assures balance and is very similar
[
BST23
, Lemma 5.2]
but uses weaker assumptions.

Lemma 13.8
.

Let
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
, with
D\subset\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
d\geq 2
, be an ergodic dynamical system that satisfies the Pisot condition and has a cylinder of positive measure corresponding to a substitution with positive incidence matrix.
Then

\lim_{C\to\infty}\nu(D\cap B_{C}^{(n)})=1\qquad(n\in\mathbb{Z}).

Proof.

Theorem
13.6
(
ii
) gives that
\nu(\bigcup_{C\in\mathbb{N}}D\cap B_{C}^{(0)})=1
.
Since
B_{C}^{(0)}\subset B_{C^{\prime}}^{(0)}
for
C<C^{\prime}
and measurability of
D\cap B_{C}^{(0)}
is proved as in
[
BST23
, Lemma 5.2]
, we obtain
\lim_{C\to\infty}\nu(D\cap B_{C}^{(0)})=1
.
Finally, (
12.17
) yields
\lim_{C\to\infty}\nu(D\cap B_{C}^{(n)})=1
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

The next result is a two-sided version of
[
BST23
, Proposition 5.3]
, again removing superfluous assumptions.
Together with Lemma
13.10
below, it shows that the assumptions of Theorem
16.1
imply the two-sided property PRICE from Definition
12.12
a.e.; the importance of this property for the tiling condition has been shown in Section
12.4
.

Proposition 13.9
.

Let
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
, with
D\subset\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
d\geq 2
, be an ergodic dynamical system that satisfies the Pisot condition and has a cylinder of positive measure corresponding to a substitution with positive incidence matrix.
Then
\nu
-almost every
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in D
is two-sided primitive and satisfies the two-sided property PRICE.

Proof.

We follow the proof of
[
BST23
, Proposition 5.3]
.
Let
[\tau_{0},\dots,\tau_{h-1}]
be a cylinder with
\nu([\tau_{0},\dots,\tau_{h-1}])>0
such that the incidence matrix
M_{\tau_{[0,h)}}
is positive.
By Lemma
13.8
, there is
C\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\nu(D\cap B_{C}^{(0)})>1-\nu(\Sigma^{h}[\tau_{0},\dots,\tau_{h-1}])
, thus
\nu(\Sigma^{h}[\tau_{0},\dots,\tau_{h-1}]\cap B_{C}^{(0)})>0
.

By ergodicity of
\nu
together with the Poincaré Recurrence Theorem, we have for almost all
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in D
some
\ell_{0}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})\geq h
such that
\Sigma^{\ell_{0}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})}\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\Sigma^{h}[\tau_{0},\dots,\tau_{h-1}]\cap B_{C}^{(0)}
, i.e.,
(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{\ell_{0}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})-1})
ends with
(\tau_{0},\dots,\tau_{h-1})
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in B_{C}^{(\ell_{0}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}))}
.
We will now extend
\ell_{0}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})
for almost all
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in D
to a sequence
(\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

•

(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{\ell_{k+1}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})-1})
ends with
(\sigma_{-\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})},\dots,\sigma_{\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})-1})
(and, a fortiori, with
(\tau_{0},\ldots,\tau_{h-1})
),

•

\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in B_{C}^{(\ell_{k+1}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}))}
,

•

\ell_{k+1}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})\geq 2\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})
,

for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
To this end, assume that
\ell_{0}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}),\ldots,\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})
are already defined for almost all
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in D
.
Since there are countably many possibilites for
\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})
and
(\sigma_{-\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})},\dots,\sigma_{\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})-1})
, the set of those
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in D
such that

\nu\big(\Sigma^{\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})}[\sigma_{-\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})},\dots,\sigma_{\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})-1}]\cap B_{C}^{(\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}))}\big)=0

has zero measure.
We have thus
\nu\big(\Sigma^{\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})}[\sigma_{-\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})},\dots,\sigma_{\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})-1}]\cap B_{C}^{(\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}))}\big)>0
for a.e.
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in D
and obtain (by the Poincaré Recurrence Theorem and ergodicity of
\nu
) some
\ell_{k+1}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})
with the required properties.
Recursively, we obtain a sequence
(\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
with the required properties for almost all
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in D
.

Setting
r_{k}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})=\ell_{k+1}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})
, we obtain that conditions (P), (R) and (C) of Property PRICE hold for almost all
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in D
.
By the Poincaré Recurrence Theorem and since
\tau_{[0,h)}
has positive incidence matrix, two-sided primitivity holds and condition (E) follows from Proposition
7.4
.
From the Pisot condition and Corollary
7.19
, we obtain that almost all
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in D
are algebraically irreducible, i.e., also (I) holds a.e. and we are done.
∎

The following lemma is needed to show that the assumptions of the theorems in Section
16.1
imply the assumptions of Lemma
13.8
and Proposition
13.9
.

Lemma 13.10
.

Assume that
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
, with
D\subset\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
d\geq 2
, has a periodic Pisot sequence with positive two-sided range. Then it has a cylinder of positive measure corresponding to a substitution with positive incidence matrix.

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{\tau}=(\tau_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in D
be a periodic Pisot sequence with two-sided positive range.
Then
\nu(\Sigma^{n}[\tau_{0},\dots,\tau_{n-1}])>0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and there is

p\geq 1
such that
\Sigma^{p}\boldsymbol{\tau}=\boldsymbol{\tau}
and
\tau_{[0,p)}
is a Pisot substitution.
Since
\tau_{[0,p)}
is a Pisot substitution, its incidence matrix is primitive by
[
CS01b
, Proposition 1.3]
and, hence,
\tau_{[0,kp)}
has positive incidence matrix for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
As
\nu
is measure-preserving, we have
\nu([\tau_{0},\dots,\tau_{kp-1}])>0
.
∎

Theorem 13.11
.

Let
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
, with
D\subset\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
d\geq 2
, be an ergodic dynamical system that satisfies the Pisot condition and has a periodic Pisot sequence with positive two-sided range and pure discrete spectrum.
Then, for
\nu
-almost every
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in D
the following assertions hold.

(i)

\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is two-sided primitive.

(ii)

\boldsymbol{\sigma}
satisfies the tiling condition with compact Rauzy fractals.

(iii)

(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma)
has pure discrete spectrum.

Proof.

Our main goal is to apply Lemma
12.17
. Thus we have to prove that its conditions are verified.

According to our assumptions, there is a sequence
(\tau_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in D
with period
p
and two-sided positive range such that
\tau_{[0,p)}
is a Pisot substitution and the substitutive dynamical system generated by
\tau_{[0,p)}
has pure discrete spectrum.
By the two-sided positive range property, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
\nu(\Sigma^{n}[\tau_{0},\dots,\tau_{n-1}]\cap E)>0
for all left saturated sets
E
with
\nu(E)\geq 1{-}\varepsilon
.
By Lemma
13.8
, there exists
C\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\nu(B_{C}^{(0)})\geq 1{-}\varepsilon
.
Since
B_{C}^{(0)}
is left saturated, we have
\nu(\Sigma^{n}[\tau_{0},\dots,\tau_{n-1}]\cap B_{C}^{(0)})>0
and thus

\nu([\tau_{0},\dots,\tau_{n-1}]\cap B_{C}^{(n)})>0\qquad(n\in\mathbb{N})

by (
12.17
).
Let
m
be as in Lemma
12.17
for the substitution
\tau_{[0,p)}
.
By the Poincaré Recurrence Theorem and the ergodicity of
\nu
, for almost all sequences
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in D
, there exists
n
such that
\Sigma^{n}\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in[\tau_{0},\dots,\tau_{mp-1}]\cap B_{C}^{(mp)}
, which is equivalent to the conditions
\sigma_{[n,n+\ell)}=\tau_{[0,p)}^{m}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in B_{C}^{(n+\ell)}
in the formulation of Lemma
12.17
.
Thus, since the two-sided property PRICE holds for a.e.
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in D
by Proposition
13.9
(which we may apply because of Lemma
13.10
), Lemma
12.17
yields that the tiling condition is satisfied for a.e.
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in D
, with compact tiles by Lemma
12.2
. Moreover, two-sided primitivity directly follows from Proposition
13.9
.
Finally, pure discrete spectrum follows because, in view of Proposition
12.10
, we can apply Theorem
12.9
for almost all
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in D
.
∎

13.4.
Metric tiling condition and pure discrete spectrum for accelerations

The following theorem shows that we can accelerate each shift in a way that the tiling condition from Definition
12.6
as well as pure discrete spectrum hold generically without assuming pure discrete spectrum for a Pisot sequence.
In its statement, for a given
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}
and a given
p\in\mathbb{N}
, we use the notation

(13.3)

\mathbf{M}_{p}=(M_{[pn,p(n+1))})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}

for the
p
-fold blocking of the sequence
\mathbf{M}
.

The pure discrete spectrum result contained in the following theorem is contained in
[
BST23
, Theorem 3.5]

Theorem 13.12
.

Let
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
, with
D\subset\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
d\geq 2
, be an ergodic dynamical system that satisfies the Pisot condition and contains a periodic Pisot sequence with two-sided positive range.
Then there is a positive integer
p
and a substitution assignment
32
32

32

It is sufficient to define the substitution assignment on matrices occurring in
D
.

\varrho
on
\mathcal{M}_{d}
such that, for
\nu
-almost every
\mathbf{M}\in D
, the sequence of substitutions
\varrho(\mathbf{M}_{p})
satisfies the tiling condition with compact Rauzy fractals, and
(\varrho(\mathbf{M}_{p}),\Sigma)
has pure discrete spectrum.

To prove Theorem
13.12
, we study accelerations
(D,\Sigma^{p})
of
(D,\Sigma)
and use Proposition
12.18
. In case that
(D,\Sigma^{p},\nu)
is not ergodic (even though
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
is ergodic), we need the following adaptation of the set
B_{C}^{(0)}
.

For a sequence
\mathbf{N}=(N_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
and
p\geq 1
, let

\displaystyle B_{C,\mathbf{N},p}

\displaystyle=\big\{(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}\,:\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in B_{C}^{(0)}\ \mbox{for all $\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}$ such that}

\displaystyle(M_{\sigma_{0}},M_{\sigma_{1}},\dots)=(N_{[-i,0)}M_{[0,p-i)},M_{[p-i,2p-i)},M_{[2p-i,3p-i)},\dots)

\displaystyle\mbox{for some}\ i\in\{0,\ldots,p{-}1\}\big\},

i.e., the set of sequences
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
such that each substitutive realization of the
p
-fold blocking of a sequence
(N_{-i},\dots,N_{-1},M_{0},M_{1},\dots)
,
0\leq i<p
, gives a
C
-balanced language.
Note that
B_{C,\mathbf{N},p}\cap D
is left saturated (and depends only on
N_{i}
with
0\leq i<p
) for all
D\subset\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Lemma 13.13
.

For
d\geq 2
let
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
with
D\subset\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be an ergodic dynamical system that satisfies the Pisot condition and and has a cylinder of positive measure corresponding to a substitution with positive incidence matrix. Then

\lim_{C\to\infty}\nu(B_{C,\mathbf{N},p}\cap D)=1\quad\mbox{for all}\quad\mathbf{N}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}},\,p\geq 1.

Proof.

In view of the proof of Lemma
13.8
, we only have to show that, given
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
\mathbf{N}=(N_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
p\geq 1
, such that
\mathbf{M}
satisfies the Pisot condition and the growth condition
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert M_{n}\rVert=0
, there exists
C\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in B_{C}^{(0)}
for each substitutive realization
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
of a sequence
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}=(M^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
with
(M^{\prime}_{0},M^{\prime}_{1},\dots)=(N_{[-i,0)}M_{[0,p-i)},M_{[p-i,2p-i)},\dots)
for some
i\in\{0,\dots,p-1\}
.

We proceed as in the proof of Theorem
11.8
(
iii
).
For
\mathbf{M},\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
as above, let
\delta_{1}(n)\geq\cdots\geq\delta_{d}(n)
be the singular values of
M_{[0,n)}
and
\delta^{\prime}_{1}(n)\geq\cdots\geq\delta^{\prime}_{d}(n)
the singular values of
M^{\prime}_{[0,n)}
.
Then we have
\beta,C_{1}>0
such that
\delta_{2}(n)\leq C_{1}\,e^{-\beta n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, hence there exists
C^{\prime}_{1}>0
(that depends only on
\mathbf{M}
,
\mathbf{N}
, and
p
) such that

\delta^{\prime}_{2}(n)\leq\delta^{\prime}_{1}(i)\,\delta_{2}(pn{-}i)\leq C^{\prime}_{1}\,e^{-\beta pn}\quad\mbox{for all}\ n\in\mathbb{N}.

The growth condition implies that there exists
C_{2}>0
such that
\lVert M_{n}\rVert_{\infty}\leq C_{2}e^{\beta n/2}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Since
\lVert M^{\prime}_{n}\rVert\leq\lVert M_{np-i}\rVert\cdots\lVert M_{np+n-i-1}\rVert
for all
n\geq 1
, we obtain a constant
C^{\prime}_{2}>0
(again depending only on
\mathbf{M}
,
\mathbf{N}
, and
p
) such that

\lVert M^{\prime}_{n}\rVert_{\infty}\leq C^{\prime}_{2}\,e^{\beta pn/2}\quad\mbox{for all}\ n\in\mathbb{N}.

Then the proof of Theorem
11.8
(
iii
) shows that there exists
C>0
(depending only on
\mathbf{M}
,
\mathbf{N}
, and
p
) such that
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
is
C
-balanced for all
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
\mathbf{M}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
.
∎

We can now conclude the Proof of Theorem
13.12
.

Proof of Theorem
13.12
.

Let
\mathbf{N}=(N_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in D
be a periodic Pisot sequence with two-sided positive range, with
p>0
such that
\Sigma^{p}\mathbf{N}=\mathbf{N}
and
N_{[0,p)}
is a Pisot matrix.
Since
N_{[0,p)}
and
N_{[-i,p-i)}=N_{[-i,0)}N_{[0,p)}N_{[-i,0)}^{-1}
are similar matrices,
N_{[-i,p-i)}
is a Pisot matrix for all
i\in\{0,\dots,p{-}1\}
.
Then Proposition
12.18
gives substitutions
\tau_{i}
having pure discrete spectrum with incidence matrix
M_{\tau_{i}}=N_{[-i,kp-i)}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Since
\{N_{[-i,kp-i)}:0\leq i<kp\}=\{N_{[-i,kp-i)}:0\leq i<p\}
, we can replace
p
by
kp
and assume w.l.o.g. that
k=1
.
We choose
\tau_{0},\ldots,\tau_{p-1}
in a way that
\tau_{i}=\tau_{j}
if
N_{[-i,p-i)}=N_{[-j,p-j)}
,
0\leq i,j<p
.

Choose a substitution assignment
\varrho:\mathcal{M}_{d}\to\mathcal{S}_{d}
such that
\varrho(N_{[-i,p-i)})=\tau_{i}
for
0\leq i<p
. Then the map

(13.4)

\boldsymbol{\psi}:\,D\to\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}},\quad(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mapsto\big(\varrho(M_{[np,(n+1)p})\big)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}

is well defined, and, setting
D^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\psi}D
we have the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}

Unfortunately, we cannot apply Theorem
13.11
to
(D^{\prime},\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\psi}_{*}\nu)
because the acceleration
(D,\Sigma^{p},\nu)
of
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
may not be ergodic (and it seems difficult to deduce a two-sided positive range property in each of the ergodic components from that in
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
).
However, each ergodic component of
(D^{\prime},\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\psi}_{*}\nu)
has a cylinder of positive measure corresponding to a substitution with positive incidence matrix, hence a.e.
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in D^{\prime}
satisfies the two-sided property PRICE by Proposition
13.9
; see
[
BST23
, Proof of Theorem 3.6]
for details on the ergodic components.

Since
(D,\Sigma^{p},\nu)
need not be ergodic, we consider
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
.
By the two-sided positive range property of
\mathbf{N}
, there is
\varepsilon>0
such that
\nu(\Sigma^{n}[N_{0},\dots,N_{n-1}]\cap F)>0
for each left saturated set
F\subset D
with
\nu(F)\geq 1{-}\varepsilon
and each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
By Lemma
13.13
, there is
C\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\nu(B_{C,\mathbf{N},p}\cap D)\geq 1{-}\varepsilon
.
Since
B_{C,\mathbf{N},p}
is left saturated, we have

\nu(\Sigma^{n}[N_{0},\dots,N_{n-1}]\cap B_{C,\mathbf{N},p})>0\quad\mbox{for all}\ n\in\mathbb{N}.

By Lemma
12.17
, there exists
m>0
such that
\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{M})
satisfies the tiling condition for each
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
that satisfies the following three conditions for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
i\in\{0,\dots,p{-}1\}
:
\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{M})
satisfies the two-sided property PRICE,
\Sigma^{np}\mathbf{M}\in[N_{-i},\dots,N_{mp-i-1}]
, and
\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{M})\in B_{C}^{(n+m)}
.
By the previous paragraph,
\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{M})
satisfies the two-sided proprerty PRICE for almost all
\mathbf{M}\in D
.
For a.e.
\mathbf{M}\in D
, by the ergodicity of
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
and the Poincaré Recurrence Theorem, there also exists
r\geq mp{+}p
such that
\Sigma^{r}\mathbf{M}\in\Sigma^{mp+p}[N_{0},\dots,N_{mp+p-1}]\cap B_{C,\mathbf{N},p}
; setting
n=\lfloor r/p\rfloor{-}m
,
i=r{-}\lfloor r/p\rfloor p
, we have then
i\in\{0,\dots,p{-}1\}
,

\displaystyle\Sigma^{np}\mathbf{M}

\displaystyle=\Sigma^{r-mp-i}\mathbf{M}\in[N_{p-i},\dots,N_{mp+p-1}]\subset[N_{-i},\dots,N_{mp-i-1}],

\displaystyle\Sigma^{np+mp}\mathbf{M}

\displaystyle=\Sigma^{r-i}\mathbf{M}\in[N_{mp+p-i},\dots,N_{mp+p-1}]=[N_{-i},\dots,N_{-1}],

thus
\Sigma^{r}\mathbf{M}\in B_{C,\mathbf{N},p}
yields that
\boldsymbol{\psi}(\Sigma^{np+mp}\mathbf{M})\in B_{C}^{(0)}
, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{M})\in B_{C}^{(n+m)}
.
Therefore, for a.e.
\mathbf{M}\in D
,
\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{M})
satisfies the three conditions of Lemma
12.17
and therefore satisfies the tiling condition, with compact tiles by Lemma
12.2
. Pure discrete spectrum of
(\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{M}),\Sigma)
follows immediately from Theorem
12.9
(recall the equivalences in Prioposition
12.10
).
∎

13.5.
Tiling condition and pure discrete spectrum for multidimensional continued fraction algorithms

We conclude this section by formulating Theorem
13.12
in the context of multidimensional continued fraction algorithms (compare
[
BST23
, Theorem 3.1]
).

Theorem 13.14
.

Let
(X,F,A,\nu)
be a positive multidimensional continued fraction algorithm satisfying the Pisot condition and
\nu\circ F\ll\nu
. Let
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
be a faithful substitutive realization of a natural extension
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
of
(X,F,A,\nu)
. Assume that there is a periodic Pisot point
(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})\in\widehat{X}
such that
\mathbf{x}_{0}
has positive range in
(X,F,A,\nu)
and
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})
has pure discrete spectrum.
Then, for
\widehat{\nu}
-almost all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\widehat{X}
,
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})
satisfies the tiling condition and
(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0}),\Sigma)
has pure discrete spectrum.

Proof.

By Remark
9.10
, the Pisot condition for
(X,F,A,\nu)
implies that the natural extension
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
satisfies the Pisot condition as well. By (
11.7
) we have
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})\overset{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}{\cong}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X}),\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}\widehat{\nu})
, thus
(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X}),\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}\widehat{\nu})
satisfies the Pisot condition. By Lemma
13.4
, for each periodic Pisot point
(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})\in\widehat{X}
such that
\mathbf{x}_{0}
has positive range in
(X,F,A,\nu)
, the sequence
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})
is a periodic Pisot sequence with two-sided positive range in
(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X}),\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}\widehat{\nu})
. Thus the dynamical system
(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X}),\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}\widehat{\nu})
satisfies all the conditions of Theorem
13.11
, which proves the theorem.
∎

Obviously, the result on accelerations contained in Theorem
13.12
can also be formulated in terms of multidimensional continued fraction algorithms. We omit the details.

Chapter V
Induced rotations and nonstationary Markov partitions

This chapter is the core part of the present work. In Section
14
, we state and prove our results on induced rotations and their relations to multidimensional continued fraction algorithms.
Indeed, we show that these algorithms act as renormalization processes for cascades of toral rotations. In Section
15
, we use the tiling property to construct nonstationary Markov partitions for
\mathcal{S}
-adic mapping families and associate nonstationary edge shifts with them. In Section
16
, we establish metric results on nonstationary Markov partitions for shifts of
\mathcal{S}
-adic mapping families. The crucial condition needed here is the Pisot condition. This section also contains interpretations of these results for multidimensional continued fraction algorithms. Again, the guiding example used to illustrate our theory is furnished by the Brun continued fraction algorithm in Section
17
.

14.
Induced rotations

We have announced in Section
3
that, for a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm
F:\,X\to X
, we can define rotations
\mathfrak{r}_{\mathbf{x}}
,
\mathbf{x}\in X
, on
\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
such that the induced map of
\mathfrak{r}_{\mathbf{x}}
on a certain subset of
\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
yields the rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{F(\mathbf{x})}
; see Definition
8.7
. We provide the details of this construction in the present section. Before we do that, we formulate according results for
\mathcal{S}
-adic systems both for a single sequence of substitutions (cf. Section
14.1
) and in the metric setting (cf. Section
14.2
