), and then consider induced rotations for continued fraction algorithms in Section
14.3
.

14.1.
Induced rotations for a single sequence

Consider a sequence of unimodular substitutions
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
that satisfies the tiling condition from Definition
12.6
with compact Rauzy fractals
\mathcal{R}_{n}
as defined in Definition
12.1
.
Recall that the
\mathcal{S}
-adic shift
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)},\Sigma)
(defined in Definition
11.1
) is conjugate to the rotation by
\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{1}}\mathbf{e}_{d}
on
\mathbf{1}^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}^{\perp})
by Theorem
12.9
and its proof.
To see the induction process, it is more convenient to view these rotations on the hyperplanes
\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}
.
Using the projections
\pi_{n}
defined in (
12.1
), we obtain the commutative diagram

(14.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}^{\perp})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}^{\perp})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}/\pi_{n}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}^{\perp})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}/\pi_{n}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}^{\perp})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{1}}\mathbf{e}_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+\pi_{n}\mathbf{e}_{d}}

(Here, the vector
\mathbf{e}_{d}
can be replaced by any
\mathbf{e}_{i}
,
i\in\mathcal{A}
, because the difference
\mathbf{e}_{d}{-}\mathbf{e}_{i}
is in
\mathbf{1}^{\perp}
.)
Note that

(14.2)

\mathbb{T}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)}=\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}/\pi_{n}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}^{\perp})

is a
(d{-}1)
-dimensional torus with fundamental domain
\mathcal{R}_{n}
.
Therefore, we write the rotation “
+\pi_{n}\mathbf{e}_{d}
” on
\mathbb{T}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)}
in (
14.1
) as

\tilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)}:\,\mathcal{R}_{n}\to\mathcal{R}_{n},\quad\mathbf{z}\mapsto\mathbf{z}+\pi_{n}\mathbf{e}_{d}\mod\pi_{n}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}^{\perp})

and obtain that
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)},\Sigma)
is measurably conjugate to
(\mathcal{R}_{n},\tilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)})
.
All dynamical systems whose transformations are rotations are equipped with the Lebesgue measure.

Our aim is to show that the
\mathcal{S}
-adic structure of the shift
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(0)},\Sigma)
can be geometrically interpreted by the fact that the induced map of
\tilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(0)}
on the subset
M_{\sigma_{[0,n)}}\mathcal{R}_{n}
of
\mathcal{R}_{0}

is conjugate to the rotation
\tilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)}
. In particular, we will show that the following diagram is commutative for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\sigma_{[0,n)}}\mathcal{R}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\sigma_{[0,n)}}\mathcal{R}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle M_{\sigma_{[0,n)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle M_{\sigma_{[0,n)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\text{induced map of}\ \tilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(0)}}

We state this result in the following theorem, which is inspired by the concept of
\sigma
-structure
introduced in
[
AI01
]
. It is the exact version of Theorem
Theorem C
.
formulated in Section
4
.

Theorem 14.1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
d\geq 2
, be a sequence of unimodular substitutions that satisfies the tiling condition.
For
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
m<n
, the induced map of the rotation
\tilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(m)}
on the subset
M_{\sigma_{[m,n)}}\mathcal{R}_{n}
of
\mathcal{R}_{m}
equals (after renormalization) the rotation
\tilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)}
.

Proof.

By the tiling condition and Proposition
12.7
, iterating the subdivision (
12.3
) and taking the union over
a\in\mathcal{A}
gives the (measurable) partition

(14.3)

\mathcal{R}_{m}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\mathcal{A}^{*},\,b\in\mathcal{A}:\\
p\prec\sigma_{[m,n)}(b)\end{subarray}}\big(\pi_{m}\boldsymbol{\ell}(p)+M_{\sigma_{[m,n)}}\mathcal{R}_{n}(b)\big).

We claim that the induced map of
\tilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(m)}
on
M_{\sigma_{[m,n)}}\mathcal{R}_{n}
maps points
M_{\sigma_{[m,n)}}\mathbf{z}
with
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(b)
,
b\in\mathcal{A}
, to
M_{\sigma_{[m,n)}}(\mathbf{z}{+}\pi_{n}\mathbf{e}_{b})
.
Indeed, for
1\leq k<|\sigma_{[m,n)}(b)|
, we have
(\tilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(m)})^{k}(M_{\sigma_{[m,n)}}\mathbf{z})=\pi_{m}\boldsymbol{\ell}(p)+M_{\sigma_{[m,n)}}\mathbf{z}
with the prefix
p
of
\sigma_{[m,n)}(b)
of length
k
.
Because the union in (
14.3
) is disjoint, this implies that
(\tilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(m)})^{k}(M_{\sigma_{[m,n)}}\mathbf{z})\notin M_{\sigma_{[m,n)}}\mathcal{R}_{n}
.
For
k=|\sigma_{[m,n)}(b)|
, we have

(\tilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(m)})^{k}(M_{\sigma_{[m,n)}}\mathbf{z})=\pi_{m}\boldsymbol{\ell}(\sigma_{[m,n)}(b))+M_{\sigma_{[m,n)}}\mathbf{z}=M_{\sigma_{[m,n)}}(\mathbf{z}{+}\pi_{n}\mathbf{e}_{b})\in M_{\sigma_{[m,n)}}\mathcal{R}_{n}

by the definition of
\mathcal{R}_{n}(b)
and
\mathcal{R}_{n}
.
This proves the claim, and renormalization by
M_{\sigma_{[m,n)}}^{-1}
gives the rotation
\tilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)}
.
∎

14.2.
Metric theory for induced rotations

Let
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
be a dynamical system with
D\subset\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
and a shift invariant Borel measure
\nu
. We want to prove that, under the Pisot condition,
\nu
-a.e. element of
D
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
14.1
. This leads to the following metric version of this theorem.

Theorem 14.2
.

Let
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
, with
D\subset\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
d\geq 2
, be an ergodic dynamical system that satisfies the Pisot condition and contains a periodic Pisot sequence having two-sided positive range and pure discrete spectrum.
Then, for
\nu
-almost every
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in D
, for all
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
m<n
, the induced map of the rotation
\tilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(m)}
on the subset
M_{\sigma_{[m,n)}}\mathcal{R}_{n}
of
\mathcal{R}_{m}
equals (after renormalization) the rotation
\tilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
13.11
, the tiling condition is satisfied for a.e.
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in D
, hence we can apply Theorem
14.1
.
∎

While in Theorem
14.2
we started with a shift of sequences of substitutions, in the following theorem, we start with sequences of
d{\times}d
matrices
\mathbf{M}
and use a substitution assignment
\varrho:\mathcal{M}_{d}\to\mathcal{S}_{d}
(see Definition
11.17
) to associate a sequence of substitutions
\varrho(\mathbf{M})
and, by means of these substitutions, a sequence of induced rotations.

Theorem 14.3
.

Let
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
, with
D\subset\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
d\geq 2
, be an ergodic dynamical system that satisfies the Pisot condition. Assume that there is a substitution assignment
\varrho
on
\mathcal{M}_{d}
for which there is a periodic Pisot sequence in
\varrho(D)
having two-sided positive range in
(\varrho(D),\Sigma,\varrho_{*}\nu)
and pure discrete spectrum.
Then, for
\nu
-almost every
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in D
, setting
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\varrho(\mathbf{M})
, for all
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
m<n
, the induced map of the rotation
\tilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(m)}
on the subset
M_{[m,n)}\mathcal{R}_{n}
of
\mathcal{R}_{m}
equals (after renormalization) the rotation
\tilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)}
.

Proof.

Theorem
14.3
follows immediately from Theorem
14.2
because the system
(\varrho(D),\Sigma,\varrho_{*}\nu)
satisfies all conditions of Theorem
14.2
.
∎

Using Theorem
13.12
, we do not need have to assume pure discrete spectrum for a Pisot sequence when we consider appropriate accelerations of the shift map.
Recall that
\mathbf{M}_{p}
denotes the
p
-fold blocking of the sequence
\mathbf{M}
, as in (
13.3
).

Theorem 14.4
.

Let
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
, with
D\subset\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
d\geq 2
, be an ergodic dynamical system that satisfies the Pisot condition and has a periodic Pisot sequence with two-sided positive range.
Then there exists a positive integer
p
and a substitution assignment
\varrho
on
\mathcal{M}_{d}
with the following property.
For
\nu
-almost every
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in D
, setting
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\varrho(\mathbf{M}_{p})
, for all
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
m<n
, the induced map of the rotation
\tilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(m)}
on the subset
M_{[mp,np)}\mathcal{R}_{n}
of
\mathcal{R}_{m}
equals (after renormalization) the rotation
\tilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
13.12
, all conditions of Theorem
14.1
are satisfied.
∎

14.3.
Induced rotations for continued fraction algorithms

Recall again some facts about multidimensional continued fraction algorithms stated earlier.
Let
(X,F,A,\nu)
be such an algorithm with natural extension
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
. In Definition
11.17
, we defined the faithful substitutive realization
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
of
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
.
If
(X,F,A,\nu)
converges weakly in the future and in the past at
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
for
\widehat{\nu}
-almost all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\widehat{X}
, then we know from (
11.7
) that the
\mathcal{S}
-adic shift
(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X}),\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}\nu)
is measurably conjugate to
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
.
The results of Sections
14.1
and
14.2
can be applied to
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
.
However, in accordance with the classical case discussed in Section
2
, we prefer to view multidimensional continued fraction algorithms as induction processes on the set of rotations on the fixed torus
\mathbf{1}^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}^{\perp})
.

Recall the map
\chi:\mathbb{P}^{d-1}\to\Delta^{d-1}
from (
9.2
), and set

(14.4)

\mathfrak{r}_{\mathbf{x}}:\ \mathbf{1}^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}^{\perp})\to\mathbf{1}^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}^{\perp}),\ \mathbf{z}\mapsto\mathbf{z}+\mathbf{e}_{d}-\chi(\mathbf{x})\bmod{\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}^{\perp}}

for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
.
Direct calculation yields that
\mathfrak{r}_{\mathbf{x}}
is a version of
\tilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}
acting on
\mathbf{1}^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}^{\perp})
for all
\mathbf{y}
satisfying
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\widehat{X}
.
More precisely, we have

\mathfrak{r}_{\mathbf{x}}=\pi_{\chi(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{1}}\circ\tilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}\circ\pi_{\chi(\mathbf{x}),\chi(\mathbf{y})}

and, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the following diagram commutes (with
\mathbb{T}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)}
defined in (
14.2
)):

(14.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}^{(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}^{(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}^{\perp})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}^{\perp})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}^{(n)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\chi(F^{n}(\mathbf{x})),\mathbf{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\chi(F^{n}(\mathbf{x})),\mathbf{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{r}_{F^{n}(\mathbf{x})}}

(Note that the projections are bijective in this diagram.)
For convenience of notation, the following theorems are formulated in terms of natural extensions, even though the occurring rotations depend only on
\mathbf{x}
.

Theorem 14.5
.

Let
(X,F,A,\mu)
be a positive multidimensional continued fraction algorithm with natural extension
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},A,\widehat{\mu})
and substitutive realization
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
.
Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
with
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\widehat{X}
.
If
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
satisfies the tiling condition with compact Rauzy fractals, then, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the induced map of the rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{\mathbf{x}}
on the subset
\pi_{\chi(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{1}}\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A^{(n)}(\mathbf{x})\mathcal{R}_{n}
of
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{\mathbf{1}}
equals (after renormalization) the rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{F^{n}(\mathbf{x})}
.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
14.1
and (
14.5
).
∎

According to Theorem
14.5
, the multidimensional continued fraction algorithm
F
can be viewed as a mapping acting on the parameter space of rotations on the torus
\mathbf{1}^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}^{\perp})\simeq\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
.
As in the classical case (discussed in Section
2
), the recurrence properties of the rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{\mathbf{x}}
are related to the continued fraction expansion of an element
\mathbf{x}
.
Indeed, good approximation properties of the convergents correspond to good contraction properties of
\vphantom{\!A}{}^{t}\!A^{(n)}(\mathbf{x})
, which entail that
\pi_{\chi(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{1}}\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A^{(n)}(\mathbf{x})\mathcal{R}_{n}
is small and that the induced map
\mathfrak{r}_{\mathbf{x}}
is close to the identity.
Recall that exponential contraction of
\vphantom{\!A}{}^{t}\!A^{(n)}(\mathbf{x})
is ensured by the Pisot condition, and the speed of convergence is governed by the first and second Lyapunov exponents of the algorithm.

For the following statement, recall the Pisot condition from Definition
9.9
and the notation
\ll
from (
9.14
).

Theorem 14.6
.

Let
(X,F,A,\nu)
be a positive multidimensional continued fraction algorithm satisfying the Pisot condition and
\nu\circ F\ll\nu
. Let
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
be a faithful substitutive realization of a natural extension
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
of
(X,F,A,\nu)
. Assume that there is a periodic Pisot point
(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})\in\widehat{X}
such that
\mathbf{x}_{0}
has positive range in
(X,F,A,\nu)
and
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})
has pure discrete spectrum.
Then, for
\widehat{\nu}
-almost all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\widehat{X}
, setting
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the induced map of the rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{\mathbf{x}}
on the subset
\pi_{\chi(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{1}}\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A^{(n)}(\mathbf{x})\mathcal{R}_{n}
of
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{\mathbf{1}}
equals (after renormalization) the rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{F^{n}(\mathbf{x})}
.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
16.1
in the same way as Theorem
13.14
follows from Theorem
13.11
. By Remark
9.10
, the Pisot condition for
(X,F,A,\nu)
implies that the natural extension
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
satisfies the Pisot condition as well. By (
11.7
) we have
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})\overset{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}{\cong}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X}),\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}\widehat{\nu})
, thus
(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X}),\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}\widehat{\nu})
satisfies the Pisot condition. By Lemma
13.4
, for each periodic Pisot point
(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})\in\widehat{X}
such that
\mathbf{x}_{0}
has positive range in
(X,F,A,\nu)
, the sequence
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})
is a periodic Pisot sequence with two-sided positive range in
(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X}),\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}\widehat{\nu})
. Thus the dynamical system
(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X}),\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}\widehat{\nu})
satisfies all the conditions of Theorem
14.2
, which proves the theorem in view of (
14.5
).
∎

We saw in Theorem
14.4
that shifts on sequences of matrices can always be accelerated in a way that the pure discrete spectrum condition is not required. The same is true in the continued fraction setting. This is the content of the following theorem.

Theorem 14.7
.

Let
(X,F,A,\nu)
be a positive multidimensional continued fraction algorithm satisfying the Pisot condition,
\nu\circ F\ll\nu
. Let
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
be a natural extension of
(X,F,A,\nu)
. Assume that there is a periodic Pisot point
\mathbf{x}_{0}\in X
with
\mathbf{x}_{0}
having positive range in
(X,F,A,\nu)
. Then there exist a positive integer
p
and a (faithful) substitutive realization
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
of
(X,F^{p},A,\nu)
with the following property.
For
\widehat{\nu}
-almost all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\widehat{X}
, setting
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the induced map of the rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{\mathbf{x}}
on the subset
\pi_{\chi(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{1}}\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A^{(np)}(\mathbf{x})\mathcal{R}_{n}
of
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{\mathbf{1}}
, regarded as fundamental domain of
\mathbf{1}^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}^{\perp})
, equals (after renormalization) the rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{F^{np}(\mathbf{x})}
.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
14.3
in the same way as Theorem
14.6
follows from Theorem
14.2
.
∎

15.
Nonstationary Markov partitions for mapping families

Given a bi-infinite sequence
\mathbf{M}
of nonnegative unimodular
d{\times}d
integer matrices, we can interpret this sequence as a mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}})
, i.e., as a sequence of toral automorphisms. The main results of this section state that (under certain natural conditions) we can define a nonstationary generating Markov partition for
(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}})
by superimposing a combinatorial structure upon
\mathbf{M}
, i.e., by considering a sequence of substitutions
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
such that
\mathbf{M}
is the sequence of incidence matrices
\mathbf{M}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
. This allows to conjugate
(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}})
to a nonstationary edge shift. We make this more precise in the following paragraph.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
d\geq 2
, be a bi-infinite sequence of unimodular substitutions. We interpret the abelianization of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
, i.e., the sequence
\mathbf{M}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
of its incidence matrices, as an
\mathcal{S}
-adic mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})=(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}})
, whose mappings are the inverses of the elements of
\mathbf{M}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
; see Definition
11.15
. In Section
15.1
, we discuss the restacking process used in the proof of Proposition
12.7
and show that the Rauzy boxes of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
can be used to define atoms of a nonstationary Markov partition for
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
. This means that we use the zero entropy dynamical system
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma)
to define the atoms of a Markov partition for the system
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
of positive entropy. In order to make this work, we need to assume the tiling condition from Definition
12.6
(with compact Rauzy fractals).
If
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is two-sided primitive, then the above mentioned Markov partition of
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
can be refined in a way that it becomes generating. This refined partition will be treated in Section
15.2
.
In Section
15.3
, we use these Markov partitions to code the mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
by a nonstationary edge shift.

15.1.
Markov partition defined in terms of Rauzy boxes

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
d\geq 2
, be a sequence of unimodular substitutions. We want to use Rauzy boxes in order to define a nonstationary Markov partition for the
\mathcal{S}
-adic mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
. In particular, let
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
be the
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy boxes associated to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
.
We assume that
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
is compact, so that it is also closed when we consider it is a subset of
\mathbb{T}^{d}
.
We will show, under the tiling condition, that the interiors of the subtiles
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a)
taken modulo the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
have the nonstationary Property
M
(see Definition
6.7
) and, hence, in view of Proposition
6.8
, form a nonstationary Markov partition for the mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
.

If we assume the tiling condition and that the Rauzy fractals are compact, then the boundary of
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a)
has measure zero for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
, and the collection

(15.1)

\mathcal{P}_{n}=\big\{\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a))\bmod{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\,:\,a\in\mathcal{A}\big\}

forms a topological partition of the
d
-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}_{n}
for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
We recall that criteria for this tiling condition are provided in Section
12
.

In order to prove that
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies the nonstationary Property M (see Definition
6.7
), we need to come up with suitable horizontal and vertical partitions
H_{n,a}
and
V_{n,a}
for each element of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
To this end, we set, for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
a\in\mathcal{A}
,
\mathbf{x}\in\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a))
,

(15.2)

\displaystyle h_{n}(\mathbf{x}\bmod\mathbb{Z}^{d})

\displaystyle=\tilde{\pi}_{n}\mathbf{x}-\operatorname{int}(\mathcal{R}_{n}(a))\bmod\mathbb{Z}^{d},

\displaystyle v_{n}(\mathbf{x}\bmod\mathbb{Z}^{d})

\displaystyle=\pi_{n}\mathbf{x}+\operatorname{int}(\tilde{\pi}_{n}\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket)\bmod\mathbb{Z}^{d},

where the projections
\pi_{n},\tilde{\pi}_{n}
are defined in (
12.1
).

Figure 17.
Atoms of a transverse partition of a Rauzy box

See Figure
17
for an example.
This immediately implies that

(15.3)

\displaystyle H_{n,a}

\displaystyle=\big\{h_{n}(\mathbf{x}\bmod\mathbb{Z}^{d})\,:\,\mathbf{x}\in\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a))\big\},

\displaystyle V_{n,a}

\displaystyle=\big\{v_{n}(\mathbf{x}\bmod\mathbb{Z}^{d})\,:\,\mathbf{x}\in\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a))\big\}

are transverse partitions of
\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a))\bmod\mathbb{Z}^{d}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

Theorem 15.1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
d\geq 2
, be a sequence of unimodular substitutions that satisfies the tiling condition with compact Rauzy fractals.
Then the sequence
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
defined in (
15.1
) forms a nonstationary Markov partition for the mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
associated to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.8
, we have to show that the mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
associated to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
has Property M. Looking at Definition
6.7
, we thus have to prove that, for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
a\in\mathcal{A}
, the atoms of the partitions
H_{n,a}
and
V_{n,a}
defined in (
15.3
) satisfy

(15.4)

\begin{gathered}v_{n}(M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathbf{x}\bmod\mathbb{Z}^{d})\subset M_{\sigma_{n}}v_{n+1}(\mathbf{x}\bmod\mathbb{Z}^{d}),\\
M_{\sigma_{n}}h_{n+1}(\mathbf{x}\bmod\mathbb{Z}^{d})\subset h_{n}(M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathbf{x}\bmod\mathbb{Z}^{d}),\end{gathered}

for all
\mathbf{x}\in(M_{\sigma_{n}}^{-1}\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a)){+}\mathbb{Z}^{d})\cap\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n+1}(b))
,
b\in\mathcal{A}
.

Since
M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathbf{x}\in(\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a)){+}\mathbb{Z}^{d})\cap M_{\sigma_{n}}\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n+1}(b))
, we obtain by (
12.13
) and (
12.10
) that
M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathbf{x}\in\boldsymbol{\ell}(p){+}\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a,p,b))
for some
p\in\mathcal{A}^{*}
such that
(a,p,b)\in E_{n}
.
Therefore, using (
12.12
),

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} v_{n}(M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathbf{x})&=\pi_{n}\big(M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathbf{x}{-}\boldsymbol{\ell}(p))+\operatorname{int}(\tilde{\pi}_{n}\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket\big)\equiv M_{\sigma_{n}}\pi_{n+1}\mathbf{x}+\tilde{\pi}_{n}\boldsymbol{\ell}(p){+}\operatorname{int}(\tilde{\pi}_{n}\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket)\\
&\subset M_{\sigma_{n}}\pi_{n+1}\mathbf{x}+M_{\sigma_{n}}\operatorname{int}(\tilde{\pi}_{n+1}\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{b}\rrbracket)=M_{\sigma_{n}}v_{n+1}(\mathbf{x}),\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} M_{\sigma_{n}}h_{n+1}(\mathbf{x})&=M_{\sigma_{n}}\big(\tilde{\pi}_{n+1}\mathbf{x}{-}\operatorname{int}(\mathcal{R}_{n+1}(b))\big)=\tilde{\pi}_{n}M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathbf{x}-M_{\sigma_{n}}\operatorname{int}(\mathcal{R}_{n+1}(b))\\
&=\tilde{\pi}_{n}M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathbf{x}+\pi_{n}\boldsymbol{\ell}(p)-\operatorname{int}(\mathcal{R}_{n}(a,p,b))\\
&\subset\tilde{\pi}_{n}(M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathbf{x}{-}\boldsymbol{\ell}(p))-\operatorname{int}(\mathcal{R}_{n}(a))\equiv h_{n}(M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathbf{x}),\end{aligned}

where we have omitted
\bmod\ \mathbb{Z}^{d}
for readability.
Hence, (
15.4
) holds, which proves the theorem.
∎

We now provide a geometric interpretation of the Markov property in terms of the
restacking
process on the Rauzy boxes used in the proof of Proposition
12.7
. This reflects the way classical Markov partitions of toral automorphisms are viewed: one first distorts their atoms linearly and then moves them back to the fundamental domain. In our context, this “moving back” can be visualized by restacking subtiles of Rauzy boxes modulo the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. In particular, let
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
be the
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy boxes associated to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
. Looking at the horizontal slices
h_{n}(\mathbf{x})=\tilde{\pi}_{n}\mathbf{x}{-}\operatorname{int}(\mathcal{R}_{n}(a))
and the vertical lines
v_{n}(\mathbf{x})=\pi_{n}\mathbf{x}{+}\operatorname{int}(\llbracket\tilde{\pi}_{n}\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket)
of the subboxes
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a)
during the restacking process performed in the proof of Proposition
12.7
, we can see Property M (see Definition
6.7
) in action. Indeed, this restacking process is the nonstationary version of the restacking done in the context of stationary Markov partitions of toral automorphisms; see for instance
[
Adl98
, Figures 9 and 10]
. For the case
d=2
, the nonstationary restacking process is explained in
[
AF05
]
.
Indeed, what we did shows the following.
The “cylinders”
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a)
of
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
are “horizontal” unions of slices of cylinders of
M_{\sigma_{n}}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n+1}
, as can be seen from the stacking process in Figure
16
.
Viewing this process in the backwards direction, we see that the cylinders
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n+1}(b)
are built by “vertical” piles of subtiles of
M_{\sigma_{n}}^{-1}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
.

15.2.
Generating Markov partition

In the previous section, we saw that the sequence
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
defined in (
15.1
) forms a Markov partition for the mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
. However, in general this Markov partition is too coarse to be
generating
.
To get a generating Markov partition for a large class of sequences of substitutions, we need the finer partitions corresponding to
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\mathrm{gen}}
.
On top of this, we need to assume strong convergence in the future and in the past.

Assume again that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
d\geq 2
, satisfies the tiling condition and that the Rauzy fractals
\mathcal{R}_{n}
are compact.
Then, by Proposition
12.7
, the collection
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\mathrm{gen}}
forms a tiling of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and

(15.5)

\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\mathrm{gen}}=\big\{\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a,p,b))\bmod\mathbb{Z}^{d}\,:\,(a,p,b)\in E_{n}\big\}

forms a topological partition of the torus
\mathbb{T}_{n}
, which is a refinement of the topological partition
\mathcal{P}_{n}
defined in (
15.1
).
Similarly to (
15.2
), we set

\displaystyle h_{n}^{\mathrm{gen}}(\mathbf{x}\bmod\mathbb{Z}^{d})

\displaystyle=\tilde{\pi}_{n}\mathbf{x}-\operatorname{int}(\mathcal{R}_{n}(a,p,b))\bmod\mathbb{Z}^{d},

\displaystyle v_{n}^{\mathrm{gen}}(\mathbf{x}\bmod\mathbb{Z}^{d})

\displaystyle=\pi_{n}\mathbf{x}+\operatorname{int}(\tilde{\pi}_{n}\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket)\bmod\mathbb{Z}^{d},

for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
(a,p,b)\in E_{n}
,
\mathbf{x}\in\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a,p,b))
.
Again, this implies, for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
(a,p,b)\in E_{n}
, that

\displaystyle H_{n,(a,p,b)}^{\mathrm{gen}}

\displaystyle=\{h_{n}^{\mathrm{gen}}(\mathbf{x}\bmod\mathbb{Z}^{d})\,:\,\mathbf{x}\in\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a,p,b))\},

\displaystyle V_{n,(a,p,b)}^{\mathrm{gen}}

\displaystyle=\{v_{n}^{\mathrm{gen}}(\mathbf{x}\bmod\mathbb{Z}^{d})\,:\,\mathbf{x}\in\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a,p,b))\}

are transverse partitions of
\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a,p,b))\bmod{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
.
Note that
v_{n}^{\mathrm{gen}}(\mathbf{x})=v_{n}(\mathbf{x})
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\bigcup_{(a,p,b)\in E_{n}}\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a,p,b))
(but usually
h_{n}^{\mathrm{gen}}(\mathbf{x})\neq h_{n}(\mathbf{x})
).
Using these partitions, we shall prove that the mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
associated to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
has Property M and, hence, is a Markov partition. We then also show that this Markov partition is generating. Before we state the according result, we need some preparations. Indeed, the crucial part is to prove that the sets considered in Lemma
15.3
have small diameter; we will use strong convergence to prove this.

Lemma 15.2
.

Assume that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies the tiling condition with compact Rauzy fractals.
Then, for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
(a,p,b)\in E_{n}
,
(a^{\prime},p^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in E_{n+1}
, we have

\displaystyle\big(M_{\sigma_{n}}^{-1}\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a,p,b)){+}\mathbb{Z}^{d}\big)\cap\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n+1}(a^{\prime},p^{\prime},b^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\tilde{\pi}_{n+1}M_{\sigma_{n}}^{-1}\big(\boldsymbol{\ell}(p){+}\operatorname{int}(\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket)\big){-}\pi_{n+1}\boldsymbol{\ell}(p^{\prime}){-}M_{\sigma_{n+1}}\operatorname{int}(\mathcal{R}_{n+2}(b^{\prime}))&\mbox{if}\ a^{\prime}=b,\\
\emptyset&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof.

First note that

(15.6)

\displaystyle\big(M_{\sigma_{n}}^{-1}\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a,p,b)){+}\mathbb{Z}^{d}\big)\cap\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n+1}(a^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\tilde{\pi}_{n+1}M_{\sigma_{n}}^{-1}\big(\boldsymbol{\ell}(p){+}\operatorname{int}(\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket)\big){-}\operatorname{int}(\mathcal{R}_{n+1}(b))&\mbox{if}\ a^{\prime}=b,\\
\emptyset&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Indeed, we have

\displaystyle M_{\sigma_{n}}^{-1}\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a,p,b))+\mathbb{Z}^{d}

\displaystyle=M_{\sigma_{n}}^{-1}\tilde{\pi}_{n}\operatorname{int}(\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket)-M_{\sigma_{n}}^{-1}\pi_{n}\boldsymbol{\ell}(p)-\operatorname{int}(\mathcal{R}_{n+1}(b))+\mathbb{Z}^{d}

\displaystyle=M_{\sigma_{n}}^{-1}\tilde{\pi}_{n}\big(\boldsymbol{\ell}(p)+\operatorname{int}(\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket)\big)-\operatorname{int}(\mathcal{R}_{n+1}(b))+\mathbb{Z}^{d}

and
M_{\sigma_{n}}^{-1}\tilde{\pi}_{n}\big(\boldsymbol{\ell}(p){+}\operatorname{int}(\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a}\rrbracket)\big){-}\operatorname{int}(\mathcal{R}_{n+1}(b))\subset\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n+1}(b))
, thus the tiling property of
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n+1}
implies (
15.6
).
If we intersecting with
\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n+1}(a^{\prime},p^{\prime},b^{\prime}))
instead of
\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n+1}(a^{\prime}))
, we obtain the lemma.
∎

For
\ell\leq n\leq m
,
(a_{k},p_{k},b_{k})\in E_{k}
for
k\in\{\ell,\dots,m\}
, define

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}((a_{k},p_{k},b_{k})_{\ell\leq k\leq m})=\bigcap_{k=\ell}^{n-1}\big(M_{\sigma_{[k,n)}}^{-1}\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{k}(a_{k},p_{k},b_{k})){+}\mathbb{Z}^{d}\big)

\displaystyle\cap\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a_{n},p_{n},b_{n}))\cap\bigcap_{k=n+1}^{m}\big(M_{\sigma_{[n,k)}}\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{k}(a_{k},p_{k},b_{k})){+}\mathbb{Z}^{d}\big).

Lemma 15.3
.

Assume that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies the tiling condition with compact Rauzy fractals.
Let
\ell\leq n\leq m
,
(a_{k},p_{k},b_{k})\in E_{k}
for
k\in\{\ell,\dots,m\}
. Then

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}((a_{k},p_{k},b_{k})_{\ell\leq k\leq m})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=\ell}^{n-1}\tilde{\pi}_{n}M_{\sigma_{[k,n)}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\ell}(p_{k})+\tilde{\pi}_{n}M_{\sigma_{[\ell,n)}}^{-1}\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a_{\ell}}\rrbracket-\sum_{k=n}^{m}\pi_{n}\boldsymbol{\ell}(\sigma_{[n,k)}(p_{k}))-M_{\sigma_{[n,m)}}\mathcal{R}_{m+1}(b_{m})

if
b_{k}=a_{k+1}
for all
k\in\{\ell,\dots,m{-}1\}
, and
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}((a_{k},p_{k},b_{k})_{\ell\leq k\leq m})=\emptyset
otherwise.

Proof.

This follows by induction from Lemma
15.2
.
∎

The next theorem is the exact statement of Theorem
Theorem D
.
. The improvement with respect to Theorem
15.1
is that the Markov partition is now generating.

Theorem 15.4
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
d\geq 2
, be a sequence of unimodular substitutions that satisfies the tiling condition with compact Rauzy fractals.
Then the sequence
(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\mathrm{gen}})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
defined in (
15.5
) forms a nonstationary Markov partition for the mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
.
If moreover
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is two-sided primitive, then this nonstationary Markov partition is generating.

Proof.

We first prove Property M following the lines of the proof of Theorem
15.1
.
For each
\mathbf{x}\in(M_{\sigma_{n}}^{-1}\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a,p,b)){+}\mathbb{Z}^{d})\cap\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n+1}(a^{\prime},p^{\prime},b^{\prime}))
, we have
a^{\prime}=b
, thus

v_{n}^{\mathrm{gen}}(M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathbf{x})=v_{n}(M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathbf{x})\subset M_{\sigma_{n}}v_{n+1}(\mathbf{x})=M_{\sigma_{n}}v_{n+1}^{\mathrm{gen}}(\mathbf{x})

by the proof of Theorem
15.1
and, by Lemma
15.2
,

\displaystyle M_{\sigma_{n}}h_{n+1}^{\mathrm{gen}}(\mathbf{x})=M_{\sigma_{n}}\big(\tilde{\pi}_{n+1}\mathbf{x}{-}\operatorname{int}(\mathcal{R}_{n+1}(a^{\prime},p^{\prime},b^{\prime}))\big)

\displaystyle\subset\tilde{\pi}_{n}M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathbf{x}-M_{\sigma_{n}}\operatorname{int}(\mathcal{R}_{n+1}(b))=\tilde{\pi}_{n}M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathbf{x}+\pi_{n}\boldsymbol{\ell}(p)-\operatorname{int}(\mathcal{R}_{n}(a,p,b))

\displaystyle\equiv\tilde{\pi}_{n}(M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathbf{x}{-}\boldsymbol{\ell}(p))-\operatorname{int}(\mathcal{R}_{n}(a,p,b))\equiv h_{n}^{\mathrm{gen}}(M_{\sigma_{n}}\mathbf{x}),

where we have omitted
\bmod\ \mathbb{Z}^{d}
for readability.
Therefore, Property M holds.

Assume now that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is two-sided primitive.
To prove that the Markov partition
(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\mathrm{gen}})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is generating, we show that
(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}((a_{k},p_{k},a_{k+1})_{-m\leq k\leq m}))_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
, which is a nested sequence of sets, converges to a single point for all
(a_{k},p_{k},a_{k+1})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\prod_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}E_{k}
.
By Lemma
15.3
, we have

(15.7)

\hskip-5.0pt\bigcap_{m\to\infty}\hskip-5.0pt\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}((a_{k},p_{k},a_{k+1})_{-m\leq k\leq m})=\bigg\{\sum_{k=-\infty}^{n-1}\hskip-5.0pt\tilde{\pi}_{n}M_{\sigma_{[k,n)}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\ell}(p_{k})-\hskip-2.5pt\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\pi_{n}\boldsymbol{\ell}(\sigma_{[n,k)}(p_{k}))\bigg\}\hskip-5.0pt

when

(15.8)

\hskip-5.0pt\lim_{m\to\infty}\hskip-2.5pt\mathrm{diam}(\tilde{\pi}_{n}M_{\sigma_{[-m,n)}}^{-1}\llbracket\mathbf{e}_{a_{-m}}\rrbracket)=0\ \mbox{and}\ \lim_{m\to\infty}\hskip-2.5pt\mathrm{diam}(M_{\sigma_{[n,m+1)}}\mathcal{R}_{m+1}(b_{m}))=0.\hskip-5.0pt

The convergence in (
15.8
) guarantees convergence of the sums in (
15.7
).
To prove the first assertion of (
15.8
), note that, for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
a\in\mathcal{A}
, we have
\tilde{\pi}_{n}M_{\sigma_{[-m,n)}}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{a}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\mathbf{u}_{n}
and
M_{\sigma_{[-m,n)}}\tilde{\pi}_{n}M_{\sigma_{[-m,n)}}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{a}=\tilde{\pi}_{-m}\mathbf{e}_{a}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\setminus[1,\infty)^{d}
.
By primitivity in the past, all coordinates of
M_{\sigma_{[-m,n)}}
tend to
\infty
as
m\to\infty
, which implies that
\lim_{m\to\infty}\tilde{\pi}_{n}M_{\sigma_{[-m,n)}}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{a}=\mathbf{0}
.
To prove the second assertion, we want to apply
[
BST23
, Lemma 5.11]
.
Since we have assumed that
\mathcal{R}_{n}
is compact, the language
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)}
is balanced for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
by Lemma
12.2
.
As we already saw in the proof of Theorem
12.9
, the tiling condition implies that
\mathbf{u}_{n}
has rationally independent coordinates. Thus the conditions of
[
BST23
, Lemma 5.11]
are satisfied, i.e., we have
\lim_{m\to\infty}\sup\big\{\lVert\pi_{\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{1}}M_{\sigma_{[n,m+1)}}\,\boldsymbol{\ell}(w)\rVert\,:\,w\in\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(m+1)}\big\}=0
, which implies the second assertion of (
15.8
) by the definition of the Rauzy fractals. Therefore, the Markov partition
(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\mathrm{gen}})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is generating.
∎

As in Section
14.1
, we obtain the following version of Theorem
15.4
for multidimensional continued fraction algorithms.

Theorem 15.5
.

Let
(X,F,A,\mu)
be a positive multidimensional continued fraction algorithm with natural extension
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},A,\widehat{\mu})
. Let
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
be a substitutive realization of this algorithm. Suppose that, for
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\widehat{X}
, the sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies the tiling condition with compact Rauzy fractals.
Then the sequence
(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\mathrm{gen}})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
defined in (
15.5
) forms a nonstationary Markov partition for the mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
.
If moreover
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is two-sided primitive, then this nonstationary Markov partition is generating.

15.3.
Nonstationary edge shifts

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be as in Theorem
15.4
.
In the present section, we show that the refined Markov partitions
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\mathrm{gen}}
from (
15.5
) can be used to construct a measurable conjugacy between a certain nonstationary edge shift and the mapping family associated to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
. This measurable conjugacy is even one-to-one outside a “small” set. This result shows as well that the matrices of the mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
are the transposed inverses of the transition matrices of this nonstationary edge shift, which is a version of a theorem of Adler
[
Adl98
, Theorem 8.4]
and Manning
[
Man02
, Theorem 2.1]
.

As in Section
6.3
, we will define the required nonstationary edge shift by using
a Bratteli diagram. We therefore recall how to associate a Bratteli diagram (or Markov compactum) to a sequence of substitutions
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
in a natural way.

Definition 15.6

(Brattteli diagram associated to a sequence of substitutions; see e.g.
[
BSTY19
]
)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
V_{n}=\mathcal{A}
and

E_{n}=\big\{(a,p,b)\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}^{*}\times\mathcal{A}\,:\,pa\preceq\sigma_{n}(b)\big\}.

Put
V=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{n}
and
E=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}E_{n}
, and define the source and range maps by
s(a,p,b)=a
and
r(a,p,b)=b
, respectively. Then
\mathscr{B}=(V,E)
is the
Bratteli diagram of the sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
.

It is immediate from the definition that the transition matrices
A_{n}
of the Bratteli diagram of
(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
are equal to
\vphantom{\!M}{}^{t}\!M_{\sigma_{n}}
. Indeed, there are
|\sigma_{n}(b)|_{a}
edges with source vertex
a\in\mathcal{A}
and range vertex
b\in\mathcal{A}
in
\mathscr{B}
.

Let
\mathscr{B}
be the Bratteli diagram of a sequence
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
. We want to give an appropriate symbolic representation for the mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
in terms of the nonstationary edge shift
(X_{\mathscr{B}},\Sigma)
; see Definition
6.11
.
In particular, we establish the following theorem, generalizing
[
AF05
, Proposition 3.6]
.
Recall that the Rauzy boxes
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(a,p,b)
are defined in (
12.9
).

Theorem 15.7
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
d\geq 2
, be a sequence of unimodular substitutions that is two-sided primitive and satisfies the tiling condition with compact Rauzy fractals. Let
\mathscr{B}=(V,E)
be the Bratteli diagram associated to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
. Then the maps

\psi_{n}:\,X_{\mathscr{B}}^{(n)}\to\mathbb{T}_{n},\;(e_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mapsto\mathbf{x}\quad\mbox{such that}\ \bigcap_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\bigcap_{|k|\leq\ell}f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{n-k}(\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{k}(e_{k})))}=\{\mathbf{x}\},

with
\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{k}(e_{k}))
viewed as a subset of
\mathbb{T}_{k}
, are continuous and one-to-one except on the set of the pullbacks of the boundary of the Rauzy boxes
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(e)
,
e\in E_{n}
. Moreover, the diagram

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma}
\textstyle{X_{\mathscr{B}}^{(-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma}
\textstyle{X_{\mathscr{B}}^{(0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma}
\textstyle{X_{\mathscr{B}}^{(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma}
\textstyle{X_{\mathscr{B}}^{(2)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{-2}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{T}_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{-1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{T}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{T}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{T}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots}

is commutative. Therefore,
\varphi:X_{\mathscr{B}}\to\mathbb{T}
, defined by
\psi_{n}
on the component
X_{\mathscr{B}}^{(n)}
, is a measurable conjugacy between
(X_{\mathscr{B}},\Sigma)
and the mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
, where
\varphi
fails to be one-to-one only on the set of the pullbacks of the boundary of the Rauzy boxes
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}(e)
,
e\in E_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Recall that, because
\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{k}(e_{k})\in\mathbb{T}_{k}
, we have

(15.9)

f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{n-k}(\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{k}(e_{k})))=\begin{cases}M_{\sigma_{[n,k)}}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{k}(e_{k})&\text{if}\ k\geq n,\\[2.15277pt]
M_{\sigma_{[k,n)}}^{-1}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{k}(e_{k})&\text{if}\ k<n.\end{cases}

Then
\bigcap_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\bigcap_{|k|\leq\ell}f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{n-k}(\operatorname{int}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{k}(e_{k})))}\neq\emptyset
by Lemma
15.2
, and, since
(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\mathrm{gen}})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
with
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\mathrm{gen}}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, as in (
15.5
) is a generating Markov partition for
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
by Theorem
15.4
, this intersection contains exactly one element of
\mathbb{T}_{n}
(which is given by (
15.7
)). Thus
\pi_{n}:X_{\mathscr{B}}^{(n)}\to\mathbb{T}_{n}
is a well-defined mapping for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. It can be proved in exactly the same way as in the proof of
[
Adl98
, Theorem 6.5]
that
\pi_{n}
is one-to-one, except for the case when
(e_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
belongs to the preimage of the boundary of some
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{k}(e)
for
e\in E_{k}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. In this case,
\psi_{n}
is instead finite-to-one.

Therefore, the disjoint union
X_{\mathscr{B}}
projects essentially one-to-one to
\mathbb{T}
via
\varphi
. It is easy to check that the shift
\Sigma:X_{\mathscr{B}}^{(n)}\to X_{\mathscr{B}}^{(n+1)}
projects to the map
f_{n}:\mathbb{T}_{n}\to\mathbb{T}_{n+1}
, and the diagram commutes.
∎

Definition 15.8
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
15.7
, we say that
(X_{\mathscr{B}},\Sigma)
is a
symbolic model
of the mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
. If
\mathbf{M}
is the sequence of incidence matrices of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
, then we also say that
(X_{\mathscr{B}},\Sigma)
is a
symbolic model
of the mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}})
.

16.
Metric results on nonstationary Markov partitions

In this section, we present metric results on Markov partitions. The theory for mapping families is provided in Section
16.1
, multidimensional continued fraction algorithms are treated in Section
16.2
.
We will apply the results to the Brun continued fraction algorithm in Section
17
.

16.1.
Metric results on Markov partitions for mapping families

Let
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
be a dynamical system with
D\subset\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
[
D\subset\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
] and a shift invariant Borel measure
\nu
.
We want to prove that
\nu
-a.e. element of
D
admits a generating Markov partition whose atoms are given by Rauzy boxes.
Our goal is to apply Theorem
15.4
to almost all elements of the shift. The crucial condition in this theorem is the tiling condition from Definition
12.6
, sufficient conditons of which are provided in Section
12.4
.

The first result of this section is a version of Theorem
Theorem E
.
(from the introduction) for sequences of substitutions. It allows to associate a nonstationary Markov partition to almost every element of a shift of substitutive sequences. Recall the definition of Pisot condition from Definition
8.12
and the definition of pure discrete spectrum in Section
11.4
.

Theorem 16.1
.

Let
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
, with
D\subset\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
d\geq 2
, be an ergodic dynamical system that satisfies the Pisot condition. Assume that there is a periodic Pisot sequence having two-sided positive range and pure discrete spectrum.

Then, for
\nu
-almost every
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in D
, the linear eventually Anosov mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
associated to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
admits a generating nonstationary Markov partition, whose atoms are explicitly given by Rauzy boxes. This Markov partition provides a symbolic model for
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
as a nonstationary edge shift.

Proof of Theorem
16.1
.

By Theorem
13.11
, a.e.
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in D
satisfies the tiling condition with compact Rauzy fractals and is two-sided primitive. Therefore, all conditions of Theorem
15.4
are satisfied, and the result follows.
∎

We mention that the Pisot condition is the only crucial condition in this theorem. All the other conditions are easily checked.

While in Theorem
16.1
we started with a shift of sequences of substitutions, in the following theorem, we start with sequences of
d{\times}d
matrices
\mathbf{M}
and use a substitution assignment
\varrho:\mathcal{M}_{d}\to\mathcal{S}_{d}
(see Definition
11.17
) to associate a sequence of substitutions
\varrho(\mathbf{M})
and, by means of these substitutions, a nonstationary Markov partition, with it. This theorem corresponds to Theorem
Theorem E
.
from the introduction.

Theorem 16.2
.

Let
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
, with
D\subset\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
d\geq 2
, be an ergodic dynamical system that satisfies the Pisot condition. Assume that there is a substitution assignment
33
33

33

It is sufficient to define the substitution assignment on matrices occurring in
D
.

\varrho
on
\mathcal{M}_{d}
for which there is a periodic Pisot sequence in
\varrho(D)
having two-sided positive range in
(\varrho(D),\Sigma,\varrho_{*}\nu)
and pure discrete spectrum.

Then, for
\nu
-almost every
\mathbf{M}\in D
, the linear eventually Anosov mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}})
associated to
\mathbf{M}
admits a generating nonstationary Markov partition, whose atoms are explicitly given by Rauzy boxes of
\varrho(\mathbf{M})
. This Markov partition also provides a symbolic model for
(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}})
as a nonstationary edge shift.

Proof.

Theorem
16.2
follows immediately from Theorem
16.1
because the system
(\varrho(D),\Sigma,\varrho_{*}\nu)
satisfies all conditions of Theorem
16.1
.
∎

The following theorem shows that we can accelerate each shift in a way that the pure discrete spectrum assumption is no longer needed for the acceleration. In its statement, for a given
\mathbf{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{M}_{d}
and a given
p\in\mathbb{N}
, we use the notation
\mathbf{M}_{p}=(M_{[pn,p(n+1))})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
for the
p
-fold blocking of the sequence
\mathbf{M}
.

Theorem 16.3
.

Let
(D,\Sigma,\nu)
, with
D\subset\mathcal{M}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
d\geq 2
, be an ergodic dynamical system that satisfies the Pisot condition and has a periodic Pisot sequence with two-sided positive range.
Then there exists a positive integer
p
and a substitution assignment
\varrho
on
\mathcal{M}_{d}
such that, for
\nu
-almost every
\mathbf{M}\in D
, the linear eventually Anosov mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}_{p}})
associated to
\mathbf{M}_{p}
admits a generating nonstationary Markov partition, whose atoms are explicitly given by Rauzy boxes of
\varrho(\mathbf{M}_{p})
. This Markov partition also provides a symbolic model for
(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}_{p}})
as a nonstationary edge shift.

Proof.

By Theorem
13.12
, all conditions of Theorem
15.4
are satisfied.
∎

16.2.
Nonstationary Markov partitions for continued fraction algorithms

In this section, we use the results from Section
16.1
to establish results on nonstationary Markov partitions for multidimensional continued fraction algorithms.
Recall again some facts about multidimensional continued fraction algorithms stated earlier.
Let
(X,F,A,\nu)
be such an algorithm with natural extension
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
. In Definition
11.17
, we defined faithful substitutive realizations
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
of
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
.
If the algorithm
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
converges a.e. weakly in the future and in the past, then we know from (
11.7
) that the
\mathcal{S}
-adic shift
(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X}),\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}\nu)
is measurably conjugate to
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
.
By Lemma
11.18
, at each point
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
of convergence, the generalized right and left eigenvectors
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{v}
of
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
are representatives of the projective vectors
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, respectively. According to Section
11.6
, via
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
we can associate
\mathcal{S}
-adic mapping families
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})})
with
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
. Our aim is to prove that for almost all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\widehat{X}
the mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})})
admits a generating Markov partition. According to Theorem
15.7
, this Markov partition provides a symbolic model for
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})})
. We now state this precisely in the following statement, whose accelerated version, namely Theorem
16.5
, corresponds to the detailed formulation for Theorem
Theorem F
.

Note that, when the conditions of Remark
9.6
hold, then the set of parameters
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
in
\widehat{X}
has positive Lebesgue measure.
For the following statement, recall the Pisot condition from Definition
9.9
and the notation
\ll
from (
9.14
).

Theorem 16.4
.

Let
(X,F,A,\nu)
be a positive multidimensional continued fraction algorithm satisfying the Pisot condition and
\nu\circ F\ll\nu
. Let
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
be a faithful substitutive realization of a natural extension
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
of
(X,F,A,\nu)
. Assume that there is a periodic Pisot point
(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})\in\widehat{X}
such that
\mathbf{x}_{0}
has positive range in
(X,F,A,\nu)
and
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})
has pure discrete spectrum.
Then, for
\widehat{\nu}
-almost all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\widehat{X}
, the mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
associated to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is eventually Anosov and admits a generating nonstationary Markov partition, whose atoms are explicitly given by Rauzy boxes. This Markov partition provides a symbolic model for
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
as a nonstationary edge shift.

Proof.

Again this follows from Theorem
16.1
in the same way as Theorem
13.14
follows from Theorem
13.11
.
Indeed, as we saw in that proof, the Pisot condition for
(X,F,A,\nu)
implies that
(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X}),\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}\widehat{\nu})
satisfies the Pisot condition, and positive range of the periodic Pisot point
(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})\in\widehat{X}
in
(X,F,A,\nu)
implies that
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})
is a periodic Pisot sequence with two-sided positive range in
(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X}),\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}\widehat{\nu})
.
Thus the dynamical system
(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\widehat{X}),\Sigma,\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}\widehat{\nu})
satisfies all the conditions of Theorem
16.1
, which proves the theorem.
∎

Again, we can omit the pure discrete spectrum condition for a Pisot point when we accelerate the algorithm appropriately.

Theorem 16.5
.

Let
(X,F,A,\nu)
be a positive multidimensional continued fraction algorithm satisfying the Pisot condition,
\nu\circ F\ll\nu
. Let
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F},\widehat{A},\widehat{\nu})
be a natural extension of
(X,F,A,\nu)
. Assume that there is a periodic Pisot point
\mathbf{x}_{0}\in X
with
\mathbf{x}_{0}
having positive range in
(X,F,A,\nu)
. Then there exist a positive integer
p
and a (faithful) substitutive realization
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
of
(X,F^{p},A,\nu)
, such that the cocycle of the acceleration
(\widehat{X},\widehat{F}^{p},\widehat{\nu})
generates sequences
\mathbf{M}
of matrices whose eventually Anosov mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}})

\widehat{\nu}
-almost always admits a generating nonstationary Markov partition, with atoms that are explicitly given by Rauzy boxes. This Markov partition also provides a symbolic model for
(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}})
as a nonstationary edge shift.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
16.2
in the same way as Theorem
16.4
follows from Theorem
16.1
.
∎

17.
Nonstationary Markov partitions and induced rotations for the Brun algorithm

In this section, we want to apply Theorems
13.14
,
14.6
, and
16.4
to the 2- and 3-dimensional cases of the versions of the Brun continued fraction algorithm studied in Section
10
. We start with the unordered case. The substitutive realization
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{U}}
for this algorithm was set up in Example
11.19
.
Recall that, for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
, the rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{\mathbf{x}}
is defined on
\mathbf{1}^{\bot}/(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}^{\bot})
by
\mathfrak{r}_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{z})=\mathbf{z}+\mathbf{e}_{d}-\chi(\mathbf{x})
; see (
14.4
).
Moreover, under the tiling condition, the Rauzy fractal
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{\mathbf{1}}
is a fundamental domain of
\mathbf{1}^{\bot}/(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}^{\bot})
.

Corollary 17.1
.

For
d\in\{3,4\}
, let
(X_{\mathrm{U}},F_{\mathrm{U}},A_{\mathrm{U}},\nu_{\mathrm{U}})
be the unordered Brun algorithm. Let
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{U}}
be the faithful substitutive realization of this algorithm that is defined by the unordered Brun substitutions

\sigma_{\mathrm{U},ij}:\begin{cases}j\mapsto ij,\\
k\mapsto k&(k\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{j\})\end{cases}\qquad(i,j\in\mathcal{A},\,i\neq j).

Then, for
\widehat{\nu}_{\mathrm{U}}
-almost all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{U}}
, the following assertions hold.

(i)

The
\mathcal{S}
-adic dynamical system
(X_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})},\Sigma)
has pure discrete spectrum.

(ii)

The mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
associated to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathrm{U}}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}=\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is eventually Anosov and admits a generating nonstationary Markov partition, whose atoms are explicitly given by Rauzy boxes. This Markov partition provides a symbolic model for
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
as a nonstationary edge shift.

(iii)

The induced map of the rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{\mathbf{x}}
on the subset
\pi_{\chi(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{1}}\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A_{\mathrm{U}}^{(n)}(\mathbf{x})\mathcal{R}_{n}
of
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{\mathbf{1}}
equals (after renormalization) the rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{F_{\mathrm{U}}^{n}(\mathbf{x})}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

We need to make sure that the conditions of Theorems
13.14
,
14.6
, and
16.4
are satisfied for the algorithm
(X_{\mathrm{U}},F_{\mathrm{U}},A_{\mathrm{U}},\nu_{\mathrm{U}})
in the 2- and 3-dimensional case. The generic Pisot condition holds by Proposition
10.13
. The fact that
\nu_{\mathrm{U}}\circ F_{\mathrm{U}}\ll\nu_{\mathrm{U}}
follows from the fact that, according to Lemma
10.3
,
\nu_{\mathrm{U}}
is defined in terms of a measure with full support that is absolutely continuous w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure. It remains to find a periodic Pisot point
(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})\in\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{U}}
such that
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})
has pure discrete spectrum.

For
d=3
, we consider

(17.1)

\tau=\sigma_{\mathrm{U},12}\circ\sigma_{\mathrm{U},23}\circ\sigma_{\mathrm{U},31}:\,\begin{cases}1\mapsto 1321,\\
2\mapsto 21,\\
3\mapsto 321.\end{cases}

One easily checks that
\tau
is a Pisot substitution and, by standard algorithms like for instance the balanced pair algorithm from
[
Liv87
,
Liv92
]
, one verifies that the substitutive dynamical system generated by
\tau
has pure discrete spectrum.
Let
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{S}_{3}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be the periodic sequence with cycle
(\sigma_{\mathrm{U},12},\sigma_{\mathrm{U},23},\sigma_{\mathrm{U},31})
.
Then
\boldsymbol{\tau}
satisfies the admissibility property (
11.9
).
Let
(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})\in\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{U}}
such hat
\chi(\mathbf{x}_{0})
and
\chi(\mathbf{y}_{0})
are the generalized right and left eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{\tau}
.
Then
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})=\boldsymbol{\tau}
, hence
(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})
is a periodic Pisot point, which has positive range because of the Markov property (
10.8
), and
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})
has pure discrete spectrum.

For
d=4
, we know from
[
BST23
, Section 6.5]
34
34

34

In comparison with
[
BST23
]
, we revert the words in the images of the substitutions. This does not change the arguments.
that for

(17.2)

\tau=\sigma_{\mathrm{U},12}\circ\sigma_{\mathrm{U},23}\circ\sigma_{\mathrm{U},34}\circ\sigma_{\mathrm{U},41}:\ \begin{cases}1\mapsto 14321,\\
2\mapsto 21,\\
3\mapsto 321,\\
4\mapsto 4321,\end{cases}

we can create a Pisot point with positive range and pure discrete spectrum in the same way. Thus all the conditions of Theorems
13.14
,
14.6
, and
16.4
are satisfied for
d\in\{3,4\}
and the corollary is established.
∎

An analogous result is obtained for the ordered version of Brun’s algorithm. Namely, we get the following corollary. The substitutive realization
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{B}}
for this algorithm was set up in Example
11.21
. Moreover, the conditions of Remark
9.6
hold, i.e., the set
\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{B}}
has positive Lebesgue measure.

Corollary 17.2
.

For
d\in\{3,4\}
, let
(X_{\mathrm{B}},F_{\mathrm{B}},A_{\mathrm{B}},\nu_{\mathrm{B}})
be the ordered Brun continued fraction algorithm. Let
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{B}}
be the faithful substitutive realization of this algorithm defined by the ordered Brun substitutions
\sigma_{\mathrm{B},k}
,
k\in\mathcal{A}
, as in (
11.11
).

Then, for
\widehat{\nu}_{\mathrm{B}}
-almost all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{B}}
, the following assertions hold.

(i)

The
\mathcal{S}
-adic dynamical system
(X_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})},\Sigma)
has pure discrete spectrum.

(ii)

The mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
associated to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathrm{B}}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}=\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is eventually Anosov and admits a generating nonstationary Markov partition, whose atoms are explicitly given by Rauzy boxes. This Markov partition provides a symbolic model for
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
as a nonstationary edge shift.

(iii)

The induced map of the rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{\mathbf{x}}
on the subset
\pi_{\chi(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{1}}\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A_{\mathrm{B}}^{(n)}(\mathbf{x})\mathcal{R}_{n}
of
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{\mathbf{1}}
equals (after renormalization) the rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{F_{\mathrm{B}}^{n}(\mathbf{x})}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

Again, we verify the conditions of Theorems
13.14
,
14.6
, and
16.4
. As in Corollary
17.1
we see from Proposition
10.13
and Lemma
10.6
that the generic Pisot condition holds and that
\nu_{\mathrm{B}}\circ F_{\mathrm{B}}\ll\nu_{\mathrm{B}}
.
Here, the constant sequence
(\sigma_{\mathrm{B},1})
gives a periodic Pisot point for
d\in\{3,4\}
; note that
\sigma_{\mathrm{B},1}^{d}
is the substitution
\tau
from (
17.1
) and (
17.2
) for
d=3
and
d=4
respectively.
∎

From Corollary
17.2
, we derive the following exact formulation of Corollary
Corollary G
.
.

Corollary 17.3
.

Let
d\in\{3,4\}
. For Lebesgue a.e. pair of vectors
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
, we can provide an eventually Anosov mapping family

(\mathbb{T},f_{\mathbf{M}})=((\mathbb{T}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}},(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})

such that for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, in each
d
-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}_{n}
the mappings
M_{m}^{-1}\cdots M_{n}^{-1}
(
m<n
)
expand

\mathbf{u}_{n}
and
contract

\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\perp}
, where

\mathbf{u}_{n}=\begin{cases}M_{[0,n)}^{-1}\,\mathbf{u}&\text{for }m\geq 0,\\
M_{[n,0)}\,\mathbf{u}&\text{for }m<0,\end{cases}\qquad\mathbf{v}_{n}=\begin{cases}\vphantom{\!M}{}^{t}\!M_{[0,n)}\,\mathbf{v}&\text{for }m\geq 0,\\
\vphantom{\!M}{}^{t}\!M_{[n,0)}^{-1}\,\mathbf{v}&\text{for }m<0.\end{cases}

The mapping family can be constructed in a way that it admits a generating Markov partition and, hence, a symbolic model as a nonstationary edge shift.

Proof.

We saw in Section
10.2
that the domain
\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{B}}
of the natural extension of the ordered Brun algorithm equals
\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{B}}=X_{\mathrm{B}}\times X_{\mathrm{B}}^{*}
with
X_{\mathrm{B}}
and
X_{\mathrm{B}}^{*}
given in (
10.10
) and (
10.18
).
Clearly, up to a set of zero measure (here we take the Lebesgue measure on
\Delta^{d-1}
transferred to
\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0}^{d-1}
via the chart
\chi
),

\bigcup_{\zeta,\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}}(\zeta X_{\mathrm{B}}\times\eta X_{\mathrm{B}}^{*})=\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0}^{d-1}.

For
\zeta\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}
, we consider the algorithm
(\zeta X_{\mathrm{B}},\zeta F_{\mathrm{B}}\zeta^{-1},\zeta A_{\mathrm{B}}\zeta^{-1},\zeta_{*}\nu_{\mathrm{B}})
.
For each
\eta\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}
, the map

\widehat{F}_{\zeta,\eta}:\,\zeta X_{\mathrm{B}}\times\eta X_{\mathrm{B}}^{*}\to\zeta X_{\mathrm{B}}\times\eta X_{\mathrm{B}}^{*},\quad(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\mapsto(\zeta\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A(\mathbf{x})^{-1}\zeta^{-1}\mathbf{x},\eta A(\mathbf{x})\eta^{-1}\mathbf{y}),

defines a natural extension
(\zeta X_{\mathrm{B}}\times\eta X_{\mathrm{B}}^{*},\widehat{F}_{\zeta,\eta},\widehat{A}_{\zeta,\eta},\widehat{\nu}_{\zeta,\eta})
, and Corollary
17.2
remains true for the algorithms
(\zeta X_{\mathrm{B}},\zeta F_{\mathrm{B}}\zeta^{-1},\zeta A_{\mathrm{B}}\zeta^{-1},\zeta_{*}\nu_{\mathrm{B}})
,
\zeta\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}
, with each of these natural extensions. Thus we get eventually Anosov mapping families for a.e.
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0}^{d-1}
(w.r.t. the appropriate measures
\widehat{\nu}_{\zeta,\eta}
taken in each of the sets
\zeta X_{\mathrm{B}}\times\eta X_{\mathrm{B}}^{*}
). Because the generalized right and left eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
are affine representatives of
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
respectively, the corollary is true for
\widehat{\mu}_{\mathrm{B}}
-a.e.
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
. The result follows because
\widehat{\mu}_{\mathrm{B}}
is equivalent to the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
.
∎

It remains to treat the modified Jacobi–Perron algorithm; for a substitutive realization
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{M}}
of this algorithm we refer to Example
11.22
. The according result reads as follows.

Corollary 17.4
.

For
d\in\{3,4\}
, let
(X_{\mathrm{M}},F_{\mathrm{M}},A_{\mathrm{M}},\nu_{\mathrm{M}})
be the modified Jacobi–Perron continued fraction algorithm. Let
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{M}}
be the faithful substitutive realization of this algorithm that is defined by the multiplicative Brun substitutions
\sigma_{\mathrm{M},k,m}
,
1\leq k<d
,
m\geq 1
, defined in (
11.13
).

Then, for
\widehat{\nu}_{\mathrm{M}}
-almost all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\widehat{X}_{\mathrm{M}}
, the following assertions hold.

(i)

The
\mathcal{S}
-adic dynamical system
(X_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})},\Sigma)
has pure discrete spectrum.

(ii)

The mapping family
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
associated to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathrm{M}}=(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}=\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is eventually Anosov and admits a generating nonstationary Markov partition, whose atoms are explicitly given by Rauzy boxes. This Markov partition provides a symbolic model for
(\mathbb{T},f_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
as a nonstationary edge shift.

(iii)

The induced map of the rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{\mathbf{x}}
on the subset
\pi_{\chi(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{1}}\vphantom{\!A}^{t}\!A_{\mathrm{M}}^{(n)}(\mathbf{x})\mathcal{R}_{n}
of
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{\mathbf{1}}
equals (after renormalization) the rotation
\mathfrak{r}_{F_{\mathrm{M}}^{n}(\mathbf{x})}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

Again, we have to assure the conditions of Theorems
13.14
,
14.6
, and
16.4
. As in Corollary
17.1
, we see from Proposition
10.13
and Lemma
10.10
that the generic Pisot condition holds and that
\nu_{\mathrm{M}}\circ F_{\mathrm{M}}\ll\nu_{\mathrm{M}}
.
As in Corollary
17.2
, the constant sequence
(\sigma_{\mathrm{M,1,1}})
gives a periodic Pisot point for
d\in\{3,4\}
; note that
\sigma_{\mathrm{M,1,1}}=\sigma_{\mathrm{B,1}}
.
∎

Chapter VI
Concluding remarks

We end this work by providing open problems and perspectives for further research. Here, problems around the Pisot condition (see Definition
8.6
for its generic form) and the tiling condition (see Definition
12.6
) play an important role. We also propose to view multidimensional continued fraction algorithms as cross sections of the Weyl chamber flow (or a variant of it). This is inspired by the interpretation of the classical continued fraction algorithm as a cross-section of the geodesic flow on the unit tangent bundle of the modular surface. Finally, we dwell on different kinds of dynamics related to our theory and their interplay.

18.
Open problems

In all our results on nonstationary Markov partitions in Section
15
, we assume the tiling condition from Definition
12.6
, which is closely related to the notion of pure discrete spectrum, as recalled in Section
12.3
.
In
[
BST19
, Conjecture 3.5]
, we have formulated an
\mathcal{S}
-adic Pisot conjecture stating that this tiling condition holds under weak assumptions.
We slightly modify this conjecture using the local Pisot condition (see Definition
7.9
).

Conjecture 18.1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a primitive sequence of unimodular substitutions satisfying the local Pisot condition and
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert M_{n}\rVert=0
.
Then the tiling condition holds, and
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma,\mu)
has pure discrete spectrum.

This conjecture generalizes the classical Pisot conjecture for unimodular irreducible Pisot substitutions (see
[
ABB+15
]
) because a stationary directive sequence formed by a unimodular irrreducible Pisot substitution satisfies the conditions of this conjecture.
Since all infinite factor balanced shifts that we are aware of are defined by sequences of substitutions satisfying the conditions of Conjecture
18.1
, we even ask the following question.

Open problem 18.2
.

Let
(X,\Sigma,\mu)
be a factor balanced subshift.
Under which conditions does
(X,\Sigma,\mu)
have pure discrete spectrum?

Like in the substitutive setting, also in the primitive
\mathcal{S}
-adic setting, the tiling condition implies pure discrete measure-theoretic spectrum by Theorem
12.9
.
However, contrary to the substitutive case (see
[
BK06
]
and
[
BST23
, Lemma 4.9]
), it is not clear if the converse is true.

Conjecture 18.3
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
d\geq 2
, be a primitive sequence of unimodular substitutions that admits generalized right and left eigenvectors.
If
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(n)},\Sigma,\mu)
has pure discrete spectrum for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, then the tiling condition holds.

In Chapter
III
, we saw that multidimensional continued fraction algorithms give rise to important families of
\mathcal{S}
-adic shifts. For our theory to be applicable to these shifts, it is important that they satisfy the Pisot condition, according to
Section
16.2
. In
[
BST21
]
, we considered this question which is intimately related to strong convergence on these algorithms. In particular, we proved that the Selmer continued fraction algorithm (see
[
Sel61
]
) satisfies the Pisot condition for dimension
d\in\{3,4\}
. Besides that, in
[
BST21
]
we gave some numerical results that indicate that the second Lyapunov exponent of many well-known multidimensional continued fraction algorithms become positive from a certain dimension onwards.
More precisely these heuristic results point towards the following conjecture.

Conjecture 18.4
.

We conjecture the following convergence behavior of some classical multidimensional continued fraction algorithms.

•

The Brun continued fraction algorithm satisfies the Pisot condition if and only if
d\leq 10
.

•

The Jacobi–Perron continued fraction algorithm (see
[
Per07
]
) satisfies the Pisot condition if and only if
d\leq 10
.

•

The Selmer continued fraction algorithm satisfies the Pisot condition if and only if
d\leq 4
.

While the Pisot property of the Brun and the Selmer continued fraction algorithms would suffice to apply our theory to these algorithms in higher dimensions, there is an additional problem with the Jacobi–Perron algorithm. Indeed, we could not establish nonstationary Markov partitions for orbits of the Jacobi–Perron algorithm because the domain
\widehat{\Delta}
defined in Section
9.2
, that would be a candidate for a natural extension, seems to have “fractal structure”; see
[
AL18
, Section 8]
. In particular, the following is a long standing open question.

Open problem 18.5
.

Define the candidate
\widehat{\Delta}
for a natural extension of the Jacobi–Perron algorithm as described in Section
9.2
.
Does
\widehat{\Delta}
have positive Lebesgue measure?

This is also of interest because, according to Section
9.2
, the natural extension can be used to calculate the invariant measure of a continued fraction algorithm. According to Lemmas
10.3
and
10.6
, this invariant measure is known for the Brun algorithm in any dimension. For the Selmer algorithm, the invariant measure is provided in
[
BFK15
,
BFK19
]
for any dimension. However, for the Jacobi–Perron algorithm, even for
d=3
, we do not know an explicit formula for the invariant measure. The only thing we know is that its density is piecewisely analytic; see
[
Sch90
, Theorem 1]
and
[
Bro96
, Théorème 2.24]
.

Open problem 18.6
.

Is there an explicit formula for the density of the invariant measure of the Jacobi–Perron algorithm?

Let us focus now on the topological properties of the boundaries of the Markov partitions we get. The subtiles of our
\mathcal{S}
-adic Rauzy fractals seem to have fractal boundaries in all our examples. When looking at the stationary case, according to
[
Bow78
]
, each atom of a Markov partition has nonsmooth boundary for
d\geq 3
. We therefore formulate the following conjecture for the nonstationary case.

Conjecture 18.7

(cf.
[
AF01
, Section 10]
)
.

For
d\geq 3
, let
(X,f)
be an
\mathcal{S}
-adic mapping family which satisfies the Pisot condition from Definition
13.5
. Assume that
(X,f)
admits a nonstationary Markov partition
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
.
Then none of the atoms of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, has a smooth boundary.

Another direction of further research concerns the topology of the atoms of a nonstationary Markov partition or, equivalently, of the underlying Rauzy fractals. In the stationary case, such properties are studied for instance in
[
ST09
]
. We formulate the following problem.

Open problem 18.8

(cf.
[
AF01
, Section 10]
)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a sequence of substitution than satisfies the tiling condition. Study topological properties of the Rauzy fractals
\mathcal{R}_{n}
and their subtiles. For instance, can we give criteria for connectedness of
\mathcal{R}_{n}
?

Finally, we ask whether we can go beyond the present unimodular Pisot case. Indeed, in the present paper we only dealt with linear eventually Anosov mapping families satisfying the Pisot condition. It is natural to ask the following question.

Open problem 18.9
.

Is it possible to construct nonstationary Markov partitions for linear eventually Anosov mapping families whose hyperbolic splitting is
not
Pisot, i.e., if each part of the hyperbolic splitting has dimension at least
2
?

For examples in the stationary case, see e.g.
[
KV98
,
AFHI11
]
.

19.
Arithmetic and geometric coding of the Weyl chamber flow

We aim at linking a continued fraction map (in the sense of Definition
9.1
) to a flow on a homogeneous space generalizing the modular surface.
According to
[
Gor07
, Section 6]
, Ledrappier and Mozes proposed to use fractal tilings in order to get an arithmetic coding of the Weyl chamber flow, a higher-dimensional analog of the Teichmüller flow.
One motivation for the present work is to start with the realization of this program by using the
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-tilings induced by the Rauzy boxes
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
and their Markov properties.

We recall that the Weyl chamber flow is an action of the diagonal subgroup of
\operatorname{SL}(d,\mathbb{R})
on the space of lattices. More precisely, the
Weyl chamber flow
is the
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
-action on the right on the space of
d
-dimensional lattices
\operatorname{SL}(d,\mathbb{Z})\backslash\operatorname{SL}(d,\mathbb{R})
of the diagonal subgroup of
\operatorname{SL}(d,\mathbb{R})
of matrices

(19.1)

W(\mathbf{t})=W(t_{1},t_{2},\dots,t_{d})=\mathrm{diag}\big(e^{t_{1}},e^{t_{2}},\dots,e^{t_{d}}\big)

such that
t_{1}{+}t_{2}{+}\cdots{+}t_{d}=0
. For
d=2
, the flow
W(\mathbf{t})
is just the geodesic flow on the unit tangent bundle of the modular surface
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})\backslash\mathbb{H}^{2}\oplus\mathrm{SO}(2,\mathbb{R})\cong\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})\backslash\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Here,
\mathbb{H}^{2}
denotes the upper half plane.

The Weyl chamber flow acts on the space
\operatorname{SL}(d,\mathbb{Z})\backslash\operatorname{SL}(d,\mathbb{R})
of lattices while the natural extension of a multidimensional continued fraction algorithm acts on a subset of
\mathbb{P}^{d-1}{\times}\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
, as detailed in Section
9.2
.
If we want to code the Weyl chamber flow, then we need to relate these objects. The idea is to realize the natural extension of a continued fraction algorithm as a Poincaré section of this flow in terms of a geometric coding with Rauzy fractals. To make the Rauzy fractals fit together with this flow, a base change has to be performed by using the vectors of the Oseledets splitting defined in terms of the cocycle of the two-sided
\mathcal{S}
-adic system associated to the continued fraction.
The point is, for almost every lattice, to define a fundamental domain given by a Rauzy box. The flow contracts the vertical direction of the box and increases the measure of its horizontal slices; every time this measure becomes larger than 1, we can renormalize by a restacking construction, as illustrated in Figure
16
, corresponding to a change of basis in the lattice.

20.
An interplay between three dynamical systems

Let us come back to the dynamical setting described in the introduction. We have reached the goal described in Chapter
I
: We found a parametrized family of rotations which is stable by induction, and for which the renormalization is realized by the matrices
(M_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
provided by the orbit of the natural extension of the underlying multidimensional continued fraction algorithm; see Theorems
14.5
and
14.6
. Moreover, by using substitutive realizations and
\mathcal{S}
-adic shifts, we exhibited families of nonstationary Markov partitions to obtain measurable conjugacy to a symbolic model for the multidimensional continued fraction algorithm; see Sections
15
and
16
.
This is a rich dynamical situation, in the sense that these results involve the interplay of different kinds of dynamics, each of which has a symbolic, a geometric, and an arithmetic interpretation.

The first kind of dynamics is a family of
\mathcal{S}
-adic shifts. Each of these
\mathcal{S}
-adic shifts
(X_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\Sigma,\mu)
is defined on the orbit closure of an
\mathcal{S}
-adic sequence, which is defined in terms of a sequence of substitutions
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
; see Definition
11.1
. We assume that the tiling condition from Definition
12.6
holds.
This entails that the shift map
\Sigma
acting on the
\mathcal{S}
-adic shift
